### [24] [RouteBridge: Reliability-Routed Bidirectional Distillation Between Neural Radiance Fields and 3D Gaussian Splatting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09606)
*YuanHang Wang,Xin Cao*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: RouteBridge is a bidirectional knowledge distillation framework that adaptively selects teaching direction between NeRF and 3D Gaussian Splatting for each ray, using a reliability estimator that combines photometric residuals with geometric evidence to avoid propagating local reconstruction errors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing cross-representation distillation methods fix one representation as teacher for the entire scene, which can propagate local reconstruction errors. A globally fixed teacher cannot handle the complementary strengths and weaknesses of NeRF and 3DGS representations.

Method: RouteBridge uses a reliability estimator combining photometric residuals with representation-specific geometric evidence to route supervision for each ray in three directions: NeRF→3DGS, 3DGS→NeRF, or abstain. A renderer-independent interface transfers color, opacity, and normalized depth without requiring shared features or point correspondence.

Result: On mip-NeRF 360: NeRF export achieves 28.56 dB, 3DGS export achieves 28.77 dB. The 3DGS export improves over vanilla 3DGS by 1.56 dB and over NeRF-GS by 0.45 dB, while reducing LPIPS to 0.207. On static three-view DTU: achieves 21.12 dB. Ablations confirm both adaptive routing and geometric ray targets contribute to improvements.

Conclusion: Bidirectional adaptive routing between NeRF and 3DGS outperforms fixed-teacher distillation approaches. The reliability-based supervision selection effectively leverages complementary inductive biases of both representations without propagating local errors.

Abstract: Neural radiance fields (NeRFs) and 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) encode a scene with complementary inductive biases, but existing cross-representation distillation typically fixes one representation as teacher for the entire scene. A globally fixed teacher can propagate local reconstruction errors. We present RouteBridge, a bidirectional framework that selects the teaching direction for each ray. Its reliability estimator combines photometric residuals with representation-specific geometric evidence and routes supervision from NeRF to 3DGS, from 3DGS to NeRF, or abstains. A renderer-independent interface transfers color, opacity, and normalized depth without shared features or point correspondence. On mip-NeRF 360, the NeRF and 3DGS exports reach 28.56 and 28.77 dB, respectively. The 3DGS export improves over 3DGS by 1.56 dB and over NeRF-GS by 0.45 dB while reducing LPIPS to 0.207. On static three-view DTU, RouteBridge obtains 21.12 dB. Ablations show that both adaptive routing and geometric ray targets contribute to the improvement.

</details>


### [25] [Marker-free eye-gaze estimation using a single image and depth from defocus](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09610)
*David Hurtubise-Martin,Feriel Fass,Djemel Ziou,Marie-Flavie Auclair-Fortier*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: This paper presents a marker-free eye-gaze estimation approach using a single 2D camera, such as an integrated laptop webcam. The gaze-related features are estimated from iris localization and head pose estimated by using depth from defocus. A variational Bayesian multinomial logistic regression framework is used as mapping from the estimated features to the position of regard, based on an 8-dimensional feature vector of head-pose and iris-displacement parameters. No external marker is needed. Experiments were conducted by estimating the gaze of people watching a computer screen at different distances and compared against five existing methods. The obtained scores demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed approach.

### [26] [Hyperbolic Geometry for Open-World Object Detection in Remote Sensing Imagery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09626)
*Wuzhou Li,Jiawei Zhou,Shenghang Wang,Xiang Li*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes HyRS-OWOD, a hyperbolic geometry-based framework for open-world object detection in remote sensing imagery. It addresses the limitation of Euclidean spaces in capturing hierarchical relationships among object categories by using hyperbolic embeddings. The method includes a two-step unknown-object discovery mechanism (DOL and HUL) and a Hyperbolic Metric Learning (HML) strategy for incremental learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing OWOD methods use Euclidean spaces which inadequately represent the latent hierarchical relationships among object categories in remote sensing imagery, limiting unknown-object recall and incremental-learning performance.

Method: 1) Decoupled Objectness Learning (DOL) module: disentangles foreground perception from semantic information to separate foreground proposals from background regions. 2) Hyperbolic Uncertainty Learning (HUL): leverages the radius of hyperbolic embeddings as an uncertainty-aware cue for known-unknown discrimination. 3) Hyperbolic Metric Learning (HML): enhances inter-class separability to facilitate incorporating novel categories while mitigating catastrophic forgetting.

Result: Consistent improvements in unknown recall and incremental learning over state-of-the-art OWOD methods on three remote sensing benchmarks.

Conclusion: Hyperbolic geometry is effective for OWOD in remote sensing imagery, providing better representation of hierarchical relationships and improving both unknown-object discovery and incremental learning capabilities.

Abstract: Open-world object detection (OWOD) extends closed-set detection by requiring models to identify unknown objects and incrementally learn them once annotations become available. In remote sensing imagery, object categories often exhibit latent hierarchical relationships that may be inadequately represented in the Euclidean spaces commonly adopted by existing methods, limiting unknown-object recall and incremental-learning performance. To address this issue, we investigate hyperbolic geometry for OWOD in remote sensing imagery and propose HyRS-OWOD. To improve unknown object recall, we design a two-step unknown-object discovery mechanism: a Decoupled Objectness Learning (DOL) module that disentangles foreground perception from semantic information to separate foreground proposals from background regions, followed by a Hyperbolic Uncertainty Learning (HUL) component that leverages the radius of hyperbolic embeddings as an uncertainty-aware cue for known-unknown discrimination. For incremental learning, we develop a Hyperbolic Metric Learning (HML) strategy that enhances inter-class separability, facilitating the incorporation of novel categories while mitigating catastrophic forgetting. Experiments on three remote sensing benchmarks demonstrate consistent improvements in unknown recall and incremental learning over state-of-the-art OWOD methods.

</details>


### [27] [LightMedSeg-ISLES: Stroke Lesion Segmentation with 81x Fewer Parameters than nnU-Net](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09634)
*Giorgi Nikvashvili,Hanxue Gu,Jie Bao,Kang Wang,Yang Yang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: LightMedSeg is a lightweight (1.26M parameter) pipeline for T1-weighted stroke lesion segmentation in ISLES'26, achieving 0.618 Dice and 0.599 lesion-wise F1, retaining 97.5% of nnU-Net's Dice performance while requiring 81.4× fewer parameters and 4.7× fewer FLOPs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large neural networks achieve strong performance in medical image segmentation but face deployment challenges due to high storage and computational demands. This work aims to develop a compact model that maintains competitive accuracy while being more efficient for practical deployment.

Method: LightMedSeg uses flip test-time augmentation (TTA) with a four-pass configuration. The model is trained with extended training duration and stronger data augmentation. Performance is evaluated on a 146-case held-out cohort and compared against large models including nnU-Net ResEnc-L (102.35M parameters), filtered UNETR++, and nnFormer.

Result: LightMedSeg achieves 0.618 mean Dice and 0.599 lesion-wise F1. The nnU-Net ResEnc-L achieves 0.634 Dice and 0.544 lesion-wise F1 after size filtering. LightMedSeg retains 97.5% of nnU-Net's Dice with 81.4× fewer parameters while improving lesion-wise F1 by 0.055. It requires 4.7× fewer FLOPs per standardized patch and slightly exceeds filtered UNETR++ and nnFormer. Extended training and stronger augmentation add 0.0358 Dice without increasing model capacity.

Conclusion: LightMedSeg establishes a strong single-checkpoint alternative to much larger models, demonstrating that efficient design with appropriate training strategies can achieve near state-of-the-art performance with dramatically reduced computational requirements, making it more suitable for practical deployment in clinical settings.

Abstract: Large networks and ensembles often lead medical image segmentation challenges, but their storage and inference demands complicate deployment. We present LightMedSeg-ISLES, a 1.26-million-parameter pipeline for T1-weighted stroke lesion segmentation in ISLES'26. On a 146-case held-out cohort, flip test-time augmentation produces 0.618 mean Dice and 0.599 lesion-wise F1. A 102.35-million-parameter nnU-Net ResEnc-L produces 0.634 Dice and 0.544 lesion-wise F1 after size filtering. LightMedSeg therefore retains 97.5\% of nnU-Net's Dice with 81.4$\times$ fewer parameters while improving lesion-wise F1 by 0.055. Its four-pass TTA operating point requires 4.7$\times$ fewer FLOPs per standardized patch than nnU-Net. It also slightly exceeds filtered UNETR++ and nnFormer. Longer training and stronger augmentation add 0.0358 Dice without increasing capacity, establishing a strong single-checkpoint alternative to much larger models.

</details>


### [28] [Recovering Biomechanical Signals from Missing Keypoints Using Temporal Interpolation in Monocular Gait Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09670)
*Shubham Jariwala*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper evaluates a simple first-order temporal interpolation scheme to recover missing ankle keypoints in monocular pose estimation for gait analysis. The method achieves near-baseline accuracy (1.1° error vs baseline) while being computationally trivial, suggesting that complex learned models may be unnecessary for gait recovery in resource-constrained settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Monocular pose estimation is sensitive to missing keypoints from occlusion or detection errors, but prior work focuses on complex learned reconstruction models. The effectiveness of simple temporal methods remains underexplored, despite gait signals having inherent temporal redundancy.

Method: The researchers evaluated knee-angle estimation under a missing-ankle-keypoint condition using a first-order temporal interpolation scheme. They tested on 527 frames of monocular walking video (428 with valid baseline detections), comparing baseline (no missing keypoints), missing ankle (no recovery), and interpolated recovery conditions.

Result: Removing the ankle keypoint increased mean angular error to 23.4° ± 46.7° and collapsed signal variance to near zero. Temporal interpolation reduced error to 1.1° ± 6.7° and restored variance and smoothness to within a few percent of baseline.

Conclusion: Gait signals possess sufficient temporal redundancy for simple interpolation to recover missing joints without learned models. This supports low-complexity, real-time-compatible designs for gait analysis in resource-constrained or occlusion-prone monocular settings.

Abstract: Monocular pose estimation enables low-cost gait analysis but is sensitive to missing keypoints caused by occlusion, detection errors, or efficiency-driven model reduction. While prior work on recovering missing joints focuses on complex learned models, the effectiveness of simple temporal methods remains underexplored. We evaluate knee-angle estimation under a missing-ankle-keypoint condition and test a first-order temporal interpolation scheme as a recovery mechanism. Across 527 frames of monocular walking video (428 with valid baseline detections), removing the ankle keypoint increased mean angular error to 23.4° +/- 46.7° and collapsed signal variance to near zero. Temporal interpolation reduced error to 1.1° +/- 6.7° and restored variance and smoothness to within a few percent of baseline. These results indicate that gait signals possess sufficient temporal redundancy for a simple, computationally trivial interpolation scheme to recover a critical missing joint, without resorting to learned reconstruction models. The findings support low-complexity, real-time-compatible designs for gait analysis in resource-constrained or occlusion-prone monocular settings.

</details>


### [29] [Cross-Species Animal Re-Identification with Semantic Consistency Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09705)
*Shuoyi Chen,Yuejia Li,Mang Ye*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes Semantic Consistency Learning (SCL), a framework for generalizable animal Re-Identification across different species. SCL addresses the challenge of learning transferable representations when animal species have vastly different anatomical structures and visual patterns. The method uses two components: FDSNorm for region-aware style normalization and CNM for capturing cross-species relational structures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Unlike person ReID where different domains share similar body structures, animal species exhibit drastically different anatomical structures and visual patterns. This makes it difficult to establish shared visual correspondences, causing representations learned across species to form fragmented embedding spaces that severely limit cross-species generalization. There is a need for methods that can learn representations stable across appearance variations while preserving semantic structures shared across species.

Method: The proposed SCL framework consists of two complementary components: (1) Foreground-Background Decoupled Spectral Normalization (FDSNorm) - stabilizes feature statistics by suppressing environment-induced style variations in a region-aware manner; (2) Cross-species Neighborhood Modeling (CNM) - captures transferable relational structures across species through dynamic feature neighborhoods.

Result: Extensive experiments on 11 public animal ReID datasets demonstrate that SCL consistently outperforms state-of-the-art methods under multiple cross-species evaluation protocols and generalizes effectively to previously unseen species and ecological domains.

Conclusion: The SCL framework successfully addresses the cross-species generalization challenge in animal ReID by combining region-aware style normalization with cross-species relational modeling. This approach enables effective recognition of individual animals across diverse species and ecological contexts, showing strong generalization to unseen species and domains.

Abstract: Generalizable animal Re-Identification (ReID) aims to recognize individual animals across species with diverse morphologies and ecological contexts. Unlike person ReID, where different domains share similar body structures, animal species often exhibit drastically different anatomical structures and visual patterns, making it difficult to establish shared visual correspondences. As a result, representations learned across species tend to form fragmented embedding spaces, which severely limits cross-species generalization. To address this challenge, we propose Semantic Consistency Learning (SCL), a framework designed to learn representations that remain stable across appearance variations while preserving semantic structures shared across species. SCL consists of two complementary components. Foreground-Background Decoupled Spectral Normalization (FDSNorm) stabilizes feature statistics by suppressing environment-induced style variations in a region-aware manner, while Cross-species Neighborhood Modeling (CNM) captures transferable relational structures across species through dynamic feature neighborhoods. Extensive experiments on 11 public animal ReID datasets demonstrate that SCL consistently outperforms state-of-the-art methods under multiple cross-species evaluation protocols and generalizes effectively to previously unseen species and ecological domains. Code is available at https://github.com/Kemalau/ECCV-26-SCL.

</details>


### [30] [VFNet: Multi-View Spatio-Temporal Model for Void Fraction Estimation in Gas-Liquid Two-Phase Flow](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09711)
*Md Adnan Faisal Hossain,Raghav Rajeev,Kumar Nishant,Justin A Weibel,Satish Kumar,Fengqing Zhu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: VFNet is a dual-branch spatio-temporal neural network that predicts void fraction (gas phase proportion) in gas-liquid two-phase flow from synchronized multi-view videos, combining local spatial feature extraction with global spatio-temporal flow evolution modeling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Void fraction is a key parameter in two-phase flow characterization, but existing methods either rely on non-generalizable flow assumptions or require intrusive sensing that disturbs flow behavior. This paper aims to develop a non-intrusive, generalized void fraction estimation method.

Method: VFNet uses two branches: (1) a local branch extracting features from confined spatial regions and fusing synchronized dual views, and (2) a spatio-temporal branch capturing global flow evolution across space and time to refine a coarse geometric estimate. The network is trained on simulated CFD data with known ground-truth void fractions.

Result: VFNet achieves the best performance across a broad range of metrics compared to learning-based and traditional baselines, and also improves downstream flow-pattern classification on real two-phase flow data.

Conclusion: The dual-branch architecture effectively combines local spatial information with global temporal dynamics for accurate void fraction prediction, demonstrating generalization from simulated to real data and utility for downstream flow analysis tasks.

Abstract: Void fraction, which quantifies the proportion of the fluid flow volume occupied by the gas phase, is a key parameter in the characterization of gas-liquid two-phase flow. Existing estimation methods either rely on flow assumptions that do not generalize across different fluids or on intrusive sensing that disturbs the flow behavior. We propose VFNet, a dual-branch spatio-temporal neural network for void-fraction prediction from synchronized multi-view videos of two-phase flow. A local branch extracts features from confined spatial regions and fuses the synchronized dual views, while a spatio-temporal branch captures the global evolution of the flow across space and time to refine a coarse geometric estimate. Trained on simulated computational fluid dynamics (CFD) data with known ground-truth void fractions and evaluated against both learning-based and traditional baselines, VFNet achieves the best performance across a broad range of metrics and also improves downstream flow-pattern classification on real two-phase flow data.

</details>


### [31] [Distilling Image Prototypes for Guided Test-Time Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09737)
*Liwen Wang,Xingbo Dong,Iman Yi Liao,Deyin Liu,Massimo Tistarelli,Lin Yuanbo Wu,Zhe Jin*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: DIPTTA is a novel Test-Time Adaptation framework that addresses error accumulation and catastrophic forgetting through two key mechanisms: a Distill Image Prototype (DIP) for dynamic feature replay and source-calibrated uncertainty estimation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Test-Time Adaptation (TTA) faces two critical challenges: (1) error accumulation from noisy pseudo-labels, and (2) catastrophic forgetting of source knowledge. Existing uncertainty-based approaches are overconfident or computationally expensive, while prototype replay strategies rely on static representations that become misaligned during adaptation.

Method: The proposed DIPTTA introduces a Distill Image Prototype (DIP) - a compact set of synthetic images serving as a dynamic and regenerative anchor of source knowledge. This enables: (1) a dynamic feature replay mechanism that generates feature prototypes aligned with the current model state, and (2) a source-calibrated uncertainty estimation method that provides less biased sample reliability measures by leveraging stable source knowledge.

Result: Extensive experiments on multiple benchmarks demonstrate that DIPTTA significantly outperforms state-of-the-art methods, particularly under severe domain shifts.

Conclusion: DIPTTA effectively addresses both error accumulation and catastrophic forgetting in TTA through its dynamic prototype mechanism and source-calibrated uncertainty estimation, showing superior performance especially in severe domain shift scenarios.

Abstract: Test-Time Adaptation (TTA) enhances the robustness of models against distribution shifts but faces two critical challenges: error accumulation from noisy pseudo-labels and catastrophic forgetting of source knowledge. Uncertainty-based approaches designed to mitigate error accumulation often yield overconfident or computationally expensive estimates, while strategies intended to prevent forgetting via prototype replay rely on static representations that easily become misaligned as the model adapts. To address these issues, this paper proposes a novel framework, Distilling Image Prototype for Guided Test-Time Adaptation (DIPTTA). The core of the proposed approach is the introduction of a Distill Image Prototype (DIP), a compact set of synthetic images that serves as a dynamic and regenerative anchor of source knowledge. This prototype enables a dynamic feature replay mechanism that continuously generates feature prototypes aligned with the current state of the model, thus effectively preventing catastrophic forgetting. Furthermore, the DIP anchors a source-calibrated uncertainty estimation method, which provides a less biased measure of sample reliability by leveraging stable source knowledge, thereby robustly suppressing error accumulation. Extensive experiments on multiple benchmarks demonstrate that DIPTTA significantly outperforms state-of-the-art methods, particularly under severe domain shifts. The source code is available at https://github.com/LiwenWang919/DIPTTA.

</details>


### [32] [MethaneFuse: Learning from Multi-Sensor Satellite Observations for Methane Plume Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09762)
*Yuyao Wang,Juliana Y. Leung,Di Niu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper introduces MethaneUnion, a multi-sensor satellite dataset for methane plume detection, and MethaneFuse, a model that learns from heterogeneous satellite observations under partial sensor availability. The dataset expands usable coverage from 3,211 to 8,981 plume cases by combining Sentinel-2, Landsat 8/9, EMIT, and Sentinel-5P observations. MethaneFuse achieves 84.87 F1 and 93.62 AUROC at 480m resolution, outperforming baselines by 5.65 F1 and 8.30 AUROC points.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Methane plume detection from satellite imagery is limited by incomplete observations—public satellites provide complementary data but real plume cases rarely have fully paired multi-sensor measurements due to revisit schedules, cloud coverage, and transient emissions. Most existing detectors rely on single-sensor inputs (especially Sentinel-2), leaving many reported plume cases unusable.

Method: The authors constructed MethaneUnion, a temporal multi-sensor dataset built from Carbon Mapper plume reports matched with Sentinel-2 (S2), Landsat 8/9 (L8/9), EMIT, and Sentinel-5P (S5P) observations. Using this dataset, they developed MethaneFuse, which learns from heterogeneous satellite observations under partial sensor availability without requiring complete four-sensor measurements.

Result: MethaneUnion expands usable coverage from 3,211 valid S2-matched plume cases to 8,981 reported plume cases with multi-sensor observations. At 480m resolution, MethaneFuse achieves 84.87 F1 and 93.62 AUROC, improving over the strongest baseline by 5.65 F1 and 8.30 AUROC points while reducing false positives by 8.19 points. Sensor-availability experiments show MethaneFuse improves detection when S2 is available and transfers knowledge to L8/9, EMIT, and S5P when S2 is unavailable.

Conclusion: The results demonstrate the value of learning from incomplete heterogeneous sensor observations for practical methane plume detection. The approach successfully handles partial sensor availability and transfers plume knowledge across different satellite sensors.

Abstract: Methane plume detection from satellite imagery is constrained by incomplete observations: public satellites provide complementary spatial, spectral, and atmospheric evidence, but real plume cases rarely contain fully paired multi-sensor measurements because of revisit schedules, cloud coverage, acquisition quality, and the transient nature of emissions. Most learning-based detectors rely on single-sensor inputs, especially Sentinel-2 (S2), leaving many reported plume cases unusable.
  We construct MethaneUnion, a temporal multi-sensor dataset built from Carbon Mapper plume reports and matched S2, Landsat 8/9 (L8/9), EMIT, and Sentinel-5P (S5P) observations. Built on MethaneUnion, MethaneFuse learns from heterogeneous satellite observations under partial sensor availability without requiring complete four-sensor measurements.
  MethaneUnion expands usable coverage from 3,211 valid S2-matched plume cases to 8,981 reported plume cases with multi-sensor observations. At the representative 480 m setting, MethaneFuse achieves 84.87 F1 and 93.62 AUROC, improving over the strongest baseline by 5.65 F1 and 8.30 AUROC points while reducing false positives by 8.19 points. Sensor-availability experiments show that MethaneFuse improves detection when S2 is available and transfers plume knowledge to L8/9, EMIT, and S5P when S2 is unavailable. These results demonstrate the value of learning from incomplete heterogeneous sensor observations for practical methane plume detection.

</details>


### [33] [LogiScope-VQA: Benchmarking Vision-Language Models for Logistics Hazard Identification in Industrial Scenarios](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09790)
*Hanjing Zhou,Mingze Yin,Ying Lian,Jun Ma,Chang-Yu Hsieh,Yanbing Zhou*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: LogiScope-VQA is a benchmark dataset for evaluating Large Multimodal Models (LMMs) in industrial warehouse settings, addressing the scarcity of real industrial data for hazard-oriented perception, understanding, and reasoning tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The deployment of LMMs in industrial warehouses requires human-expert-level hazard perception capabilities, but the scarcity of real industrial data (commercial terms) significantly hampers further advancement. This gap necessitates a practical benchmark to evaluate mainstream LMMs in real-world logistics operations.

Method: Curated LogiScope-VQA comprising 2,476 images and 2,918 videos from real logistics parks, with 10,274 VQAs annotated by humans. Based on 18 core objects and 20 risk types, with 39 subtasks across three themes: industrial element perception, warehouse knowledge understanding, and potential risk reasoning. Includes dynamic thinking-budget configurations and dual-dimensional risk bias analyses.

Result: Even powerful proprietary models (GPT-5.5, Gemini-3.1-Pro, Claude-Opus-4.7) show significant performance gap relative to human experts. The challenge of jointly integrating perception, understanding, and reasoning for hazard identification remains substantial. A pervasive security bias issue was identified that impedes LLMs' practical deployment.

Conclusion: LogiScope-VQA reveals substantial headroom for LMM improvement in industrial hazard perception. The joint integration of perception, understanding, and reasoning poses the primary challenge. Additionally, security bias must be addressed for real-world deployment. The dataset is publicly available under CC BY-NC-SA 4.0 license.

Abstract: Large Multimodal Models (LMMs) large-scale deployment in industrial warehouse settings specifically necessitates that models exhibit human-expert-level hazard-oriented perception, understanding, and reasoning capabilities. However, the scarcity of real industrial data, tightly coupled to commercial terms, significantly hampers further advancement. To bridge this gap, we curate LogiScope-VQA to investigate the practical applicability of mainstream LMMs in real-world logistics operations. LogiScope-VQA comprises 2,476 images and 2,918 videos primarily sourced from real-world logistics parks, along with 10,274 VQAs meticulously curated and validated by human annotators. Grounded in 18 core objects and 20 risk types, we devise 39 subtasks aligned with three principal themes: industrial element perception, warehouse knowledge understanding, and potential risk reasoning. Furthermore, we incorporate dynamic thinking-budget configurations and dual-dimensional risk bias analyses to elucidate the properties of LMMs. Extensive experiments unveil that even powerful proprietary models, including GPT-5.5, Gemini-3.1-Pro, and Claude-Opus-4.7, exhibit a significant gap relative to human performance. The unique challenge of jointly integrating perception, understanding, and reasoning for hazard identification poses substantial headroom for further improvement on LogiScope-VQA. We additionally reveal the pervasive security bias issue that impedes LLMs' practical deployment in real-world settings. The industrial dataset is publicly available under the CC BY-NC-SA 4.0 license.

</details>


### [34] [Freezing of Gait Prediction Under Spatial Occlusion: An IMU-Supervised Cross-Modal Distillation Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09826)
*Chandan Biswas,Aryan Singh,Anabik Pal*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes a cross-modal subspace distillation framework for freezing-of-gait (FOG) detection in Parkinson's disease patients, combining the accuracy of IMU-based sensors with the practicality of video-based detection. The method uses invariant latent topologies from a kinematic oracle to supervise a visual architecture, with a dual-stream model that dynamically adjusts reliance based on tracking confidence to handle occlusions during turning tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current FOG detection methods have limitations: video-based approaches suffer from detection errors during continuous turning due to geometric self-occlusion degrading pose estimation, while IMU-based approaches are accurate but difficult to deploy outside clinical settings due to the need for secure sensor attachment. The authors aim to combine the strengths of both modalities.

Method: The proposed framework uses: (1) A pre-trained kinematic oracle that extracts invariant latent topologies to structurally supervise a non-encoded visual architecture during training; (2) A dual-stream visual model that probabilistically fuses skeletal graph nodes and continuous spatial pixels, dynamically shifting reliance to uninterrupted pixel boundaries as joint tracking confidence drops during severe spatial occlusion.

Result: Empirical results on a public multi-modal sequence dataset of Parkinson's individuals executing continuous 360° turns demonstrate that applying sensory boundary topologies strictly mitigates tracking evaluation entropy. The constrained optimisation confirms that highly precise FOG prediction bounds can be achieved over zero-wearable inference environments.

Conclusion: The cross-modal subspace distillation framework successfully combines IMU accuracy with video-based practicality, achieving precise FOG prediction without requiring wearable sensors. This approach effectively handles occlusion issues during turning tasks through dynamic fusion of skeletal and pixel data, enabling deployment outside clinical settings.

Abstract: Parkinson's disease is a progressive neurodegenerative disorder characterised by gradual deterioration of movement control. Automated freezing-of-gait (FOG) detection supports the objective assessment of gait-related motor impairment. Two common approaches are used for FOG prediction: (i) analysing video recordings of the patient's movements and (ii) analysing data collected using inertial measurement unit (IMU) wearable sensors attached to the patient's lower limbs. Video-based approaches may suffer detection errors during continuous turning-in-place tasks because the lower limbs undergo substantial geometric self-occlusion, degrading pose-estimation accuracy. IMU-based approaches are generally less affected by visual occlusion; however, they are difficult to deploy outside clinical or laboratory settings, as the sensors must be attached securely and remain in place throughout the assessment. Motivated by this, we propose a cross-modal subspace distillation framework to mitigate the limitations of unimodal FOG detection by combining IMU accuracy with video-based practicality. We extract invariant latent topologies from a pre-trained kinematic oracle to structurally supervise a non-encoded visual architecture during training. To resolve periods of severe spatial occlusion, a dual-stream visual model probabilistically fuses skeletal graph nodes and continuous spatial pixels, dynamically shifting reliance to uninterrupted pixel boundaries as joint tracking confidence drops. Evaluated against a public, multi-modal sequence dataset of Parkinson's individuals executing continuous $360^\circ$ turns, empirical results demonstrate that applying sensory boundary topologies strictly mitigates tracking evaluation entropy. Our constrained optimisation confirms that highly precise FOG prediction bounds can be achieved over zero-wearable inference environments.

</details>


### [35] [Layerwise Tunable Lifting Scheme for the Convolutional Neural Network](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09827)
*Abdumannon Yovkochov,An Le,Sungbal Seo,You-Suk Bae,Truong Nguyen*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes a family of tunable lifting schemes for biorthogonal wavelet filter banks using a lattice-based lifting structure. Three strategies are introduced: low-pass tuning, high-pass tuning, and sequential joint adaptation. The methods are evaluated on image classification (ResNet-18, DTD) and anomaly detection (MVTec-AD, KRC102S), showing consistent performance improvements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to develop flexible and tunable wavelet filter banks that can adapt to different image processing tasks while maintaining mathematical guarantees of invertibility and stability. Traditional wavelet filter banks are fixed, limiting their adaptability to specific applications.

Method: The paper proposes three lifting strategies using a lattice-based lifting structure: (1) LS-LayLatt-LP for low-pass tuning, (2) LS-LayLatt-HP for high-pass tuning, and (3) LS-LayLatt-Sequential for joint adaptation of both branches. The lattice structure ensures invertibility and stability for arbitrary parameter values within the lifting functions.

Result: Experimental results demonstrate consistent performance improvements across all evaluated tasks: image classification on the Describable Textures Dataset (DTD) using ResNet-18, and anomaly detection on hazelnut images from MVTec-AD and private KRC102S datasets.

Conclusion: The proposed tunable lifting schemes successfully integrate with deep learning architectures (ResNet-18) and provide performance gains in both classification and anomaly detection tasks, with the mathematical guarantees of invertibility and stability.

Abstract: This work introduces a family of tunable lifting schemes for biorthogonal wavelet filter banks. We propose three lifting strategies: low-pass tuning (LS-LayLatt-LP), high-pass tuning (LS-LayLatt-HP), and a sequential lifting scheme that jointly adapts low- and high-frequency branches (LS-LayLatt-Sequential). All proposed designs are formulated using a lattice-based lifting structure, which guarantees invertibility and stability for arbitrary parameter values within the lifting functions. We evaluated the proposed methods by integrating them into a ResNet-18 backbone for image classification on the Describable Textures Dataset (DTD), as well as for anomaly detection on hazelnut images from the MVTec-AD dataset and private KRC102S dataset. Experimental results demonstrate consistent performance improvements across all evaluated tasks.

</details>


### [36] [SkNeXt enables topology-guided neuronal reconstruction from petabyte-scale microscopy data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09832)
*Jiayi Ding,Hu Zhao*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SkNeXt is a topology-first framework for scalable neuronal reconstruction from large volumetric microscopy datasets (TB-PB scale). It converts neuronal morphology into compact SWC skeletons that serve as both proofreading targets and spatial indices for selective data access, reducing reconstruction cost to scale with neuronal morphology rather than total dataset size.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-resolution fluorescence and electron microscopy produce terabyte- to petabyte-scale brain datasets, making complete neuronal reconstruction prohibitively expensive in computation, data movement, and manual proofreading due to the need to process entire imaging volumes.

Method: SkNeXt uses a topology-first approach: (1) Convert neuronal morphology into compact SWC skeletons preserving long-range connectivity; (2) Perform proofreading on sparse neuronal trees to correct branch, continuity, and connectivity errors; (3) Use corrected skeletons as persistent structural priors for recovering detailed morphology while preserving identity and topology; (4) Use skeletons as spatial indices for selective data access, retrieving high-resolution image regions only along reconstructed trajectories.

Result: Successfully reconstructed neurons from a petabyte-scale super-resolution fluorescence dataset of the mouse brain on a single GPU within one week, without requiring exhaustive dense inference across the complete imaging volume.

Conclusion: SkNeXt enables efficient neuronal reconstruction by bypassing most background and signal-free volumes through skeleton-based selective data access, allowing reconstruction cost to scale with neuronal morphology rather than total dataset size.

Abstract: Recent advances in high-resolution fluorescence and electron microscopy have enabled nanoscale imaging across increasingly large brain volumes, but the resulting terabyte- to petabyte-scale datasets make complete neuronal reconstruction prohibitively expensive in computation, data movement, and manual proofreading. Here, we present SkNeXt, a topology-first framework for scalable neuronal reconstruction from large volumetric microscopy datasets. Instead of densely processing entire image volumes, SkNeXt first converts neuronal morphology into compact SWC skeletons that preserve long-range connectivity. Proofreading is therefore focused on sparse neuronal trees, allowing branch, continuity, and connectivity errors to be corrected before high-resolution reconstruction. The corrected skeletons then serve as persistent structural priors for recovering detailed morphology while preserving neuronal identity and topology. Crucially, SkNeXt also uses neuronal skeletons as spatial indices for selective data access, retrieving high-resolution image regions only along reconstructed trajectories and bypassing most background and signal-free volumes. This substantially reduces I/O and computational overhead, allowing reconstruction cost to scale with neuronal morphology rather than total dataset size. Using SkNeXt, we reconstructed neurons from a petabyte-scale super-resolution fluorescence dataset of the mouse brain on a single GPU within one week, without requiring exhaustive dense inference across the complete imaging volume.

</details>


### [37] [Pretraining and Distillation Matter More Than Architecture Family for Label-Free Single-Cell Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09863)
*Philip Graemer,Giuseppe Di Caprio*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper presents a controlled benchmark comparing CNNs (EfficientNet) and transformers (ViT, EVA-02) for label-free single-cell classification on LIVECell microscopy data, using source-image-disjoint splits to prevent data leakage. The study disentangles effects of architecture, pretraining, fine-tuning, tokenisation, and distillation, finding that pretraining is more important than architecture choice, while knowledge distillation enables compact models to outperform larger teachers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To resolve conflicting conclusions from previous microscopy benchmarks about CNNs versus transformers for single-cell classification, by using rigorous controlled experiments with proper data splitting and matched protocols.

Method: Controlled benchmark on LIVECell phase-contrast microscopy data using source-image-disjoint train/validation/test splits to prevent parent-image leakage. Matched optimisation, augmentation, and evaluation protocols across EfficientNet, Vision Transformer (ViT), and EVA-02 models. Systematic evaluation of pretraining effects, architectural choices, tokenisation strategies, and knowledge distillation.

Result: 1) Pretraining improves macro-F1 by 3-4 points and explains most of the previously reported CNN advantage. 2) Gap between best pretrained CNN and transformer is below 0.5 points. 3) ViT-S/8 outperforms ViT-S/16 and matches 4× larger ViT-B/16 at quarter parameters. 4) Layer-wise learning-rate decay degrades performance. 5) Compact EfficientNet-B0 students distilled from teacher councils outperform all individually trained backbones including EfficientNet-B5 and EVA-02 teachers.

Conclusion: Rigorous control of pretraining and evaluation is essential for interpreting biomedical architecture benchmarks. Pretraining matters more than architecture choice. Knowledge distillation is a more effective route to practical single-cell classification than architecture choice alone, as it enables compact models to outperform larger teachers.

Abstract: Choosing a deep learning architecture for label-free single-cell classification remains an open question, with microscopy benchmarks reporting conflicting conclusions about CNNs versus transformers. We present a controlled benchmark on LIVECell phase-contrast microscopy data using source-image-disjoint train/validation/test splits to prevent parent-image leakage and matched optimisation, augmentation, and evaluation protocols across EfficientNet, Vision Transformer (ViT), and EVA-02 models. This allows the effects of architecture, pretraining, fine-tuning, tokenisation, and distillation to be disentangled. We find that the previously reported CNN advantage is largely explained by pretraining rather than architecture: the smallest pretrained model outperforms the strongest model trained from scratch despite far fewer parameters. Pretraining improves macro-F1 by 3-4 points, while the gap between the best pretrained CNN and transformer is below 0.5 points. Architectural choices nevertheless matter: ViT-S/8 outperforms ViT-S/16 and matches the four-times-larger ViT-B/16 at a quarter of the parameters, showing that finer tokenisation benefits small cell crops. Conversely, layer-wise learning-rate decay, central to the EVA-02 fine-tuning recipe, degrades performance, highlighting that transfer heuristics from natural-image recognition may not generalise to microscopy. Finally, knowledge distillation substantially improves the deployment frontier: compact EfficientNet-B0 students distilled from teacher councils outperform every individually trained backbone, including the EfficientNet-B5 and EVA-02 teachers. Overall, our results show that rigorous control of pretraining and evaluation is essential for interpreting biomedical architecture benchmarks, while distillation may be a more effective route to practical single-cell classification than architecture choice alone.

</details>


### [38] [From Pixels to Hierarchical Sequences: Quadtree Mask Encoding for Vision-Language Binary Change Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09876)
*Xiao An,Ruikang Zhang,Chen Zhong,Xuli Shen,Jiaxing Sun,Jiang Wu,Wei He*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: QUAKE-CD is a framework for dense change detection in remote sensing that represents binary change masks as grammar-constrained quadtree token sequences, enabling syntax-verifiable structured generation within autoregressive VLMs. It outperforms decoder-based and flat text-as-mask approaches, achieving 78.31% accumulated F1.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing VLMs for change detection are limited to captioning outputs or rely on external decoders with flat text-as-mask serialization, which are ineffective for small and fragmented changes. There is a need for compact, syntactically checkable, and deterministically decodable mask representations within autoregressive generation.

Method: QUAKE-CD represents binary change masks as grammar-constrained quadtree token sequences. QUAKE-CoT pairs these sequences with chain-of-thought traces grounded in visual evidence, using progressive curriculum learning followed by grammar-gated dual-reward reinforcement learning to jointly optimize textual reasoning and spatial dense prediction.

Result: QUAKE-CD achieves 78.31% accumulated F1 on QUAKE-CoT, outperforming decoder-based and flat text-as-mask VLMs while producing more faithful bi-temporal reasoning.

Conclusion: Recasting dense change prediction as syntax-verifiable structured generation using quadtree token sequences is effective for remote sensing change detection, enabling compact, checkable, and decodable representations within autoregressive VLMs.

Abstract: Dense change detection in remote sensing requires vision-language models (VLMs) to compare bi-temporal images and generate accurate pixel-level masks. Existing VLMs are largely confined to change captioning outputs, and the few that produce pixel-level masks still rely on external decoders or flat text-as-mask serialization, which are less effective for small and fragmented changes. We introduce QUAKE-CD, a framework that recasts dense change prediction as syntax-verifiable structured generation. QUAKE-CD represents binary change masks as grammar-constrained quadtree token sequences, making the masks compact, syntactically checkable, and deterministically decodable within an autoregressive generation space. We further construct QUAKE-CoT, which pairs these sequences with chain-of-thought traces grounded in visual evidence, and jointly optimizes textual reasoning and spatial dense prediction through a progressive curriculum followed by grammar-gated dual-reward RL. On QUAKE-CoT, QUAKE-CD achieves 78.31% accumulated F1, outperforming decoder-based and flat text-as-mask VLMs while producing more faithful bi-temporal reasoning.

</details>


### [39] [CLFTv2: Efficient Camera-LiDAR Fusion for Semantic Segmentation via Hierarchical Feature Pyramids](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09881)
*Toomas Tahves,Mauro Bellone,Raivo Sell*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: CLFTv2 is a hierarchical camera-LiDAR fusion framework for semantic segmentation in autonomous driving that replaces global ViT attention with a Swin-based multi-scale encoder and lightweight FPN-style residual decoder. It achieves state-of-the-art VRU detection performance while being more computationally efficient than query-based decoders.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Semantic segmentation for autonomous driving requires reliable detection of vulnerable road users (VRUs) despite heavy class imbalance. The prior CLFT model used global ViT attention which has computational overhead. This work aims to improve efficiency while maintaining or improving VRU detection accuracy.

Method: CLFTv2 uses a Swin-based multi-scale encoder with shifted-window attention and a lightweight FPN-style residual decoder. It operates in the 2D perspective domain and integrates multi-scale geometric cues through per-scale residual fusion, avoiding the computational overhead of query-matching decoders.

Result: On ZOD, CLFTv2-Large achieves 53.5% mIoU, improving pedestrian IoU from 35.5% to 44.9% over the prior CLFT model. On Waymo, CLFTv2 reaches 61.7% mIoU. A modality-isolation study suggests ViT's global receptive field yields stronger fusion gains only under dense LiDAR returns. Compared to Swin-based Mask2Former, CLFTv2 requires 1.4× fewer GFLOPs and delivers 2.2× higher throughput while achieving comparable overall accuracy.

Conclusion: Hierarchical local-attention fusion offers an efficient, scalable alternative to global-attention and query-based decoders for real-time on-vehicle perception in intelligent transportation systems.

Abstract: Semantic segmentation for autonomous driving requires reliable detection of vulnerable road users (VRUs) despite heavy class imbalance. We introduce CLFTv2, a hierarchical camera-LiDAR fusion framework replacing global ViT attention with a Swin-based multi-scale encoder and a lightweight FPN-style residual decoder. Operating in the 2D perspective domain, CLFTv2 integrates multi-scale geometric cues through shifted-window attention and per-scale residual fusion, avoiding the computational overhead of query-matching decoders. Across three driving datasets, CLFTv2 consistently improves VRU recall. On ZOD, CLFTv2-Large achieves 53.5\% mIoU, improving pedestrian IoU from 35.5\% to 44.9\% over the prior CLFT model. On Waymo, CLFTv2 reaches 61.7\% mIoU. Additionally, a modality-isolation study suggests ViT's global receptive field yields stronger fusion gains only under dense LiDAR returns. Compared to a Swin-based Mask2Former adaptation, CLFTv2 requires 1.4$\times$ fewer GFLOPs and delivers 2.2$\times$ higher throughput, while achieving comparable overall accuracy. These results demonstrate that hierarchical local-attention fusion offers an efficient, scalable alternative to global-attention and query-based decoders for real-time on-vehicle perception in intelligent transportation systems. Source code is publicly available.

</details>


### [40] [Albedo Estimation via Latent Bridge Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09884)
*Carme Corbi,David Serrano-Lozano,Javier Vazquez-Corral,Maria Vanrell*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper addresses three key limitations in Intrinsic Image Decomposition (IID) for albedo estimation: physical consistency, computational cost, and generalization. The proposed solution uses a Latent Bridge Matching (LBM)-based architecture with pixel reconstruction loss, shading conditioning, and albedo-conditioned shading estimation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Recent advances in IID rely on generative models but face three critical challenges: insufficient physical consistency, high inference computational cost, and limited generalization across diverse datasets. The authors propose LBM as an effective solution to address all three limitations simultaneously.

Method: The paper introduces a novel LBM-based architecture with three key components: (1) pixel reconstruction loss to enforce physical consistency, (2) LBM's inherent efficiency for low-cost inference, and (3) shading conditioning to improve generalization across datasets. Additionally, the shading estimator is conditioned on predicted albedo to further improve reconstruction fidelity.

Result: The method is benchmarked against state-of-the-art IID methods across five real and synthetic datasets, demonstrating improved performance in terms of physical consistency, inference efficiency, and generalization capabilities.

Conclusion: Latent Bridge Matching (LBM) effectively addresses the three key limitations in IID for albedo estimation. The proposed architecture achieves better physical consistency through reconstruction loss, reduced computational cost via LBM inference, and improved generalization through shading conditioning and albedo-conditioned shading estimation.

Abstract: Recent advances in Intrinsic Image Decomposition (IID) have increasingly relied on generative models. However, progress remains limited by three key challenges: (a) insufficient physical consistency, (b) high computational cost at inference time, and (c) limited generalization capabilities. In this work, we show that latent bridge matching (LBM) effectively addresses these limitations for albedo estimation. We introduce a novel LBM-based architecture that enforces physical consistency through a pixel reconstruction loss, benefits from the inherent efficiency of LBM low-cost inference, and improves generalization across diverse datasets by incorporating a shading conditioning. In this extended version, we additionally show that conditioning the shading estimator itself on the predicted albedo further improves reconstruction fidelity, and we benchmark our best model against stateof-the-art IID methods across five real and synthetic datasets.

</details>


### [41] [StreetDiff: Multi-view Street Scenes Generation via Cross-view Consistent Multi-view Stable Diffusion with Structure Prompts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09890)
*Qi Zhang,Yanyifan Wang,Weiyuan Zhang,Hui Huang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: StreetDiff is a multi-view diffusion model designed to generate consistent urban street scenes by explicitly enforcing cross-view alignment during denoising. It addresses object duplication, structural distortion, and layout inconsistency issues that existing methods face in complex urban environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multi-view diffusion models struggle with cross-view consistency under camera rotation in structurally complex urban environments, lacking explicit modeling of spherical correspondence across views.

Method: The framework introduces a Panorama-Perspective Synergy design that decouples global layout reasoning from local detail synthesis, and incorporates a Panorama Alignment Module (PAM) that establishes spherical-projection-based attention constraints across views. The method injects structured alignment constraints without modifying the diffusion backbone.

Result: StreetDiff significantly improves structural consistency and visual fidelity compared to prior multi-view diffusion generation methods in challenging urban street scene generation tasks.

Conclusion: The proposed approach achieves robust cross-view coherence in urban street scene generation by leveraging spherical-projection-based alignment constraints while maintaining the diffusion backbone architecture.

Abstract: Multi-view diffusion models have shown strong performance in scenes with strong geometric priors and sparse semantics, such as indoor rooms or simple outdoor environments (e.g., fields, courtyards). However, they often fail to maintain cross-view consistency under camera rotation, especially in structurally complex urban environments. Without explicit modeling of spherical correspondence across views, existing approaches tend to produce object duplication, structural distortion, and layout inconsistency. To address this limitation, we propose StreetDiff, a multi-view diffusion framework that explicitly enforces cross-view alignment during denoising. StreetDiff introduces a Panorama--Perspective Synergy design to decouple global layout reasoning from local detail synthesis, and incorporates a Panorama Alignment Module (PAM) that establishes spherical-projection-based attention constraints across views. By injecting structured alignment constraints without modifying the diffusion backbone, our framework achieves robust cross-view coherence in challenging urban street scene generation tasks. In addition, we construct Street360, a large-scale HDR multi-view urban panorama dataset. Extensive experiments demonstrate that StreetDiff significantly improves structural consistency and visual fidelity compared to prior multi-view diffusion generation methods.

</details>


### [42] [Can We Trust Video Hallucination Detectors? VidHalLoc for Evaluating the Evaluators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09895)
*Xinyu Chen,Adnan Mahmood,Mark Dras*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: VidHalLoc is a benchmark for evaluating hallucination detection methods in video-language models and video agents, using 2,000 adversarial samples across Video QA and Video Captioning tasks, covering Ontology and Dynamic hallucination categories. The study introduces VideoHALO, a multi-agent workflow for efficient benchmark construction. Evaluation of 15 methods shows limited reliability with peak accuracy of only 34.63%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Video-language models and video agents can produce hallucinations that conflict with spatiotemporal evidence. Existing benchmarks mainly evaluate model hallucinations, and heterogeneous mechanisms make detector reliability difficult to compare. There was a need for a unified diagnostic evaluation protocol to assess hallucination detection methods.

Method: The researchers introduced VideoHALO, a Harness Engineering-informed multi-agent workflow that decomposes data construction into four executable stages supported by a memory system and a communication protocol. They constructed VidHalLoc with 2,000 adversarial hallucination samples across Video Question Answering and Video Captioning tasks, spanning Ontology and Dynamic hallucination categories.

Result: Evaluation of fifteen methods reveals that the four dedicated detectors peak at an Overall accuracy of only 34.63%, indicating limited reliability across video hallucination types.

Conclusion: Current hallucination detection methods have limited reliability across different video hallucination types. TheVidHalLoc benchmark provides a unified diagnostic evaluation protocol for comparing detection methods, highlighting the need for improved hallucination detection in video-language models.

Abstract: Video-language models and video agents can produce hallucinations that conflict with spatiotemporal evidence. Existing benchmarks mainly evaluate model hallucinations, and heterogeneous mechanisms make detector reliability difficult to compare. We introduce VidHalLoc, a benchmark that evaluates hallucination detection methods under a unified diagnostic evaluation protocol using 2,000 adversarial hallucination samples across Video Question Answering and Video Captioning tasks, spanning Ontology and Dynamic hallucination categories. To construct VidHalLoc efficiently, we introduce VideoHALO, a Harness Engineering-informed multi-agent workflow that decomposes data construction into four executable stages supported by a memory system and a communication protocol. Evaluation of fifteen methods reveals that the four dedicated detectors peak at an Overall accuracy of only 34.63%, indicating limited reliability across video hallucination types [Dataset Repository: https://huggingface.co/datasets/wesfggfd/VidHalLoc].

</details>


### [43] [Multimodal Emotion Recognition in Conversations via Class-Wise Adaptive Modality Fusion and Affective Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09924)
*Oriol Marín,Roger Marí,Gloria Haro,Rafael Redondo*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper extends Self-Distillation Transformer architecture for Emotion Recognition in Conversations (ERC) by incorporating three key enhancements: appearance+geometry visual representations, class-wise adaptive modality fusion, and valence-arousal prior for affective transitions. The method is evaluated on MELD and IEMOCAP datasets, showing complementary benefits from structured facial cues, emotion-dependent modality weighting, and affective geometry.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Emotion Recognition in Conversations (ERC) requires integrating heterogeneous multimodal cues (textual, audio, visual) while accounting for conversational context and emotional dynamics. The motivation is to improve multimodal ERC by addressing limitations in visual representations (using geometry cues), modality fusion (using class-wise adaptive weighting), and emotional dynamics (using valence-arousal prior).

Method: The paper proposes three enhancements to the Self-Distillation Transformer architecture: (1) Geometry-enhanced visual representations combining appearance and facial geometry features; (2) Class-wise adaptive modality fusion replacing original softmax gate with emotion-class-specific fusion weights; (3) Valence-arousal prior incorporated into the model to capture affective transitions in conversations.

Result: On MELD dataset: geometry features improve weighted F1 by 0.27 points; class-wise adaptive fusion gains 0.17 points over softmax gate; valence-arousal prior improves emotionally shifted utterances by 0.30 accuracy points. On IEMOCAP dataset: geometry features improve weighted F1 by 4.36 points; adaptive fusion gains 0.25 points; valence-arousal prior improves emotionally shifted utterances by 0.74 accuracy points.

Conclusion: Structured facial cues (geometry), emotion-dependent modality weighting (class-wise adaptive fusion), and affective geometry (valence-arousal prior) provide complementary benefits for multimodal ERC. The proposed enhancements collectively improve performance, with geometry features providing the largest gains, particularly on IEMOCAP dataset.

Abstract: Emotion Recognition in Conversations (ERC) requires integrating heterogeneous textual, audio, and visual cues while accounting for conversational context and emotional dynamics. We extend the Self-Distillation Transformer architecture for ERC with appearance+geometry visual representations, class-wise adaptive modality fusion, and a valence-arousal prior for affective transitions. On the MELD and IEMOCAP datasets, geometry-enhanced visual representations improve weighted F1 by 0.27 and 4.36 points over appearance-only features, respectively, while class-wise adaptive fusion provides further gains of 0.17 and 0.25 points over the original softmax gate. The valence-arousal prior yields targeted improvements of 0.30 and 0.74 accuracy points on emotionally shifted utterances while preserving performance on stable turns. These results indicate that structured facial cues, emotion-dependent modality weighting, and affective geometry provide complementary benefits for multimodal ERC.

</details>


### [44] [Vague2Detect: Handling Ambiguous Prompts in Knowledge-Based Open-World Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09949)
*Ibrohimjon Muminov,Jihie Kim*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Vague2Detect is a hybrid object detection pipeline that addresses YOLO-World's limitations in handling ambiguous/vague language prompts by combining a fine-tuned Sentence-BERT for candidate retrieval from a Knowledge Base, YOLO-World for visual verification, and GPT-3.5-turbo for dynamic KB expansion. The approach achieves 61% VPSR (up to 85% with GPT fallback), compared to YOLO-World's baseline of 32%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world detectors must interpret functional or ambiguous prompts, but conventional models like YOLO are restricted to fixed class lists. Even open-vocabulary models like YOLO-World misalign vague language with intended objects. This limits practical deployment in dynamic household environments.

Method: Vague2Detect uses a hybrid pipeline: (1) Fine-tuned Sentence-BERT retrieves candidate objects from a structured household Knowledge Base (KB); (2) YOLO-World verifies candidate presence in the image; (3) For prompts outside the KB, GPT-3.5-turbo generates candidate descriptions to dynamically expand the KB for novel concepts.

Result: On a household scene benchmark (custom images + Open Images V7 subset): YOLO-World alone achieves 32% VPSR. Vague2Detect improves to 61% VPSR with high precision. With GPT fallback augmentation, performance reaches up to 85% VPSR.

Conclusion: The hybrid approach effectively bridges the gap between vague language queries and visual object detection by leveraging semantic matching via Sentence-BERT, visual verification via YOLO-World, and dynamic knowledge expansion via LLMs. This significantly improves the ability to map ambiguous queries to correct detections in household scenes.

Abstract: Real-world detectors must often interpret functional or ambiguous prompts, yet conventional models such as YOLO remain restricted to fixed class lists. Even open-vocabulary models like YOLO-World frequently misalign vague language with the intended objects. Building on our prior work Commonsense-Guided Open-World Object Detection Using LLMs and Visual-Semantic Matching, we address YOLO-World's limitations in grounding task-driven queries. We propose Vague2Detect, a hybrid pipeline in which a fine-tuned Sentence-BERT retrieves candidates from a structured household Knowledge Base (KB), and YOLO-World verifies their presence in the image. For prompts outside the KB, a large language model (GPT-3.5-turbo) generates candidate descriptions, dynamically expanding the KB to cover novel concepts. On a benchmark of household scenes using custom images and an Open Images V7 subset, YOLO-World alone achieves only 32% Vague Prompt Success Rate (VPSR), the ability to map ambiguous queries to correct detections. In contrast, Vague2Detect improves performance to 61% VPSR with high precision, and up to 85% when augmented with GPT fallback.

</details>


### [45] [Putting Captions to the Test: Evaluating Video Caption Quality through Multiple-Choice Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09973)
*Zizhen Wang,Bo Feng,Zhengfeng Lai,Shiyu Li,Yang Lu,Meng Cao,Ping Huang,Xiaoming Simon Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: CapQuiz is a novel reference-free benchmark for evaluating video captioning in VLLMs. It assesses caption quality through the lens of information fidelity by testing how well captions help answer human-verified, fine-grained multiple-choice questions derived from videos. The benchmark includes 10 question types across 24 video domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing video captioning metrics rely on lexical matching against ground-truth references, which fails to account for the "one-to-many" nature of video descriptions where valid captions can differ in wording. Current metrics are also one-dimensional and lack fine-grained analysis capabilities.

Method: Redefining caption quality through information fidelity: maximizing coverage of salient visual information while ensuring strict factuality. CapQuiz uses human-verified, fine-grained multiple-choice questions derived from videos to assess caption utility, with a hierarchical taxonomy of 10 question types spanning Descriptive and Inferential categories across 24 video domains.

Result: CapQuiz correlates significantly better with human judgments than existing metrics and provides interpretable insights into model performance across different question types and video domains.

Conclusion: CapQuiz offers a more effective and nuanced approach to evaluating video captioning for VLLMs by moving beyond reference-based matching to a question-answering paradigm that better captures information fidelity and provides fine-grained, interpretable performance analysis.

Abstract: Evaluating video captioning remains a critical challenge for Visual Large Language Models (VLLMs). Existing metrics primarily rely on matching generated text against ground-truth references. This paradigm suffers from the ``one-to-many'' nature of video description, where high-quality captions are often penalized for lexical mismatches or valid shifts in visual focus. Furthermore, such assessments are typically one-dimensional, failing to provide a fine-grained analysis of caption quality. To address this, we redefine caption quality through the lens of information fidelity: A caption must maximize the coverage of salient visual information while ensuring strict factuality. We introduce CapQuiz, a novel reference-free benchmark that assesses captions based on their utility in answering human-verified, fine-grained, multiple-choice questions derived from the video. CapQuiz features a hierarchical taxonomy of 10 question types (spanning Descriptive and Inferential categories) across 24 diverse video domains. Extensive experiments demonstrate that CapQuiz correlates significantly better with human judgments than existing metrics and offers interpretable insights into model performance.

</details>


### [46] [From Few-Shot Segmentation to Clinician-in-the-Loop Medical Image Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10001)
*Yazhou Zhu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This Perspective paper reframes few-shot medical image segmentation (FSMIS) as a sequential clinician-model decision problem with separate support budget (K) and interaction budget (B). The framework addresses domain shift challenges by enabling selective expert feedback allocation through expected value of information, rather than assuming fixed task-defining evidence at inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current FSMIS formulations assume task-defining evidence is fixed before inference, which is fragile when query cases exhibit acquisition shift, atypical pathology, ambiguous boundaries, or poor image quality. Existing solutions (prototype learning, cross-domain matching, interactive segmentation, uncertainty estimation, test-time adaptation, promptable foundation models) address parts of this problem but have not been jointly evaluated under a common model of expert attention and clinical risk.

Method: The paper proposes a sequential decision framework where at each step the system can: (1) accept the current segmentation, (2) request feedback from clinicians, or (3) defer to full expert review. Queries are selected using response-conditioned net expected value of information; clinician feedback informs bounded adaptation only after prespecified provenance, consistency, and safety gates. The framework separates distributional atypicality from predicted clinical failure and treats clinician responses as informative but fallible observations.

Result: The paper synthesizes the transition from few-shot and cross-domain segmentation to interactive and selective adaptation, delineates the integration gap, and defines four research directions with falsifiable hypotheses. Evaluation approaches span external-domain calibration, quality-effort trade-offs, reader studies, and prospective workflow assessment.

Conclusion: The central claim is that while interaction alone does not resolve domain shift, scarce expert attention should be allocated only when it is expected to reduce clinically relevant risk. This requires a principled framework for deciding when to request clinician input versus when to accept automated segmentation or defer to full expert review.

Abstract: Few-shot medical image segmentation (FSMIS) seeks to delineate unseen structures from a small support set, but its standard formulation fixes task-defining evidence before inference. This assumption is fragile when query cases exhibit acquisition shift, atypical pathology, ambiguous boundaries, or poor image quality. Prototype learning, cross-domain matching, interactive segmentation, uncertainty estimation, test-time adaptation, and promptable foundation models address parts of this problem, yet have not been jointly evaluated under a common model of expert attention and clinical risk. This Perspective reframes FSMIS as a sequential clinician-model decision problem with a static support budget $K$ and a distinct interaction budget $B$. At each step, a system accepts the current segmentation, requests feedback, or defers to full expert review. Queries vary in location and modality and are selected by response-conditioned net expected value of information; clinician-provided feedback informs bounded adaptation only after prespecified provenance, consistency, and safety gates. The framework separates distributional atypicality from predicted clinical failure and treats clinician responses as informative but fallible observations. We synthesize the transition from few-shot and cross-domain segmentation to interactive and selective adaptation, delineate the integration gap, and define four research directions with falsifiable hypotheses. Evaluation spans external-domain calibration, quality-effort trade-offs, reader studies, and prospective workflow assessment. The central claim is not that interaction alone resolves domain shift, but that scarce expert attention should be allocated only when it is expected to reduce clinically relevant risk.

</details>


### [47] [What Makes Adversarial Examples Transfer Across Deepfake Detectors?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10002)
*Rafael M. Mamede,Pedro C. Neto,Ana F. Sequeira*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper investigates how source-target compatibility affects transfer-based black-box attacks against deepfake detectors. Through controlled evaluation of 60 detectors with varying backbones, pretraining regimes, and training data, the study reveals that sharing architectural features significantly increases adversarial transferability, with the effect varying by attack type.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deepfake detectors remain vulnerable to transfer-based black-box attacks, yet the factors governing source-target compatibility and their impact on attack success are poorly understood. Prior studies evaluate limited detector pools and rarely disentangle architectural from training factors.

Method: Controlled evaluation of adversarial transferability across 60 detectors spanning six backbones, two pretraining regimes, and five training-data configurations, using two attack procedures: AutoAttack (AA) and Carlini-Wagner attack with Expectation over Transformation (CW-EOT).

Result: Matched comparisons reveal significantly higher transfer when source and target share exact backbone, architecture family, pretraining regime, or training data. This compatibility structure is attack-dependent: exact backbone compatibility has the largest effect under AA, whereas shared pretraining and training data have the largest effects under CW-EOT. Mean ASR is 7.21% (AA) and 19.52% (CW-EOT) when averaged across non-target sources. A multi-source oracle combining both attacks attains 64.48% mean ASR after excluding exact backbone and training-data matches.

Conclusion: Source-target compatibility and source-model selection are central dimensions of credible transfer-based black-box robustness evaluation. The study releases 240,000 adversarially perturbed images, complete pairwise transfer results, detector configurations, and evaluation code.

Abstract: Deepfake detectors remain vulnerable to transfer-based black-box attacks, in which adversarial examples are generated on a source surrogate model and transferred to a target model, unknown to the attacker. Yet how source--target compatibility shapes attack success remains poorly understood. Prior studies evaluate limited detector pools and rarely disentangle architectural from training factors. We conduct a controlled evaluation of adversarial transferability across 60 detectors spanning six backbones, two pretraining regimes, and five training-data configurations, using two attack procedures: AutoAttack (AA) and the Carlini--Wagner attack with Expectation over Transformation (CW--EOT). Matched comparisons reveal significantly higher transfer when source and target share an exact backbone, architecture family, pretraining regime, or training data. This compatibility structure is attack-dependent: exact backbone compatibility has the largest effect under AA, whereas shared pretraining and training data have the largest effects under CW--EOT. When transfer is averaged across non-target sources, mean attack success rate (ASR) is $7.21\%$ under AA and $19.52\%$ under CW--EOT. By contrast, a multi-source oracle combining both attacks attains a \(64.48\%\) mean ASR after excluding exact backbone and training-data matches, showing that source averaging can substantially understate target vulnerability. We release 240,000 adversarially perturbed images, complete pairwise transfer results, detector configurations, and evaluation code. These findings establish source--target compatibility and source-model selection as central dimensions of credible transfer-based black-box robustness evaluation.

</details>


### [48] [Beyond Similarity: Foundation Models as an Efficient Backbone for Training-Free Composed Video Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10008)
*Dmitry Demidov,Muhammad Zaigham Zaheer,Omkar Thawakar,Abdelrahman Mohamed Shaker,Rao Anwer*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A training-free framework for composed video retrieval (CoVR) that adaptively orchestrates frozen foundation models at different inference depths based on query difficulty, achieving state-of-the-art results (89.55 and 93.43 R@1) on two benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the fundamental tension in CoVR between compact embeddings (efficient but miss fine-grained details) and uniformly applied large multimodal models (accurate but unscalable). The goal is to combine scalable retrieval with fine-grained reasoning without task-specific training.

Method: Multi-stage adaptive framework with: (1) Initial composed-query embedding searches reusable video-only gallery representations; (2) Uncertain queries undergo bounded reranking and candidate expansion; (3) Ambiguous edits trigger target-description generation; (4) Only close candidates reach multimodal verification. Frame selection, spatial resolution, and time cues are adapted to each stage.

Result: State-of-the-art performance among training-free approaches: 89.55 R@1 on Dense-WebVid-CoVR and 93.43 R@1 on CoVR-R, representing +35% and +25% absolute margins over the closest counterpart.

Conclusion: Adaptively orchestrating foundation-model capabilities can effectively combine scalable retrieval with fine-grained reasoning without requiring task-specific training, demonstrating the value of differentiated inference depths based on query complexity.

Abstract: Composed video retrieval (CoVR) searches a gallery for the target video that realizes a natural-language modification of a source clip. However, at gallery scale, this creates a fundamental tension: compact embeddings enable efficient, reusable search but can miss the transient actions, state changes, and subtle constraints that demand fine-grained video reasoning, whereas applying large multimodal models uniformly sacrifices scalability. To address these limitations, we propose that frozen foundation models should instead occupy complementary roles, with inference depth adapted to query difficulty. Based on this premise, we introduce \methodname{}, a framework for training-free \methodexpansion{}.
