TL;DR: This paper proposes a shape-guided Gaussian splatting framework for sparse-view X-ray 3D reconstruction. The method addresses the challenge of reconstructing 3D density fields from very few X-ray projections (as few as 5 views) by incorporating anatomical information through statistical shape models, achieving 2.83 dB PSNR improvement over state-of-the-art methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sparse-view X-ray 3D reconstruction is important for reducing radiation exposure, but existing 3D Gaussian Splatting methods require dozens of views and suffer severe quality degradation with fewer views. The key insight is that anatomical structures have bounded variations across populations, which statistical shape models can capture.

Method: The proposed framework integrates statistical shape models into 3D Gaussian Splatting by: (1) driving Gaussian positions toward anatomically valid configurations, and (2) applying atlas-based density regularization to ensure anatomically consistent reconstruction.

Result: The method achieves 2.83 dB PSNR improvement over state-of-the-art Gaussian splatting baseline with as few as 5 views.

Conclusion: The shape-guided approach successfully combines the expressiveness of 3D Gaussian Splatting with anatomical priors, enabling high-quality sparse-view X-ray 3D reconstruction with reduced radiation exposure. The code is available on GitHub.

Abstract: Sparse-view X-ray 3D reconstruction is essential for reducing radiation exposure, but recovering a density field from a handful of X-ray projections is severely ill-posed. Recently, 3D Gaussian Splatting has achieved state-of-the-art performance in sparse-view reconstruction by representing the volume using explicit, optimized primitives, but it requires dozens of projected views. With fewer views, reconstruction quality degrades severely since the explicit primitives are optimized freely without any anatomical information. Anatomical structures, in contrast, share similar geometry and density across a population. Their variations are bounded within a limited range that statistical shape models can capture. This paper proposes a shape-guided Gaussian splatting framework for sparse-view X-ray 3D reconstructions. Our contribution lies in driving Gaussian positions toward anatomically valid configurations, alongside atlas-based density regularization. Our method ensures anatomically consistent reconstruction and improves PSNR by 2.83 dB over a state-of-the-art Gaussian splatting baseline with as few as 5 views. Code Available: https://github.com/polyshape-lab/ShapeGuidedGaussian

</details>


### [72] [Enhanced Deformable Convolution with Center-invariant Offset and Edge-aware Mask](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10387)
*Yixiao Li,Xiaoyuan Yang,Jin Jiang,Minghao Zou,Guanghui Yue,Baoquan Zhao,Jun Liu,Wei Zhou*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Enhanced Deformable ConvNets (EDCN) addresses limitations in deformable convolution networks for semantic segmentation by introducing a novel Enhanced Deformable Convolution (EDC) that combines Center-invariant Offset Module (COM) and Edge-aware Mask Module (EMM) to achieve more precise spatial modeling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deformable convolution networks face two key limitations: (1) dense deformable offsets and (2) lack of longer-range dependencies, preventing precise deformations for feature representations in semantic segmentation tasks.

Method: The proposed EDC integrates two modules: (1) COM uses larger kernels and eliminates deformations at the kernel center to obtain more accurate offsets from richer spatial information; (2) EMM uses Sobel edge detection to determine content significance and selectively applies deformations, minimizing unnecessary deformations from less informative regions.

Result: EDC outperforms state-of-the-art deformable convolution variants (Deformable ConvNets V1-V4 and Entire Deformable ConvNets) across mainstream segmentation datasets with various decoder settings. Ablation studies confirm component effectiveness, and visualizations show enhanced spatial adaptation and target focus. The method also extends to image classification with larger kernels.

Conclusion: The proposed EDCN effectively addresses deformable convolution limitations through COM and EMM, achieving superior performance in semantic segmentation and demonstrating good extendibility to image classification tasks.

Abstract: Deformable convolution networks have recently become popular for many computer vision tasks, especially for semantic segmentation, because of their exceptional capabilities in dynamic spatial modeling. However, due to the dense deformable offsets and the lack of longer-range dependencies, they can not fully adopt proper and precise deformations for feature representations. To tackle the issues, in this paper, we propose Enhanced Deformable ConvNets (EDCN) for semantic segmentation. Specifically, a novel Enhanced Deformable Convolution (EDC) is exploited in the decoder, which integrates the Center-invariant Offset Module (COM) and Edge-aware Mask Module (EMM). The COM employs larger kernels and eliminates deformations at the kernel center, obtaining offsets that are more in line with the target from richer spatial information. Concurrently, the EMM obtains the significance of image content via Sobel edge detection, then selectively applies deformations based on the content significance, minimizing unnecessary deformations associated with relatively less important information, thereby avoiding impact from less informative regions. Experiments show that EDC outperforms state-of-the-art deformable convolution variants, including Deformable ConvNets V1-V4 and Entire Deformable ConvNets, across mainstream segmentation datasets with various decoder settings. Moreover, ablation studies confirm the effectiveness of each component. In addition, visualizations illustrate that EDC enhances spatial adaptation and target focus. We further analyze the extendibility of EDC to larger kernels on the image classification benchmark. Code will be publicly released.

</details>


### [73] [Advanced Brain Tissue Imaging with Data-Consistent Diffusion Priors in Laminographic X-Ray Nanoimaging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10456)
*Wenxuan Fang,Abraham L. Levitan,Ana Diaz,Carles Bosch,Adrian Wanner,Andreas T. Schaefer,Mirko Holler,Tomas Aidukas,Nicholas W. Phillips,Yuxin Zhang,Alexandra Pacureanu,Manuel Guizar-Sicairos,Luis Barba*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: LUCID is a deep learning framework combining multi-view diffusion priors with projection-domain data consistency to address the missing-cone problem in X-ray laminography imaging of mammalian brains, enabling recovery of unmeasured Fourier information while maintaining alignment with the laminography forward model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: X-ray laminography enables high-throughput imaging of brain specimens but suffers from incomplete Fourier-space coverage due to tilted acquisition geometry, creating a "missing cone" of information that conventional methods cannot recover, resulting in artifacts that distort fine brain structures critical for connectomics.

Method: LUCID (Laminography with Unified Consistent Diffusion) integrates multi-view diffusion priors with projection-domain data consistency, combining complementary 3D structural information while enforcing strict alignment with the laminography forward model. The model was trained on fully sampled tomographic volumes.

Result: On simulated datasets, LUCID substantially improves spatial fidelity, restores missing Fourier components, and outperforms baseline methods. On experimental laminography data, it generalizes robustly despite being trained only on fully sampled tomographic volumes, and effectively recovers unmeasured Fourier information.

Conclusion: LUCID successfully bridges the gap between deep learning approaches and physical imaging constraints by enforcing data consistency with the laminography forward model, enabling robust recovery of missing Fourier information for high-fidelity nanoscale brain imaging.

Abstract: Nanoscale imaging of mammalian brains is critical for connectomics. X-ray laminography enables high-throughput imaging of extended, plate-like biological specimens. However, the tilted acquisition geometry leads to incomplete Fourier-space coverage, giving rise to a missing-cone of information. Conventional reconstruction methods cannot recover unmeasured information within the cone, resulting in artifacts that distort fine brain structures. While resolving these requires modeling 3D structure, direct 3D deep learning approaches are limited by data scarcity and computational cost. Here we introduce LUCID (Laminography with Unified Consistent Diffusion), a framework that combines multi-view diffusion priors with projection-domain data consistency. LUCID integrates complementary 3D structural information while enforcing strict alignment with the laminography forward model. On simulated datasets, LUCID substantially improves spatial fidelity and restores missing Fourier components, outperforming baseline methods. Applied to experimental laminography data, LUCID generalizes robustly despite being trained exclusively on fully sampled tomographic volumes, and effectively recovers unmeasured Fourier information.

</details>


### [74] [AgroVisNet: A lightweight Convolutional Network and the BD-PlantDX Expert-Validated Benchmark for Radish, Potato and Pointed Gourd Disease Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10469)
*Md. Abdullah Mandal,Saad Ahmed,Md. Khalid Syfullah*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes AgroVisNet, a compact CNN (290K parameters) for plant disease diagnosis, together with BD-PlantDX, an expert-validated benchmark of 12,432 images from Bangladesh. The model achieves 99.52% accuracy and 99.52% weighted F1 on BD-PlantDX, outperforming 6 ImageNet-pretrained lightweight backbones while using 8.7-16.8x fewer parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Three obstacles limit practical deployment of automated plant disease diagnosis in resource-limited regions: (1) public benchmarks are dominated by non-native crops, (2) region-specific datasets lack expert validation, and (3) competitive architectures require parameter budgets unsuitable for low-cost hardware. The authors address this gap by creating a compact, locally-validated solution for Bangladeshi crops.

Method: AgroVisNet combines grouped bottleneck residual blocks with sequential channel and spatial attention, multi-scale depthwise blocks, and a dual-pooling classification head. The model was trained from scratch on BD-PlantDX, an expert-validated benchmark containing 12,432 field images across 12 classes of radish, potato, and pointed gourd (healthy and diseased) from Bogura and Nilphamari districts, Bangladesh.

Result: AgroVisNet achieves 99.52% test accuracy and 99.52% weighted F1 on BD-PlantDX, surpassing all six ImageNet-pretrained lightweight backbones evaluated under identical protocol. It uses 8.7 to 16.8 times fewer parameters and 1.3 to 8.5 times fewer MACs. The model quantizes to 0.46 MB at only 0.22 percentage-point accuracy cost and runs inference in 8.40 ms on a single CPU. Across five random seeds, accuracy remains stable at 99.57 ± 0.10%. Ablation studies confirm each component's contribution. The architecture transfers to two independent datasets achieving 98.71% and 99.05% accuracy. Grad-CAM shows predictions rely on lesion-bearing leaf regions rather than background.

Conclusion: The proposed AgroVisNet demonstrates that a compact CNN trained from scratch can outperform larger ImageNet-pretrained models on region-specific plant disease diagnosis tasks. The combination of an expert-validated benchmark (BD-PlantDX) and a hardware-efficient architecture addresses the three identified obstacles, enabling practical deployment on farmer-held devices in regions with limited connectivity and agronomic expertise.

Abstract: Automated plant disease diagnosis is increasingly deployed on farmer-held devices in regions where agronomic expertise is scarce and network connectivity is unreliable. Three obstacles limit its practical value: public benchmarks are dominated by a small set of non-native crops, region-specific datasets are rarely validated by domain experts, and the architectures that reach competitive accuracy carry parameter budgets that are unsuited to low-cost hardware. We propose AgroVisNet, a compact convolutional network trained from scratch, together with BD-PlantDX, an expert-validated benchmark of 12,432 field images spanning 12 classes of radish, potato and pointed gourd in healthy and diseased states, collected across the Bogura and Nilphamari districts of Bangladesh. AgroVisNet couples grouped bottleneck residual blocks carrying sequential channel and spatial attention with multi-scale depthwise blocks and a dual-pooling classification head, reaching 290,572 trainable parameters. On BD-PlantDX the model attains 99.52% test accuracy and 99.52% weighted F1, exceeding all six ImageNet-pretrained lightweight backbones evaluated under an identical protocol while using 8.7 to 16.8 times fewer parameters and 1.3 to 8.5 times fewer multiply-accumulate operations. Exported for deployment, the model quantises to a 0.46 MB full-integer network at a 0.22 percentage-point accuracy cost and classifies an image in 8.40 ms on a single CPU. Across five random seeds accuracy remains at 99.57 +- 0.10%, a ten-variant ablation isolates the contribution of each component, and the same architecture transfers without redesign to two independently collected datasets at 98.71% and 99.05% accuracy. Grad-CAM evidence indicates that predictions rest on lesion-bearing leaf regions rather than on background cues.

</details>


### [75] [Artificial Intelligence Literacy and Sustainable Development: An Ethical Governance and Development Goals Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10489)
*Md. Masudul Islam,Mirza Niaz Morshed,Md. Shafiqul Islam*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This study proposes AI literacy as a governance capacity that supports all 17 SDGs, introducing a six-level taxonomy of AI reasoning and ethics. A survey of 300 participants reveals a gap between technical awareness and ethical/governance readiness, with ethical reasoning and reflective thinking identified as key predictors of responsible AI use.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The research addresses the need to move beyond basic AI literacy toward higher-order competencies (governance, critical evaluation, strategic decision-making) that are essential for ethical and sustainable AI deployment. The authors argue that while AI literacy provides foundational competencies, advanced capabilities require ethical judgement and strategic foresight that traditional learning models lack.

Method: The study employed a survey methodology with 300 participants from diverse professional backgrounds within a national context. The six-level taxonomy of AI reasoning and ethics was developed as the theoretical foundation, which was then used to assess participants' competencies across technical, ethical, and governance dimensions.

Result: The survey revealed strong technical awareness among participants but limited ethical and governance readiness. Ethical reasoning and reflective thinking emerged as the strongest predictors of sustainable and trustworthy AI use, highlighting critical gaps in public capacity for responsible AI management.

Conclusion: The study proposes embedding literacy-based competencies into educational curricula, institutional policies, and governance mechanisms to accelerate equitable and responsible progress toward sustainable development goals. This integration of AI literacy into governance frameworks represents a novel approach to ensuring AI contributes positively to sustainable development.

Abstract: AI literacy provides foundational competencies that support ethical, transparent, and sustainable technological development, although higher-order capabilities such as governance, critical evaluation, and strategic decision-making extend beyond basic literacy into advanced levels of AI competency. This study positions AI literacy as a governance capacity that complements and strengthens all 17 SDGs. It introduces a six-level taxonomy of artificial intelligence reasoning and ethics that extends traditional learning models by incorporating ethical judgement and strategic foresight. This taxonomy forms the foundation of an integrated framework linking education, governance, and sustainable development. A survey of 300 participants from diverse professional backgrounds within a national context which reveals strong technical awareness but limited ethical and governance readiness, highlighting critical gaps in public capacity to manage artificial intelligence responsibly. Findings show that ethical reasoning and reflective thinking are the strongest predictors of sustainable and trustworthy artificial intelligence use. The study proposed to embed literacy-based competencies into curricula, institutional policies, and governance mechanisms to accelerate equitable and responsible progress toward sustainable development goals

</details>


### [76] [Cross-Model Agreement as a Deployment-Time Reliability Signal for Automatic Polyp Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10495)
*Siddharth Gupta,Jitin Singla*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: RBQE is a reference-free quality estimation framework for polyp segmentation in colonoscopy that measures agreement between a primary segmentation model and an independently trained referee to detect failures without ground-truth annotations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In real-time colonoscopy, ground-truth annotations are unavailable at inference, causing polyp segmentation models to fail silently. There is a need for a reliable method to estimate model confidence without reference data.

Method: RBQE measures agreement between a primary segmentation model and an independently trained referee on the same image. Evaluated on 1,223-image external benchmark from four public datasets using four referee configurations testing two design axes: referee independence and architectural diversity. Uses Agreement Dice descriptor to quantify reliability.

Result: Same-architecture referee (different only in random initialization) achieves ROC-AUC = 0.923. Cross-architecture SegFormer-B0 achieves ROC-AUC = 0.960, significantly outperforming same-architecture control and UNet++, exceeding Test-Time Augmentation baseline by 0.055 ROC-AUC. Prompt-coupled MedSAM underperforms despite maximal architectural diversity. Restricted evaluation (excluding empty-mask cases): SegFormer-B0 = 0.876 (1,046 images), same-architecture = 0.783 (975 images).

Conclusion: Cross-model agreement is a practical, interpretable reliability framework for automated polyp segmentation. Independent training alone provides useful reliability signal, while cross-architecture referees further improve performance. RBQE enables selective prediction by progressively rejecting low-agreement cases while requiring only one additional deterministic referee forward pass at inference.

Abstract: In real-time colonoscopy, ground-truth annotations are unavailable at inference, so polyp segmentation models can fail silently. We propose Referee-Based Quality Estimation (RBQE), a reference-free framework measuring agreement between a primary segmentation model and an independently trained referee on the same image. RBQE is evaluated on a standardized 1,223-image external benchmark drawn from four public datasets, using four referee configurations chosen to separate two design axes: referee independence and architectural diversity. Using a common Agreement Dice descriptor, a same-architecture referee differing from the primary model only in random initialization already yields a useful reliability signal (ROC-AUC = 0.923), showing that independent training alone is sufficient. Cross-architecture referees improve further: SegFormer-B0 achieves the strongest performance (ROC-AUC = 0.960), significantly outperforming the same-architecture control and UNet++, and exceeding a representative Test-Time Augmentation baseline by 0.055 ROC-AUC under an identical protocol, whereas a prompt-coupled MedSAM referee underperforms despite maximal architectural diversity. Because empty-mask agreement is trivially separable, we also report a restricted evaluation excluding such cases: ROC-AUC falls to 0.876 (SegFormer-B0, 1,046 images) and 0.783 (same-architecture control, 975 images), yet RBQE's margin over both baselines widens on this identical subset. RBQE additionally increases the mean Dice of retained predictions as low-agreement cases are progressively rejected, supporting selective prediction, and requires only one additional deterministic referee forward pass at inference. Our study therefore supports cross-model agreement as a practical, interpretable reliability framework for automated polyp segmentation.

</details>


### [77] [Field Converter: Geometry-Initialized Temporal Residual Refinement for World-Grounded Player Pose Estimation from Soccer Broadcasts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10498)
*Simon Khan,Laurent Gajny,Jennyfer Lecompte,Sébastien Laporte*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Field Converter is a geometry-initialized temporal residual framework for world-grounded 3D player pose estimation from calibrated soccer broadcasts. It uses camera and pitch geometry for initial root localization via ray-ground intersection, then applies temporal residual correction to achieve 10-14cm root error (down from 49cm geometry alone) and 13.2cm world-space MPJPE.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Recovering 3D human pose from monocular sports broadcasts is challenging when players must be localized in a shared metric world coordinate system rather than only relative to their own body. Existing methods struggle with world-grounded localization.

Method: 1) Geometry initialization: Use camera parameters and pitch geometry to initialize player root through ray-ground intersection. 2) Temporal residual correction: Predict residual correction from pose, image, camera, and geometric cues using temporal networks (MLP, TCN, or Transformer).

Result: Residual refinement reduces root error from 49cm (geometry alone) to 14cm (MLP), 10cm (TCN), and 11cm (Transformer). World-space MPJPE reaches 13.2cm. Ablations show residual prediction outperforms direct global-root regression, and temporal context matters more than the specific backbone.

Conclusion: The geometry-initialized temporal residual approach effectively bridges the gap between image-space pose estimation and world-space localization. Airborne motion remains the main limitation of ground-based geometric initialization.

Abstract: Recovering 3D human pose from monocular sports broadcasts remains challenging when players must be localized in a shared metric world coordinate system rather than only reconstructed relative to their own body. We introduce Field Converter, a geometry-initialized temporal residual framework for world-grounded 3D player pose estimation from calibrated soccer broadcasts. Our method first uses camera and pitch geometry to initialize the player root through ray-ground intersection, then predicts a temporal residual correction from pose, image, camera, and geometric cues. On match-disjoint evaluation sequences, residual refinement reduces root error from 49cm with geometry alone to 14cm with a frame-wise MLP and 10cm with a TCN, while a Transformer achieves a comparable 11cm. The resulting world-space MPJPE reaches 13.2cm, and ablations show that residual prediction clearly outperforms direct global-root regression while temporal context matters more than the specific temporal backbone. Failure analysis further identifies airborne motion as the main limitation of the ground-based geometric initialization.

</details>


### [78] [BrainTaskonomy: Learning How to Pretrain and What to Transfer in fMRI Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10518)
*Junfeng Xia,Wenhao Ye,Junxiang Zhang,Jiayu Zuo,Mo Wang,Quanying Liu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes organizing fMRI foundation model pretraining and adaptation using measured learning relations rather than treating domains and tasks as independent flat sets. The approach uses a Brain-DiT proxy to create a priority-guided curriculum during pretraining and budgeted integer programming for task selection during adaptation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current fMRI foundation models aggregate heterogeneous data across brain states, cohorts, and acquisition settings, but pretraining domains are treated as flat mixtures and downstream tasks are adapted independently. This work aims to leverage measured learning relations to organize both stages without modifying the backbone architecture.

Method: Two-stage approach: (1) Pretraining uses a lightweight Brain-DiT proxy to estimate difficulty and directed facilitation across 10 fMRI domains, creating a priority-guided cumulative domain curriculum with high-to-low-noise timestep scheduling and joint consolidation. (2) Adaptation uses controlled first- and higher-order transfer across 15 tasks to construct a directed taskonomy, from which budgeted integer programming (BIP) selects source tasks and target-specific routes.

Result: The joint priority-domain and high-to-low-timestep curriculum reduces v-NMSE, PSD-NMSE, and FC-MSE by 6.5%, 16.3%, and 10.5% respectively compared to uniform sampling. Strong downstream performance achieved across 6 in- and out-of-domain tasks. The taskonomy reveals asymmetric, target-dependent transfer. BIP policies show larger gains when higher-order route spaces are available.

Conclusion: Organizing fMRI pretraining and adaptation by measured learning relations (rather than treating domains and tasks as independent flat sets) significantly improves performance on multiple metrics and downstream tasks.

Abstract: fMRI foundation models increasingly aggregate heterogeneous data across brain states, cohorts, and acquisition settings, yet pretraining domains are commonly treated as a flat mixture and downstream tasks are adapted independently. We study whether measured learning relations can organize both stages without modifying the backbone. During pretraining, a lightweight Brain-DiT proxy estimates difficulty and directed facilitation across ten fMRI domains, yielding a priority-guided cumulative domain curriculum combined with high-to-low-noise timestep scheduling and joint consolidation. During adaptation, controlled first- and higher-order transfer across fifteen tasks constructs a directed taskonomy, from which budgeted integer programming (BIP) selects directly supervised source tasks and target-specific routes. The joint priority-domain and high-to-low-timestep curriculum reduces v-NMSE, PSD-NMSE, and FC-MSE by 6.5%, 16.3%, and 10.5%, respectively, relative to uniform sampling over both dimensions, and shows strong downstream performance across six in- and out-of-domain tasks. The taskonomy reveals asymmetric, target-dependent transfer, while exploratory sealed-test evaluation shows larger descriptive gains for BIP policies when higher-order route spaces are available than for matched random controls. Together, these findings support organizing fMRI pretraining and adaptation by measured learning relations rather than treating domains and tasks as independent flat sets.

</details>


### [79] [Precision in Rice Variety Classification using Stacking-Based Ensemble Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10524)
*Md. Masudul Islam,Galib Muhammad Shahriar Himel,Md. Golam Moazzam,Mohammad Shorif Uddin*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A stacked ensemble machine learning model achieves 100% accuracy in classifying 20 rice varieties based on visual characteristics, integrated into a mobile application for consumer use.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Rice variety identification is challenging due to diverse varieties, leading to fraudulent practices like unauthorized mixing. Existing methods lack robust and efficient classification based on external characteristics (color, size, texture), creating a need for automated identification systems.

Method: Developed a stacked ensemble model for rice variety classification using a curated dataset of 20 rice varieties with unique visual attributes. Integrated the model into a mobile application for smartphone-based grain image identification.

Result: Achieved unprecedented classification accuracy of 100% on the 20 rice variety dataset. The mobile app enables novice users to identify rice varieties using smartphone camera images.

Conclusion: Advanced machine learning techniques can effectively mitigate fraudulent practices and ensure quality control in rice supply chains. This work provides implications for agricultural stakeholders and paves the way for automated crop identification and precision agriculture.

Abstract: Rice, a staple food for a significant portion of the global population, exhibits remarkable diversity in its varieties, presenting substantial challenges for accurate identification by consumers, traders, and farmers. This complexity often facilitates fraudulent practices, such as the unauthorized mixing of rice types, which undermines quality and trust in the supply chain. Despite its critical importance, existing research falls short of providing robust and efficient methods for precise rice variety classification based on external characteristics like color, size, and texture. To address this gap, our study introduces a comprehensive rice variety identification framework designed to enhance transparency and quality assurance. We developed a stacked ensemble model tailored for rice variety classification and curated a comprehensive dataset comprising 20 rice varieties, each distinguished by unique visual attributes. The proposed approach achieved an unprecedented classification accuracy of 100%. Furthermore, we integrated our model into a mobile application, enabling even novice users to effortlessly identify rice varieties using grain images from a smartphone camera. These findings underscore the transformative potential of advanced machine learning techniques in mitigating fraudulent practices and ensuring stringent rice quality control. Our work holds significant implications for agricultural stakeholders, paving the way for automated crop identification systems and advancing precision agriculture practices.

</details>


### [80] [Guiding Image-to-3D Generation with Test-Time Partial Observations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10531)
*Jerred Chen,Simon Weber,Ronald Clark*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A training-free framework that enables pretrained image-to-3D generative models to incorporate partial geometric observations at test time without retraining, using ray-consistent observation likelihood over occupancy representation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Image-to-3D models often produce geometry loosely constrained by observations, limiting their use in applications requiring geometric fidelity. The authors address this by allowing integration of partial geometric observations available at test time.

Method: The approach uses a ray-consistent observation likelihood defined over the model's occupancy representation, combining surface occupancy and free-space evidence. This test-time guidance is applied to SAM 3D and its multi-view extension without any retraining or finetuning.

Result: Substantially improves geometric fidelity across different levels of observability, as well as visual quality.

Conclusion: Pretrained image-to-3D models can effectively integrate partial geometric observations through explicit test-time guidance, complementing their learned generative priors without modifying the underlying model.

Abstract: Image-to-3D models can generate visually compelling 3D assets from a single RGB image, but their geometry is often only loosely constrained by the available observations, limiting their use in applications that require geometric fidelity. In many real-world settings, however, partial geometric observations of the object may be available at test time. We introduce a training-free framework for incorporating such evidence into pretrained image-to-3D generative models without retraining or finetuning. To do this, we guide generation using a ray-consistent observation likelihood defined over the model's occupancy representation, combining surface occupancy and free-space evidence. Applied to SAM 3D and its multi-view extension, our approach substantially improves geometric fidelity across different levels of observability, as well as visual quality. Our results demonstrate that pretrained image-to-3D models can effectively integrate partial geometric observations through explicit test-time guidance, complementing their learned generative priors without modifying the underlying model.

</details>


### [81] [Programmable World Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10540)
*Zheng-Hui Huang,Guixu Lin,Jiacheng Lin,Yi-Chuan Huang,Ruihan Yu,Muyao Niu,Siqi Yang,Yu-Lun Liu,Yung-Yu Chuang,Kaipeng Zhang,Zhixiang Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper introduces Programmable World Model, a framework that decouples world-state evolution from visual generation in video world models. It uses natural-language-to-program translation, a lightweight execution engine for persistent state management, and state-augmented 3D OBBs as an intermediate representation for generating video via a pretrained model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current video world models can generate realistic and interactive visuals but lack mechanisms for maintaining persistent world state and enforcing programmable rules over extended interactions. This limits their ability to create playable games with consistent mechanics and direct entity control.

Method: The framework consists of three components: (1) An agent that translates natural-language instructions into executable programs specifying entity states and transition rules; (2) A lightweight engine that executes these programs to maintain an explicit, persistent global world state including off-screen entities and non-visual attributes; (3) State-augmented 3D oriented bounding boxes (OBBs) as an intermediate representation that compiles with camera trajectories into spatiotemporal conditioning signals for a pretrained video generation model.

Result: On CombatStateBench, the method achieves 94% Count Accuracy and 98% State Accuracy, substantially outperforming existing interactive video world models while supporting coherent long-horizon generation.

Conclusion: Separating explicit state evolution from generative rendering is effective for building persistent, programmable worlds with predefined mechanics, direct entity control, and persistent world state throughout gameplay.

Abstract: Recent video world models generate increasingly realistic and interactive visual experiences, yet lack reliable mechanisms for maintaining persistent world state and enforcing programmable rules over extended interactions. We introduce Programmable World Model, a framework that decouples world-state evolution from visual observation generation. An agent translates natural-language instructions into executable programs that specify entity states and state-transition rules, enabling direct control over individual entities and their interactions. A lightweight engine executes these programs to update and maintain an explicit, persistent global world state, including off-screen entities and non-visual attributes. To connect world state with visual generation, we introduce state-augmented 3D oriented bounding boxes (OBBs) as an intermediate representation. This representation, together with the target camera trajectory, is deterministically compiled into pixel-aligned spatiotemporal conditioning signals for a pretrained video model serving as the generative renderer. This design allows users to create playable games with predefined mechanics, direct control over individual entities, and persistent world state throughout gameplay. We further introduce CombatStateBench, a benchmark for evaluating programmable world models. On CombatStateBench, our method achieves 94% Count Accuracy and 98% State Accuracy, substantially outperforming existing interactive video world models while supporting coherent long-horizon generation. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of separating explicit state evolution from generative rendering for building persistent, programmable worlds.

</details>


<div id='cs.SE'></div>

# cs.SE [[Back]](#toc)

### [82] [OASIS: A Rubric-Based Multimodal Assessment Platform Using Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09180)
*Ameer H. Shakur,Shinyoung Kang,David Hein,Michael J. Holcomb,Huong-Tra Ngo,Aarash Zakeri,Minhan Park,Licheng Yi,Dhanush Jain,Hunter Schuler,Andrew R. Jamieson*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: OASIS is a systems platform for scalable rubric-based automated grading of video, audio, and text using large language models. It provides encounter management, rubric versioning, multimodal execution, and provenance capture, deployed in medical education at UT Southwestern since Fall 2023 with over 7,000 encounters processed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation addresses the challenge of deploying LLM-based assessment at scale. While scoring a single artifact is straightforward, production deployment requires sophisticated infrastructure for encounter management, rubric versioning, modality-aware execution, provenance capture, and human review capabilities. The authors developed a domain-agnostic solution applicable wherever structured performance can be evaluated from recorded or written artifacts.

Method: OASIS combines a standalone command-line interface with an integrated Elephant + MAPLES stack for encounter management and multimodal grading orchestration. It supports both hosted APIs and self-hosted open-weight models through Ollama and OpenAI-compatible endpoints (vLLM). Optional extensions include SimRubrics for rubric authoring and Wayfinder conversational agent gateway. Key architectural features include rubric-as-program compilation, progressive execution plans, content-addressable grading identity, transcript-augmented multimodal grading, explicit review state, and a shared command surface for humans and autonomous agents.

Result: The platform has been in production at UT Southwestern Medical Center since Fall 2023 and has processed more than 7,000 encounters, producing per-criterion scores, evidence, and rationales while preserving execution artifacts for audit.

Conclusion: OASIS demonstrates a viable architecture for scalable LLM-based assessment in education. Though developed for medical education, its domain-agnostic design makes it applicable to any field where structured performance can be evaluated from recorded or written artifacts. The paper reports project information but does not include source code, binaries, installation materials, sample data, or tagged releases.

Abstract: OASIS (Open Assessment and Scoring Infrastructure Stack) is a systems platform for rubric-based grading of video, audio, and text with large language models. Scoring one artifact with an LLM is straightforward; deploying assessment at scale requires encounter management, rubric versioning, modality-aware execution, provenance capture, and human review. OASIS pairs a standalone command-line interface with a canonical integrated Elephant + MAPLES stack for encounter management and multimodal grading orchestration. Both paths can target hosted APIs or self-hosted open-weight models through Ollama and OpenAI-compatible endpoints such as vLLM. SimRubrics rubric authoring and the Wayfinder conversational agent gateway are optional extensions that use the same authenticated interfaces as human operators. Given a rubric and recorded encounters, OASIS produces per-criterion scores, evidence, and rationales, preserving execution artifacts for audit. Distinctive features include rubric-as-program compilation, progressive execution plans, content-addressable grading identity, transcript-augmented multimodal grading, explicit review state, and a shared command surface for humans and autonomous agents. Though developed in medical education, the architecture is domain-agnostic, applying wherever structured performance can be evaluated from recorded or written artifacts. In production at UT Southwestern Medical Center since Fall 2023, the platform has processed more than 7,000 encounters. This publication includes the report and project information, not application source, binaries, installation materials, sample data, or a tagged software release.

</details>


### [83] [Evaluating Enterprise Analytics Agents: An End-to-End, Trace-Backed Methodology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09182)
*Teja Venkat Kolli,Sang Su Lee,Xueying Yan,Jessie Chen,Chi Cheng,Kartik Ravisankar,Shishir Dash,Vijay Anand Raghavan*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper presents an end-to-end evaluation methodology for enterprise analytics agents that goes beyond traditional SQL correctness or final answer quality assessment. The methodology evaluates agents across three dimensions: semantic understanding, execution quality, and reliability, using question banks with golden answers, repeated runs, and runtime traces. A case study on a large online marketplace with 300 traces reveals that even higher-capability configurations exhibit significant issues including tool-round budget exhaustion (16%), schema-exploration budget overruns (77%), and inconsistent table interpretation (41/50 questions).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluation methods for analytics agents focus on grading final answers, which fails to capture critical failure modes. A plausible-looking answer can use wrong data sources, skip required decompositions, claim unsupported causality, or inconsistently interpret tables across runs. This hidden complexity in agent behavior requires a more comprehensive evaluation approach.

Method: The methodology uses tiered, abstention-aware scoring across three evaluation families: (1) Semantic Understanding - whether agents correctly interpret business intent and metric definitions; (2) Execution Quality - proper data source selection, tool execution, and result inspection; (3) Reliability - consistency across repeated runs. Each run passes through a run-validity check before scoring. The case study used 50 analytics questions, two anonymous model configurations, and three randomized repetitions per configuration, yielding 300 traces.

Result: The higher-capability configuration reduced early refusal from 73% to 0% and increased real-data answers from 21% to 73%. However, it also showed significant issues: tool-round budget exhaustion (16% of runs), schema-exploration budget overruns (77% of traces), and table interpretation changes on 41 of 50 questions. Finance questions showed improved source table use and escalation, but canonical decomposition remained weak in both configurations.

Conclusion: Trust in analytics agents requires end-to-end, trace-backed evaluation rather than SQL correctness or final answer quality alone. The methodology presented provides a decision framework rather than a release gate, allowing for nuanced assessment of agent capabilities across multiple dimensions while accounting for abstention behaviors.

Abstract: Enterprise analytics agents are not only text-to-SQL systems. They interpret business intent and choose metric definitions. They select data sources, execute tools, inspect results, and produce natural-language answers. Those answers may influence operational, financial, or executive decisions. Grading final answers hides where these agents fail. A plausible answer can use the wrong source of truth. It can skip a required decomposition, claim causality without support, or change its table interpretation across repeated runs. We present an end-to-end evaluation methodology for analytics agents. The methodology grades agent behavior across three families: semantic understanding, execution quality, and reliability. Grading uses question banks with human-written golden answers, repeated runs, and runtime traces. Each run first passes a run-validity check, then receives tiered, abstention-aware scores that feed a decision framework rather than a release gate. We instantiate the methodology on a controlled internal analytics agent at a large online marketplace. The case study uses 50 analytics questions, two anonymous model configurations, and three randomized repetitions per configuration, yielding 300 traces. The higher-capability configuration reduced early refusal from 73% to 0% and increased real-data answers from 21% to 73%. However, it also exhausted the tool-round budget on 16% of runs. It overran the schema-exploration budget on 77% of traces. It changed its table interpretation on 41 of 50 questions. On finance questions with structured golden answers, source table use and escalation improved, but canonical decomposition remained weak in both configurations. These results show why trust in analytics agents requires end-to-end, trace-backed evaluation rather than SQL correctness or final answer quality alone.

</details>


### [84] [The Double Measurement Confound in Agent Benchmarks: De-Scaffolding, Ground-Truth Scoring, and Reliability Beyond the Mean](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09218)
*Yonghong Zhang,Shadi Motaali,Vu Phong Dinh,Avin Piroutiniya,Jorge E. López de Vergara,Luis de Pedro,Ricardo Correia,Isabel M. Parra,Yong Xie*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper addresses a critical issue in LLM agent evaluation: benchmarks may measure pipeline properties rather than actual model capabilities due to two confounds - fixed scaffolds making execution-critical decisions and evaluators using shape-based criteria instead of task correctness. The authors propose a measurement-theoretic framework and an audit-and-repair protocol to fix these issues.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agent benchmarks are widely used to compare LLMs and guide deployment, but their scores may be invalid if they measure evaluation pipeline properties rather than true model capability. The authors identified a "double measurement confound" where scaffold decisions and shape-based scoring can distort benchmark results.

Method: The authors developed a measurement-theoretic framework characterizing when benchmark scores reflect true capability. They instantiated an audit-and-repair protocol with three components: (1) transferring execution-critical decisions from scaffold to model, (2) replacing shape-based evaluation with seeded ground-truth scoring, and (3) reporting reliability through worst-case and tail-risk metrics beyond mean scores.

Result: Experiments on ComtradeBench showed that the joint intervention transformed a nearly flat leaderboard into a reliability spectrum distinguishing both average performance and robustness across seeds. The audit revealed that scorer validity is benchmark-specific, while scaffold ownership is an uncontrolled variable across benchmarks.

Conclusion: Benchmark scores should be interpreted alongside their scaffolding level, scoring criterion, and reliability profile. This provides a practical framework for more valid evaluation of LLM agents, highlighting the need to account for evaluation pipeline properties when assessing model capabilities.

Abstract: Agent benchmarks are increasingly used to compare large language models (LLMs) and guide deployment decisions, yet benchmark scores are meaningful only if they measure model capability rather than properties of the evaluation pipeline. We identify a double measurement confound: execution-critical decisions are performed by a fixed scaffold instead of the model, while the scorer evaluates outputs using criteria that may not reflect task correctness. We unify these issues within a measurement-theoretic framework that characterizes when benchmark scores can be interpreted as evidence of model capability, and instantiate it with an audit-and-repair protocol that (i) transfers execution-critical decisions from the scaffold to the model, (ii) replaces shape-based evaluation with seeded ground-truth scoring, and (iii) reports reliability beyond the mean through worst-case and tail-risk metrics. Experiments on ComtradeBench show that the joint intervention transforms a nearly flat leaderboard into a reliability spectrum that distinguishes both average performance and robustness across seeds. Applying the audit to existing benchmarks further shows that scorer validity is benchmark-specific, whereas scaffold ownership is an uncontrolled axis wherever we probed it. Our results suggest that benchmark scores should be interpreted together with their scaffolding level, scoring criterion, and reliability profile, providing a practical framework for more valid evaluation of LLM agents.

</details>


### [85] [Cost-Aware Post-Hoc Deferral Under Calibration and Shift: An Environmental AI Case Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09235)
*Haoran Yu,Lifei Liu,Danping Zhang*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper introduces EcoTrust, a post-hoc framework for deciding when to defer automated classifier decisions to human review. It addresses key challenges in real-world deployment: confidence miscalibration, asymmetric error costs, reviewer errors, and data distribution shift. The framework uses a six-group error-risk estimator with class-asymmetric costs, reviewer accuracy modeling, and an optional support gate to detect extrapolation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors recognize that simply ranking uncertain cases is insufficient for deferral decisions. Real-world constraints matter: confidence scores may be poorly calibrated, different types of errors have different costs, human reviewers can make mistakes, and deployment data may fall outside the calibration support (extrapolation). Existing methods fail to account for these interactions.

Method: The study uses the Columbia River thermal-stress testbed as the primary domain. They implement EcoTrust with: (1) a six-group error-risk estimator for case-level ranking, (2) class-asymmetric cost modeling, (3) reviewer accuracy modeling, and (4) an optional support gate for extrapolation detection. They evaluate across 12 off-the-shelf classifier backends and test transfer to ten river stations.

Result: The learned risk estimator improves error-ranking AUC from 0.869 to 0.889, but Chow's simpler confidence rule achieves lower in-distribution cost (0.416 vs 0.567 per day). Across 12 backends, learned risk and a calibration-matched class-aware confidence estimator each outperform raw Chow on 6 backends. A paired year-block bootstrap cannot resolve their mean cost difference. In transfer to 10 river stations, the support gate flags every case and defaults to always-review, achieving lowest cost on only 8 stations when review is perfect and unconstrained.

Conclusion: The results characterize decision boundaries on a controlled task: richer risk signals do not reliably outperform well-calibrated confidence, and detected extrapolation (data outside calibration support) does not necessarily imply transferable case-level ranking. The work highlights the complexity of real-world deferral policies and the need to consider multiple interacting factors beyond simple uncertainty ranking.

Abstract: Choosing a deferral policy for a frozen classifier requires more than ranking uncertain cases: confidence may be miscalibrated, errors have unequal costs, reviewers can err, and deployment data can leave calibration support. We study these interactions through EcoTrust, a post-hoc framework that compares automatic action with review using a six-group error-risk estimator, class-asymmetric costs, reviewer accuracy, and an optional support gate. On a Columbia River thermal-stress testbed, the learned estimator improves error-ranking area under the receiver operating characteristic curve from 0.869 to 0.889, but Chow's confidence rule has lower in-distribution cost (0.416 versus 0.567 per day). Across 12 off-the-shelf backends, learned risk and a calibration-matched, class-aware confidence estimator each beat raw Chow on six; a paired year-block bootstrap does not resolve their mean cost difference. In transfer to ten river stations, the gate flags every case and becomes an always-review fallback, attaining the lowest cost on eight stations only when review is perfect and unconstrained. These results characterize decision boundaries on one controlled task: richer risk signals do not reliably improve on calibrated confidence, and detected extrapolation does not imply transferable case-level ranking.

</details>


### [86] [Talking to Itself While Coding: What Makes Comments Help Code Generation?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09242)
*Dangfeng Pan,Zhensu Sun,Cenyuan Zhang,David Lo,Xiaoning Du*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper investigates how natural-language comments embedded in code context affect LLM code-generation performance. Through observational analyses and controlled experiments, the authors find that comments help not because they are comments per se, but because they convey correct solution content from stronger models that weaker models cannot reliably generate on their own.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand which properties of comments (frequency, intent, content) affect code-generation performance in LLMs, as comments are commonly generated during code writing and influence subsequent code generation.

Method: 1) Observational analysis on LiveCodeBench examining comment frequency and intent vs pass@1; 2) Controlled intervention: prefilling weaker recipient models with comment blocks from stronger source models to separate comment surface form from solution content; 3) Testing across multiple models and prompt variants to measure recovery of external-comment gain.

Result: 1) Comment frequency and broad intent do not reliably predict pass@1; 2) Comments from source solutions that pass tests raise recipient pass@1 by 17.2% on average; 3) Comments describing failed solutions provide no reliable gain; 4) Comments written for a different problem reduce pass@1 by 20.8%; 5) Most models show no significant recovery of external-comment gain, with best case recovering only 24%.

Conclusion: Comments aid code generation not merely by being comments, but by providing correct solution content that prompting cannot reliably elicit. The study demonstrates that the semantic content of comments (correct solution logic) matters more than their structural presence, highlighting the importance of high-quality solution demonstrations in prompting strategies.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) often generate natural-language comments while writing code, and these comments become part of the context used to generate the code that follows. However, it remains unclear which properties of comments affect code-generation performance. We study this question through observational analyses and controlled interventions. On LiveCodeBench, neither comment frequency nor broad comment intent reliably predicts pass@1. We then prefill weaker recipient models with comment blocks written by stronger source models, allowing us to separate comment surface form from the solution content they convey. Comments from source solutions that pass the tests raise recipient pass@1 by 17.2% on average. In contrast, comments describing failed solutions provide no reliable gain, while comments written for a different problem reduce pass@1 by 20.8%. Finally, across a wide range of models and prompt variants, most recipient models show no significant recovery of the external-comment gain, and the best case recovers only 24%. These results show that comments help code generation not merely because they are comments, but because they can provide correct solution content that prompting cannot reliably elicit.

</details>


### [87] [How effective are traditional test criteria at detecting bugs in large language models generated code?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09315)
*Asma Hamidi,Michael Konstantinou,Renzo Degiovanni,Mike Papadakis*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper investigates the effectiveness of traditional test adequacy criteria (statement coverage, branch coverage, mutation testing) in detecting faults introduced by LLMs in automatically generated code and tests. Using 5 LLMs, 4 benchmarks, and 6,000+ faulty programs, the study reveals that while LLM faults are often trivial, challenging faults are difficult to trigger, and actual fault detection rates are near zero due to failing test oracles.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation stems from the increasing use of LLMs for code generation, which raises questions about whether traditional test adequacy criteria remain effective for detecting LLM-induced faults. Prior research focused on human-written programs, but LLMs introduce a new paradigm of faults that may require different evaluation approaches.

Method: The study conducted an empirical evaluation involving 5 LLMs and 4 benchmarks, simulating end-to-end workflows where both code and tests are automatically generated. They collected 6,000+ faulty program instances and evaluated 3 widely used adequacy criteria: statement coverage, branch coverage, and mutation testing.

Result: Key findings: (1) Most LLM faults are trivial to catch; (2) Challenging faults are difficult to trigger using coverage-based or mutation-based criteria; (3) Actual fault detection rates are near zero because test oracles fail to capture faulty behavior; (4) Prompt-aware oracles improve detection but remain limited; (5) Mutation testing only marginally outperforms traditional coverage criteria.

Conclusion: The study concludes that automated test generation has critical limitations in detecting LLM faults. Users cannot rely solely on adequacy criteria and must manually reason about test assertions. The marginal benefit of mutation testing over coverage criteria raises questions about its higher computational cost in LLM-generated code contexts.

Abstract: Test adequacy criteria are widely used to evaluate and guide software testing. Although prior research has extensively examined these criteria using human-written programs, faults, and tests, the increasing adoption of Large Language Models (LLMs) for code generation raises important questions about their effectiveness in detecting LLM-induced faults. To investigate this, we conduct an empirical study involving 5 LLMs and 4 benchmarks, simulating end-to-end workflows in which both code and tests are automatically generated. We collect 6,000+ faulty program instances and evaluate the effectiveness and efficiency of 3 widely used adequacy criteria: statement coverage, branch coverage, and mutation testing. Our findings reveal several key insights. First, most faults introduced by LLMs are relatively trivial to catch. Second, the challenging faults are difficult to trigger using either traditional coverage-based or mutation-based criteria. Third, actual fault detection rates remain extremely low, often near zero, because test oracles fail to capture faulty behavior triggered by the generated test prefixes, exposing a critical limitation of automated test generation. Fourth, prompt-aware oracles can improve fault detection, but their overall effectiveness remains limited, highlighting the need for users to manually reason about test assertions. We further observe that mutation testing only marginally outperforms traditional coverage criteria in both triggering and detecting faults, raising questions about whether its significantly higher application cost is justified in this context.

</details>


### [88] [The Vibe Shift in Software Engineering: Evaluating AI-Led Conversational Programming for Performance, Cognition, and Responsible Adoption](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09560)
*Sales G. Aribe,Louie Jay S. Labastida*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This study evaluates "Vibe Coding," an AI-led conversational programming paradigm where developers generate software through natural language interactions with LLMs. Using a mixed-methods study with 30 participants, it compares Vibe Coding with traditional and AI-assisted coding environments on efficiency, cognitive load, and software quality. Results show 27% faster task completion vs traditional coding, but with lower maintainability and higher security vulnerabilities. The study proposes a three-pillar framework for responsible adoption.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To evaluate Vibe Coding, an emerging AI-led conversational programming paradigm that enables developers to generate software through natural-language interaction with large language models, in comparison with traditional and AI-assisted coding environments.

Method: Mixed-methods design with 30 participants (professional developers and advanced computing students) completing equivalent programming tasks under three experimental conditions. Quantitative data analyzed using descriptive statistics and repeated-measures ANOVA; qualitative data examined through thematic analysis.

Result: Vibe Coding improved development efficiency by 27% vs traditional coding and 12% vs AI-assisted coding. However, it resulted in lower maintainability indices and higher security vulnerabilities. Usability: SUS = 71.4 (good rating). Cognitive workload: NASA-TLX = 55.5 (moderate). Thematic analysis identified trust calibration, loss of control, cognitive adaptation, and prompt-engineering strategy as key constructs. Perceived loss of control was associated with increased security risks due to reduced transparency and validation of AI-generated outputs.

Conclusion: Vibe Coding enhances productivity but requires critical oversight. The study proposes a three-pillar framework for responsible adoption: (1) hybrid integration of human and AI capabilities, (2) human oversight and transparent accountability, and (3) context-aware deployment. Vibe Coding is characterized as a transformative yet transitional paradigm in software development.

Abstract: This study evaluates Vibe Coding, an emerging AI-led conversational programming paradigm that enables developers to generate software through natural-language interaction with large language models. Using a mixed-methods design, the study assessed performance efficiency, cognitive implications, and responsible adoption in comparison with traditional and AI-assisted coding environments. Thirty participants, including professional developers and advanced computing students, completed equivalent programming tasks under three experimental conditions. Quantitative data were analyzed using descriptive statistics and repeated-measures ANOVA, while qualitative data were examined through thematic analysis. Results show that vibe coding significantly improved development efficiency, reducing task completion time by 27% compared with traditional coding and 12% compared with AI-assisted coding. However, these gains were accompanied by lower maintainability indices and higher security vulnerabilities, indicating trade-offs in software quality. Usability results yielded a good rating (SUS = 71.4), while cognitive workload remained moderate (NASA-TLX = 55.5), reflecting reduced syntactic effort but increased linguistic reasoning. Thematic analysis identified trust calibration, loss of control, cognitive adaptation, and prompt-engineering strategy as key constructs. Notably, perceived loss of control was associated with increased security risks due to reduced transparency and validation of AI-generated outputs. Based on these findings, the study proposes a three-pillar framework for responsible adoption: hybrid integration of human and AI capabilities, human oversight and transparent accountability, and context-aware deployment. Overall, vibe coding enhances productivity but requires critical oversight, reinforcing its role as a transformative yet transitional paradigm in software development.

</details>


### [89] [Introducing Consort: A Spec-First Agent Framework for Enforced, Test-Driven Development on Live Database Branches](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09671)
*Kevin Hartman*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper introduces Consort, a spec-first, test-driven agent framework that enforces engineering discipline through controls agents cannot bypass, using a deterministic orchestrator to drive separate role agents through spec-first design and test-driven build lanes on a live database branch.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As spec-first, agent-driven frameworks have gained traction since 2025, the authors identify a critical challenge: how to enforce engineering discipline (clean, correct, maintainable code) when agents write code. While existing frameworks agree on capturing intent upfront, they differ in how they enforce discipline.

Method: The authors characterize three modes of enforcement: (1) persuasion via prompts, (2) front-loaded structure with strong specs and trusted build, and (3) controls the agent cannot edit (deterministic orchestrator, human-approved gates, immutable tests). They then introduce Consort, implementing the third mode with a deterministic orchestrator driving separate role agents through spec-first design and test-driven build lanes on a live, branched database.

Result: The paper introduces Consort framework and frames two claims as pre-registered, testable hypotheses: (1) enforcing tests and gates in code keeps agent-written code honest and verifiable, and (2) specialized roles make the framework maintainable.

Conclusion: The authors argue that enforcing discipline through controls the agent cannot bypass—rather than through persuasion or structure alone—produces more honest and verifiable agent-written code, with specialized roles (like human roles before them) being essential for maintainability.

Abstract: When an agent writes code, the development framework becomes the control system for a non-deterministic worker. Spec-first, agent-driven frameworks have gained rapid traction since 2025; the installable ones, GitHub Spec Kit, obra/superpowers, BMAD, and GSD, and our own, all capture intent through a specification or durable planning artifacts. Since they agree on capturing intent up front, what separates them is how each enforces the engineering discipline that keeps agent-written code clean, correct, and maintainable. Every framework enforces that discipline somehow; they differ in how. We characterize three modes: enforcement by persuasion (prompt discipline the model may ignore), by front-loaded structure (strong specs, then a trusted build), and through controls the agent cannot edit (a deterministic orchestrator, human-approved gates, immutable tests, and a green result that must pass against a live, branched database). We introduce Consort, a spec-first, test-driven agent framework built on the third, enforcing that discipline through controls the agent runs inside but cannot bypass, in which a deterministic orchestrator drives separate role agents through a spec-first design lane and a test-driven build lane on a live database branch. We argue that enforcing the tests and gates in code keeps agent-written code honest and verifiable, while its specialized roles, like the human roles before them, are what make it maintainable, claims we frame as a pre-registered, testable hypothesis.

</details>


### [90] [XAgent: eXecution-guided Agentic AI for Effective Localization and Resolution of GitHub Issues](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09769)
*Hieu Huynh,Patanamon Thongtanunam,Michael Fu,Bach Le,Kla Tantithamthavorn*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: XAgent is an execution-guided agentic framework that leverages dynamic behavior analysis and additional program context to autonomously resolve repository-level GitHub issues, outperforming existing approaches with 62.0% resolve rate and 72.8% function localization accuracy on SWE-bench-lite.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing agentic approaches rely on limited static issue descriptions, causing incorrect localization and incomplete validation. This biases LLM reasoning toward narrow scope, leading to incomplete patches that fail to address underlying issues.

Method: XAgent analyzes dynamic behavior and additional program context to localize and validate issues, using an execution-guided approach rather than relying solely on static issue descriptions.

Result: Achieved 62.0% resolve rate and 72.8% function localization accuracy on SWE-bench-lite dataset while maintaining cost efficiency. Successfully resolved 7 additional issues that top existing baselines failed to address.

Conclusion: This work highlights a paradigm shift from static, description-oriented patch generation toward dynamic execution-guided issue resolution, opening new opportunities for LLM-based coding agents to achieve more robust and generalizable software maintenance.

Abstract: Agentic AI has enabled capabilities in leveraging Large Language Models (LLMs) to autonomously resolve repository-level GitHub issues. However, due to the reliance on limited static description of issues, existing agentic approaches suffer from incorrect localization and incomplete validation. Solely relying on this information can bias LLM reasoning toward the narrow scope of the issue description, leading to incomplete patches that fail to address the underlying issue. In this paper, we present XAgent, an execution-guided agentic framework that analyzes dynamic behavior and additional program context to localize and validate issues. The experimental results on the SWE-bench-lite dataset demonstrate that XAgent outperforms other existing approaches, achieving a resolve rate of 62.0% and a function localization accuracy of 72.8%, while maintaining cost efficiency. Our analysis further shows that XAgent successfully resolves 7 additional issues that the top existing baselines fail to address. This work highlights a shift from static, description-oriented patch generation toward dynamic execution-guided issue resolution, opening new opportunities for LLM-based coding agents to achieve more robust and generalizable software maintenance.

</details>


### [91] [Keep Evaluation Fair: Detecting Data Leakage in Code Generation Benchmarks via Membership Inference Attacks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09865)
*Dongdong Zhao,Jian Chen,Guancheng Lin,Jianwen Xiang,Jacky Wai Keung,Xiao Yu*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: CGMIA is a membership inference attack method specifically designed to detect data leakage in code generation benchmarks. It improves upon prior methods by using multiple expert features (CodeBLEU, edit distance, test pass rate, perplexity) combined with semantic features from CodeBERT, rather than relying solely on perplexity scores.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmark leakage detection method (DetectLeak) relies on perplexity scores, which mainly reflects general familiarity with code patterns and performs poorly on complex or rare samples. It also overlooks other useful signals like code similarity, functional correctness, and semantic representations.

Method: CGMIA fine-tunes a shadow model on a subset of benchmark samples to construct labeled member/non-member data. For each sample, it collects input prompts, generated code, and reference solutions, then extracts: (1) expert features (CodeBLEU, edit distance, test pass rate, perplexity) and (2) semantic features from CodeBERT embeddings. An integrated learning module combines these features to enable a classifier to predict training set membership.

Result: CGMIA outperforms eight existing membership inference methods on eight code generation benchmarks in most cases. It also effectively detects known leaked APPS samples in StarCoder-7B's training data.

Conclusion: CGMIA successfully addresses the limitations of perplexity-based leakage detection by combining multiple surface-level memorization signals with deeper behavioral patterns, providing a more robust solution for detecting benchmark data leakage in code generation models.

Abstract: Code generation benchmarks are widely used to evaluate Large Language Models (LLMs), but benchmark data leakage into training sets can inflate performance and undermine evaluation validity. DetectLeak, a method specifically designed for code generation benchmark leakage detection, relies on perplexity scores to identify likely leaked samples. However, perplexity mainly reflects general familiarity with code patterns and may perform poorly on complex or rare samples. It also overlooks other useful signals, such as code similarity, functional correctness, and semantic representations. To address these limitations, we propose CGMIA (Code-Generation-specific Membership Inference Attack), a method for detecting leakage in code generation benchmarks. CGMIA fine-tunes a shadow model on a subset of benchmark samples to construct labeled member and non-member data. For each sample, it collects the input prompt, generated code, and reference solution, and extracts expert features, including CodeBLEU, edit distance, test pass rate, and perplexity, together with semantic features from CodeBERT embeddings. An integrated learning module combines these features to capture both surface-level memorization signals and deeper behavioral patterns, enabling a classifier to predict whether a sample was included in the target model's training set. Experiments on eight code generation benchmarks show that CGMIA outperforms eight existing membership inference methods in most cases. It also effectively detects known leaked APPS samples in StarCoder-7B's training data.

</details>


### [92] [Socio-technical and Ethical Dimensions of Architecture Practices in FLOSS](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09975)
*Sven Thielen*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: A research project investigating how software architecture practices in Free/Libre Open Source Software (FLOSS) are shaped by socio-technical and ethical factors, and how education can support more explicit, inclusive, and reflective architectural work. The study addresses the problem that architectural decisions in FLOSS are often undocumented and scattered across various communication channels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The research is motivated by FLOSS's critical role in digital sovereignty. It addresses a gap in existing literature: while prior research has studied architectural artifacts, erosion, and communication, the interplay between architectural work, governance arrangements, and ethical commitments in FLOSS remains underexplored.

Method: The research follows a three-phase design: (1) multi-method case studies of 3-4 domain-pairs of architecturally non-trivial FLOSS projects, (2) framework and intervention design with practitioners and educators, and (3) pilot evaluations in projects and courses.

Result: The project will produce: (i) cross-case empirical evidence on FLOSS architecture practices, (ii) a conceptual framework linking architecture practices to socio-technical conditions and ethical dimensions, and (iii) lightweight practices and teaching formats that render architectural work more explicit and inclusive.

Conclusion: This research contributes to understanding the socio-technical and ethical dimensions of FLOSS architecture, and provides practical tools and educational approaches to improve architectural practices, ultimately supporting digital sovereignty through better-documented and more inclusive architectural work.

Abstract: This project investigates how software architecture practices in Free/Libre Open Source Software (FLOSS) are shaped by socio-technical and ethical factors, and how education can support more explicit, inclusive, and reflective architectural work. Motivated by FLOSS's role in digital sovereignty, it is observed that architectural decisions are often undocumented and scattered across issues, pull requests, and mailing lists. While prior research has studied architectural artifacts, erosion, and communication, the interplay between architectural work, governance arrangements, and ethical commitments in FLOSS remains underexplored. The research follows a three-phase design: (1) multi-method case studies of 3-4 domain-pairs of architecturally non-trivial FLOSS projects, (2) framework and intervention design with practitioners and educators, and (3) pilot evaluations in projects and courses. It will produce (i) cross-case empirical evidence on FLOSS architecture practices, (ii) a conceptual framework linking architecture practices to socio-technical conditions and ethical dimensions, and (iii) lightweight practices and teaching formats that render architectural work more explicit and inclusive.

</details>


### [93] [Beyond Repository Boundaries: Cross-Repository Graph Retrieval for Code Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09987)
*Minh Le-Anh,Nam Le Hai,Quyen Tran,Anh Nguyen Hoang,Linh Ngo Van,Bach Le,Nghi D. Q. Bui*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: CrossCoder is a cross-repository code generation framework that incorporates external libraries into retrieval context through a unified knowledge graph, improving functional correctness by up to 6.3% on pass@1 and robustness to dependency-version changes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing retrieval-based code generation methods mainly retrieve context from local repositories and rely on model's pretrained knowledge for external APIs, which is insufficient for unseen or version-specific APIs. Current retrieval strategies also focus on one-hop evidence and overlook structural relationships among code components.

Method: CrossCoder uses a unified knowledge graph over repository and library entities to incorporate external libraries into retrieval context. It identifies important nodes via planning and semantic retrieval, then selectively expands neighboring nodes to retrieve richer multi-hop contextual evidence for generation. VersionExec is an execution-based benchmark derived from BigCodeBench to evaluate generation under different dependency versions.

Result: CrossCoder consistently improves both functional correctness (up to 6.3% on pass@1) and robustness to dependency-version changes on RepoExec, DevEval, and VersionExec benchmarks.

Conclusion: The proposed CrossCoder framework effectively addresses limitations in existing retrieval-based code generation methods by explicitly incorporating external library knowledge through knowledge graphs and multi-hop retrieval, demonstrating significant improvements in both accuracy and version compatibility.

Abstract: Repository-level code generation requires generated code to be compatible not only with the target repository but also with its dependency environment. Existing retrieval-based methods mainly retrieve context from the local repository, leaving external API usage dependent on the model's pretrained knowledge, which can be insufficient for unseen or version-specific APIs. Moreover, current retrieval strategies largely focus on one-hop evidence and overlook the structural relationships among code components.
