We propose CrossCoder, a cross-repository code generation framework that explicitly incorporates external libraries into the retrieval context through a unified knowledge graph over repository and library entities. CrossCoder identifies important nodes via planning and semantic retrieval, then selectively expands neighboring nodes to retrieve richer multi-hop contextual evidence for generation. To further evaluate dependency-version compatibility, we introduce VersionExec, an execution-based benchmark derived from BigCodeBench that evaluates generation under different dependency versions. Experimental results on RepoExec, DevEval, and VersionExec demonstrate that CrossCoder consistently improves both functional correctness (up to 6.3% on pass@1) and robustness to dependency-version changes.

</details>


### [94] [GraphDroid: Asynchronous LLM-Based Mobile App GUI Testing via History-Aware Exploration and Hybrid Intent Fulfillment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10031)
*Xiaolei Li,Jialun Cao,Zhijian Hou,Yuzhi Zhao,Yepang Liu,Shing-Chi Cheung*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: GraphDroid is an intent-driven GUI testing framework for Android apps that addresses three key limitations of existing LLM-based tools: loss of historical context, synchronous intent generation blocking exploration, and high cost/latency from per-step LLM fulfillment. It uses a cluster-based memory mechanism, asynchronous intent generation, and a hybrid intent fulfillment strategy combining LLM for complex intents with lightweight heuristics for simple intents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Automated GUI testing is widely used for mobile app quality assurance, but covering complex functionalities requiring multi-step action sequences remains challenging. Traditional tools lack semantic understanding, while LLM-based tools suffer from three limitations: 1) losing historical context for identifying uncovered functionalities, 2) synchronous intent generation blocking exploration, and 3) per-step LLM-driven fulfillment incurring high cost and latency.

Method: GraphDroid integrates three key innovations: (1) a cluster-based memory mechanism to identify uncovered functionalities from historically visited states, (2) an asynchronous intent generation paradigm to eliminate latency bottleneck, and (3) a hybrid intent fulfillment strategy that reserves LLM for complex intents while delegating simple intents to a lightweight heuristic algorithm.

Result: GraphDroid was evaluated on 41 real-world Android apps against six state-of-the-art baselines. It achieved up to 36.4% higher code coverage while incurring less than one eighth of the cost of the best pure LLM-based baseline. It exposed 19 bugs and detected 13 of 52 crashes in the Themis benchmark, surpassing all baselines. Seven of the 19 bugs were previously unknown and reported to developers; four have been confirmed and fixed.

Conclusion: GraphDroid significantly outperforms all six state-of-the-art baselines in GUI testing effectiveness (code coverage, bug detection) and efficiency (cost reduction). The hybrid approach combining LLM for complex tasks with heuristics for simple tasks proves effective in balancing capability and performance. The discovery and confirmation of real bugs validates its practical utility for mobile application testing.

Abstract: Automated GUI testing is a widely adopted technique for ensuring mobile application quality by simulating user interactions to exercise functionalities. Despite the research breakthroughs in the past decades, covering complex functionalities that require multi-step action sequences still remains challenging. Traditional tools lack semantic understanding capability and can rarely synthesize such action sequences. Recent LLM-based tools can generate test intents describing target functionalities and leverage the LLM to fulfill the intents, but suffer from three key limitations: 1) loss of historical context for identifying uncovered functionalities, 2) synchronous intent generation that blocks exploration, and 3) per-step LLM-driven fulfillment incurring high cost and latency. To address these limitations, we propose GraphDroid, an intent-driven GUI testing framework that integrates a cluster-based memory mechanism to effectively identify uncovered functionalities from historically visited states for comprehensive application testing. For improving testing efficiency, GraphDroid adopts an asynchronous intent generation paradigm that eliminates the latency bottleneck and a hybrid intent fulfillment strategy that reserves the LLM for fulfilling complex intents while delegating simple intents to a lightweight heuristic algorithm. We evaluate GraphDroid on 41 real-world Android apps against six state-of-the-art baselines. Results show that GraphDroid outperforms all baselines, achieving up to 36.4% higher code coverage while incurring less than one eighth of the cost of the best pure LLM-based baseline. GraphDroid also exposes 19 bugs in the 41 apps and detects 13 of 52 crashes in the Themis bug benchmark, surpassing all the six baselines. Seven of the 19 bugs were previously unknown and we reported them to the developers. So far, four bugs have been confirmed and fixed.

</details>


### [95] [If It's Not Buggy, Don't Fix It: On the Dynamics of Iterative Bug-fixing with LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10123)
*Xietao Wang-Lin,Anton Isopoussu,Louis Mahon*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper investigates the iterative blind use of LLMs as automated bug-fixers and reveals significant issues: LLMs falsely detect bugs in bug-free programs, cause more damage to correct programs than they repair, and enter infinite pseudo-bug-fixing cycles. The study also discovers a steering vector controlling editing propensity, suggesting LLMs have internal representations of "buggy code" that are falsely activated.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to understand the dynamics and risks of fully autonomous LLM-based bug-fixing systems, particularly as these tools are increasingly used in code review processes without proper stopping conditions or verification.

Method: The researchers conducted experiments across multiple LLMs and repair environments, using iterative blind application of LLMs as bug-fixers. They also employed mechanistic probing to investigate the internal representations controlling editing behavior.

Result: LLMs consistently: (1) falsely detect bugs in entirely bug-free programs; (2) cause more damage to correct programs than successful repairs in buggy programs; (3) enter pseudo-bug-fixing cycles where the same changes are repeatedly added and removed. Mechanistic probing revealed a steering vector controlling editing propensity, indicating LLMs have internal representations of "buggy code" that are falsely activated.

Conclusion: These findings highlight critical risks in fully autonomous bug-fixing systems and the need for proper stopping conditions when goals are ambiguous. The existence of a steering vector for editing propensity suggests it's possible to control and potentially mitigate these false bug-fixing behaviors.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have become ubiquitous in software development, with LLM-based automated program repair tools increasingly used during code review. In this report, we explore the iterative blind use of LLMs as bug-fixers. Across multiple models and repair environments, we find that LLMs consistently claim to detect bugs in entirely bug-free programs while the rate of repair of buggy programs is less than that of the damage to correct programs. We also explore the long-term dynamics of this iterative process, and find that this frequently reaches a pseudo-bug-fixing cycle where the same changes are added and removed again ad infinitum. Lastly, via mechanistic probing, we unveil the existence of a steering vector which controls the editing propensity, suggesting that LLMs have an internal representation of ``buggy code", and that this representation is what is falsely activated to induce pseudo-bug fixing. These results provide insight towards the dynamics of fully autonomous bug-fixing systems, as well as stopping conditions under ambiguous goals.

</details>


### [96] [A-JIT: Agentic Just-In-Time Software Construction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10248)
*Mark Marron,Earl T. Barr*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: A-JIT is a novel software paradigm that replaces traditional static binaries with dynamic, self-evolving software systems. It integrates an AI agent directly into the application runtime to continuously observe system usage and execution traces, enabling on-the-fly code synthesis, capability generation, and adaptation to end-user behavior—similar to how JIT compilers specialize machine code to runtime execution paths.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional software delivery assumes a static paradigm where code is fixed after compilation and deployment. This approach cannot adapt to changing user needs or evolving contexts. The authors propose A-JIT to address this limitation by enabling software to perpetually evolve and specialize to meet specific end-user needs in real-time.

Method: A-JIT integrates synthesis directly into the ambient application lifecycle. The system comprises three core components: (1) code, (2) a runtime harness, and (3) an embedded AI agent. The AI agent continuously observes system usage and live execution traces, then dynamically constructs missing implementations, generates new capabilities, and adapts software logic, workflows, and tool interfaces to the end-user.

Result: The model enables trace-driven human-AI co-construction and opens a new design space for adaptive, self-evolving software systems that can perpetually evolve to meet changing demands.

Conclusion: A-JIT represents a fundamental shift from static software delivery to dynamic, AI-driven software construction. By bringing synthesis into the runtime lifecycle, applications can continuously adapt to user behavior, potentially revolutionizing how software is developed, deployed, and maintained.

Abstract: Traditional software delivery assumes a static paradigm: code is constructed prior to execution and deployed as a fixed artifact. We present Agentic Just-In-Time Software Construction (A-JIT), a paradigm that replaces static binaries with dynamic, software systems that can perpetually evolve to meet changing demands. In A-JIT, an application is an integrated assembly comprising code, a runtime harness, and an embedded AI agent that continuously observes system usage and live execution traces. Much like a traditional JIT compiler specializes machine code to runtime execution paths, A-JIT specializes software logic, workflows, and tool interfaces to meet the specific needs of the end-user. By integrating synthesis directly into the ambient application lifecycle, A-JIT enables applications to dynamically construct missing implementations, generate new capabilities on the fly, and continuously adapt to end-user behavior. We demonstrate how this model supports trace-driven human-AI co-construction and opens a new design space for adaptive, self-evolving software.

</details>


### [97] [Ensembling LLMs for AI-Augmented Cybersecurity Software Requirements Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10316)
*Santiago Perez-Acuna,Yod-Samuel Martín,Juan C. Yelmo*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper proposes post-generation ensembling to improve LLM-generated cybersecurity requirements from security standards. By aggregating multiple stochastic LLM runs using information-retrieval data-fusion operators (Uniform and Naive-Bayes fusion), the method recovers all 72 valid requirements while filtering out 111 hallucinations, significantly outperforming single configurations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Translating security standards into concrete system-specific requirements is labor-intensive and recall-sensitive. LLMs can accelerate this but suffer from unreliability: missing valid safeguards and introducing plausible hallucinations. Output also varies across runs and models, which is typically seen as a problem to solve.

Method: The authors reframe output variability as a resource rather than a problem. They propose two post-generation ensembling strategies: (1) Uniform fusion rewards cross-run agreement, weighting all runs equally; (2) Naive-Bayes fusion weights each run by its estimated reliability. Evaluation used 24 runs from 12 configurations across four model families, generated for 10 ISO/IEC 27002:2022 controls, judged against a gold standard of 72 valid requirements.

Result: Pooling every run's output recovered all 72 valid requirements (vs. under half for single configurations) but also introduced 111 hallucinations. Fusion effectively separated valid requirements from hallucinations. Uniform fusion alone surpassed every original run and configuration by 0.142 in AUC-PR and 0.118 in ROC-AUC. Naive-Bayes weighting added further gains of 0.039 and 0.052, reaching 0.864 and 0.869 respectively. Gains remained positive in 92% of bootstrap resamples and all structured-perturbation samples.

Conclusion: Post-generation fusion transforms LLM output variability from noise into a practical asset. A lightweight fusion layer provides broader coverage and better-prioritized review queues using affordable, below-frontier models alone. This approach enables reliable automation of security requirements engineering without requiring state-of-the-art models.

Abstract: Translating high-level controls from security standards into concrete, system-specific requirements is central to cybersecurity requirements engineering. Large language models (LLMs) can accelerate this labor-intensive, recall-sensitive task, but any single run is unreliable: it misses valid safeguards while introducing plausible hallucinations, and outputs shift across runs and models. We reframe this variability as a resource: rather than selecting one output, we study post-generation ensembling, aggregating stochastic runs with information-retrieval data-fusion operators. We propose two strategies: Uniform fusion rewards mere cross-run agreement, whereas Naive-Bayes fusion weights each run by its estimated reliability. We evaluate both over 24 runs from 12 configurations across four model families, generated for ten ISO/IEC 27002:2022 controls and expert-judged against a gold standard of 72 valid requirements. Pooling every run's output recovers all 72 (whereas single configurations recover on average under half) but also 111 hallucinations. Fusion separates the wheat from the chaff, ranking valid requirements well ahead of hallucinations. In the areas under the precision-recall and ROC curves, Uniform fusion alone largely surpasses every original run and configuration by 0.142 and 0.118 over the best configuration. Naive-Bayes weighting adds a further 0.039 and 0.052, reaching 0.864 and 0.869 while attaining useful operating points earlier. Internal validation confirms the stability of these gains: they stay positive in at least 92% of out-of-bag bootstrap resamples and every structured-perturbation sample. Post-generation fusion thus turns apparent noise into a practical asset: a lightweight layer giving analysts broader coverage and a better prioritized review queue, using affordable, below-frontier models alone.

</details>


### [98] [Retrofitting Code Using LLMs to Support Exceptional Behavior](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10397)
*Linghan Zhong,Jiyang Zhang,Jayanth Srinivasa,Junyi Jessy Li,Milos Gligoric*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper introduces EXCODER, a novel tool that automatically generates Exception Related Code (ERC) in existing Java codebases by combining static/dynamic program analysis with Large Language Models. Given code without ERC and Exceptional Behavior Tests (EBTs), EXCODER retrofits the missing exception handling code to make tests pass.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Manually writing Exception Related Code (throw statements, if guards, try/catch blocks) across large codebases is tedious and time-consuming. There was no existing automated way to implement ERC following test-driven development principles.

Method: EXCODER performs context engineering by integrating static and dynamic program analysis with LLMs. It provides extracted contextual information to LLMs to help them generate appropriate ERC. The benchmark was built by systematically removing ERC from 304 methods in 75 GitHub Java repositories.

Result: When combined with Qwen 2.5 Coder 32b, EXCODER achieves pass@1 rate of 85.92%, pass@5 of 86.18%, and pass@10 of 86.51%, representing improvements of 12.56, 12.82, and 13.15 percentage points over the baseline respectively.

Conclusion: EXCODER provides an effective, though imperfect, solution for automated ERC generation. It offers developers the first way to implement exception handling code following test-driven development. Manual inspection reveals limitations that point to directions for future work.

Abstract: Exception Related Code (ERC), which includes throw statements, conditions (if statements) that guard those throw statements, and try/catch blocks, is an essential component of software systems, allowing developers to detect and handle exceptional states that deviate from the expected program behavior. However, manually writing ERC across large codebases is tedious. We propose a novel task: retrofitting existing code with ERC. Namely, given code (without ERC) and Exceptional Behavior Tests (EBTs) (e.g., check if method throws InvalidArgumentException if null is given as the value to the argument) we aim to automatically generate missing ERC, such that the given tests pass. We design and implement Exception Coder (EXCODER) that performs context engineering to help Large Language Models (LLMs) tackle this task. EXCODER integrates static and dynamic program analysis with LLMs by providing the extracted contextual information to the LLMs. To evaluate EXCODER, we build a benchmark constructed from GitHub Java repositories, where we systematically remove ERC in 304 methods from 75 projects. Our results demonstrate that EXCODER provides an effective, though imperfect, solution to this problem in automated code generation, offering developers the first way to implement ERC following test-driven development. When combined with Qwen 2.5 Coder 32b, EXCODER achieves pass@1, 5, and 10 rates of 85.92% (12.56 percentage points over baseline), 86.18% (12.82 p.p. over baseline), and 86.51% (13.15 p.p. over baseline), respectively, on developer-written test suites. Our manual inspection of the generated code further reveals limitations of EXCODER, pointing to directions for future work.

</details>


### [99] [Towards Scalable and Cost-Efficient Vulnerability Detection: A Study on Automatic Query Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10412)
*Ivana Clairine Irsan,Ratnadira Widyasari,Huihui Huang,Ting Zhang,Yue Liu,Ouh Eng Lieh,Shar Lwin Khin,Kang Hong Jin,David Lo*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper evaluates LLMs' ability to automatically generate CodeQL queries for vulnerability detection, finding that LLM-generated queries achieve 82% improvement in F1-score over baseline CodeQL queries while being more cost-effective than direct LLM-based scanning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Static analysis tools like CodeQL require significant manual effort to develop comprehensive query suites. This paper addresses the challenge of automating the creation of security queries by leveraging LLMs to bridge the gap between unstructured vulnerability reports and formal static analysis specifications.

Method: The authors conducted an empirical study using vulnerability data from the National Vulnerability Database (NVD) to evaluate various LLM architectures' ability to synthesize CodeQL queries. They measured detection coverage and precision across diverse real-world vulnerabilities and performed a cost-benefit analysis comparing LLM-generated CodeQL queries versus direct LLM-based repository scanning.

Result: LLM-generated queries significantly enhanced baseline CodeQL queries, yielding an 82% improvement in average F1-score. The cost-benefit analysis revealed that while direct LLM scanning of entire repositories is computationally and financially prohibitive, using LLMs to synthesize CodeQL queries offers a scalable and cost-effective alternative for large-scale vulnerability detection.

Conclusion: LLMs can effectively bridge the gap between unstructured vulnerability reports and formal static analysis specifications, providing a scalable path toward comprehensive automated vulnerability detection. This approach offers a practical solution for improving static analysis coverage while managing computational costs.

Abstract: Static analysis remains a cornerstone of software security, yet the effectiveness of tools such as CodeQL is often limited by the substantial manual effort required to develop high-coverage query suites. While large language models (LLMs) have emerged as a potential solution for automated code reasoning, their practical utility in generating structured, executable security queries remains underexplored. In this paper, we conduct an empirical study to evaluate the ability of LLMs to synthesize CodeQL queries using vulnerability data from the National Vulnerability Database. Through this investigation, we explore the potential of using LLMs as an automatic CodeQL query generator. Subsequently, we systematically evaluate the performance of various LLM architectures across a diverse set of real-world vulnerabilities, measuring their ability to improve detection coverage and precision. Our findings reveal that LLM-generated queries significantly enhance the baseline CodeQL queries, yielding 82% improvement in average F1-score. Furthermore, we provide a detailed cost- benefit analysis showing that while direct LLM-based scanning of entire repositories is often computationally and financially prohibitive, leveraging LLMs to synthesize CodeQL queries offers a scalable and cost-effective alternative for large-scale vulnerability detection. Our results suggest that LLMs can effectively bridge the gap between unstructured vulnerability reports and formal static analysis specifications, offering a scalable path toward comprehensive automated vulnerability detection.

</details>


<div id='cs.GR'></div>

# cs.GR [[Back]](#toc)

### [100] [InstantMimic: A High Performance System for Learning Physics-based Skills in Seconds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09821)
*Ikjun Choi,Geonho Leem,Jungdam Won*

Main category: cs.GR

TL;DR: InstantMimic is a GPU-native training system for physics-based character control that reduces training time for diverse skills to a few seconds by integrating simulation, environment computation, policy inference, and policy updates into a unified pipeline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current Deep RL approaches like DeepMimic for physics-based character control are computationally inefficient despite GPU-accelerated simulation, due to overheads from fragmented GPU kernels and CPU memory access in the critical path, making end-to-end pipelines underutilize hardware.

Method: Built on a GPU-native physics backend, InstantMimic makes the entire training loop GPU-native by integrating simulation, environment computation, policy inference, and policy updates within a single execution flow.

Result: InstantMimic reduces training time for diverse physics-based skills to a few seconds and makes LLM-agent-driven hyperparameter search practical.

Conclusion: The proposed GPU-native unified pipeline effectively addresses computational inefficiencies in physics-based character control training, enabling practical LLM-agent-driven hyperparameter search.

Abstract: Physics-based character control is a long-standing challenge in computer graphics and robotics, requiring policies that satisfy complex dynamics while producing realistic motion. Recent Deep RL approaches, particularly imitation learning methods such as DeepMimic, have had broad impact beyond animation, influencing robotics by enabling agile and expressive behaviors. While these approaches achieve impressive results, they remain computationally inefficient to train in practice. Despite GPU-accelerated simulation, we find that end-to-end pipelines often underutilize hardware due to overheads outside the physics solver, caused by fragmented GPU kernels and CPU memory access in the critical path. We present InstantMimic, a system that addresses these inefficiencies by making the entire training loop GPU-native. Built on a GPU-native physics backend, our unified pipeline integrates simulation, environment computation, policy inference, and policy updates within a single execution flow. As a result, InstantMimic reduces training time for diverse physics-based skills to a few seconds and makes LLM-agent-driven hyperparameter search practical.

</details>


### [101] [RealSimLoop: Online Real-to-Sim Adaptation via Differentiable Reduced-Order Simulation with Vision Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09828)
*Zhihao Cen,Chuhua Xian,Hailin Sun,Yuliang Liufu,Zhen Zhang,Xiangyu Chu,Hongmin Cai,Yunbo Zhang,Guoxin Fang*

Main category: cs.GR

TL;DR: RealSimLoop is a differentiable framework for online real-to-sim adaptation of deformable objects using vision data as physical feedback. It uses a reduced-order neural subspace with differentiable simulation to achieve quasi-real-time performance, coupled with differentiable rendering for gradient-based parameter refinement. A sliding-window objective enables robust online adaptation to time-varying material properties.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world observations of deformable objects are sparse/surface-level, while downstream tasks require hidden physical quantities (internal deformation, stress fields, interaction forces). Physics-based simulation can recover these, but online real-to-sim adaptation is challenging due to costly full-space optimization, limited feedback, and time-varying material properties.

Method: 1) Reduced-order neural subspace for differentiable simulation (accelerates optimization)
2) Differentiable rendering for direct gradient backpropagation from pixel data
3) Sliding-window objective function for robust online adaptation to time-varying properties

Result: Outperforms conventional offline methods; validated for external force prediction and 3D stress field reconstruction with novel view synthesis.

Conclusion: RealSimLoop enables quasi-real-time real-to-sim adaptation using vision feedback, effectively bridging the real-to-sim gap from model reduction or unmodeled dynamics, with applications in force prediction and stress field reconstruction.

Abstract: Real-world observations of deformable objects are often sparse or surface-level, while downstream tasks require hidden physical quantities such as internal deformation, stress fields, and interaction forces. Physics-based simulation can recover these quantities, but online real-to-sim adaptation remains challenging due to costly full-space optimization, limited feedback, and time-varying material properties. To address these challenges, we propose RealSimLoop, a differentiable framework for online real-to-sim adaptation using vision data as physical feedback. Our approach achieves quasi-real-time performance by executing differentiable simulation within a reduced-order neural subspace, drastically accelerating the optimization loop. We couple this efficient dynamics model with differentiable rendering, enabling direct gradient backpropagation that leverages high-fidelity pixel data to refine physical parameters such as material stiffness. Furthermore, by employing a sliding-window objective function, RealSimLoop enables robust online adaptation, allowing the system to track time-varying material properties and effectively bridge the real-to-sim gap arising from model reduction or unmodeled dynamics. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our method outperforms conventional offline methods, and we validate the framework's versatility in downstream applications, including external force prediction and 3D stress field reconstruction with novel view synthesis.

</details>


### [102] [Semi-Implicit Pairwise Descent for Nonlocal Continuum Mechanics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09834)
*Xukun Luo,Xiao Cheng,Yuzhong Guo,Ying Qiao,Wencheng Wang,Xiaowei He*

Main category: cs.GR

TL;DR: SIPD proposes a unified nonlocal pairwise framework for simulating large-scale hyperelastic materials with complex contact and friction, avoiding costly Hessian computations while maintaining stability and convergence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The simulation of large-scale hyperelastic materials involving complex contact and friction faces challenges with computational costs (especially Hessian computations) and integration of contact/friction into elastic solver frameworks. The paper aims to address these efficiency and integration challenges.

Method: The method involves three key innovations: 1) Reformulating FEM equations of motion into a pairwise force representation from a nonlocal perspective to avoid costly Hessian computations; 2) An analytical projection strategy for projecting Hessian-free coefficient matrices to positive semi-definiteness; 3) Treating contact and friction as a unified anisotropic elastic energy for seamless integration into the elastic solver framework.

Result: The method achieves real-time performance for million-scale simulations under intricate contact and friction conditions. The mathematical proof establishes unconditional stability and numerical convergence.

Conclusion: SIPD provides an efficient, stable, and convergent approach for large-scale hyperelastic material simulation with complex contact and friction, significantly reducing computational overhead while maintaining numerical robustness.

Abstract: We propose Semi-Implicit Pairwise Descent (SIPD), a unified nonlocal pairwise framework for simulating large-scale hyperelastic materials involving complex contact and friction. By reformulating the Finite Element Method (FEM) equations of motion into a pairwise force representation from a nonlocal perspective, our approach avoids costly Hessian computations, leading to a reduction in per-iteration computational overhead. Furthermore, we propose an analytical projection strategy for projecting our Hessian-free coefficient matrices to positive semi-definiteness. And we treat contact and friction as a unified anisotropic elastic energy, allowing for a seamless integration into the elastic solver framework. We mathematically prove that our method is unconditionally stable and numerically convergent.Experimental results demonstrate that SIPD achieves real-time performance for million-scale simulations even under intricate contact and friction conditions.

</details>


### [103] [GLOSS: Geometric Local Self-Similarity Learning for Faithful Reference-Guided Texture Fill](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.25461)
*Chenyue Cai,Anita Hu,James Lucas,Szymon Rusinkiewicz,Masha Shugrina*

Main category: cs.GR

TL;DR: This paper presents a shape-specific local texture generation and completion model that leverages geometric self-similarity and geometry-texture correlation. The approach trains on a single 3D shape using image model priors and geometry-aware reference patches, enabling patchwise texture transfer for novel references. It supports local geometry-conditioned texture inpainting, PBR materials, and texture transfer to unseen meshes, with a Blender addon evaluated by 3D professionals.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current generative methods struggle to generate full object textures while adhering to fine-scale geometric detail and single view references. Existing automatic models lack flexibility for artists to explore multiple textures from varied sources interactively. The authors seek a local, less data-hungry approach with explicit artist control rather than relying on large 3D datasets.

Method: The approach trains a shape-specific local texture generation and completion model using a single 3D shape. It leverages geometric self-similarity and geometry-texture correlation by attending to geometry-aware reference patches. The trained network transfers any novel reference to full target object texture through patchwise inpainting. The model enables local geometry-conditioned texture inpainting guided by artist-selected references.

Result: The model shows improved or comparable quality to strong image-conditioned texture generation baselines. It supports local geometry-conditioned texture inpainting, generalizes to PBR materials and unseen meshes for texture transfer, and was piloted as a Blender addon with positive feedback from 3D texturing professionals on controllability, practical usefulness, and creative affordances.

Conclusion: Local texturing represents a promising research direction. The shape-specific approach successfully provides artist control while maintaining texture quality, demonstrating practical usefulness through the Blender addon evaluation with industry professionals.

Abstract: Using conditional image generators, texture artists can explore many single-view looks for an existing 3D shape. Despite impressive progress, state-of-the-art generative methods still struggle to generate a full object texture while closely adhering to fine scale geometric detail and single view references, leaving little room for artists guidance. Furthermore, current automatic models lack the flexibility for artist to explore multiple textures from varied sources in an interactive and controllable manner. Unlike methods trained on large 3D datasets that generate full object textures from global guidance, our work explores a local and less data-hungry approach to texture with explicit artist control.
  We leverage the geometric self-similarity and geometry-texture correlation existing in many natural and man-made shapes; and train a shape-specific local texture generation and completion model. This model learns from existing image model priors and a single 3D shape, and is guided by attending to a set of geometry-aware reference patches. The trained shape-specific network can transfer any novel reference to the full target object texture through patchwise inpainting. We show improved or comparable quality to strong image-conditioned texture generation baselines, suggesting local texturing as a promising research direction.
  Our model also enables local geometry-conditioned texture inpainting, guided by artist-selected references, and generalizes to PBR materials and unseen meshes for texture transfer. We piloted our novel texture fill capability as a Blender addon with several 3D texturing professionals who reported positive feedback on the model's controllability, practical usefulness, and creative affordances.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [104] [OpenDiscoveryTrace: Process Traces for Evaluating AI Scientist Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09203)
*Aayam Bansal,Keertan Balaji*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OpenDiscoveryTrace is a public dataset of 558 AI scientific agent trajectories that captures reasoning processes (not just outputs) across 124 tasks in drug discovery, materials science, genomics, and literature analysis. The dataset reveals that models with comparable success rates (84-89%) have vastly different error profiles—Claude Opus 4.6 makes 30× more errors than GPT-5.4, primarily from tool misuse vs. reasoning errors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks for autonomous AI scientists only evaluate final outputs (code, hypotheses, papers) and discard the reasoning process, making it impossible to audit scientific methodology, diagnose failures, or distinguish systematic reasoning from lucky guesses. This work addresses the need for process-level evaluation of AI scientific agents.

Method: The authors created a structured 9-field-per-step trace schema capturing thoughts, tool calls, observations, errors, revision triggers, and self-reported confidence. They evaluated 7 models across 124 scientific tasks: 3 frontier models (124 trajectories each) and 4 open-weight models (30 each), plus 60 live-retrieval variants. Pilot analysis used LLM judges on 363 trajectories.

Result: All three frontier models achieve similar success rates (84-89%), but Claude Opus 4.6 produces 30× more errors than GPT-5.4 (2.5 vs. 0.08 per trajectory, p<0.0001, Cliff's δ=0.613). Error profiles differ qualitatively: Claude shows 66.7% tool misuse while GPT-5.4 shows 83.6% reasoning errors. Five benchmark tasks with baselines (logistic regression, random forests, LSTMs, Transformers) are defined.

Conclusion: Process traces expose behavioral differences invisible to output-only evaluation. The OpenDiscoveryTrace dataset, trace schema, agent harness, and benchmark definitions are publicly available under CC BY 4.0 to support research on process-level evaluation, scientific agent auditing, and AI governance.

Abstract: Existing benchmarks for autonomous AI scientists evaluate only final outputs---generated code, hypotheses, or papers---yet discard the reasoning process by which those outputs were obtained. This makes it impossible to audit scientific methodology, diagnose failure modes, or distinguish systematic reasoning from fortunate guessing. We present \textbf{OpenDiscoveryTrace}, a public dataset of 558 complete AI scientific agent trajectories that captures how models reason, not just what they produce. Each trajectory records a structured 9-field-per-step trace---including thoughts, tool calls, observations, errors, revision triggers, and self-reported confidence---as models execute 124 scientific tasks spanning drug discovery, materials science, genomics, and scientific literature analysis. The dataset covers seven models: three frontier models (GPT-5.4, Claude Opus 4.6, and Gemini 3.1 Pro; 124 trajectories each, fully balanced across domains and difficulty levels) and four open-weight models (Qwen2.5-7B, Mistral-7B-v0.3, Phi-3.5-mini, and Qwen2.5-1.5B; 30 each), plus 60 live-retrieval variant trajectories. Pilot analysis on 363 LLM-judged trajectories reveals that process traces expose behavioral differences invisible to output-only evaluation: all three frontier models achieve comparable success rates (84--89%), yet Claude Opus 4.6 produces 30$\times$ more errors than GPT-5.4 (2.5 vs. 0.08 per trajectory, $p < 0.0001$, Cliff's $δ= 0.613$), with qualitatively different error profiles---66.7% tool misuse for Claude versus 83.6% reasoning errors for GPT-5.4. We define five benchmark tasks with baselines from logistic regression, random forests, LSTMs, and Transformer models. The dataset, trace schema, agent harness, and benchmark definitions are publicly available under CC BY 4.0 to support research on process-level evaluation, scientific agent auditing, and AI governance.

</details>


### [105] [Adaptive Entangled Game Modules in Artificial General Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09226)
*Haochen Li,Xinshuai Guo,Jingdong Ouyang,Wei Zhang,Leilei Shi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a probability-wave framework using a Generalized Behavioral Intelligence (GBI) nonlocal probability-wave equation to model collective behavior of adaptive agents. The framework is tested on Chinese intraday stock market data to examine the Liu-Chen-Ao (LCA) hypothesis about nonlocal entangled nerve fibers in the brain. Results show 82-94% of decision patterns are explained by adaptive entangled game modes, supporting the LCA hypothesis and suggesting implications for AGI architecture development.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation appears to be two-fold: (1) to develop a theoretical framework that can explain collective human intelligence behaviors through probability-wave mechanisms, and (2) to empirically test the Liu-Chen-Ao (LCA) hypothesis about nonlocal entangled nerve fibers in the brain through observable trading behaviors in financial markets.

Method: The study employs a probability-wave framework with a GBI nonlocal probability-wave equation to derive testable eigenmodes. Empirical analysis was conducted using Chinese intraday stock market data to examine collective trader behaviors as an indirect method to test the LCA hypothesis about nonlocal entangled nerve fibers in the brain.

Result: The empirical analysis demonstrates that adaptive entangled game modes explain 82-94% (89% overall) of observed decision patterns in Chinese intraday stock trading. Only 2-12% of behaviors show adaptation to intraday news, events, and environments, characterized by dual equilibrium states and abrupt reference point shifts. Purely independent modes occur in less than 5% of cases. These findings are presented as empirical support for the LCA hypothesis.

Conclusion: The conclusions are: (1) The LCA hypothesis about nonlocal entangled nerve fibers in the brain is empirically supported through observable trading behaviors; (2) Adaptive entangled game modules should be incorporated into AGI architectures to address limitations of conventional ANN-based AI; (3) Integrating ANN-based AI with probability-wave-based entangled-brain simulations could facilitate development of human-like processing units (HPUs) for more compact, efficient, and robust AGI systems.

Abstract: We introduce a probability-wave framework for modeling the collective behavior of interacting adaptive agents, deriving testable eigenmodes through a generalized behavioral intelligence (GBI) nonlocal probability-wave equation. This framework captures a broad range of human intelligence behaviors with analytical mechanisms and offers an indirect method to examine the Liu-Chen-Ao (LCA) hypothesis of nonlocal entangled nerve fibers in the brain through collective trader behaviors. Our empirical analysis of Chinese intraday stock market data demonstrates that adaptive entangled game modes explain 82-94% (89% overall) of observed decision patterns, a sharp contrast to the predictions of neoclassical finance based on independent rational agents. Moreover, 2-12% of behaviors show adaption to intraday news, events, and environments, characterized by dual equilibrium states and abrupt reference point shifts, while purely independent modes occur in less than 5% of cases. These findings empirically support the LCA hypothesis, as observable trading behaviors reflect underlying brain mechanisms and internal intelligence decision-making in behavioral psychology. Our results highlight the necessity of incorporating adaptive entangled game modules into artificial general intelligence (AGI) architectures, addressing the limitations of conventional artificial neural network (ANN)-based AI, which relies on trillions of opaque parameters. By integrating ANN-based AI with probability-wave-based entangled-brain simulations, machine learning can enrich AGI foundation models (FMs) and facilitate the development of human-like processing units (HPUs) that leverage brain-inspired mechanisms. Such HPUs may ultimately create more compact, efficient, and robust AGI systems, particularly for embodied intelligence and robotics.

</details>


### [106] [Subagents vs Agent Skills: Executing Reusable Knowledge for Long-Horizon Agentic Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09233)
*Wasu Top Piriyakulkij,Rachel Lawrence,Alicia Curth,Sushrut Karmalkar,Niranjani Prasad*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: How can language model agents effectively leverage libraries of reusable knowledge to solve long-horizon tasks? Recent work has increasingly focused on agent skills: reusable capabilities represented as skill packages, i.e., multi-file bundles containing instructions, scripts, and other resources that help agents perform specific tasks. Agent skills are typically executed by loading their skill instructions into an agent's context and relying on the agent to follow them. As task horizons grow, however, this approach becomes increasingly brittle, because reasoning quality degrades as more information accumulates in the context window. We investigate an alternative approach in which skill packages are instead invoked as subagents. Rather than loading skill instructions into the main context, subagent execution spawns fresh context windows dedicated to solving individual subtasks. We show that subagent execution outperforms agent-skill execution when skill packages expose clear input-output contracts and their instructions encode the procedural knowledge needed to fulfill those contracts. The tradeoff is additional communication overhead, as extra tokens are required to coordinate between the main agent and its subagents. Our results show that the benefit of reusable knowledge depends not only on its content, but also on how it is organized and invoked.

</details>


### [107] [Gradland: On Phenomenal Experience, Differentiated Across Many Dimensions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09306)
*David Balduzzi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes that the mathematical structure of gradients/Jacobians in physical interactions can characterize phenomenal experience. Using an idealized world called "Gradland" with differentiable neural networks, the authors introduce two measures (effective rank and cohesion based on Kirchhoff complexity) to test this hypothesis across various aspects of consciousness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors seek to understand the fundamental structure of phenomenal experience by linking it to the first-order structure of physical interactions (gradients/Jacobians). They aim to explain various aspects of consciousness through a unified mathematical framework.

Method: The paper introduces two measures: effective rank and cohesion, based on Kirchhoff complexity. These measures are applied to a series of worked examples in an idealized world (Gradland) inhabited by neural networks where physics are known and functions are differentiable.

Result: The hypothesis accounts for seven phenomena: (1) duration of experience spanning hundreds of milliseconds; (2) difference between vivid and obscure experiences; (3) experience of texture; (4) blooming buzzing confusion in newborns; (5) distinction between distinct and confused ideas; (6) the nature of learning; (7) the function of rich, dense experience.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that the first-order structure of physical interactions (gradients/Jacobians) can characterize the structure of phenomenal experience, providing a unified framework to explain various aspects of consciousness from duration to learning.

Abstract: This paper investigates the hypothesis that the first-order structure of physical interactions, i.e. gradients or Jacobians, characterizes the structure of phenomenal experience. It does so in an idealized world inhabited by neural networks, Gradland, where the physics are known and the functions are (mostly) differentiable. The paper introduces two measures of Jacobian structure: effective rank and cohesion, based on Kirchhoff complexity. Applying the measures to a series of worked examples shows the hypothesis accounts for: (1) the duration of experience, that it can prolong over hundreds of milliseconds; (2) the difference between what is experienced vividly and obscurely; (3) the experience of texture; (4) the blooming buzzing confusion presumably experienced by newborns; (5) the difference between ideas that are held distinctly in mind and ideas that are confused; (6) what learning is like; and finally (7) the paper explains the function of rich, dense experience.

</details>


### [108] [An Autonomous GeoAI Agent for Arctic Eco-Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09374)
*Samira Alkaee Taleghan,Younghyun Koo,Farnoush Banaei-Kashani*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A multi-agent GeoAI system for Arctic eco-navigation that integrates operational, physical, ecological, and community criteria into a unified routing framework, with human-in-the-loop decision support to balance vessel safety, efficiency, and environmental/social impacts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Arctic maritime navigation is expanding due to changing sea-ice conditions, but existing routing methods prioritize travel time, fuel use, and navigational risk while overlooking ecological and community impacts. This creates a need for a more comprehensive routing approach.

Method: A human-in-the-loop, multi-agent GeoAI system with specialized agents coordinating geospatial data acquisition/preparation, multi-objective route generation, and skyline-based decision support. The system explicitly accounts for ecological criteria including Essential Fish Habitat and seal critical habitat, while keeping value judgments under human control.

Result: A framework that integrates operational, physical, ecological, and community-related criteria within a unified routing system, supporting safer, more transparent, and socially responsible Arctic navigation.

Conclusion: The proposed system enables more comprehensive Arctic route planning that considers ecosystem impacts and potential community burdens alongside traditional operational factors, with consequential decisions remaining under human control.

Abstract: Arctic maritime navigation is becoming increasingly important as changing sea-ice conditions expand seasonal accessibility while simultaneously introducing substantial operational, environmental, and community risks. Arctic route planning is inherently a multi-criteria problem: routes that improve vessel safety or efficiency may increase exposure to sea ice, sensitive ecosystems, or nearby communities. Existing routing methods prioritize travel time, fuel use, and navigational risk, often overlooking ecological and community impacts. We introduce a human-in-the-loop, multi-agent GeoAI system for Arctic eco-navigation that integrates operational, physical, ecological, and community-related criteria within a unified routing framework. Multiple specialized agents coordinate geospatial data acquisition and preparation, multi-objective route generation, and skyline-based decision support. The ecological criteria explicitly account for exposure to sensitive areas, including Essential Fish Habitat and seal critical habitat. By considering these ecosystem impacts and potential community burdens while keeping consequential value judgments under human control, the framework supports safer, more transparent, and socially responsible Arctic navigation. Project page and code are publicly available. https://samiraat.github.io/Arctic-Eco-Navigation-Agent/, https://github.com/samiraat/Arctic-Eco-Navigation-Agent

</details>


### [109] [The Menu Is an Execution Prior: State-Path Tool Menus for Online Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09395)
*Bo Yan,Weikai Lin,Song Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces the "State-Path Tool Menu" framework to address the challenge of tool selection in practical AI agents dealing with large tool libraries. The approach learns a pre-execution route from the observable request state to the desired outcome, treating the tool menu as an execution prior. It achieves significant improvements in success rates (0.737 to 0.898) on ToolBench while using fewer tools than baseline approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current tool ranking methods in practical agents focus on request relevance but fail to account for prerequisite tools that produce necessary inputs. When libraries contain thousands of tools, agents need an ordered subset (tool menu) that includes both final actions and their prerequisite producers in a usable order.

Method: The framework uses three key components: (1) An encoder representing tool compatibility with current state, output-input satisfaction, and recurrent order patterns from training paths; (2) A retriever covering executable entries, missing-input producers, and final actions; (3) A reranker that places producers before consumers in the menu.

Result: On ToolBench, the State-Path Tool Menu raises online success from 0.737 to 0.898. It outperforms retrieval, reranking, generation, and routing baselines. Notably, 32 tools in the State-Path menu covers more complete chains than 128 tools in the official list. The success improvement persists across executor families with different model capacities.

Conclusion: The State-Path Tool Menu effectively addresses the tool selection problem by learning execution routes that include both final actions and their prerequisite producers. The approach demonstrates that a smaller, intelligently ordered tool menu can outperform larger traditional lists, with gains generalizing across different executor architectures.

Abstract: Language models act through tools, yet practical agents face libraries containing thousands of interfaces. We introduce the tool menu as the short, ordered subset of available tools shown to an agent before execution. The agent can call only tools in this menu. Multi-step tasks require the final action and the prerequisite tools that create its inputs in a usable order. Current constructors rank tools by request relevance, which can surface the final action while omitting or delaying less obvious producers. We introduce the state path, a pre-execution route from the observable request state to the desired outcome, and propose State-Path Tool Menu to learn it. Our framework treats the menu as an execution prior over these routes. Its encoder represents which tools can run from the current state, how their outputs satisfy later inputs, and which orders recur in training paths. A retriever covers an executable entry, the missing-input producers, and the final action. A reranker then places producers before consumers. On ToolBench, our menu raises online success from 0.737 to 0.898 and outperforms retrieval, reranking, generation, and routing baselines without changing the agent. The State-Path menu also covers more complete chains with 32 tools than the official list covers with 128, and its success gain persists across executor families with different model capacities. Our code is at https://github.com/Met2348/State-Path.

</details>


### [110] [Decision-Focused Active Learning for Scale-Aware Critical-Materials Recovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09413)
*Niranjan Srinivas,Debajyoti Ray,Elias Nakouzi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents an adaptive experimental design framework for optimizing recovery processes of rare-earth elements from recycled magnets (NdFeB and SmCo). The approach uses active learning and Bayesian decision theory to efficiently identify optimal recovery conditions with fewer experiments compared to non-adaptive methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scale-up of recovery processes for rare-earth elements requires connecting laboratory results with product requirements, process costs, and scale effects. The key challenge is efficiently finding optimal process conditions while minimizing experimental resources.

Method: The study employs: (1) Active learning using prior results to select experiments; (2) Conditional retrospective benchmark with fitted models; (3) Two-stage reconstruction for adaptive alternatives; (4) Bayesian decision theory for batch selection based on expected reduction in downstream Bayes risk; (5) Exploratory simulations comparing hybrid filtering vs joint search approaches.

Result: Active learning reaches the recorded enrichment maximum by 16-24 wells versus 48 for non-adaptive space filling. Two-stage reconstruction ties adaptive alternatives at 16 wells. For SmCo magnets, Round 2 shows a tradeoff between purity and nominal yield. NdFeB Round 1 routes differ in enrichment. The hybrid filtering approach shows lower estimated loss than joint search, though differences are small relative to estimation uncertainty.

Conclusion: The proposed method of selecting batches by expected reduction in downstream Bayes risk shows promise for efficient process optimization. A pre-registered prospective test is outlined, requiring clarified measurements, defined process decisions, credible economic inputs, and validation at intended scale. Further work is needed on measurement standardization and economic input validation.

Abstract: Choosing a recovery process for scale-up requires connecting laboratory results with product requirements, process costs, and scale effects. We analyze records from Pacific Northwest National Laboratory's Computer Intelligence for Critical Element Recovery and Optimization (CICERO) workflow for autonomous selective precipitation. Active learning uses prior results to choose experiments. In a conditional retrospective benchmark with fitted models and recycled neodymium-iron-boron (NdFeB) magnet records, active learning finds the best recorded result with fewer experiments than nonadaptive space filling. Enrichment is the selected rare-earth-to-iron ratio relative to that in the feed. Adaptive policies reach the recorded enrichment maximum by 16 to 24 wells (individual experiments), versus 48. Our two-stage reconstruction ties two adaptive alternatives at 16 wells. Conditional analyses of recycled samarium-cobalt (SmCo) magnets show a Round 2 tradeoff between purity and nominal yield, the recovery fraction calculated from an assumed starting amount - NdFeB Round 1 routes differ in enrichment. Rankings for produced water from oil and gas extraction depend on phase and dilution assumptions requiring confirmation.
  We propose choosing batches by their expected reduction in downstream Bayes risk: the minimum expected loss among available process decisions under current beliefs. In exploratory simulations, a hybrid that filters candidates has lower estimated loss than the implemented joint search across routes and conditions. Differences involving the synthetic two-stage policy are small relative to estimation uncertainty. We outline a pre-registered prospective test under a shared loss and logging standard, requiring clarified measurements and records, a defined process decision and relevant outputs, credible economic inputs, and validation at the intended scale.

</details>


### [111] [Valerant: An Automatic Navigable Game Map Generator via Action-Conditioned World Model Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09418)
*Yiran Qiao,Feng Wang,Jing Ma*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Valerant is a training-free framework that transforms pretrained action-conditioned world models into World Action Models (WAMs) for 3D game map creation by combining predictive visual rollouts with SLAM-based spatial reconstruction and exploration-driven action selection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing game-oriented WAM approaches operate in 2D visual observation space and lack persistent 3D geometry. Unlike robotics/autonomous driving where physical environments exist independently, games require virtual world instantiation. This creates a distinct challenge for extending WAMs to 3D games.

Method: Valerant couples predictive visual rollouts with SLAM-based spatial reconstruction and exploration-driven action selection to progressively transform a single image into a persistent 3D game map.

Result: The framework extends WAM-based interaction beyond 2D visual simulation, enabling automated 3D game map creation without training.

Conclusion: Valerant offers a new approach to reducing manual effort in 3D game-map creation by transforming action-conditioned world models into WAMs capable of persistent 3D geometry instantiation.

Abstract: World Action Models (WAMs) couple predictive world modeling with action generation, allowing anticipated future states to guide agent behavior. Although WAMs are rapidly advancing embodied AI, general-purpose counterparts remain largely unexplored in games. Existing game-oriented approaches often combine action-conditioned world models with external policies and reward functions to realize WAM-like decision-making, yet they operate mainly in 2D visual observation space and do not instantiate persistent 3D geometry. Extending this paradigm to 3D games introduces a distinct challenge. In autonomous driving and robotics, the physical environment exists independently of the model, providing a persistent 3D world in which selected actions can be executed. Games have no such external substrate; the virtual world itself must be instantiated. Most playable games require a persistent and navigable space, while 3D games additionally require explicit geometry that supports movement and interaction. Action-conditioned video rollouts provide visual observations but not this spatial representation. We present \textsc{Valerant}, a training-free framework that transforms a pretrained action-conditioned world model into a WAM for exploring and constructing 3D game maps. By coupling predictive visual rollouts with SLAM-based spatial reconstruction and exploration-driven action selection, \textsc{Valerant} progressively transforms a single image into a persistent 3D game map. This framework extends WAM-based interaction beyond 2D visual simulation and offers a new approach to reducing manual effort in 3D game-map creation.

</details>


### [112] [XAI-Arena: Can LLMs Assess the Quality of XAI Explanations?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09428)
*Yanfei Hu Fleischhauer,Alona Zharova,Nadja Klein,Stefan Feuerriegel*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces XAI-Arena, an LLM-as-a-judge framework for scalable and reproducible evaluation of XAI explanations, addressing limitations of subjective human assessment. The framework evaluates explanations across 8 dimensions and shows strong correlation (rho=.693) with human ratings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluating XAI explanations relies on subjective human judgment, which limits reproducibility, scalability, and comparability between studies. There is a need for a more systematic and scalable approach to assess XAI explanation quality.

Method: The paper proposes XAI-Arena, an LLM-as-a-judge framework that evaluates XAI explanations across 8 dimensions: perceived simplicity, clarity, task adequacy, trust calibration, actionability, transparency, faithfulness, and overall interpretability. The framework is benchmarked across various datasets, ML models, and stakeholder personas. Human validation is conducted to assess the correlation between LLM and human ratings.

Result: Human validation demonstrates a strong positive association between LLM-generated and human ratings (Spearman's rho=.693, p<.001). LLM-based evaluations can capture systematic differences in XAI explanation quality.

Conclusion: LLM-based evaluations provide a scalable and reproducible framework for comparative assessment of XAI explanations, enabling multidimensional and stakeholder-sensitive evaluation that aligns with human judgment.

Abstract: Evaluating the quality of explanations produced by explainable AI (XAI) methods remains challenging because existing approaches often rely on subjective human judgment, limiting reproducibility, scalability, and comparability between studies. We examine whether LLMs can serve as a reproducible and scalable mechanism to make comparative assessments of the quality of XAI explanations. We introduce XAI-Arena, an LLM-as-a-judge framework for scalable, reproducible, multidimensional, and stakeholder-sensitive evaluation of XAI explanation quality. XAI-Arena then allows us to compare XAI explanations along various dimensions, namely, perceived simplicity, clarity, task adequacy, trust calibration, actionability, transparency, faithfulness, and overall interpretability. We then benchmark XAI explanation methods across various datasets, machine learning models, and stakeholder personas. Human validation shows a strong positive association between LLM-generated and human ratings (Spearman's rho=.693, p<.001). Together, LLM-based evaluations can capture systematic differences in XAI explanation quality and provide a scalable and reproducible framework for comparative assessment of XAI explanations.

</details>


### [113] [Do Agents Know When They Succeed? Calibrating Agent Confidence from Internal Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09448)
*Priyanka Mary Mammen,Emil Joswin,Srujananjali Medicherla*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper addresses confidence measurement in agentic AI systems by proposing two methods - LTD (Latent Trajectory Dynamics) and ARP (Action Representation Probe) - that use internal model representations to predict task success in multi-turn agentic interactions, outperforming existing calibration baselines without requiring prompt changes or multiple rollouts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic systems are rapidly adopted in safety-critical applications, but traditional confidence measures fail due to complex failure modes involving planning, tool invocation, and dynamic environment interactions. There's a need for reliable confidence signals in multi-turn agentic setups.

Method: Two complementary methods: (1) Latent Trajectory Dynamics (LTD) - summarizes changes in residual-stream representations across interaction trajectories; (2) Action Representation Probe (ARP) - predicts success from representations formed at action decisions. Both methods use internal model representations rather than surface-level signals.

Result: Across three interactive benchmarks (Bash, SQL, Python) and three model families (Qwen14B, Qwen7B, DeepSeek6.7B), these methods consistently outperform surface-level generation and sequence-based calibration baselines.

Conclusion: The proposed methods provide a zero-overhead reliability monitor that requires neither prompt alterations nor multi-sample rollouts, offering a practical solution for confidence estimation in agentic systems.

Abstract: As agentic systems getting adopted rapidly in safety critical applications, it is vital to measure the confidence associated with the agentic actions. In comparison to the traditional machine learning systems, agentic workflows have complex failure modes with planning, tool invocation and dynamic environment interactions. In this paper, we investigate whether model's internal representations provide stronger signals of eventual task success in multi-turn agentic setups. We introduce two complementary methods: Latent Trajectory Dynamics (LTD), which summarizes changes in residual-stream representations across an an interaction trajectory, and the Action Representation Probe (ARP), which predicts success from representations formed at action decisions. Across three interactive benchmarks (Bash, SQL, Python) and three model families (Qwen14B, Qwen7B, DeepSeek6.7B), our methods consistently outperform surface level generation and sequence-based calibration baselines providing a zero-overhead reliability monitor that requires neither prompt alterations nor multi-sample rollouts.

</details>


### [114] [ContractEval: Query-Conditioned Execution Matching for Procedural Instruction Conformance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09458)
*Praphul Singh,Shanu Kumar,Akshat Agarwal,Ganesh Kumar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CONTRACTEVAL is a diagnostic framework that evaluates LLM agents carrying out procedures by making active obligations explicit and detecting conformance failures (omissions, wrong branches, ordering errors, extra actions, invariant breaches, output-contract violations) that output-only and trace-aware evaluation methods miss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM evaluation methods (output-only and trace-aware) fail to identify procedural failures where the final answer looks acceptable but the system skipped critical checks, branches, dependencies, or invariants. This creates a gap in detecting structural failures in procedural tasks.

Method: The framework represents procedural instructions as query-active obligations and matches them against response or trace evidence. It uses gold expected and observed graphs to detect and localize conformance failures including: omissions, wrong branches, ordering errors, extra actions, invariant breaches, and output-contract violations.

Result: On a controlled suite of audited procedural contracts, output-only and trace-aware LLM judges miss many injected structural failures. ContractEval detects and localizes all of them under gold graphs. LLM-backed extraction preserves much of the signal but remains calibration-sensitive.

Conclusion: ContractEval is not a compliance guarantee but makes procedural conformance auditable rather than implicit in final-answer quality. It fills the gap between output-only evaluation (sees answer) and trace-aware judging (sees activity) by identifying which obligations were active for the query.

Abstract: As LLM agents move from answering questions to carrying out procedures, failures can be unwarranted rather than visibly wrong: the final response looks acceptable even though the system skipped the check, branch, dependency, or invariant that made the answer justified. Output-only evaluation sees the answer, and trace-aware judging sees activity, but neither identifies which obligations were active for the query. We introduce CONTRACTEVAL, a diagnostic framework for making those active obligations explicit. It represents procedural instructions as query-active obligations and matches them against response or trace evidence, turning omissions, wrong branches, ordering errors, extra actions, invariant breaches, and output-contract violations into distinct conformance failures. On a controlled suite of audited procedural contracts, output-only and trace-aware LLM judges miss many injected structural failures; under gold expected and observed graphs, ContractEval detects and localizes all of them. LLM-backed extraction preserves much of this signal but remains calibration-sensitive. ContractEval is therefore not a compliance guarantee; it makes procedural conformance auditable rather than implicit in final-answer quality.

</details>


### [115] [Multi-Agent Agentic Graph Learning via Structural Signatures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09565)
*Liang Qu,Jianxin Li,Hua Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MAAGL is a multi-agent framework for agentic graph learning that partitions graphs into communities, assigns independent agents for region-specific specialization, and uses permutation-invariant structural signatures with debate-style collaboration to overcome limitations of shared reasoning policies and context explosion in existing AGL methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing AGL methods rely on shared reasoning policy across graph regions, which is suboptimal for heterogeneous graphs. Additionally, verbalizing graph structures breaks permutation invariance, and incorporating large neighborhoods leads to context explosion.

Method: MAAGL partitions the graph into communities with independent agents for each community. Structural evidence is represented by dynamically updated permutation-invariant signatures of fixed size, while semantic evidence is filtered to top-k relevant nodes. Agents estimate confidence based on historical trajectories with similar signatures and trigger debate-style collaboration when needed.

Result: Extensive experiments on four benchmark datasets show that MAAGL outperforms state-of-the-art AGL methods.

Conclusion: Multi-agent collaboration with region-specific specialization, permutation-invariant structural representations, and selective semantic evidence proves effective for graph reasoning tasks, addressing key challenges of heterogeneity, invariance, and context efficiency in AGL.

Abstract: Agentic graph learning (AGL) has recently achieved promising results on graph reasoning tasks, where an agent powered by a large language model (LLM) sequentially samples the graph as evidence to support its final prediction. Existing methods either employ a single agent or orchestrate multiple role-based agents to reason and learn over the entire graph, but both essentially rely on a shared reasoning policy across different graph regions, which can be suboptimal for graphs with heterogeneous structural and semantic patterns. Inspired by the progress of multi-agent collaboration on complex reasoning tasks, a natural remedy is to let multiple agents own different memory and collaborate; however, applying this paradigm to graphs directly faces two challenges. First, existing AGL methods typically verbalize graph structures into natural-language descriptions for LLM agents, making the reasoning process sensitive to the ordering of structural information and thereby breaking the permutation-invariant nature of graphs. Second, incorporating increasingly large sampled neighborhoods leads to rapidly growing contexts. To address these challenges, this paper introduces a multi-agent agentic graph learning (i.e., MAAGL) framework. MAAGL partitions the graph into communities and assigns an independent agent to each community for region-specific specialization. MAAGL represents structural and semantic evidence separately. Structural evidence is summarized by a dynamically updated structural signature that is permutation-invariant and fixed in size, while semantic evidence is filtered to the top-k nodes ranked by relevance. Based on historical trajectories with similar signatures, agents estimate their confidence and trigger debate-style collaboration when needed. Extensive experiments on four benchmark datasets show that MAAGL outperforms SOTA AGL methods.

</details>


### [116] [CityPlanner: A Sandbox Agent for Executable Urban Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09578)
*Wentao Zhang,Jingyuan Wang,Zetong Zhou,Yifan Yang,Wenrui Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CityPlanner is a sandbox-agent framework for executable urban planning that uses a unified file-based environment (UrbanSandbox) where agents can inspect files, generate plans, run evaluators, and revise decisions based on executable feedback. It employs atomic-task RL decomposing the task into BuildPlan for initial construction and ImprovePlan for feedback-based refinement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing optimization and RL methods for urban planning are effective for fixed formulations but depend on task-specific representations and constraint handling. There's a need for a more general, executable framework that can handle the real-world complexity of urban planning problems.

Method: The paper proposes two key innovations: (1) UrbanSandbox, a unified file-based environment enabling agents to inspect task files, generate plans, run evaluators, and revise decisions based on executable feedback; (2) Atomic-task RL that decomposes long sandbox trajectories into BuildPlan for initial construction and ImprovePlan for feedback-based refinement.

Result: Experiments on a real-world benchmark show that CityPlanner consistently outperforms heuristic, task-specific RL, and general LLM-agent baselines. Ablations verify the contributions of UrbanSandbox, atomic-task RL, and iterative deployment.

Conclusion: The proposed CityPlanner framework successfully addresses the limitations of existing urban planning methods by providing a general, executable sandbox-agent approach with atomic-task RL, achieving superior performance across multiple baseline comparisons.

Abstract: Urban planning is a real-world spatial optimization problem that requires selecting feasible actions from large candidate spaces under practical objectives such as cost and service quality. Existing optimization and reinforcement learning methods are effective for fixed formulations, but often depend on task-specific representations and constraint handling. We propose \emph{CityPlanner}, a sandbox-agent framework for executable urban planning. CityPlanner introduces \emph{UrbanSandbox}, a unified file-based environment where agents inspect task files, generate plans, run evaluators, and revise decisions based on executable feedback. To make learning tractable, we further propose atomic-task reinforcement learning, which decomposes long sandbox trajectories into \emph{BuildPlan} for initial construction and \emph{ImprovePlan} for feedback-based refinement. Experiments on a real-world benchmark show that CityPlanner consistently outperforms heuristic, task-specific RL, and general LLM-agent baselines. Ablations verify the contributions of UrbanSandbox, atomic-task RL, and iterative deployment. We release the code and dataset at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/co-agent-C1C8

</details>


### [117] [A Function-Space Approach to the Statistical Mechanics of Learning Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09589)
*Yizhou Zhang,Weichen Wu,Lun Du,Zhengjie Miao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper develops a statistical-mechanical framework for analyzing learning in deep neural networks directly in function space, treating parameter configurations as microscopic states and functions with dynamical operators as macroscopic variables. For mean-squared loss, the error dynamics are governed by learning operator M = JJ*, and combining the dynamical Boltzmann weight with parameter-space density of states yields a fluctuation term that reveals preferences for certain network configurations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep neural networks exhibit regular macroscopic behavior despite operating in highly nonlinear, vast parameter spaces. This work seeks to understand the underlying principles of stable collective organization in learning by developing a macroscopic description directly in function space, rather than focusing on parameter-level dynamics.

Method: The authors treat parameter configurations as microscopic realizations and functions with dynamical operators as macroscopic variables. For mean-squared loss, they analyze exact error dynamics governed by learning operator M = JJ*. They combine the dynamical Boltzmann weight of conditional stochastic dynamics with the parameter-space density of states (whose local curvature defines statistical operator B), then integrate over local fluctuations to derive the fluctuation term.

Result: The key result is the fluctuation term Φ_fluc(M;B) = (σ_ξ²/2)log det(M⁻¹ + B) + const. At fixed spectrum, this term is rotationally stationary when [M,B] = 0, minimized by pairing large eigenvalues of M with small eigenvalues of B, and generates local restoring contribution against rotational mismatch. For ReLU function spaces under stable conditions, B = σ_ξ²L*KL, where low-B sectors correspond to low structural curvature, implying faster relaxation along smooth, data-adaptive directions.

Conclusion: Function space serves as a natural macroscopic level for studying stable collective organization in learning. The framework reveals that the interplay between learning operator M and statistical operator B determines network behavior, with preferences for configurations that balance dynamical learning with structural stability. This provides a principled foundation for understanding generalization and optimization in deep neural networks.
