Abstract: Deep neural networks exhibit regular macroscopic behavior despite highly nonlinear dynamics in vast parameter spaces. We develop a statistical-mechanical description of learning directly in function space, treating parameter configurations as microscopic realizations and functions with their dynamical operators as macroscopic variables. For mean-squared loss, the exact error dynamics are governed by the learning operator \(M=JJ^\ast\). Combining the dynamical Boltzmann weight of the conditional stochastic dynamics with the parameter-space density of states, whose local curvature defines a statistical operator \(B\), and integrating over local fluctuations yields
  $$ Φ_{\mathrm{fluc}}(M;B)=\frac{σ_ξ^2}{2}\log\det(M^{-1}+B)+\mathrm{const}. $$
  At fixed spectrum, this term is rotationally stationary when \([M,B]=0\), is minimized by pairing large eigenvalues of \(M\) with small eigenvalues of \(B\), and generates a local restoring contribution against rotational mismatch. For ReLU-type function spaces under mild stable statistical conditions, \(B=σ_ξ^2L^\ast\mathcal K L\), where \(L\) measures coarse-grained second-order structure. Thus the low-\(B\) sector corresponds, up to bounded anisotropy of \(\mathcal K\), to low structural curvature, implying a preference for faster relaxation along smooth, data-adaptive directions. These results identify function space as a natural macroscopic level for studying stable collective organization in learning.

</details>


### [118] [From State Synchronization to Cognitive Self-Evolution: An Operational Architecture for Cognitive Digital Twins](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09625)
*Haoran Gao,An Li,Zhen Li,Jun Cai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a four-layer Cognitive Digital Twin (CDT) architecture (physical, digital-twin, cognitive, task layers) with a self-evolving closed operational loop to systematically integrate cognition into Digital Twin systems. The framework enables task-specific cognitive modeling through knowledge, memory, and attention mechanisms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing CDT studies focus on specific enabling techniques (learning modules, knowledge graphs, LLMs) but provide limited insight into systematic integration of cognition into DT architectures. There is a need for a structured framework for CDT system design.

Method: The paper proposes a four-layer CDT architecture: (1) Physical layer, (2) Digital-twin layer, (3) Cognitive layer, and (4) Task layer. This creates a self-evolving closed operational loop where physical states sync to digital representations, cognition constructs task-specific cognitive models through knowledge/memory/attention, and task-level decisions are generated under constraints. Operational feedback refines cognitive experience and updates digital representations. Two operation modes are characterized: user-request-driven and self-driven cognition.

Result: A lightweight simulation demonstrates reliable closed-loop task feasibility under limited semantic information and improved operational efficiency through accumulated task experience. Key enabling mechanisms and deployment challenges are discussed for semantic communication, knowledge querying, task orchestration, and closed-loop synchronization.

Conclusion: The proposed four-layer framework provides a structured foundation for the design and development of future Cognitive Digital Twin systems, enabling systematic integration of cognitive capabilities into DT architectures.

Abstract: As Digital Twin (DT) systems evolve beyond state synchronization toward task-oriented and knowledge-driven operation, Cognitive Digital Twins (CDTs) have emerged as an extension that incorporates cognitive capabilities into twin operation. Existing CDT studies often focus on specific enabling techniques, such as learning modules, knowledge graphs, and large language models, while providing limited insight into how cognition can be systematically integrated into DT architectures. To address this issue, this paper proposes a four-layer CDT architecture consisting of the physical layer, digital-twin layer, cognitive layer, and task layer. The proposed architecture establishes a self-evolving closed operational loop spanning these four layers, in which physical states are synchronized into digital representations, cognition constructs task-specific cognitive models through knowledge, memory, and attention, and task-level decisions are generated under practical constraints. Operational feedback further refines cognitive experience and updates relationships and annotations in the digital representation, enabling subsequent task interpretation, initiation, and reasoning to evolve with system operation. Based on this framework, two representative operation modes are characterized: user-request-driven cognition and self-driven cognition. We further discuss key enabling mechanisms and deployment challenges associated with semantic communication, knowledge querying, task orchestration, and closed-loop synchronization. A lightweight simulation study illustrates reliable closed-loop task feasibility under limited semantic information and improved operational efficiency through accumulated task experience. The proposed framework provides a structured foundation for the design and development of future CDT systems.

</details>


### [119] [Seven Sources of Physical AI Capability Formation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09627)
*Gang Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a novel taxonomy of seven "capability-formation sources" to explain how Physical AI capabilities arise, moving beyond traditional morphologies, architectures, or task-based classifications. Using reconstructive induction on 49 evidence records, the authors claim theoretical saturation, suggesting these seven sources (RE, PM, EI, SE, MG, EC, ED) can explain all capabilities within their scope.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing taxonomies for Physical AI organize by morphology, architecture, learning algorithm, task, or domain—but these don't explain what gives rise to a capability. The authors seek a more fundamental classification based on how capabilities actually form.

Method: Reconstructive induction with theoretical saturation: (1) traced research matrix to primary studies, (2) deduplicated literature, (3) set coding rules, (4) conducted three rounds of maximum-difference and negative-case sampling. Scope fixed as of September 4, 2026.

Result: All 49 evidence records were explainable by the seven sources individually or in combination. No irreducible eighth source emerged from R1-R3 challenges. R3 required no new core definition or substantive boundary rule.

Conclusion: Theoretical saturation is claimed within the stated scope (not logical completeness or exhaustive future coverage). The framework distinguishes similarity in observed capability from similarity in formation process, supporting analysis of explanation, transfer, replication, dependencies, governance evidence, and geoeconomic foundations.

Abstract: Capabilities relevant to Physical AI can arise from materially different formation histories, yet existing taxonomies organized by morphology, architecture, learning algorithm, task, or domain do not directly answer what gives rise to a capability. We define a capability-formation source as a factor materially contributing to capability formation, distinct from components or construction steps. We identify seven non-exclusive sources: Recorded-Experience (RE), Predictive-Modeling (PM), Evaluative-Interaction (EI), Surrogate-Environment (SE), Mechanism-Grounded (MG), Embodied-Coupling (EC), and Evolution-Driven (ED) Formation. Using reconstructive induction with theoretical saturation, we traced a research matrix to primary studies, deduplicated the literature, set coding rules, and conducted three rounds of maximum-difference and negative-case sampling. Challenges included curriculum and self-supervised learning, active inference, open-ended and developmental learning, planning and search, neuro-symbolic architectures, digital twins, generative physical world models, and morphology-control co-design. Within the scope and criteria fixed as of September 4, 2026, all 49 evidence records were explainable by the seven sources individually or in combination. No R1-R3 challenge produced an irreducible eighth source, and R3 required no new core definition or substantive boundary rule. We therefore claim theoretical saturation within the stated scope, not logical completeness or exhaustive future coverage. The framework distinguishes similarity in observed capability from similarity in how it was formed, supporting analysis of explanation, transfer, replication, dependencies, governance evidence, and geoeconomic foundations.

</details>


### [120] [RobustSGPO: Search-Space Control for Agent Harness Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09646)
*Zibo Zhao,Jijun Shi,Mo Zhou,Zhongyuan Wang,Shifu Bie,Yunfei Zhang,Xuanting Zhou,Xiangyu Wu,Bin Liu,Ruiming Tang,Wenwu Ou,Kun Gai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RobustSGPO is a prompt optimization method for agent harnesses that improves upon SGPO by specifying edit requests, constructing/checking patches, and continuing search from retained snapshots. It achieves 80% completion on held-out tasks (vs 60%) and improves test quality from 3.77 to 4.14 under a 20M token budget.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: SGPO improves agent harnesses using execution feedback but has unresolved issues with local update rules regarding edit scope and operation choices. The authors aim to create a more robust optimization framework that can handle these ambiguities.

Method: RobustSGPO introduces: (1) specifying requested edits, (2) constructing and checking patches, (3) continuing search from either incumbent or retained snapshots. Evaluation uses permission scheduling, cumulative controls, and task-family transfer in the AgentX brainstorming workflow across 120 tasks, 95 runs, and 7,350 candidate attempts.

Result: Periodic 1→2→3 scheduling exceeds fixed maximum permission by 0.28 test-score points. RobustSGPO increases completion on 30 held-out tasks from 60.0% to 80.0% and improves test quality from 3.77 to 4.14 under a 20-million-token budget. Category retention reduces source-task degradation after shift while random retention reaches higher destination endpoints. Search-space control benefits quality through executable edits and alternative starting points.

Conclusion: RobustSGPO successfully addresses SGPO's limitations through explicit edit specification, patch validation, and flexible search continuation. The method shows significant improvements in task completion (20% absolute gain) and test quality (0.37 point improvement) while revealing trade-offs between category retention (stability) and random retention (peak performance).

Abstract: Semantic-gradient-based prompt optimization (SGPO) improves agent harnesses using execution feedback, but its local update rule leaves the choice of edit scope and operation unresolved. We introduce RobustSGPO, which specifies the requested edit, constructs and checks the patch, and continues search from either the incumbent or retained snapshots. We evaluate permission scheduling, cumulative controls, and task-family transfer in the AgentX brainstorming workflow using 120 tasks, 95 runs, and 7,350 candidate attempts. Periodic $1\to2\to3$ scheduling exceeds fixed maximum permission by 0.28 test-score points. RobustSGPO increases completion on 30 held-out tasks from 60.0% to 80.0% and improves test quality from 3.77 to 4.14 under a 20-million-token budget. Category retention reduces source-task degradation after a shift, whereas random retention reaches a higher destination endpoint. Search-space control benefits quality through executable edits and alternative starting points, with measurable retention overhead.

</details>


### [121] [Black-Box Red Teaming of Agentic AI: A Taxonomy-Driven Framework for Automated Risk Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09647)
*Divyanshu Kumar,Nitin Aravind Birur,Tanay Baswa,Sahil Agarwal,Prashanth Harshangi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents a systematic black-box framework for evaluating security risks in agentic systems (AI agents that can take autonomous actions). The framework includes a seven-domain risk taxonomy, automated red teaming (SAGE-RT) generating 120 adversarial scenarios per domain, and LLM-based evaluation. Testing on CrewAI and AutoGen architectures revealed significant vulnerabilities: 56.25% governance risk, 65% privacy risk in multi-agent setups, and 85% behavior vulnerabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic systems are increasingly deployed in production environments where they handle untrusted inputs, execute tools with real permissions, and act autonomously—expanding the security attack surface beyond traditional chat models. Existing evaluations are inadequate because they remain single-turn and fail to capture multi-step agent vulnerabilities that emerge in complex workflows.

Method: The researchers developed a black-box evaluation framework requiring only basic system descriptions. Their approach involved: (1) creating a seven-domain taxonomy mapping observable behaviors to risk categories, (2) developing fully automated SAGE-RT red teaming that produces 120 adversarial scenarios per domain, and (3) conducting human-validated evaluation using LLM judges. Empirical validation was performed across two agent architectures (CrewAI and AutoGen) with four base models.

Result: The evaluation revealed alarming security patterns: 56.25% average governance risk across systems, 65% privacy risk specifically in multi-agent configurations, and agent behavior vulnerabilities reaching 85%. The black-box approach successfully identified critical architectural vulnerabilities without requiring privileged access to the systems.

Conclusion: The proposed black-box framework effectively identifies critical security vulnerabilities in agentic systems without requiring insider knowledge or special access. This provides a scalable pathway toward safer agent deployments, enabling systematic risk assessment even for proprietary or closed-source agent systems. The findings highlight the urgent need for improved security measures in production agentic systems.

Abstract: Agentic systems are rapidly moving to production, where they read untrusted inputs, call tools with real permissions, and act autonomously, expanding the security surface beyond chat-only models. Yet standard evaluations remain single-turn and fail to capture multi-step agent vulnerabilities. We present a systematic black-box framework for risk-aware agent evaluation requiring only basic system descriptions. Our approach introduces: (1) a seven-domain taxonomy mapping observable behaviors to risk categories, (2) fully automated SAGE-RT red teaming producing 120 adversarial scenarios per domain, and (3) human-validated evaluation using LLM judges. Empirical validation across two agent architectures (CrewAI and AutoGen) with four base models reveals alarming patterns: 56.25\% average governance risk, 65\% privacy risk in multi-agent configurations, and agent behavior vulnerabilities reaching 85\%. Our black-box approach effectively identifies critical architectural vulnerabilities without privileged access, providing a scalable path toward safer agent deployments.

</details>


### [122] [RESCUE-BENCH: Towards Relation-Aware Multi-Party Emotional Support Conversation Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09657)
*Haichuan Hu,Yang Xiao,Mingni Tang,Jiawen Duan,Quanjun Zhang,Congqing He,Hao Zhang,Jiashuo Wang,Johan F. Hoorn,Wenjie Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces relation-aware emotional support conversation, a new task that evaluates LLMs' ability to capture and utilize interpersonal relationship dynamics for effective emotional support. The authors construct RESCUE benchmark from real couple and family interviews, containing 191 samples with 7,079 annotated turns. Experiments on 10 LLMs reveal that while models perform well on local emotional/intervention tasks, they struggle with relation-intensive tasks involving relation patterns, viewpoints, and support strategies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing emotional support conversation systems focus on one-on-one seeker-supporter interactions and individual emotional states, leaving interpersonal relations in multi-party scenarios underexplored. The motivation is to address this gap by evaluating whether LLMs can capture and utilize evolving relationship dynamics to offer more effective emotional support.

Method: The authors construct RESCUE (Relation-aware Emotional Support Conversation Understanding and Evaluation Benchmark) from real couple and family interview conversations. The benchmark contains 191 samples, 7,079 annotated turns, and 1,064.8 minutes of video. Based on rich annotations of socio-emotional and support-related dynamics, RESCUE defines six tasks evaluating two core capabilities: Relational Understanding and Relation-Sensitive Support.

Result: Experiments with ten LLMs show that current models perform relatively well on tasks relying on local emotional or intervention cues, but struggle with relation-intensive tasks such as relation pattern prediction, viewpoint prediction, and support strategy prediction.

Conclusion: The findings reveal the limitations of current LLMs in modeling interpersonal relations and making relation-sensitive support decisions, highlighting the need for future research to improve LLMs' ability to understand and utilize relational dynamics in emotional support contexts.

Abstract: Existing emotional support conversation systems mainly focus on one-on-one seeker-supporter interactions and individual emotional states, leaving interpersonal relations in multi-party scenarios underexplored. In this work, we introduce relation-aware emotional support conversation, a new task that evaluates whether LLMs can capture and utilize the evolving dynamics of relationships to offer more effective emotional support. We construct RESCUE (Relation-aware Emotional Support Conversation Understanding and Evaluation Benchmark) from real couple and family interview conversations, containing 191 samples, 7,079 annotated turns, and 1,064.8 minutes of video. Based on rich annotations of socio-emotional and support-related dynamics, RESCUE defines six tasks that evaluate two core capabilities required for relation-aware emotional support: Relational Understanding and Relation-Sensitive Support. Experiments with ten LLMs show that current models perform relatively well on tasks relying on local emotional or intervention cues, but struggle with relation-intensive tasks such as relation pattern prediction, viewpoint prediction, and support strategy prediction. These findings reveal the limitations of current LLMs in modeling interpersonal relations and making relation-sensitive support decisions.

</details>


### [123] [PRAGMA: Evaluating Personalized Guidance with Memory Alignment in Lifelong Conversations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09664)
*Hyojeong Yu,Hyukhun Koh,Minsung Kim,Yunah Jang,Kyomin Jung*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PRAGMA is a benchmark for evaluating personalized guidance in long-term conversations, addressing the gap in existing memory evaluations that focus only on retrieval and factual recall. The benchmark includes longitudinal conversation histories, evidence annotations, and guidance scenarios based on evolving user contexts and incorrect assumptions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLMs are deployed as personalized assistants interacting over extended periods, relying on full interaction histories becomes inefficient. Long contexts introduce computational overhead and make it difficult for models to identify relevant information. While memory systems have been developed, existing evaluations focus on retrieval and factual recall, missing practical guidance scenarios like recommendations, planning, and decision support that require integrating information across multiple conversations and reasoning about changing user preferences.

Method: The researchers created PRAGMA benchmark containing curated longitudinal conversation histories, evidence annotations, and guidance scenarios grounded in evolving user contexts and incorrect user assumptions. They conducted experiments across retrieval systems, memory systems, and long-context models to evaluate their performance.

Result: Current systems struggle both to recover appropriate conversational evidence and to effectively use it for personalized guidance. The experiments reveal significant challenges in memory-grounded reasoning beyond simple evidence recall.

Conclusion: The results highlight the need for memory architectures that support robust conversational retrieval and memory-grounded reasoning beyond evidence recall. Current approaches are inadequate for practical personalized guidance tasks.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed as personalized assistants that interact with users over extended periods of time. As conversations grow longer, relying on full interaction histories becomes increasingly inefficient and unreliable: long contexts introduce substantial computational overhead, making it difficult for models to consistently identify and utilize the most relevant information for the current request. These challenges have motivated memory systems that structure and retrieve user-specific information. In realistic interactions, users often seek practical guidance such as recommendations, planning, and decision support. Unlike factual recall tasks, personalized guidance requires models to integrate information across multiple past conversations and reason about changing user preferences and experiences. However, existing conversational memory evaluations mainly focus on retrieval and factual recall. To study this challenge, we introduce PRAGMA, a benchmark for evaluating personalized guidance in long-term conversations. PRGAMA contains curated longitudinal conversation histories, evidence annotations, and guidance scenarios grounded in evolving user contexts and incorrect user assumptions. Experiments across retrieval systems, memory systems, and long-context models reveal that current systems struggle both to recover the appropriate conversational evidence and to effectively use it for personalized guidance. Our results highlight the need for memory architectures that support robust conversational retrieval and memory-grounded reasoning beyond evidence recall.

</details>


### [124] [Safe to Stop? Risk-Constrained Stopping for Sequential Clinical Diagnosis Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09678)
*Yuexin Wu,Vasile Rus*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents Cros, a risk-constrained stopping layer for clinical diagnosis agents that decides when to diagnose or defer, rather than just what test to request. It uses state-wise error ranking, policy design on disjoint development splits, and LTT-style exact tests to provide finite-sample guarantees for selective diagnostic error and autonomous coverage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing clinical diagnosis agent benchmarks evaluate accuracy after fixed or unconstrained interaction, leaving autonomous stopping reliability implicit. There was a need for a framework that explicitly handles the decision of when to stop testing and diagnose versus when to continue gathering information.

Method: Cros combines three key components: (1) state-wise error ranking to predict diagnostic error risk at each state, (2) policy design on disjoint development splits to prevent overfitting, and (3) LTT-style exact tests of selective diagnostic error and minimum autonomous coverage. The finite-sample guarantee requires the candidate family, testing rule, and any randomization to be frozen before calibration labels are accessed.

Result: On a 1,834-episode MIMIC-derived abdominal-pain benchmark, the full ranker achieved exploratory state-error AUROC of 0.853, compared to 0.715 for maximum class probability and 0.552 for the backbone's native stop score. On the 367-episode evaluation split, Cros achieved 16.9% selective error at 78.8% coverage with cost 5.57 and 0.68 tests, versus 30.8% error at 100% coverage with cost 8.14 and 1.53 tests under native stopping. However, Cros only satisfied the joint criterion in 6 of 20 development resplits.

Conclusion: The findings provide exploratory feasibility and audit evidence for risk-constrained stopping in clinical diagnosis agents, but not a confirmatory safety certificate. The authors acknowledge that evaluation labels were inspected during earlier development, so the results should be interpreted as exploratory rather than confirmatory. The non-monotonic nature of forced continuation (error 28.3% with HPI alone vs 34.3% after full workup) highlights the complexity of optimal stopping in clinical settings.

Abstract: Clinical diagnosis agents must decide not only what test to request next, but also when to diagnose or defer. Existing agent benchmarks largely evaluate accuracy after fixed or unconstrained interaction, leaving autonomous stopping reliability implicit. We present Cros, a risk-constrained stopping layer combining state-wise error ranking, policy design on disjoint development splits, and LTT-style exact tests of selective diagnostic error and minimum autonomous coverage for complete sequential policies. Its finite-sample guarantee requires the candidate family, testing rule, and any randomization to be frozen before calibration labels are accessed. On a 1,834-episode MIMIC-derived abdominal-pain benchmark, the full ranker achieves exploratory state-error AUROC 0.853, compared with 0.715 for maximum class probability and 0.552 for the backbone's native stop score. On the previously viewed 367-episode evaluation split, analytically averaging over the frozen Cros weights yields 16.9% selective error at 78.8% coverage, cost 5.57, and 0.68 tests, versus 30.8% error at 100% coverage, cost 8.14, and 1.53 tests under native stopping. Forced continuation is non-monotone: error is 28.3% with HPI alone and 34.3% after full workup. However, the uniform-weight mixture ablation is cheaper on this viewed split despite missing the locked development margins, and Cros nominally satisfies the joint criterion in only 6 of 20 development resplits. Because evaluation labels were inspected during earlier development, these findings provide exploratory feasibility and audit evidence, not a confirmatory safety certificate.

</details>


### [125] [Decision Shifts, Lost Label Functionality, and an Inconclusive Grounding Audit in Correctness-Gated Multi-Teacher Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09702)
*Xiaofei Feng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper evaluates correctness-weighted multi-teacher knowledge distillation against unfiltered distillation and hard filtering for training a 63.9M-parameter student model on a three-response classification task. Despite improvements in accuracy (+0.166) and macro-F1 (+0.132), the weighted approach showed no incremental benefit over hard filtering and failed to establish evidence grounding gains in human audit.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The researchers aimed to distinguish between decision correctness and rationale grounding in knowledge distillation, testing whether correctness-weighted aggregation of multiple teachers could improve student model performance beyond simple hard filtering or unfiltered distillation.

Method: Eight experimental arms were trained: one baseline (source-label SFT), one hard filtering arm, and six distillation arms (unfiltered vs. weighted, with different teacher configurations). The weighted arm used correctness-weighted multi-teacher distillation. Evaluation was on 267 held-out examples across three seeds, with an additional availability-amended audit at one reference seed.

Result: The correctness-weighted arm showed improvements in accuracy (0.166), macro-F1 (0.132), and unsafe-action rate (-0.498) relative to unfiltered distillation. However, source-label SFT achieved the highest macro-F1 (0.586). The weighted arm had zero Refuted recall and two seeds assigned NotEnoughInfo to all 167 claim examples. Audit showed 0/20 vs 1/20 evidence-supported positives for weighted vs unfiltered, with high rates of unsupported material in both.

Conclusion: The implemented weighted arm showed no demonstrated incremental decision benefit over hard filtering (0.660 accuracy, 0.530 macro-F1, 0.135 conditional unsafe rate). The analysis represents a "fixed-matrix failure" showing decision redistribution with lost label functionality. The human audit cannot estimate common-source grounding effects and is inconclusive about system-level improvement or harm. Correctness weighting does not appear to solve the grounding problem in knowledge distillation.

Abstract: Candidate decision correctness and rationale grounding are different objectives. We examine correctness-gated multi-teacher distillation in a fixed experiment. Eight arms share 4,330 sources, a 63.9M-parameter student, 12,990 optimization rows, 406 updates, evidence inputs, and a decoder; seven teacher-based arms use one fixed three-response pool. Three seeds are evaluated on 267 held-out examples. Relative to unfiltered distillation, the correctness-weighted arm differed in accuracy by +0.1660 (95% observed-matrix interval [0.0670, 0.2455]), five-label macro-F1 by +0.1323 ([0.0916, 0.1731]), and task-defined conditional unsafe-action rate by -0.4979 ([-0.5926, -0.3686]). These shifts do not imply uniformly better behavior. Source-label SFT had the highest mean macro-F1 (0.586). The weighted arm had zero Refuted recall in every seed, and two seeds assigned NotEnoughInfo to all 167 claim examples. In an availability-amended audit at one reference seed, weighted and unfiltered outputs had 0/20 versus 1/20 evidence-supported positives and 20/20 versus 19/20 positives containing unsupported material. Samples were non-paired, source overlap was not serialized, and the amendment followed automatic summarization but preceded annotation. The audit therefore cannot estimate a common-source grounding effect and is inconclusive about system-level improvement or harm. Hard filtering already achieved 0.660 accuracy, 0.530 macro-F1, and 0.135 conditional unsafe rate. The implemented weighted arm showed no demonstrated incremental decision benefit over hard filtering. This fixed-matrix failure analysis shows decision redistribution with lost label functionality; the available human audit does not establish a grounding gain.

</details>


### [126] [Which Tokens Should SFT Actually Learn? A Token-Trimming Perspective on Mathematical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09707)
*Yaning Jia,Chunhui Zhang,Wenxuan Xu,Xingjian Diao,Xiaoyuan Wang,Soroush Vosoughi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TrimSFT is a token-level reweighting method for supervised fine-tuning that scales loss according to the logit gap between the gold token and its strongest competitor, trimming supervision from both over-mastered and under-confident tokens using a Gaussian weighting scheme centered at a margin with bandwidth τ.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard SFT applies uniform cross-entropy loss to all tokens, which over-sharpens already mastered tokens while amplifying learning pressure on uncertain, low-confidence tokens, leading to suboptimal training dynamics for mathematical reasoning.

Method: TrimSFT uses a Gaussian weight function centered at margin m with bandwidth τ to reweight token-level loss. It trims supervision away from tokens with large logit gaps (mastered) and tokens with small/negative logit gaps (weakly supported), concentrating learning in an intermediate logit-gap region. This requires no reference model or additional forward pass.

Result: TrimSFT consistently improves over standard SFT on six base models (Llama, Qwen, DeepMath families) across five mathematical reasoning benchmarks, achieving best average performance on five out of six models with gains up to +26.9 points on MATH500. Bandwidth τ matters more than exact margin location, and half-trim variants yield inferior trade-offs.

Conclusion: Reasoning SFT can benefit from trimming both extremes (over-mastered and under-confident tokens) rather than treating all tokens uniformly. TrimSFT reshapes model confidence more balanced than uniform SFT or monotonic reweighting methods.

Abstract: Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) applies a uniform cross-entropy loss to all target tokens, even though different tokens provide unequal learning signals for mathematical reasoning. This uniform treatment can over-sharpen already mastered tokens while amplifying learning pressure on uncertain, low-confidence tokens, leading to suboptimal training dynamics. We propose Trimmed Logit-Gap SFT (TrimSFT), a simple token-level reweighting method that scales the SFT loss according to the logit gap between the gold token and its strongest competitor. TrimSFT trims supervision away from both extremes: tokens already mastered (large logit gap) and tokens weakly supported by the current model (small or negative logit gap), concentrating learning within an intermediate logit-gap region between them. We instantiate this principle with a Gaussian weight centered at margin m with bandwidth τ, requiring no reference model or additional forward pass. We evaluate TrimSFT on six base models from the Llama, Qwen, and DeepMath families across five mathematical reasoning benchmarks. TrimSFT consistently improves over standard SFT, achieving the best average performance on five out of six models, with gains of up to +26.9 points over SFT on MATH500. Further analyses show that the bandwidth τ matters more than the exact margin location, and that half-trim variants that remove supervision pressure from only one side yield inferior trade-offs. A token-level logit-gap distribution analysis suggests that TrimSFT reshapes model confidence in a more balanced way than uniform SFT or monotonic reweighting methods. These results suggest that reasoning SFT can benefit from trimming both extremes rather than treating all tokens uniformly.

</details>


### [127] [Can Artificial Intelligence Support Healthcare and Mental Health Through Early Cyberbullying Detection ? The Impact of Emotion-Aware AI on Proactive Online Safety](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09735)
*Hamed Jelodar,Amir Firouzi,Yen-Wu Lo,Maryam Tanha,Sajjad Dadkhah*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CareGuard is an early-warning framework for healthcare-driven mental health protection that detects cyberbullying-related content using advanced NLP techniques, combining zero-shot semantic labeling with fine-tuned transformer models (BERT, DistilBERT, RoBERTa) and emotion-aware filtering for efficient, scalable deployment in healthcare systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Healthcare systems, mental health, and public well-being are increasingly affected by cyberbullying and harmful online interactions, necessitating proactive detection tools for mental health protection.

Method: CareGuard integrates zero-shot semantic labeling with fine-tuned transformer-based models (BERT, DistilBERT, RoBERTa), incorporating emotion-aware filtering and cosine similarity-based semantic screening to enable robust, context-aware classification across sensitive cyberbullying categories while reducing unnecessary computation.

Result: Experimental results on benchmark datasets demonstrate that CareGuard effectively balances detection accuracy and computational efficiency.

Conclusion: CareGuard shows potential for scalable deployment in healthcare systems, mental health monitoring, and online safety applications.

Abstract: Healthcare systems, mental health, and public well-being are increasingly affected by cyberbullying and harmful online interactions. This paper presents CareGuard, an early-warning framework designed to support healthcare-driven mental health protection and proactive online safety through the detection of cyberbullying-related content using advanced natural language processing techniques. CareGuard integrates zero-shot semantic labeling with fine-tuned transformer-based models, including BERT, DistilBERT, and RoBERTa, to enable robust and context-aware classification across sensitive cyberbullying categories. To improve efficiency and reduce unnecessary computation in healthcare-oriented monitoring settings, the framework incorporates an emotion-aware filtering mechanism alongside cosine similarity-based semantic screening, allowing the system to focus on semantically relevant and emotionally salient content. Experimental results on benchmark datasets demonstrate that CareGuard effectively balances detection accuracy and computational efficiency, highlighting its potential for scalable deployment in healthcare systems, mental health monitoring, and online safety applications.

</details>


### [128] [LexAgentHallu: A Hierarchical Benchmark for Profiling Hallucinations in Legal Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09754)
*Yujin Zhou,Mingxuan Zheng,Chuxue Cao,Huang Yidan,Jiale Chen,Yike Guo,Sirui Han*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LexAgentHallu is a benchmark for evaluating agentic hallucinations in legal AI agents, containing 3,414 instances across 17 legal categories with a dual-layer taxonomy of 7 high-level and 27 fine-grained hallucination categories. It reveals that legal agents exhibit a "Right-Answer-Wrong-Reason" effect and that hallucination patterns cluster into distinct profiles.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing legal benchmarks evaluate only single-turn QA with outcome-level metrics, while agentic hallucination benchmarks lack legal-specific diagnostic capability. Neither addresses the extent and manner of hallucination along an agent's multi-step trajectory, particularly critical in legal contexts where errors can cascade into fabricated holdings and miscited authority.

Method: Built through a four-stage expert-in-the-loop pipeline, the benchmark annotates each instance under a dual-layer hallucination taxonomy covering 7 high-level categories and 27 fine-grained subclasses (substantive errors and agent-procedural failures). Fine-grained metrics were designed to quantify failure extent and localize failures along the agent's execution path. Evaluation was conducted across 18 proprietary and open-source legal agents.

Result: The benchmark uncovers a "Right-Answer-Wrong-Reason" effect where agents may arrive at correct conclusions through incorrect reasoning. Hallucination subclasses cluster rather than scatter, forming distinct agentic framework, legal task, and category profiles - findings invisible to outcome-level evaluation.

Conclusion: LexAgentHallu provides diagnostic power for evaluating agentic hallucination in legal AI, revealing failure patterns that standard outcome-based metrics miss. The clustered nature of hallucination subclasses suggests systematic failure modes that can be addressed through targeted improvements in legal agent design.

Abstract: As large language models are increasingly deployed as tool-augmented legal agents, they introduce agentic hallucinations where tool-call and reasoning errors cascade into fabricated holdings and miscited authority. However, existing legal benchmarks evaluate only single-turn QA with outcome-level metrics, while agentic hallucination benchmarks lack legal-specific diagnostic capability. Neither answers to what extent and how a legal agent hallucinates along its trajectory. To address these limitations, we introduce LexAgentHallu, a legal agentic hallucination benchmark designed to evaluate to what extent and how legal agents fail along multi-step trajectories. Built through a four-stage expert-in-the-loop pipeline, LexAgentHallu contains 3414 instances across 17 legal categories and 6 task types. Each instance is annotated under a dual-layer hallucination taxonomy of 7 high-level categories and 27 fine-grained subclasses, covering both substantive errors and agent-procedural failures. We further design fine-grained metrics that quantify to what extent and localize how each failure occurs along an agent's execution path. Our evaluation across 18 proprietary and open-source agents uncovers a Right-Answer-Wrong-Reason effect and reveals that hallucination subclasses cluster rather than scatter, forming distinct agentic framework, legal task, and category profiles. These findings, invisible to outcome-level evaluation, validate the diagnostic power of LexAgentHallu for evaluating agentic hallucination in law.

</details>


### [129] [Procedural Memory Under Change: Reuse and Interference in Controlled Web Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09774)
*Yanze Cao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether procedural memory in language agents becomes behaviorally disruptive when stored routines become inapplicable due to interface changes. The study combines a retrospective case analysis (WebShop V1-V6 development) with controlled experiments testing four types of mismatch across 32 formal cells. Results show no interference signatures appeared when current-task evidence was explicit, identifying a "tested region of non-interference" where memory mismatch does not cause observable error.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The researchers question what happens when the core assumption of procedural memory—that stored routines remain applicable—fails. This is crucial for understanding memory-agent safety, as autonomous agents relying on procedural memory could make errors if the stored routines no longer match the current environment.

Method: 1) Retrospective human-assisted case analysis of BrowserGym TimeWarp (WebShop V1-V6), examining interface-specific code adaptation while high-level procedures remained unchanged. 2) Controlled experiments on synthetic shopping decisions comparing: no-memory vs. two memory conditions. 3) Tested 4 mismatch types: changed quantities, different evidence representation, local vs. global optimization conflict, and distributed promotion evidence. 4) Each of 32 cells used temperature-0 generation with qwen3:8b model and no adaptive retry.

Result: Early pilot (1 task): two memory conditions selected more expensive items; no-memory condition selected reference minimum. No recurring row-order or identity-binding pattern found. Across 32 formal cells: none of the predefined diagnostic interference signatures appeared when current-task evidence was explicit and sufficient. Result: procedural memory can be mismatched without becoming behaviorally disruptive under tested conditions.

Conclusion: The study identifies a "tested region of non-interference"—where applicability mismatch does not cause observable memory-caused error. However, this does not establish general safety or explain the underlying mechanism. The key remaining question is: which additional conditions (beyond explicit/sufficient current-task evidence) would turn applicability mismatch into observable, memory-caused error?

Abstract: Procedural memory lets language agents reuse successful routines, but reuse presumes that a stored routine remains applicable. We study what happens when that presumption is deliberately violated. The study combines a retrospective, human-assisted interface-adaptation case from BrowserGym TimeWarp with controlled frozen-memory comparisons on synthetic shopping decisions. During the documented WebShop V1-V6 development path, interface-specific code was adapted while the separately stored high-level procedure was not reported to change; this phase does not constitute an autonomous memory-agent evaluation. In the controlled phase, an early pilot produced one task on which two memory conditions selected a more expensive item while the no-memory condition selected the reference minimum. Follow-up probes did not establish a recurring row-order or identity-binding pattern. We then tested four forms of mismatch: changed quantities, a different evidence representation, a conflict between local and global optimization, and distributed promotion evidence, across 32 formal cells. Each cell used one temperature-0 generation with the same local qwen3:8b configuration and no adaptive retry. Across these pairs, none of the predefined diagnostic interference signatures appeared on the tasks for which they were defined when current-task evidence was explicit and sufficient. The result identifies a tested region of non-interference: a procedural memory can be mismatched without becoming behaviorally disruptive. It does not establish general safety or a mechanism. The remaining question is which additional conditions turn applicability mismatch into observable, memory-caused error.

</details>


### [130] [Proof-Carrying Cognition: Closing the Verification Gap with Reality-Settled Reward](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09776)
*Eshwar Reddy M,Sourav Karmakar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper addresses the "verification gap" in language model reasoning - the lack of scalable, incorruptible reward signals outside formal domains. The authors propose that unsound verifiers fail under optimization pressure, while reality-anchored settlement can maintain soundness. They introduce "proof-carrying cognition" as a paradigm and "Soundness-under-Pressure" as a benchmark metric.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The field's progress in language-model reasoning via reinforcement learning is constrained by the inability to verify reasoning quality outside formal domains. Current approaches rely on verifiers that become unsound under optimization pressure, creating a "hacking gap" where models exploit verifier weaknesses rather than improving true reasoning capabilities.

Method: The authors use: (1) Theoretical modeling with a joint-Gaussian model of best-of-N selection to derive exchange rates between test-time compute and verifier capability; (2) Program-synthesis experiments with executable ground truth, including pre-registered scaled replication; (3) Real LLM judges as verifiers tested under i.i.d. and adversarial pressure; (4) GRPO training experiments comparing frozen vs. refit reward models.

Result: Key findings: (1) Unsound verifiers lose Soundness-under-Pressure from 0.94 to 0.32 at N=4096 while sound verifiers improve monotonically; (2) Reality-anchored settlement eliminates the hacking gap (~0.27 to ~0); (3) Soundness scales log-linearly with settled labels, with on-policy settlement 10x more label-efficient; (4) Weak judges lose soundness under best-of-N (p<0.001), stronger judges are more robust; (5) Selection alone manufactures +0.53 hacking gaps from honest samples; (6) Frozen reward models show 90% reward collapse in overoptimization, while refitting on 10% settlement stream preserves 6x more executed reward.

Conclusion: The verification gap is the binding constraint on language-model reasoning. Proof-carrying cognition, where reasoning steps are typed probabilistic claims settled by proper scoring rules against a reality-trained world model, offers a paradigm shift. Soundness-under-Pressure should be the headline metric for reality-settled reasoning benchmarks. The key insight is that unsound verifiers deteriorate under optimization, while properly settled verifiers maintain and improve soundness.

Abstract: Frontier gains in language-model reasoning come from reinforcement learning on reasoning traces and are concentrated in domains with a cheap, sound verifier. We argue the field's binding constraint is the verification gap: no scalable, incorruptible reward for reasoning outside formal domains. We make four contributions. (1) Theory: in a joint-Gaussian model of best-of-N selection, verifier-gold correlation rho is the exact exchange rate between test-time compute and capability, and an unsound verifier pays a polynomial penalty N^(1/rho^2); a margin-free copula form predicts realized soundness of real LLM judges to 4% median error. (2) Demonstration: in program-synthesis testbeds with executable ground truth, including a pre-registered scaled replication, unsound verifiers lose Soundness-under-Pressure as optimization grows (0.94 to 0.32 at N=4096) while a sound verifier improves monotonically; reality-anchored settlement beats a frozen verifier under i.i.d. and adversarial pressure, driving the hacking gap from ~0.27 to ~0; soundness scales log-linearly with settled labels, with on-policy settlement ~10x more label-efficient than random labeling. With real LLM judges and unit-test execution as gold, a weak judge loses soundness under best-of-N (p<0.001), a stronger judge is more robust, and selection alone manufactures +0.53 hacking gaps from honest samples. Under real GRPO training, a frozen reward model traces the full overoptimization curve (executed reward collapses 90%) while the same model refit on a 10% settlement stream preserves 6x the executed reward. (3) Paradigm: proof-carrying cognition, where reasoning steps are typed probabilistic claims priced by a self-built world model trained only on held-out reality and settled by proper scoring rules. (4) Benchmark: we specify Soundness-under-Pressure as the headline metric for a reality-settled reasoning benchmark.

</details>


### [131] [UnitBoost: Managing Compound LLM Systems with a Merge Operator, Not a Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09815)
*Xing Zhang,Guanghui Wang,Yanwei Cui,Mengdie Flora Wang,Peiyang He*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: UnitBoost replaces generative meta-agents in compound LLM systems with a defined meta-level operator consisting of a task-given unit map, constrained argmax, and explicit residual mechanism. This approach achieves order invariance, unit provenance, and testable failure conditions while outperforming generative managers by 0.048-0.076 absolute task-score points on three benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Compound LLM systems solve coordination problems by adding a higher-level LLM as manager, but this concentrates three control decisions (reading worker outputs, writing final answer, allocating calls) in an opaque, order-sensitive model call. The authors question whether the manager needs to be generative at all, seeking a more transparent and controllable approach.

Method: UnitBoost uses three components: (1) A task-given unit map that turns worker outputs into slot-value proposals, (2) A constrained argmax that assembles the output, (3) An explicit residual mechanism for unfilled or unsupported slots for the next round. The operator is order-free and records unit provenance. The theoretical guarantee states that without coupling constraints, unit-wise maximization under the same admission score dominates selection of any complete candidate.

Result: On three held-out benchmarks, UnitBoost exceeds the best single candidate chosen with gold labels by 0.060-0.195 absolute task-score points and input-matched generative managers by 0.048-0.076. Replacing only the management step improves six compound-system configurations by 0.013-0.182. Residual-directed rounds raise FanOutQA cell F1 from 0.4778 to 0.5524. True residual outperforms random targets and ordinary rereading. A label-free supply signal flags exhaustion after one unproductive round. Three conditions show no gain: one indivisible unit, unavailable unit identity, and endpoint charging per emitted unit. Cross-unit coupling is quantified as repair cost.

Conclusion: The manager gives up semantic freedom and gains order invariance, unit provenance, and testable failure conditions. UnitBoost demonstrates that replacing generative meta-agents with defined operators can improve performance while providing more transparency and theoretical guarantees. The approach identifies specific conditions where residual mechanisms fail and quantifies the cost of cross-unit coupling.

Abstract: Compound LLM systems often solve a coordination problem by adding a higher-level LLM. The resulting meta-agent reads workers' outputs, writes the final answer, allocates later calls, and decides when to stop. It is expressive, but it also concentrates three control decisions in an opaque, order-sensitive model call. We ask whether the manager needs to be generative at all. UnitBoost replaces that model with a defined meta-level operator: a task-given unit map turns worker outputs into slot-value proposals, a constrained argmax assembles the output, and the slots left unfilled or unsupported become an explicit residual for the next round. The operator is order-free, records unit provenance, and gives a simple guarantee: without coupling constraints, unit-wise maximization under the same admission score dominates selection of any complete candidate. On three held-out benchmarks, it exceeds the best single candidate chosen with gold labels by 0.060-0.195 absolute task-score points and input-matched generative managers by 0.048-0.076. Replacing only the management step improves six compound-system configurations by 0.013-0.182. Residual-directed rounds raise FanOutQA cell F1 from 0.4778 to 0.5524; matched controls show that the true residual outperforms random targets and ordinary rereading, while a label-free supply signal flags exhaustion after one unproductive round. The same analysis measures three conditions in which no such gain is available (one indivisible unit, unavailable unit identity, and an endpoint that charges for every emitted unit) and quantifies cross-unit coupling as a repair cost. The manager gives up semantic freedom and gains order invariance, unit provenance, and testable failure conditions.

</details>


### [132] [The Era by Eon Benchmark: A Generated Enterprise Estate with Exact Ground Truth for Benchmarking LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09853)
*Benjamin Gruenbaum,Doron Porat,Assaf Natanzon,Roy Zavida,Chen Dinachi,Or Itzahary*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The Era by Eon Benchmark is a novel evaluation framework for LLM agents that interact with enterprise tools. It creates a complete fictional company with consistent internal databases and product simulators (Salesforce, Zendesk, Slack, Gong), enabling exact answer grading by computing expected answers from the final records. The benchmark generates 23 companies with realism scores improving from 61.8 to 97.0, and tested 9 models on 33 questions showing accuracy ranging from 42.4% to 76.8%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents for enterprise systems of record cannot be evaluated on customer production data due to privacy/security concerns, and no existing benchmark provides ground truth for evaluating these agents in enterprise contexts.

Method: The benchmark uses a seeded entity graph to supply shared company data to multiple product simulators. A question-conditioned generator creates database schemas and records from the same entity graph, ensuring consistency across all components. Expected answers are computed directly from final records for exact grading. Validation includes design/answer-key checks for databases, and realism scorecard plus adversarial detector for the entity graph.

Result: Across 23 generated companies, mean realism score improved from 61.8 to 97.0 with zero records flagged as synthetic. In the simulator-track comparison, nine models answered 33 questions three times each, with accuracy ranging from 42.4% to 76.8%. Three of 36 pairwise model differences remained statistically significant after correction.

Conclusion: The Era benchmark provides a realistic, grounded truth evaluation framework for enterprise LLM agents. The high final realism score (97.0) and zero synthetic flag rate demonstrate the approach's effectiveness in creating believable enterprise scenarios. The significant variance in model performance (42.4%-76.8% accuracy) indicates the benchmark successfully differentiates LLM agent capabilities in enterprise tool usage.

Abstract: LLM agents for enterprise systems of record cannot be evaluated on customer production data, and no existing substitute provides ground truth. We present the Era by Eon Benchmark for evaluating LLM agents that use enterprise tools. The benchmark is built around a complete fictional company. It includes product simulators, company-specific internal databases, benchmark questions, and computed answer keys. Industry, company size, business model, application portfolio, and a seed define each company. One seeded entity graph supplies shared company data to simulators of Salesforce, Zendesk, Slack, Gong, and other products. A questionconditioned generator creates the schemas and records for internal databases. It takes shared entities, keys, and values from the same graph before generating database-specific facts. Both mechanisms therefore describe one consistent enterprise estate. Every expected answer is computed from the final records, so grading is exact. Design and answer-key checks validate the internal databases. A realism scorecard and adversarial detector validate the entity graph. Across 23 generated companies, the mean realism score rose from 61.8 to 97.0, with zero records flagged as synthetic. In the reported simulator-track comparison, nine models answered the same 33 questions three times each. Accuracy estimates ranged from 42.4% to 76.8%, and three of 36 pairwise differences remained supported after correction.

</details>


### [133] [Shifting Relational Paradigms for Affective Computing: Affective Resonance, Vitality Affects, and Vocal Interaction Fields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09864)
*Cy Gorman,Yihang Yao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes shifting affective computing from an individual-state paradigm to a relational framework where the primary unit of analysis is the "interactional field" within vocal dynamics. Using self-supervised speech representations, the authors detect directional expressive coupling in multi-party conversations, finding it to be regime-specific, sub-second, and absent in speech-only controls—supporting a relational account of affective dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current affective computing follows an individual-state paradigm, extracting discrete emotion labels or arousal/valence from isolated speakers. The authors argue this framing is incomplete for interaction because it ignores the relational, dynamic nature of affective phenomena in social contexts.

Method: The study uses continuous self-supervised speech representations to detect directional expressive coupling in multi-party conversation. They employ null-calibrated directional coupling analyses across different interaction regimes.

Result: Expressive coupling is regime-specific, concentrated at sub-second timescales, and collapses under exclusive-speech negative controls. These findings are consistent with a relational account of affective dynamics.

Conclusion: The authors introduce design frameworks for "Artificial Affective Resonance Intelligence" grounded in "Affective Resonance Dynamic Ontologies," proposing a paradigm shift from individual to relational affective analysis in human-computer interaction.

Abstract: Affective computing has largely followed an individual-state paradigm, extracting discrete emotion labels or arousal/valence from isolated speakers. We argue this framing is incomplete for interaction. Drawing on affective resonance and vitality-contour accounts, we propose a relational framework in which the primary unit of affective analysis is the interactional field constituted within vocal dynamics. As a proof of concept, we present a preliminary empirical study using continuous self-supervised speech representations to detect directional expressive coupling in multi-party conversation. Coupling is regime-specific, concentrated at sub-second timescales, and collapses under exclusive-speech negative controls, consistent with a relational account of affective dynamics. We introduce design frameworks for Artificial Affective Resonance Intelligence grounded in Affective Resonance Dynamic Ontologies, supported by null-calibrated directional coupling analyses across interaction regimes.

</details>


### [134] [AgentAudit: An Open, Extensible Framework for Full-Lifecycle Trust Evaluation of AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09875)
*Shrey Nag,Sachita,Abhishek Kumar Singh,Lipi Goel,Rajeshwar Singh Janwar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AgentAudit is a comprehensive evaluation framework for LLM-based AI agents that assesses the entire execution trace across ten dimensions (instruction integrity, planner, memory, tool selection, tool invocation, tool correctness, alignment, tool faithfulness, security, and execution integrity), combined with behavioral classification and failure attribution to identify exact failure stages. It evaluates five language models, with Claude Sonnet 5 (95.1) and GPT-5 (80.6) outperforming Sarvam 105B (57.6), Llama 3.3 70B (45.7), and Gemini 2.5 Flash (22.6).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluation frameworks (AgentBench, AgentDojo, ASB) only assess individual aspects of AI agents (task completion or security robustness) rather than the complete pipeline of planning, tool selection, tool execution, memory, and reasoning. These benchmarks cannot identify the precise source of failures since failures can occur at any stage.

Method: AgentAudit attaches to the agent rather than replacing it, reads only the recorded execution trace without interfering with the agent's operation, and evaluates across ten capability, grounding, security, and behavioral dimensions. It uses behavioral classification and failure attribution to pinpoint the exact stage responsible for observed failures. A single fixed judge model (one of the evaluated models) scored all traces.

Result: Claude Sonnet 5 achieved the highest mean Composite Trust Score (95.1/100), followed by GPT-5 (80.6), Sarvam 105B (57.6), Llama 3.3 70B (45.7), and Gemini 2.5 Flash (22.6). Key finding: models with similar task-completion behavior can diverge sharply in trustworthiness, as non-frontier models are repeatedly classified as Unsafe_Compliance on adversarial tasks rather than merely failing—a distinction pass/fail benchmarks cannot surface.

Conclusion: AgentAudit provides a comprehensive evaluation framework that can assess any LLM-based AI agent without constraining its internal implementation. The framework reveals that trustworthyness varies significantly among models with similar performance, and that behavioral classification (Safe vs Unsafe_Compliance) is crucial for identifying malicious compliance rather than simple failure. Limitations include using a single judge model for all evaluations.

Abstract: Existing evaluation frameworks mostly assess only one part of AI agents, such as task completion (AgentBench) or security robustness (AgentDojo, ASB), rather than the complete pipeline of planning, tool selection, tool execution, memory and reasoning. Failures can occur at any stage, yet existing benchmarks rarely identify their precise source. AgentAudit evaluates the entire execution trace across ten capability, grounding, security and behavioural dimensions, namely instruction integrity, planner, memory, tool selection, tool invocation, tool correctness, alignment, tool faithfulness, security and execution integrity, combined with behavioural classification and failure attribution to pinpoint the exact stage responsible for an observed failure. AgentAudit can evaluate any LLM-based AI agent, since it attaches to the agent instead of replacing it. It reads only the recorded execution trace and does not interfere with how the agent runs, so it places no constraint on the agent's internal implementation. We evaluate five language models (OpenAI GPT-5, Claude Sonnet 5, Sarvam 105B, Llama 3.3 70B and Gemini 2.5 Flash) across nine capability and adversarial tasks. Claude Sonnet 5 and GPT-5 obtain the highest mean Composite Trust Scores (95.1 and 80.6 out of 100, respectively), while Sarvam 105B, Llama 3.3 70B and Gemini 2.5 Flash trail substantially (57.6, 45.7 and 22.6). All traces were scored by a single fixed judge model, which was itself one of the evaluated models, a limitation discussed in Section VII.E. More importantly, models with similar task-completion behaviour can diverge sharply in trustworthiness, as several non-frontier models are repeatedly classified Unsafe_Compliance on adversarial tasks rather than merely failing them, a distinction that pass/fail benchmarks cannot surface.

</details>


### [135] [Scored vs. Generated Readouts in Behavioral Language Models: An Empirical Study of Elicitation Format](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09882)
*Touchapon Kraisingkorn,Krittin Pachtrachai,Wachiravit Modecrua*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper compares two readout methods from language models fine-tuned on customer behavior: scoring answer tokens versus predictions generated after a written rationale. The scored readout significantly outperforms the rationale-based readout in 12 out of 13 experimental cells, with AUC improvements of 1.5 to 14.5 points.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Language models fine-tuned on customer behavior can both predict outcomes and generate explanations, but these readouts are often treated as interchangeable. This paper investigates whether these two methods provide equivalent predictive power.

Method: The authors compared probability readouts under fixed model checkpoint and prompt conditions across 13 model-domain cells covering four retail tasks in three markets (including two cells using fully public data and checkpoints). They analyzed approximately 9,000 generated rationales and used paired bootstrap confidence intervals with two-sided sign tests for statistical validation.

Result: The scored readout ranks outcomes more accurately in 12 of 13 cells (p ≈ 0.003), with AUC improvements ranging from 1.5 to 14.5 points. The gap varies widely (-2.2 to +13.7 points) depending on task-specific supervision and training-serving format mismatch. Two correlates were identified: reduced reliance on the dominant predictive feature and convergence on stock formulations. A third readout method (eliciting probability before any verdict) improves calibration (Brier score from 0.47 to 0.15) but only works well when outcome rates are represented in training data.

Conclusion: The differences between readout methods can be interpreted through the objectives each method matches. The authors identify training choices that narrow the gap and propose retaining generated rationales for interpretation while sourcing ranking predictions from the scored head for better performance.

Abstract: Language models fine-tuned on customer behavior can predict outcomes and generate explanations, but these readouts are often treated as interchangeable. Holding model checkpoint and prompt content fixed, we compare probabilities obtained by scoring answer tokens with predictions generated after a written rationale. Across 13 model-domain cells covering four retail tasks in three markets, including two using fully public data and checkpoints, the scored readout ranks outcomes more accurately in 12 of 13 cells (two-sided sign test, p approximately 0.003), by 1.5 to 14.5 points in area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUC). Paired bootstrap confidence intervals exclude zero in every newly measured cell. The gap varies with task-specific supervision and mismatch between training and serving formats, ranging from -2.2 points for an untuned base model to +13.7 for rationale-format supervision. Analysis of approximately 9,000 rationales identifies two correlates: reduced reliance on the dominant predictive feature and convergence on stock formulations. Probability saturation does not track the gap. A third readout, eliciting a probability before any verdict, improves calibration (Brier score from 0.47 to 0.15) while ranking within noise of scoring, but only for outcome rates represented in training; it is worse than scoring when the scored head is already calibrated. We interpret these differences through the objectives matched by each readout, identify training choices that narrow the gap, and propose retaining generated rationales while sourcing ranking from the scored head.

</details>


### [136] [Decision Transformer for UAV-Mounted RIS-Assisted Dynamic D2D Communications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09885)
*Yaxuan Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates UAV-mounted reconfigurable intelligent surface (RIS)-assisted device-to-device (D2D) communication with stochastic link activation, employing deep reinforcement learning and Decision Transformer for joint optimization of UAV trajectory, attitude, and RIS phases to maximize average sum rate.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to enhance D2D communication performance by leveraging UAV-mounted RIS with joint trajectory, attitude, and phase optimization, while addressing challenges from mobility, energy, hardware constraints, and cross-scenario generalization.

Method: The paper models UAV motion and attitude, time-varying Rician angles, and angle-dependent RIS reflection. It formulates a joint optimization problem and solves it using deep reinforcement learning (DRL) and a Decision Transformer trained on expert trajectories from multiple scenarios.

Result: The results demonstrate effective cross-scenario generalization, with zero-shot transfer outperforming direct DRL transfer, and online fine-tuning achieving competitive performance with fewer interactions.

Conclusion: The Decision Transformer approach with expert trajectory training enables superior cross-scenario generalization compared to traditional DRL methods, offering a promising solution for UAV-RIS-assisted D2D communication systems.

Abstract: This paper studies unmanned aerial vehicle (UAV)-mouted reconfigurable intelligent surface (RIS)-assisted device-to-device (D2D) communication with stochastic link activation. It models UAV motion and attitude, time-varying Rician angles, and angle-dependent RIS reflection. A joint optimization of UAV trajectory, attitude, and RIS phases is formulated to maximize average sum rate under mobility, energy, and hardware constraints. The problem is addressed using deep reinforcement learning and a Decision Transformer trained on expert trajectories from multiple scenarios. Results demonstrate effective cross-scenario generalization, with zero-shot transfer outperforming direct DRL transfer and online fine-tuning achieving competitive performance with fewer interactions.

</details>


### [137] [Grounded Evaluation and Repair for NL-to-PDDL Problem Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09898)
*Joana Rosa,Pedro Santos,Valdemar Oliveira,Romão Silva,L. Miguel Silveira,Bruno Martins*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper evaluates LLMs for translating Natural Language planning descriptions into PDDL problem instances, revealing that standard evaluation metrics (syntactic validity, planner success) overestimate task fidelity. The authors propose an end-to-end pipeline with iterative repair and demonstrate substantial divergence between operational success and benchmark-reference reconstruction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard evaluation criteria (syntactic validity, planner success) substantially overestimate the faithfulness of LLM-generated PDDL problems to the intended task. A generated problem may be parseable and solvable while misrepresenting the initial state, goal, object structure, or optimization target.

Method: The paper proposes an end-to-end NL-to-PDDL pipeline combining: (1) LLM generation, (2) checks for PDDL parsing, planning, and validation, (3) a domain-conformance checker, (4) an LLM critic, and (5) iterative repair using fine-grained feedback from domain descriptions, generated problems, NL descriptions, and operational diagnostics. Reference-based comparisons against curated benchmarks include renaming-invariant structural matching and semantic equivalence checks.

Result: Across Planetarium, AutoPlanBench, and curated PDDL~2.1 problems, operational success and benchmark-reference reconstruction can diverge substantially. Structured repair is useful, and PDDL~2.1 remains challenging for reference reconstruction even when operational success improves.

Conclusion: Standard metrics overestimate PDDL generation quality. The proposed pipeline with iterative repair helps, but significant challenges remain, especially for PDDL~2.1 reference reconstruction. The field needs more rigorous evaluation beyond syntactic validity and planner success.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown promise for translating Natural Language (NL) planning descriptions into PDDL problem instances. However, standard evaluation criteria such as syntactic validity or planner success can substantially overestimate faithfulness to the described task: a generated problem may be parseable and solvable while misrepresenting the intended initial state, goal, object structure, or optimization target. This paper studies an end-to-end NL-to-PDDL pipeline that combines LLM generation, checks in terms of PDDL parsing, planning and validation, a domain-conformance checker, an LLM critic, and iterative repair. Fine-grained repair feedback is constructed from the domain description, the generated problem, the natural language problem description, and operational diagnostics. Reference-based comparisons against curated benchmark PDDL problem descriptions are used for post-hoc benchmark analysis, and these offline checks include renaming-invariant structural matching and semantic equivalence, where domain support is available. Across Planetarium, AutoPlanBench, and curated PDDL~2.1 problems, results show that operational success and benchmark-reference reconstruction can diverge substantially. Results also show that structured repair can be useful, and that PDDL~2.1 remains challenging for reference reconstruction, even when operational success improves.

</details>


### [138] [Time-Frequency Geometric Cross-Attention for Chunked Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09925)
*Shengye Dong,Haochen Niu,Hao Liu,Peiwen Lin,Chuang Wang,Shanmin Pang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces Time-Frequency Geometric Cross-Attention (TFGCA), a drop-in module for Vision-Language-Action (VLA) policies that addresses two limitations in current action chunk prediction: frequency decomposition (smooth trend vs. corrective motion) and cross-phase geometry (near-orthogonal motion directions across phases like reach, contact, grasp adjustment).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current VLA policies treat action chunks as sequences of generic per-timestep hidden tokens, which fails to capture: (1) multi-scale frequency structures superimposing smooth global trends with fine corrective motions, and (2) cross-phase geometry where motions of different phases (reach, contact, grasp adjustment, settling) unfold along near-orthogonal directions in representation space. Dot-product attention is least sensitive near orthogonality, failing to capture these relationships.

Method: TFGCA uses a per-dimension learnable stationary wavelet transform to decompose action chunks into time-frequency tokens. Each time token retrieves information via cross-attention that fuses dot product (similarity) with wedge-product magnitude (sensitive to near-orthogonality) through a learnable weight. A zero-initialized residual reproduces base behavior at initialization, enabling drop-in integration onto pretrained VLA models for joint fine-tuning.

Result: TFGCA improves performance across multiple benchmarks: in-distribution LIBERO (+1.5 average), OOD LIBERO-Plus (+6.3), randomized average under RoboTwin domain randomization (+28.5), and real-robot AgiBot A2 tasks (+11.67 points overall success rate). Larger gains are observed out of distribution.

Conclusion: TFGCA successfully addresses both frequency and cross-phase geometric blind spots in VLA policy action chunking through time-frequency decomposition and geometric-aware cross-attention. The drop-in design allows seamless integration with pretrained models, with particularly strong OOD generalization benefits.

Abstract: Modern vision-language-action (VLA) policies predict a whole chunk of actions: one to two seconds of coordinated motion emitted in a single forward pass. Yet an action chunk is essentially a short multivariate trajectory, but inside these models it is a sequence of generic per-timestep hidden tokens decoded by a linear head. This under-serves two motion structures. First, frequency: a chunk superimposes a smooth global trend and fine corrective motion across time scales, and a single token entangles them. Second, cross-phase geometry: motions of different phases (reach, contact, grasp adjustment, settling) unfold along very different, near-orthogonal directions in representation space, yet are tightly related for the task and arise across the time axis. Dot-product attention scores alignment by an inner product, so it favors aligned tokens and is least sensitive near orthogonality, leaving such relationships for the network to recover through a detour.
  We introduce Time-Frequency Geometric Cross-Attention (TFGCA), a drop-in module repairing both blind spots. TFGCA uses a per-dimension learnable stationary wavelet transform to decompose the action chunk into time-frequency tokens, and each time token retrieves information from them via a cross-attention that fuses the dot product (similarity) with the wedge-product magnitude (sensitive to near-orthogonality) through a learnable weight. A zero-initialized residual reproduces the base behavior at initialization, so it can be dropped onto a pretrained VLA and fine-tuned jointly. Relative to the same-source base, TFGCA improves in-distribution LIBERO by +1.5 on average, the OOD LIBERO-Plus by +6.3, the randomized average under RoboTwin domain randomization by +28.5, and the overall success rate on three real-robot AgiBot A2 tasks by +11.67 points, with larger gains out of distribution.

</details>


### [139] [Structural Process Supervision for Latent Chain-of-Thought Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09928)
*Yiqi Li,Xu Chen,Chen Ju,Jiangchao Yao,Zhaoyang Li,Jinsong Lan,Xiaoyong Zhu,Bo Zheng,Yu Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PMPS introduces learnable reasoning prototypes as semantic anchors to provide process-level supervision for latent reasoning, addressing representation collapse and uneven information distribution issues in existing methods. It projects latent and explicit CoT embeddings into a shared prototype space for alignment, and uses a Progressive Sequential Alignment module to gradually relax positional constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing latent reasoning approaches lack direct process supervision over compact continuous-space embeddings, leading to representation collapse and uneven information distribution. The need is for structural process-level supervision that can align latent embeddings with explicit chain-of-thought representations.
