Method: Prototype-Mediated Process Supervision (PMPS) with two key components: (1) Learnable reasoning prototypes as semantic anchors that project both latent and explicit CoT embeddings into a shared prototype space, achieving many-to-many soft alignment between unequal-length representations through prototype assignment; (2) Progressive Sequential Alignment (PSA) module that uses positional priors initially to encourage sequential alignment structure, then gradually relaxes to permit adaptive matching.

Result: PMPS compresses output token length to under 50% of explicit CoT on GSM8K-Aug. Achieves 2.08% average accuracy gains over SIM-CoT across different model families. On GPT-2, PMPS surpasses CoT-SFT. On larger models and challenging tasks, PMPS consistently achieves highest accuracy among latent reasoning methods with comparable output length.

Conclusion: PMPS successfully addresses the lack of process supervision in latent reasoning by using prototype-based alignment and progressive sequential alignment. The method achieves superior accuracy while significantly compressing output token length, demonstrating effectiveness across different model scales and task difficulties.

Abstract: Latent reasoning approaches enhance token-level efficiency and robustness by replacing verbose, explicit chain-of-thought (CoT) tokens with compact continuous-space embeddings. However, existing methods lack direct process supervision over these latent embeddings, which often leads to representation collapse and uneven information distribution. To address this, we propose Prototype-Mediated Process Supervision (PMPS), which introduces learnable reasoning prototypes as semantic anchors to provide structural process-level supervision for latent reasoning. PMPS projects latent embeddings and explicit CoT embeddings into a shared prototype space, achieving many-to-many soft alignment between unequal-length representations through prototype assignment. Meanwhile, we introduce a Progressive Sequential Alignment (PSA) module to further guide training: positional priors initially encourage sequential alignment structure, then gradually relax to permit adaptive matching. Experimental results show that PMPS compresses output token length to under 50% of explicit CoT on GSM8K-Aug. Compared to leading baseline SIM-CoT, our method achieves average accuracy gains of 2.08% across different model families. On GPT-2, PMPS even surpasses CoT-SFT. On larger models and a more challenging task, PMPS consistently attains the highest accuracy among all latent reasoning methods with comparable output length.

</details>


### [140] [Belief-State Engine: Augmenting LLMs for Principled Planning Under Partial Observability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10036)
*Arnab Chattopadhayay,Debdipta Halder*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes Belief-State Engine (BSE), an architectural fix for LLM agents operating in partially observable environments. BSE maintains a Bayesian posterior over latent POMDP states and only exposes this posterior to the LLM, preventing raw history from causing erroneous belief collapse.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents fail in partially observable environments due to: (1) ambiguous feedback causing premature commitment, (2) single informative observations collapsing uncertainty onto wrong hypotheses, and (3) policy drift as history grows. These failures stem from LLM agents lacking explicit belief states over hidden variables.

Method: The BSE is an inference module placed outside the LLM that maintains a Bayesian posterior over latent POMDP states. The authors propose a four-axiom specification for belief-consistent internal states and prove that LLM + BSE forms a sound Markov policy on the belief MDP. The key constraint is that raw action-observation logs are never shown to the LLM.

Result: BSE-augmented agents improved task return, belief calibration, and decision consistency across both the Tiger POMDP and red-team attack-graph tasks, compared to six baselines: reactive LLM, Chain-of-Thought, ReAct, natural-language belief tracker, QMDP, and POMCP. Ten ablations confirmed the effect is not model-specific.

Conclusion: The BSE architecture enables LLM agents to inherit Bellman optimality guarantees from classical POMDP theory by ensuring the LLM is never exposed to raw history. This provides a principled solution for deploying LLMs in partially observable real-world tasks.

Abstract: Large language model agents produce fluent action sequences across a wide range of tasks, yet they fail in characteristic ways once the environment becomes partially observable. Ambiguous feedback pushes them into premature commitments. A single informative observation can collapse their uncertainty onto the wrong hypothesis. Policies drift as the history grows. We trace these symptoms to a common structural cause. An LLM agent, as commonly deployed, is a history-conditioned policy with no explicit belief over hidden state.
  We propose an architectural fix. The Belief-State Engine (BSE) is an inference module placed outside the LLM. It maintains a Bayesian posterior over the latent states of a given POMDP (Partially Observable Markov Decision Process) model, and at each decision step it exposes only that posterior to the LLM. The raw action-observation log is not shown. We set out a minimal four-axiom specification of what a belief-consistent internal state must satisfy, and prove that the LLM paired with the BSE is a sound Markov policy on the belief MDP induced by the underlying POMDP. It therefore inherits the Bellman optimality guarantees of classical POMDP theory, provided the LLM is never exposed to the raw history.
  We evaluate the architecture on the Tiger POMDP and a red-team attack-graph task, against six baselines: a reactive LLM, Chain-of-Thought, ReAct, a natural-language belief tracker, QMDP, and POMCP. Across both domains, the BSE-augmented agent improves task return, belief calibration, and decision consistency. Ten targeted ablations isolate the contribution of each architectural choice confirms that the effect is not specific to any one model. Code, environment specifications, prompt templates, and seed logs accompany this paper.

</details>


### [141] [OntologyAligner: Ontology-Aligned Retrieval and Hierarchy-Guided Large Language Model Reranking for Biomedical Ontology Normalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10055)
*Jie Song,Zhichuan Xu,Ziyu Lu,Meng Xiao,Cheng Bi,Yuxin Zhang,Xin Zheng,Xiaoran Li,Qiongfang Cao,Hao Yang,Bairong Shen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents OntologyAligner, a three-stage framework for biomedical ontology normalization that combines retrieval, LLM reranking, and hierarchy-guided refinement. It also introduces PhenoNormBench, a benchmark with 13,390 samples from seven HPO datasets. The method achieves state-of-the-art performance with 88.78% Macro Top-1 Accuracy and 86.75% Micro Top-1 Accuracy on HPO normalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Biomedical ontology normalization is challenging due to lexical variation and subtle distinctions among hierarchically related concepts. Existing methods struggle with concept boundary ambiguity. The motivation is to develop a more accurate framework for mapping free-text biomedical expressions to standardized ontology concepts.

Method: The proposed OntologyAligner framework consists of three stages: (1) ontology-aligned retrieval, (2) large language model candidate reranking, and (3) selective hierarchy-guided refinement. Additionally, the authors construct PhenoNormBench, a unified benchmark comprising 13,390 samples from seven Human Phenotype Ontology datasets.

Result: OntologyAligner achieved state-of-the-art performance on HPO normalization with 88.78% Macro Top-1 Accuracy and 86.75% Micro Top-1 Accuracy, exceeding the strongest baseline by 4.85 and 5.07 percentage points respectively. Ablation analyses confirmed complementary contributions from all three stages. The method also demonstrated portability to other ontologies (MONDO, MEDIC, NCBITaxon) and showed stability across candidate-set sizes and model backbones.

Conclusion: OntologyAligner offers a generalizable framework for accurate mapping of biomedical text to structured ontology concepts. The three-stage approach effectively addresses the challenges of lexical variation and hierarchical concept distinctions. PhenoNormBench provides a standardized benchmark for evaluating ontology normalization methods.

Abstract: Biomedical ontology normalization maps free-text expressions to standardized concepts, enabling consistent integration and analysis of biomedical data. This task remains challenging because lexical variation and subtle distinctions among hierarchically related concepts can obscure concept boundaries. We present OntologyAligner, a three-stage framework that combines ontology-aligned retrieval, large language model candidate reranking, and selective hierarchy-guided refinement. We also construct PhenoNormBench, a unified benchmark comprising 13,390 samples from seven Human Phenotype Ontology datasets. OntologyAligner achieved state-of-the-art performance on HPO normalization, with 88.78% Macro Top-1 Accuracy and 86.75% Micro Top-1 Accuracy, exceeding the strongest baseline by 4.85 and 5.07 percentage points, respectively. Ablation analyses showed complementary contributions from all three stages, and sensitivity analyses demonstrated stability across candidate-set sizes and model backbones. Applications to MONDO, MEDIC, and NCBITaxon further established portability to other ontologies. OntologyAligner offers a generalizable framework for accurate mapping of biomedical text to structured ontology concepts. PhenoNormBench and the code are publicly available at https://github.com/zhelishisongjie/OntologyAligner.

</details>


### [142] [Reference-Based Bias Detection in LLMs via Relative Representations of Hidden States](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10060)
*Marek Jeliński,Jan Dubiński,Maciej Chrabaszcz,Sebastian Cygert*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes ΔB (Representational Bias Shift), a reference-based method that audits bias in hidden-state representations by encoding sentences as similarities to anchor sentences, measuring how target groups shift in association with positive/negative attributes across model variants.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing bias auditing methods rely on model outputs, requiring costly benchmarks or judge models and potentially missing internal shifts that never appear in generated text. A more efficient and comprehensive approach is needed.

Method: The method encodes each sentence by its similarities to a fixed set of anchor sentences, yielding relative representations in a shared comparison space. It then measures how target groups shift in their association with positive and negative attributes, quantifying this as the Representational Bias Shift (ΔB).

Result: Across three model families and three benchmarks (WildGuardMix, DecodingTrust, ToxiGen), ΔB correlates with output-level bias change in 15 of 18 settings, reaching |r| = 0.84 (p < 0.001) under full fine-tuning. Thresholding ΔB detects bias-increased checkpoints with ROC AUC between 0.65 and 0.99, outperforming a SEAT-based baseline. The method is stable under changes to anchor sets, attribute sets, and target templates, requires no task-specific evaluation data, and audits a model in about three minutes using 3-50x less compute than output-level benchmarks.

Conclusion: ΔB provides a complementary approach to output-based bias auditing, offering efficient detection of internal representation shifts with strong correlation to output-level bias changes, particularly effective under full fine-tuning conditions.

Abstract: Existing bias auditing methods typically rely on model outputs, requiring costly benchmarks or judge models and potentially missing internal shifts that never appear in generated text. We propose a reference-based method that audits bias in hidden-state representations across related model variants, for example before and after fine-tuning. Because fine-tuning reshapes representation geometry, absolute hidden states are not directly comparable, so we encode each sentence by its similarities to a fixed set of anchor sentences, yielding relative representations in a shared comparison space. There we measure how target groups shift in their association with positive and negative attributes, a quantity we call the Representational Bias Shift $ΔB$. Across three model families and the WildGuardMix, DecodingTrust and ToxiGen benchmarks, $ΔB$ correlates with output-level bias change in 15 of the 18 settings we test, reaching $|r| = 0.84$ ($p < 0.001$) under full fine-tuning and becoming more model-dependent under parameter-efficient adaptation. Thresholding $ΔB$ detects checkpoints whose bias increased with ROC AUC between $0.65$ and $0.99$, and on WildGuardMix and DecodingTrust it separates them better than a SEAT-based baseline for all three families. $ΔB$ is also stable under changes to the anchor set, attribute sets and target templates. Our method requires no task-specific evaluation data and audits a model in about three minutes, using $3$-$50\times$ less compute than the output-level benchmarks considered here. We view it as complementary to output-based auditing rather than a replacement for it.

</details>


### [143] [RAP: Research Attention Prediction Reveals Target-Conditioned Evidence Acquisition Biases](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10092)
*Yingqian Wu,Jingcong Liang,Siyuan Wang,Zhenfei Yin,Philip Torr,Junchi Yu,Zhongyu Wei*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces Research Attention Prediction (RAP), a benchmark for evaluating LLMs' ability to predict research trends in AI/ML fields. The study finds that while search functionality helps, all tested LLM models underperform a simple EWMA baseline, with two key bottlenecks identified: State carry-forward methods outperform direct Forecast approaches, and even with exact historical data, future-specific prediction remains challenging.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to evaluate LLMs' ability to track and predict shifts in research attention, which is difficult because research reviews and ideas lack uniquely verifiable outcomes. The authors create a systematic benchmark to assess this capability.

Method: The authors created RAP, a rolling benchmark covering 278 AI/ML fields and 1,390 episodes. At each cut-off, LLM agents search a temporally restricted arXiv corpus and predict the next six months' paper shares across eight research directions. They tested four diagnostic models and compared against an exponentially weighted moving average (EWMA) baseline.

Result: Search generally helps but all four diagnostic models perform worse than the exact-count EWMA baseline. State carry-forward outperforms direct Forecast for all models. Frozen-evidence replay links this to Forecast-oriented policies retrieving less recent evidence. Only GPT-5.5 with reopened Search slightly surpasses EWMA. Fine-tuning Qwen3-4B improved forecast Spearman correlation by 0.105 on held-out fields.

Conclusion: The study identifies two linked bottlenecks in LLM-based research prediction: (1) cumulative-history access favors carry-forward approaches over direct forecasting, and (2) Forecast-oriented policies retrieve insufficient recent evidence. Even with exact historical data, future-specific updating remains limited, suggesting current LLMs struggle with true predictive reasoning about research trends.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) increasingly act as research agents, yet their ability to track shifts in research attention is difficult to evaluate because reviews and research ideas lack uniquely verifiable outcomes. We introduce Research Attention Prediction (RAP), a rolling benchmark covering 278 AI/ML fields and 1,390 episodes. At each cut-off, an LLM agent searches a temporally restricted arXiv corpus and predicts the next six months' paper shares across eight frozen research directions. Search generally helps, but all four diagnostic models perform worse than an exact-count exponentially weighted moving average (EWMA) baseline in compositional accuracy. We identify two linked bottlenecks. Under cumulative-history access, State carry-forward outperforms direct Forecast for all four diagnostic models; frozen-evidence replay links a shared component of this reversal to Forecast-oriented policies retrieving a smaller share of recent evidence. Even with exact historical activity, future-specific updating remains limited, with only GPT-5.5 plus reopened Search slightly surpassing EWMA. Fine-tuning on realised outcomes improves Qwen3-4B's forecast Spearman correlation by 0.105 on held-out fields at later origins, with gains also on change-rich episodes.

</details>


### [144] [Agent-Based ML-LLM Fusion with Self-Optimizing Prompts for Plateau Weather Alerts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10135)
*Shuai Yan,Yang Xu,Shan He*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SmartWeatherAgent is a unified three-stage architecture for tourism meteorological services that integrates intent recognition, hazard prediction, and reasoning-enhanced generation. It combines rule-based methods with large language models and uses a LightGBM model with highland-specific features to achieve an F1-Macro score of 0.605 with 1.60 ms latency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address three critical limitations in tourism meteorological services: insufficient contextualization, weak generalization, and poor scenario adaptation.

Method: The system employs a three-stage architecture integrating: (1) rule-based methods fused with large language models for multi-granularity query parsing; (2) a LightGBM model enriched with highland-specific features (e.g., wind speed abruptness rate); (3) a 12-round micro-step prompt self-optimization loop for composite warning quality improvement.

Result: The LightGBM model achieved an F1-Macro score of 0.605 with 1.60 ms latency on high-wind, precipitation, and low-temperature events. The prompt optimization loop boosted the composite warning quality score S_final from 4.2 (B01) to 8.9 (B12, +112%). B08 improved from 6.5 to 8.5 via data source citation, B10 maintained high performance through physical mechanism explanation, and B12 achieved a peak scientific rigor score of 9.2 through explicit uncertainty statements.

Conclusion: SmartWeatherAgent autonomously generates structured warnings integrating causal mechanisms, spatiotemporal evolution, quantitative evidence, regulatory references, and confidence statements. The system enhances professional depth, logical rigor, and scientific soundness, advancing meteorological services toward proactive perception, explainable decision-making, and intelligent agency.

Abstract: To address insufficient contextualization, weak generalization, and poor scenario adaptation in tourism meteorological services, we propose SmartWeatherAgent--a unified three-stage architecture integrating intent recognition, hazard prediction, and reasoning-enhanced generation. The system fuses rule-based methods with large language models to parse queries at multiple granularities and employs a LightGBM model enriched with highland-specific features (e.g., wind speed abruptness rate), achieving an F1-Macro score of 0.605 with 1.60 ms latency on high-wind, precipitation, and low-temperature events. A 12-round micro-step prompt self-optimization loop boosts the composite warning quality score S_final from 4.2 (B01) to 8.9 (B12, +112%). Key improvements include a sharp rise in B08 from data source citation (6.5 -> 8.5), sustained high performance in B10 via physical mechanism explanation, and a peak scientific rigor score of 9.2 in B12 through explicit uncertainty statements. The system autonomously generates structured warnings that integrate causal mechanisms, spatiotemporal evolution, quantitative evidence, regulatory references, and confidence statements--enhancing professional depth, logical rigor, and scientific soundness, and advancing meteorological services toward proactive perception, explainable decision-making, and intelligent agency.

</details>


### [145] [Kernel-Managed Shared Memory for System-Wide Personalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10144)
*Ryan Lum,Yongfeng Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes kernel-managed shared memory, a system-level abstraction where the agent-system kernel (not individual agents) governs memory retrieval, privacy enforcement, and prompt injection in multi-agent AI systems. This approach significantly outperforms unmanaged memory (Mem0), standard retrieval-augmented generation, and nearly matches full context concatenation while reducing latency by 15-61%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The core problem is that in multi-agent systems, useful context learned by one agent often remains unavailable to others. Current approaches either leave memory management to individual agents (leading to inconsistent retrieval and privacy issues) or use unfiltered context concatenation (which is costly and inefficient).

Method: The authors implemented kernel-managed shared memory on AIOS and evaluated it across three assistant models (GPT-4o, Llama-3.1:8B, Qwen-2.5:7B) using 1,800 total trials. They compared against three alternatives: (1) Mem0 (unmanaged external memory), (2) standard retrieval-augmented injection, and (3) full unfiltered context concatenation.

Result: Kernel-managed retrieval improved personalization scores by 2.4-4.0 points on a 5-point scale (e.g., 1.05 to 4.69 profile usage on GPT-4o), with p < 10^-18. Against full context, it matched performance on 2/3 models with 15-61% lower latency and reduced token usage/inference costs.

Conclusion: Centralizing memory management in the agent-system kernel delivers most personalization benefits of unconstrained context at a fraction of its cost, solving the multi-agent memory sharing problem while maintaining privacy and efficiency.

Abstract: AI systems become more useful when they can adapt to the people using them, but in multi-agent systems, useful context learned by one agent often remains unavailable to others. We present kernel-managed shared memory, a system-level abstraction in which specialized agents write structured, tagged memories while the agent-system kernel, not individual agents, governs retrieval, privacy enforcement, and prompt injection. We implement and evaluate this design on AIOS and compare it against three alternatives across three assistant models (GPT-4o, Llama-3.1:8B, Qwen-2.5:7B) and 1,800 total trials. Against an unmanaged external memory backend (Mem0) using identical underlying storage, kernel-managed retrieval and injection improve personalization scores by 2.4-4.0 points on a 5-point scale (e.g., 1.05 to 4.69 profile usage on GPT-4o), with every comparison significant at p < 10^-18. Against standard retrieval-augmented injection, gains are similarly large and consistent across all three models. Against full, unfiltered context concatenation, a soft ceiling on available context rather than on response quality, kernel-managed injection statistically matches performance on two of three models and shows a small, model-specific deficit on the third, while using substantially shorter prompts: end-to-end latency is 15-61% lower across all three models, with corresponding reductions in per-call token usage and inference cost. These results indicate that centralizing memory management in the agent-system kernel, rather than leaving retrieval and privacy enforcement to individual agents, delivers most of the personalization benefit of unconstrained context at a fraction of its cost.

</details>


### [146] [Beyond Surface Imitation: Contrastive Modeling for Reasoning Path Alignment in Multimodal In-Context Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10177)
*Mingbo Yang,Wenqiang Wang,Zhaolu Kang,Peng Chen,Yannan Chen,Sunshang Wang,Yan Xiao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a new multimodal in-context learning (ICL) framework that combines contrastive demonstration modeling with self-refinement capabilities in multimodal large language models (MLLMs). The approach contrasts suboptimal responses with better responses, includes reasoning paths, uses response-conditioned retrieval, and employs an alignment controller to determine when refinement is needed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multimodal ICL methods rely on surface-level imitation of demonstrations, which prevents MLLMs from aligning their responses with the reasoning path required by multimodal inputs. This limitation is particularly pronounced in complex multimodal tasks, restricting further performance improvements.

Method: The framework has three main components: (1) Contrastive demonstration modeling - reformulates demonstrations by explicitly contrasting suboptimal responses with better responses under the same input, along with reasoning paths revealing how to refine; (2) Response-conditioned retrieval mechanism - selects demonstrations whose reasoning paths are more relevant to the current response; (3) Lightweight alignment controller - predicts response quality and determines whether further refinement is needed.

Result: Experiments on three types of multimodal tasks show consistent improvements in MLLM performance, with particularly notable gains on visual question answering (VQA).

Conclusion: The proposed contrastive multimodal ICL framework effectively addresses the limitation of surface-level imitation in existing methods by making reasoning paths more explicit and enabling self-refinement. The response-conditioned retrieval and alignment controller further enhance the framework's effectiveness, demonstrating particular promise for VQA tasks.

Abstract: In-context learning (ICL) is widely used in multimodal large language models (MLLMs) and achieves strong performance across a wide range of multimodal tasks. However, existing multimodal ICL methods often rely on surface level imitation of in-context demonstrations, making it difficult for MLLMs to align their responses with the reasoning path required by the given multimodal input. This limitation becomes more pronounced in complex multimodal tasks, thereby restricting further improvements in MLLM performance. To address this issue, we propose a new multimodal ICL framework that combines contrastive demonstration modeling with the self-refinement capability of MLLMs. Specifically, our framework reformulates each demonstration by explicitly contrasting a suboptimal response with a better response under the same input, together with a reasoning path that reveals how the response should be refined. This contrastive formulation makes the reasoning path toward the desired response more explicit and guides the MLLM beyond superficial imitation. Furthermore, because effective refinement depends on the current response, we introduce a response-conditioned retrieval mechanism to select demonstrations whose reasoning paths are more relevant to the current response. In addition, we use a lightweight alignment controller to predict response quality and determine whether further refinement is needed. Experiments on three types of multimodal tasks show that the proposed framework consistently improves MLLM performance, with particularly notable gains on visual question answering (VQA).

</details>


### [147] [Why Sample What You Can Enumerate? Exact Policy Optimization for Genomic Tool Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10221)
*Haoyue Liu,Xiaoyu Ma,Ye Chen,Zhichao Wang,Xiaoying Tang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper identifies a structural mismatch in applying GRPO (Group Relative Policy Optimization) to specialist scientific settings where tool subsets are enumerable. As training progresses and the policy concentrates on preferred subsets, sampled rewards collide causing the group-normalized advantage to vanish. The authors propose FGPO (Full-Group Policy Optimization) which computes exact action expectations over all tool subsets and precomputes rewards into an exhaustive table, eliminating frozen-reasoner calls from training.FGPO outperforms GRPO across all 15 experimental settings with an average improvement of 6.75 points.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The standard recipe of reinforcement learning over a frozen reasoner (like GRPO) becomes problematic in specialist scientific settings where the complete tool-subset space is enumerable. The sampling-based approach degrades as training succeeds because the policy concentrates on preferred subsets, leading to reward collisions and vanishing advantage estimates. This was demonstrated empirically on genomic reasoning where the fraction of questions yielding no reward signal rose from 0.2% (uniform policy) to 20.8% (after GRPO training).

Method: The authors propose FGPO (Full-Group Policy Optimization) with two key innovations: (1) Score every tool subset and optimize the exact action expectation rather than sampling, ensuring each update sees the complete action space. (2) Precompute the reward of each question-subset pair into an exhaustive table during a preprocessing phase, removing frozen-reasoner calls from the training loop entirely.

Result: FGPO outperforms GRPO in all 15 settings (5 frozen reasoners × 3 genomic benchmarks) by an average of 6.75 points and up to 14.20 points. Compared to standard on-demand GRPO, FGPO requires 2.4× fewer frozen-reasoner reward evaluations. On GenomeQA, FGPO reduces invoked tools per question from 2.36 to 1.40.

Conclusion: FGPO provides a more efficient and effective approach for reinforcement learning in specialist scientific settings with enumerable tool-subset spaces. By computing exact action expectations and precomputing rewards, it avoids the advantage vanishing problem that plagues GRPO while also reducing computational overhead.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning over a frozen reasoner has become a common recipe for teaching a policy which external tools to invoke. We show that this recipe becomes structurally mismatched in specialist scientific settings where the complete tool-subset space is enumerable. There, a small set of recurring computational capabilities covers the domain, so the space of tool subsets is combinatorial yet small enough to enumerate, and GRPO still estimates an action expectation from a handful of sampled rollouts. Worse, the approximation degrades as training succeeds: as the policy concentrates on preferred subsets it resamples them, sampled rewards collide, and the group-normalized advantage vanishes. On genomic reasoning the fraction of questions yielding no reward signal rises from 0.2% under a uniform reference policy to 20.8% after GRPO training. As a remedy, we introduce FGPO (Full-Group Policy Optimization), which (1) scores every tool subset and optimizes the exact action expectation, so each update sees the complete action space, and (2) precomputes the reward of each question--subset pair into an exhaustive table, removing frozen-reasoner calls from the training loop entirely. Across five frozen reasoners and three genomic benchmarks, FGPO outperforms GRPO in all 15 settings by 6.75 points on average and up to 14.20, while a standard on-demand GRPO schedule would require 2.4 times as many frozen-reasoner reward evaluations and, on GenomeQA, FGPO cuts invoked tools per question from 2.36 to 1.40.

</details>


### [148] [What Should an Agent Forget? Separating What Is Stored from What Is Used](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10263)
*Yuhang Li,Yuchen Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RD-Forget is a training-free framework that separates stored experience from answer-time evidence selection for persistent language agents. It uses a retained source archive, query-conditioned memory views, semantic slot grouping, and intent-aware retrieval to suppress obsolete facts for current queries while preserving historical access, guided by a rate-distortion formulation for memory efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The core challenge is that persistent language agents need stored experience to remain available across time, but each answer requires evidence suited to a particular question. Superseded facts can mislead current-state answers while still being essential for historical queries. Existing approaches lack a principled way to separate what an agent stores from what it uses for specific queries.

Method: RD-Forget uses a retained source archive to preserve all observations, with a query-conditioned memory view controlling evidence influence at answer time. A frozen language-model curator extracts relevant evidence and groups facts into semantic slots while preserving multi-hop reasoning relations. Same-slot replacement links suppress superseded values in current-state contexts, while intent-aware retrieval makes earlier evidence eligible for historical queries. A rate-distortion formulation guides the construction of answer-time views within memory constraints.

Result: Experiments across conversational memory, knowledge updating, fact consolidation, long-context reasoning, and personalization demonstrate that accurate answers depend on both query-relevant evidence construction and control over obsolete alternatives. Configurations without forgetting or query conditioning show the largest score deficits. Slot grouping, historical access, and relation preservation each contribute complementary functions to overall performance.

Conclusion: RD-Forget provides a practical solution for managing evolving knowledge in persistent language agents by separating stored experience from answer-time evidence selection. The framework achieves accurate answers through complementary mechanisms: slot grouping for semantic organization, same-slot replacement for suppressing obsolete facts in current-state queries, and intent-aware retrieval for preserving historical access. The rate-distortion formulation enables efficient memory budget management. This training-free approach offers a principled way to accommodate changing facts while remaining prepared for future questions.

Abstract: Persistent language agents need stored experience to remain available across time, while each answer requires evidence suited to a particular question. A superseded fact can mislead a current-state answer and still be essential for a historical query. We present RD-Forget, a training-free framework that separates what an agent stores from what it uses. A retained source archive preserves observations, and a query-conditioned memory view controls their influence on the current answer. A frozen language-model curator extracts relevant evidence, groups facts into semantic slots, and preserves the relations needed for multi-hop reasoning. Same-slot replacement links suppress superseded values in current-state contexts, while intent-aware retrieval makes earlier evidence eligible again. A rate-distortion formulation guides construction of the answer-time view within a memory budget. Experiments span conversational memory, knowledge updating, fact consolidation, long-context reasoning, and personalization under a shared answering pipeline. The results associate accurate answers with both query-relevant evidence construction and control over obsolete alternatives. Configurations without forgetting or query conditioning have the largest score deficits, while slot grouping, historical access, and relation preservation contribute complementary functions. Retaining history while selectively controlling its use offers a practical way to accommodate changing facts and future questions.

</details>


### [149] [TRACE: Training Reasoning Agents for Causal Exploration with Synthesized Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10315)
*Rui Sun,Zhan Shi,Bing He*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents TRACE, a simulation-based approach that enables reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) for diagnostic reasoning tasks by using controllable simulators to generate oracle labels. On a digital-advertising diagnostic environment with 12 root causes, the method achieves 0.757 FullAttr@1, outperforming all prompted baselines including frontier models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional RLVR excels in domains with easy answer verification (math, code), but diagnostic reasoning lacks this advantage because establishing true causes requires costly expert investigation. This paper engineers verification by sampling interventions, injecting them into controlled simulators, and generating observations with oracle labels.

Method: The approach instantiates TRACE, a digital-advertising diagnostic environment with 12 root causes and fine-grained segment attribution. Agents investigate using Python and SQL to identify root causes and affected segments. The method combines supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on the simulated data followed by RL with synthesized rewards. Evaluation uses FullAttr@1 metric on a 235-episode held-out test set.

Result: Claude Opus 5 (strongest prompted baseline) achieves 0.686 FullAttr@1. SFT raises Qwen3.5-35B-A3B from 0.159 to 0.637. Subsequent RL achieves 0.757, outperforming all prompted baselines including frontier closed-source models and prompted Qwen3.5-122B-A10B. The policy also uses substantially fewer tool calls than prompted 35B base.

Conclusion: Access to a scalable, objective training signal can be more important than model scale alone. Simulation-based verification can make otherwise ambiguous diagnostic reasoning tasks amenable to scalable reinforcement learning, enabling RLVR to extend beyond domains with easily verifiable answers.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) has advanced language-model reasoning in domains such as mathematics and code, where objective answers are inexpensive to check. Diagnostic reasoning over complex data lacks this advantage: establishing the true cause of an anomaly often requires costly expert investigation and may remain ambiguous after the fact. We ask whether this asymmetry of verification can instead be engineered. We sample an intervention, inject it into a controlled simulator, and generate the observations it would produce. The hidden intervention provides an oracle label and objective reward, while the agent must still investigate noisy, confounded, and distributed evidence.
  We instantiate this approach in TRACE, a digital-advertising diagnostic environment with 12 root causes and fine-grained segment attribution. Agents investigate each episode using Python and SQL and must identify both the root cause and, when applicable, the affected segment assignment. On a held-out 235-episode test set, the strongest prompted baseline, Claude Opus 5, reaches 0.686 FullAttr@1. Supervised fine-tuning raises Qwen3.5-35B-A3B from 0.159 to 0.637, and subsequent RL with synthesized rewards reaches 0.757, outperforming all evaluated prompted baselines, including frontier closed-source models and a prompted Qwen3.5-122B-A10B model. The resulting policy also uses substantially fewer tool calls than the prompted 35B base. These results provide evidence that access to a scalable, objective training signal can be a more important constraint than model scale alone. More broadly, simulation-based verification can make otherwise ambiguous diagnostic reasoning tasks amenable to scalable reinforcement learning.

</details>


### [150] [From Symbolic Perception to Logical Deduction: A Framework for Guiding Language Models in Geometric Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10335)
*Weichen Dai,Rafael Medeiros Cabral,Ziyi Shou,Yan Cao,Xin Shen,Dongcai Lu,Yi Zhou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A framework demonstrating that pure LLMs with specialized modules can rival state-of-the-art LMMs in solving complex plane geometry problems, using a Geometric Vision Parser for diagram-to-symbol translation and a Symbolic Solver for formal deductions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Plane geometry remains a significant AI challenge requiring visuo-linguistic integration. While LMMs handle this naturally, they are computationally intensive and opaque. This work explores whether pure LLMs with specialized modules can achieve comparable performance with better interpretability.

Method: The framework integrates two specialized modules: (1) Geometric Vision Parser - translates visual diagrams into symbolic form; (2) Symbolic Solver - performs formal mathematical deductions. This architecture mitigates hallucinations and promotes interpretable reasoning.

Result: The approach achieves performance comparable to Gemini 2.5 Pro while delivering clearer, human-like solutions. The benchmark from 2025 Chinese Zhongkao examinations ensures data novelty and tests deeper deductive skills.

Conclusion: Pure LLMs with specialized modules can rival state-of-the-art LMMs on complex geometry problems, offering better interpretability and clearer reasoning paths without the computational overhead of multimodal approaches.

Abstract: Plane geometry remains a significant challenge in AI, requiring the integration of visual perception and mathematical reasoning. While Large Multimodal Models (LMMs) naturally handle visuo-linguistic inputs, they are often computationally intensive and opaque. We demonstrate that a pure Large Language Model (LLM), when equipped with specialized modules, can rival state-of-the-art LMMs on complex geometry problems. Our framework integrates a Geometric Vision Parser, which translates diagrams into symbolic form, with a Symbolic Solver that performs formal deductions, thereby mitigating hallucinations and promoting interpretable reasoning. To enable rigorous evaluation, we curate a benchmark of challenging problems from the 2025 Chinese Zhongkao examinations, ensuring data novelty and testing deeper deductive skills. Experiments demonstrate that our approach achieves performance comparable to Gemini 2.5 Pro while delivering clearer, human-like solutions.

</details>


### [151] [Cyber-Financial Contagion: Modeling the Propagation of an AI Vendor Compromise Through the Banking System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10350)
*Alex Leytes*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper models how a security breach at shared AI vendors serving banks can cascade through the financial system, creating losses that resemble a banking crisis. It proposes CFC-Prop (epidemic-clearing model) and CFC-GNN (early-warning GNN) on a four-layer financial network, demonstrating that AI vendor concentration is a critical financial stability risk.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The banking sector relies on a small set of shared AI vendors for critical functions (fraud detection, credit decisions, AML, customer analytics). A compromise at one vendor could propagate through operational, informational, and financial linkages, potentially triggering system-wide losses that appear as a classical banking crisis.

Method: The authors built a four-layer heterogeneous network coupling: (1) AI vendors, (2) financial institutions, (3) interbank exposures, and (4) customer accounts. They propose CFC-Prop, a stochastic epidemic-and-clearing model that runs on this network to simulate cascade propagation. They also developed CFC-GNN, a graph neural network-based early-warning model using vendor-side incident telemetry and graph structure to flag high-cascade-risk vendors.

Result: On a synthetic dataset (60 vendors, 220 banks, ~2,500 vendor-bank edges, 1,400 interbank exposures), CFC-Prop reproduces heavy-tailed loss distributions and sharp dependence on patch latency consistent with cyber-financial evidence. CFC-GNN achieves AUROC 0.82 and AUPRC 0.60 across four baselines, with bounded calibration errors.

Conclusion: Cyber concentration among AI vendors represents a first-order financial-stability problem. The proposed models provide supervisors with concrete quantitative tools to assess and reason about this systemic risk. The full code, synthetic data, and reproducible scripts are released.

Abstract: The banking system now depends on a small set of shared artificial intelligence vendors for fraud screening, credit decisioning, anti-money-laundering triage, customer analytics, and internal decision support. This paper studies how a compromise inside one of those vendors can propagate along a chain of operational, informational, and financial linkages until it triggers losses that look, from the outside, like a classical banking crisis. We build a four-layer heterogeneous network that couples AI vendors, financial institutions, interbank exposures, and customer accounts, and we propose CFC-Prop, a stochastic epidemic-and-clearing model that runs on that network. On a synthetic dataset with 60 vendors, 220 banks, roughly 2,500 vendor-bank service edges, and 1,400 interbank exposures, CFC-Prop reproduces the heavy-tailed loss distributions and the sharp dependence on patch latency that are consistent with prior cyber-financial evidence. We also train an early-warning model, CFC-GNN, that uses vendor-side incident telemetry and graph structure to flag high-cascade-risk vendors before impact. Across four baselines the proposed model reaches AUROC 0.82 and AUPRC 0.60 while keeping calibration errors bounded. We release the full code, synthetic data, and reproducible scripts. The results argue that cyber concentration among AI vendors is a first-order financial-stability problem and give supervisors a concrete quantitative tool for reasoning about it.

</details>


### [152] [Fortunate Recall: Ontology-Driven Memory Lifecycle Management for Persistent Coherence in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10413)
*Ansuman Mullick,Eray Tüzün*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Fortunate Recall (FR) is a composable policy layer for LLM memory systems that classifies personal facts into a behavioral ontology and applies category-specific lifecycle policies. It achieves 76.9% on LifecycleBench (vs 61-70.5% for competitors) and reduces confabulation by half while improving correctness from 18.6% to 31.2%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM memory systems treat all personal facts identically, causing unbounded memory growth and degraded retrieval precision. The core challenge is lifecycle management: determining which memories should persist, be replaced, or decay based on the behavioral type of each fact.

Method: FR classifies personal facts into a 10+1 behavioral ontology and applies category-specific lifecycle policies (differential temporal decay, slot-key supersession, event-time validity, and category-aware retrieval routing) as deterministic functions over LLM-extracted metadata. FR-Bank is the infrastructure-independent implementation.

Result: FR-Bank achieves 76.9% on LifecycleBench (516-question benchmark), ahead of Mem0, A-MEM, Memory-R1, and MemoryOS (61-70.5%). It achieves 75.2% on LongMemEval-S. Ablation shows the behavioral ontology halves downstream confabulation (12.0% vs 24.2%, p<0.001). End-to-end, FR-Bank cuts confabulation from 45.1% to 22.4% while improving correctness from 18.6% to 31.2%. The approach transfers to BEAM benchmark (46.8% vs 32.9%).

Conclusion: The behavioral ontology provides the calibration advantage (halving confabulation), while generic lifecycle metadata carries the correctness advantage. The decomposition benefits transfer to independent benchmarks, with the ontology's benefit concentrated in contradiction resolution and saturating near seven policy clusters.

Abstract: Current LLM memory systems treat all personal facts identically, so stores grow without bound while retrieval precision degrades. The core challenge is lifecycle management: which memories should persist, which should be replaced, and at what rate, conditioned on the behavioral type of each fact. Fortunate Recall (FR) is a composable policy layer that classifies personal facts into a 10+1 behavioral ontology and applies category-specific lifecycle policies (differential temporal decay, slot-key supersession, event-time validity, and category-aware retrieval routing) as deterministic functions over LLM-extracted metadata. FR-Bank, our infrastructure-independent implementation, reaches a 76.9% pass rate on LifecycleBench, a new 516-question temporal-disambiguation benchmark, ahead of Mem0, A-MEM, Memory-R1, and MemoryOS (61% to 70.5%), and 75.2% on the full LongMemEval-S under the canonical Wu et al. judge protocol, so lifecycle policies impose no measurable cost on standard retrieval. A pre-registered ablation locates the gains: replacing the typed layer with three generic lifecycle primitives leaves correctness statistically unchanged (-1.7pp, 95% CI [-6.0, +2.7]), so the generic lifecycle metadata carries the correctness advantage, while the behavioral ontology carries calibration, halving downstream confabulation (12.0% vs 24.2%, p<0.001). End-to-end, FR-Bank cuts confabulation from Mem0's 45.1% to 22.4% over answered queries and from 32.2% to 13.0% over all queries while answering more of them correctly (31.2% vs 18.6%); the ranking replicates on the open-weight Kimi K2.5. The decomposition transfers to BEAM, an independently built benchmark: 46.8% correct vs Mem0's 32.9% over 280 questions, with the ontology's benefit concentrated in contradiction resolution and saturating near seven policy clusters. The ontology, benchmark, and code are released.

</details>


### [153] [ConvMem: Convolutional Memory for Long-Context Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10441)
*Hongming Zhang,Zhaozhen Gu,Fengshuo Bai,Ming Hao,Qingyang Zhang,Yuanyuan Wang,Shiyang Tang,Yanna Wang,Bo Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ConvMem is a training-free framework that reformulates long-context reasoning in LLMs as hierarchical convolution, treating an LLM prompted with a query as a convolutional kernel. It shortens the reasoning path from linear to logarithmic complexity and enables massive parallelization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs struggle with extremely long contexts due to fixed context limits. Existing sequential approaches like MemAgent suffer from high latency and require costly RL training that can lead to overfitting on specific datasets.

Method: ConvMem uses three key mechanisms: (1) Configurable Strides and Skip Connections for robust evidence capture and propagation, (2) Multi-Kernel Convolution to decompose complex queries into disentangled semantic channels, and (3) hierarchical convolution that summarizes text segments recursively, shortening reasoning path from linear chain to logarithmic tree.

Result: ConvMem outperforms training-free baselines on RULER-HotpotQA and RULER-2WikiMultiHopQA, and avoids the risk of overfitting to parametric priors often observed in RL-trained models on out-of-distribution tasks.

Conclusion: ConvMem provides an effective training-free solution for long-context reasoning that is highly parallelizable, achieves strong performance on multi-hop QA tasks, and generalizes better than RL-trained approaches on out-of-distribution data.

Abstract: While Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated impressive capabilities, they often struggle with extremely long contexts due to fixed context limits. To address this, sequential approaches like MemAgent extend the effective context by reading text in segments and iteratively updating a fixed-size memory. However, this sequential paradigm suffers from high latency and requires costly reinforcement learning (RL) training, which can lead to overfitting on specific datasets. To overcome these limitations, we propose ConvMem, a training-free, highly parallelizable framework that reformulates long-context reasoning as a hierarchical convolution. Inspired by CNNs, ConvMem treats an LLM prompted with a specific query as a convolutional kernel. This kernel summarizes text segments hierarchically, shortening the reasoning path from a linear chain into a logarithmic tree. Specifically, ConvMem integrates \textit{Configurable Strides} and \textit{Skip Connections} to ensure robust evidence capture and propagation, while employing \textit{Multi-Kernel Convolution} to decompose complex queries into disentangled semantic channels. This design not only mitigates error accumulation but also enables massive parallelization across both text segments and reasoning threads. Experiments on RULER-HotpotQA and RULER-2WikiMultiHopQA demonstrate that ConvMem outperforms training-free baselines and avoids the risk of overfitting to parametric priors often observed in RL-trained models on out-of-distribution tasks.

</details>


### [154] [JarvisGUI: Towards Cross-Device GUI Agents with Dynamic Task Composition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10451)
*Zixiang Chen,Yuheng Lu,Zihao Cheng,Zeming Liu,Jizeng Bai,Ziye Huang,Zhiyin Lin,Zihan Li,Yuhang Guo,Yunhong Wang,Haifeng Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: JarvisGUI is a dynamic benchmark that evaluates GUI agents on cross-device workflows across heterogeneous platforms (Android, Windows, Ubuntu), addressing the gap that existing single-device benchmarks fail to capture.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world GUI usage involves workflows spanning multiple devices and platforms, but existing benchmarks only evaluate single-device tasks, providing an overly optimistic assessment of agents' real-world readiness.

Method: Formulates GUI tasks as input-output transformations under a lightweight type system, enabling automatic composition of multi-step, cross-device workflows and dynamic evaluation within a unified framework using virtual environments across multiple operating systems.

Result: State-of-the-art open-source GUI agents struggle with state-transfer awareness, cross-platform contextual reasoning, and long-horizon dependency management required for real-world workflows.

Conclusion: JarvisGUI reveals a critical capability gap in current GUI agents that existing benchmarks fail to capture, highlighting the need for better cross-device coordination and reasoning capabilities in agent design.

Abstract: Real-world GUI usage frequently involves workflows that span multiple devices and platforms, requiring the transfer of intermediate results, maintenance of shared state, and coordination across heterogeneous environments. However, existing GUI benchmarks overwhelmingly evaluate agents on single-device, statically defined tasks, thus leaving such cross-device capabilities largely unexamined, resulting in an overly optimistic assessment of agents' readiness for real-world usage. We introduce JarvisGUI, a dynamic benchmark that evaluates GUI agents on cross-device workflows requiring coordinated interaction across heterogeneous platforms, including Android, Windows, and Ubuntu. Specifically, JarvisGUI formulates GUI tasks as input-output transformations under a lightweight type system, which allows us to automatically compose multi-step, cross-device workflows and dynamically evaluate agent performance within a unified framework. By evaluating agents in virtual environments spanning multiple operating systems, JarvisGUI reveals that state-of-the-art open-source GUI agents struggle with the state-transfer awareness, cross-platform contextual reasoning, and long-horizon dependency management required for real-world workflows, exposing a critical capability gap invisible to existing benchmarks.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [155] [Spectral origin of the topological gap exponent d + η: mechanism, kernel, decomposition, and scope](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09159)
*Matthew Loftus*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates the scaling behavior of topological gap Δ (excess H1 total persistence) in critical point clouds, deriving analytical predictions that Δ ∼ L^(d+η). The authors decompose the spectral integral I(α) into volume and shape factors, prove the mechanism requires d < 2+η, and provide numerical evidence from Potts model simulations suggesting the IR-dominated regime is only accessible in d=2.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to understand the scaling properties of topological features (specifically H1 persistence) in critical point clouds from statistical mechanical systems. The authors seek to explain why density normalization is needed in d=3 but not d=2, and to establish the connection between the topological gap and known critical exponents (particularly the anomalous dimension η).

Method: The authors use: (1) analytical derivation of spectral integral scaling I(α) ∼ L^(2-α-η) under IR dominance; (2) decomposition into volume factor I_0 ∝ N(1-m^2) and shape factor I_shape; (3) numerical simulations of Potts model q=4 at L=32-256 with α-sweeps; (4) per-configuration correlation analysis between Δ and I(α); (5) hyperscaling product analysis ⟨m^2 · I(-2η)⟩ with covariance corrections.

Result: Key results: (1) Δ ∼ L^(d+η) scaling derived analytically; (2) mechanism requires d < 2+η, confining IR dominance to d=2; (3) d=3 is UV-dominated, explaining need for density normalization; (4) Potts q=4 finds α_opt ∈ [-0.75, -0.5], consistent with -2η_Ising but inconsistent with -2η_q=4 = -1; (5) ⟨m^2 · I(-2η)⟩ hyperscaling is dominated by correlation r(m^2, I) ≈ -0.98; (6) Per-configuration R^2 = 0.91 collapses when |M| is partialed out.

Conclusion: The authors conclude that the topological gap scaling is explained by the I_0 volume factor via magnetization-driven per-configuration variance, while the η-dependent shape factor is established through ensemble L-scaling. The mechanism is confined to d=2 for physical systems. The tentative α_opt finding for Potts q=4 needs L ≥ 1024 confirmation. The per-configuration agreement between Δ and I(-0.5) is primarily mediated by magnetization correlation rather than direct topological-density correspondence.

Abstract: The topological gap $Δ$ -- the excess $H_1$ total persistence of a critical point cloud over a density-matched null -- scales as $Δ\sim L^{d+η}$. We derive this analytically: the spectral integral $I(α) = \sum_{k\neq 0} S_{\mathrm{conn}}(k)\,|k|^α$ scales as $L^{2-α-η}$ when IR-dominated, giving $I(-2η) \sim L^{d+η}$. The decomposition $I(-2η) = I_0 \cdot I_{\mathrm{shape}}$ separates volume ($I_0 \propto N(1-m^2) \sim L^d$) from anomalous dimension ($I_{\mathrm{shape}} \sim L^η$); the volume factor accounts for the magnetization-driven per-configuration variance of $Δ$. We prove the mechanism requires $d < 2 + η$ (IR dominance), confining it to $d = 2$ for physical systems; in $d = 3$ the spectral integral is UV-dominated, explaining why density normalization is needed. An $α$-sweep for Potts $q = 4$ at $L = 32$--$256$ finds $α_{\mathrm{opt}}$ in $[-0.75, -0.5]$, consistent with $-2η_{\mathrm{Ising}}$ and inconsistent with $-2η_{q=4} = -1$; we flag this as tentative pending $L \geq 1024$ confirmation. The $\langle m^2 \cdot I(-2η)\rangle$ hyperscaling product is dominated by the correlation $r(m^2, I) \approx -0.98$ via the shared $I_0$ amplitude, so we report it as a covariance-correction analysis. Under a heuristic argument extending Divol--Polonik to inhomogeneous Poisson intensities, the bare PH kernel is flat; the effective kernel acquires $k$-dependence only at criticality. The per-configuration agreement between $Δ$ and $I(-0.5)$ is primarily a magnetization correlation: $R^2 = 0.91$ at $L = 256$ collapses to $R^2 \approx 0$ once $|M|$ is partialed out. Per-configuration evidence corroborates the $I_0$ Parseval identity but not the $|k|^{-2η}$ shape factor; the latter is established by ensemble $L$-scaling.

</details>


### [156] [EEGBind: Detecting Source-Level Interictal Epileptiform Discharges via EEG-Centric Multimodal Binding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09728)
*Muchen Li,Anglin Liu,Xuetian Gao,Ruijian Xu,Jintai Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: EEGBind is an EEG-centric multimodal framework for five-class source-level interictal epileptiform discharge (IED) classification. It treats EEG as the primary modality and binds synchronized video-context features around an EEG-centric representation, using video as auxiliary evidence rather than early fusion to preserve source-sensitive EEG features.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Source-level analysis of IEDs is clinically relevant for presurgical evaluation and treatment planning. However, classifying IEDs into meaningful brain-region categories is challenging due to subtle, partial source evidence in short EEG windows, subject variability, class imbalance, and imperfect multimodal context.

Method: EEGBind uses EEG as the primary modality and binds synchronized video-context features around an EEG-centric representation. It avoids early or overly strong multimodal fusion that could perturb the source-sensitive EEG representation. A view-consistent repair stage is employed to improve hidden-set robustness while preserving the learned source-class boundary.

Result: On the NeuroMM 2026 Grand Challenge Track 3 NMM-Source-IED benchmark, EEGBind achieves 0.8395 weighted-F1 and outperforms strong competitors.

Conclusion: EEG-centric multimodal binding represents a practical and effective strategy for source-level IED classification, preserving EEG's sensitivity while leveraging video context as auxiliary evidence.

Abstract: Source-level analysis of interictal epileptiform discharges (IEDs) is relevant to presurgical evaluation and treatment planning because it helps characterize where epileptiform activity is likely to arise. Beyond detecting whether an IED is present, this setting requires assigning IED-positive activity to clinically meaningful brain-region categories. This setting is challenging because source-region evidence in short electroencephalography (EEG) windows can be subtle, partial, and affected by subject variability, class imbalance, and imperfect multimodal context. We present EEGBind, an EEG-centric multimodal binding framework for five-class source-level IED classification. EEGBind treats EEG as the primary modality and binds synchronized video-context features around an EEG-centric representation. Instead of relying on early or overly strong multimodal fusion, which may perturb the source-sensitive EEG representation, EEGBind uses video context as auxiliary evidence for robust classification. A view-consistent repair stage is further used to improve hidden-set robustness while preserving the learned source-class boundary. On the NeuroMM 2026 Grand Challenge Track 3 NMM-Source-IED benchmark, EEGBind achieves 0.8395 on weighted-F1 and outperforms strong competitors. These results support EEG-centric multimodal binding as a practical strategy for source-level IED classification. The open-source code is available at https://github.com/HKUSTGZ-ML4Health-Lab/NeuroMM2026_IED_Detection.

</details>


### [157] [Physics-informed neural networks by Gradient-Guided Gaussian Adaptive Sampling (3GAS-PINNs)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09162)
*Yousen Wang,Wei Zhao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 3GAS-PINNs introduces a gradient-guided adaptive sampling approach for physics-informed neural networks, combining uniform and Gaussian-smoothed probability distributions to concentrate collocation points in high-gradient regions. The method is validated on three nonlinear PDEs (Burgers, KdV, and nonlinear Schrödinger equations), achieving up to 14x accuracy improvement over baseline PINNs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard PINNs suffer from slow convergence, gradient imbalance, and inability to capture localized intermittent structures (shock waves, solitons) due to fixed loss weights and uniform collocation point distributions that cannot adapt to evolving solution complexity during training.

Method: The proposed 3GAS-PINNs combines uniform probability distribution with Gaussian-smoothed probability distribution derived from spatial gradients of the solution. This dual approach maintains global constraint satisfaction while concentrating collocation points in high-gradient regions where intermittent structures occur.

Result: 3GAS-PINNs effectively promotes physical consistency in intermittent regions across three benchmark nonlinear problems: 1D forced Burgers equation, Korteweg-de Vries (KdV) equation, and nonlinear Schrödinger equation. The method achieves up to 14x improvement in numerical simulation accuracy compared to baseline PINNs.

Conclusion: The gradient-guided adaptive sampling strategy successfully enables PINNs to accurately capture steep gradients and strong nonlinearity in nonlinear PDEs, particularly intermittency structures like shock waves and solitons. This addresses a fundamental limitation of fixed-weight, uniform sampling approaches in PINN training.

Abstract: Physics-informed neural networks (PINNs) provide a mesh-free framework for solving partial differential equations, yet their performance in nonlinear problems is often limited by slow convergence, gradient imbalance, and insufficient resolution to capture localized intermittent structures such as shock waves[1]. These issues arise primarily from the use of fixed weights of loss and uniform collocation point distributions, which cannot adapt to the evolving complexity of the solution field during training. To address these challenges, Gradient-Guided Gaussian Adaptive Sampling Physics-Informed Neural Networks (3GAS-PINNs) is proposed in this paper, which combines uniform probability distribution and Gaussian-smoothed probability distribution derived from the spatial gradients of solution, to maintain global constraint satisfaction as well as concentrating collocation points in regions of high gradient. Thus, intermittency structures like shock wave and solitons can be accurately captured. The method is evaluated on three benchmark nonlinear problems, including one-dimensional forced Burgers equation, Korteweg-de Vries (KdV) equation and nonlinear Schrodinger equation, all of which exhibit steep gradients or strong nonlinearity. In comparison with baseline PINNs, 3GAS-PINNs can effectively promote the physical consistency in intermittent regions. The accuracy of the numerical simulation can be improved by a factor of up to 14.

</details>


### [158] [World-Time Compute with Verified Code World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09163)
*James Schwoebel,Ingrida Semenec,Jenia Rousseva,Marcos Ortiz,Collin Overbay,Christopher Klaus,Anderson Edmond,Manish Bhatt,Rome Thorstenson,Jessica Tsai,Martin G. Frasch*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces "world-time compute" - a method to generate synthetic training data by creating executable, verifiable programs (worlds) from code templates. By training LLMs on trajectories through many such worlds, the method improves generalization to held-out worlds. The approach works best for small/weak models (+29 points at 0.5B) and fades for complex tasks, perception-based tasks, and saturated tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models require many labeled examples to generalize across domains, but most domains lack such data. The authors aim to manufacture cheap labeled data by leveraging domains whose dynamics can be written as code, creating exact labeled trajectories through synthesized worlds.

Method: The method involves: (1) Creating world templates that instantiate into many executable, verifiable programs over symbolic state; (2) Generating inexhaustible exactly-labeled trajectories through these worlds; (3) Fine-tuning LLMs on these trajectories ("world-time compute"). The approach is compared against corrupted-label controls to isolate the effect of label exactness versus task variety.

Result: Key results: (1) +29 points improvement at 0.5B model scale; (2) Largest model's lift is within noise (saturation); (3) Synthesized dynamics are exact over 20-step rollouts and answer 10x out-of-distribution probes exactly (100%); (4) On List Functions cross-world setting: one adapter on 128 worlds reaches 40% vs 6% for control (+34 points, CI [29, 39]); (5) Gains are largest for few-step reasoning and small/weak models, fading for long chains, perception-induced tasks, and saturated tasks; (6) Cross-task transfer is weak without shared skill.

Conclusion: World-time compute is an effective training-time analogue of test-time compute for improving LLM generalization on symbolic domains. The gains are driven by label exactness from verified code, not task variety. The approach works best where capability is scarcest (small models, simple reasoning), and fades for more complex tasks. The framework is implemented in OpenWorld, a zero-dependency framework. Pixel-native domains remain territory of learned models.

Abstract: LLMs generalize across a domain only after seeing many real, labeled examples, which most domains lack. We study a way to manufacture it cheaply. When a domain's dynamics can be written as code, one template instantiates into many world models: executable, verifiable programs over symbolic state, each an inexhaustible source of exactly-labeled trajectories. Fine-tuning an LLM on trajectories through many such worlds, which we call world-time compute, a training-time analogue of test-time compute, lifts generalization to held-out worlds it never trained on (synthesized world families). Gains are largest where capability is scarcest: +29 points at 0.5B; the largest model's lift is within noise, consistent with saturation. Labels can be trusted because the worlds are verified code: synthesized-then-checked dynamics are exact over 20-step rollouts and answer 10x out-of-distribution probes exactly (100%), whereas per-step LLM and MLP predictors compound error and collapse. Unlike domain randomization, each world is independently authored and verified; a corrupted-label control shows label exactness, not task variety, drives the gains. On real benchmarks (ARC-AGI grids, List Functions, CLRS) the same lever holds as per-world test-time training. On List Functions the harder cross-world form holds: one adapter trained on 128 disjoint worlds reaches 40% on held-out worlds versus 6% for a corrupted-label control (+34 points, CI [29, 39]). The gain is a saturating regularity, not a law: largest for few-step reasoning and small/weak models, fading for long chains, perception-induced tasks, and saturated tasks; cross-task transfer is weak without shared skill. Worlds are authored and served by OpenWorld, a zero-dependency framework (companion paper). Scope: symbolic state; pixel-native domains remain territory of learned models. All code, recipes, and this manuscript regenerate from one repository.

</details>


### [159] [Scaling Post-Training Ternarisation to Qwen3-8B Capability Retention, Reproduction, Lossless Packing, and Packed Execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09240)
*Anirudh Malik,M Sparsh Mehra,Poojith Devan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper presents an end-to-end characterization of scaling up aggressive post-training quantization from Qwen3-4B to Qwen3-8B models using ultra-low-bit (1.58-bit) representations. The study validates that larger models are more robust to aggressive discretization, with the 8B model achieving 78.5% accuracy retention compared to FP16 baseline while maintaining feasible direct packed execution at 15.52 tokens/s.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ultra-low-bit language models (1.58-bit) promise storage and memory reductions, but the actual deployed representation and execution costs are not specified by nominal bit labels. The authors seek to characterize the scale-up of aggressive post-training quantization methods from 4B to 8B parameter models.

Method: The conversion pipeline combines: (1) KOTMS rotation, (2) E2M-ATQ adaptive ternarisation, (3) GPTQ-style error compensation, all in weight-only A16 configuration. The study includes external reproduction gate, matched 4B/8B capability analysis, cross-corpus perplexity evaluation, effective-bit accounting, lossless lattice-aware packing, and direct packed execution benchmarking.

Result: The 8B model achieves a three-corpus perplexity ratio of 1.361x (WikiText-2: 1.318x, C4: 1.393x, PTB: 1.371x). On eight zero-shot tasks, mean accuracy is 64.6% versus 72.4% FP16 (78.5% retention). The 4B model retains 69.6%, yielding an 8.9-point 8B advantage. The packed checkpoint is 8.24 GiB, and direct packed execution reaches 15.52 tokens/s in 7.35 GiB memory.

Conclusion: The study provides a validated scale-up baseline demonstrating that: (1) larger model size improves robustness to aggressive post-training discretization, (2) actual serialization is solved for the measured artifact with preserved perplexity, and (3) direct execution is feasible. Open challenges remain in broader seeds, calibration distributions, and kernel optimization, with packed GEMV still slower than FP16 cuBLAS.

Abstract: Ultra-low-bit language models promise reductions in storage and memory traffic, but a nominal "1.58-bit" label does not specify the deployed representation or its execution cost. We study a scale-up of an aggressive post-training conversion pipeline from Qwen3-4B to Qwen3-8B.
  The conversion uses KOTMS rotation, E2M-ATQ adaptive ternarisation, and GPTQ-style error compensation in a weight-only A16 configuration. We do not claim these algorithms as new. Our contribution is the end-to-end scale-up characterisation: an external reproduction gate, matched 4B/8B capability analysis, cross-corpus perplexity, effective-bit accounting, lossless lattice-aware packing, and direct packed execution.
  The 8B model reaches a three-corpus perplexity ratio of 1.361x, with WikiText-2, C4, and PTB ratios of 1.318x, 1.393x, and 1.371x. On eight zero-shot tasks at n = 500, mean accuracy is 64.6% versus 72.4% for FP16, corresponding to 78.5% chance-corrected retention and a 7.8-point absolute cost. The matched 4B run retains 69.6%, yielding an 8.9-point 8B advantage.
  The packed checkpoint is 8.24 GiB and preserves the recorded perplexity to measurement precision. Direct packed execution reaches 15.52 tokens/s in 7.35 GiB, while a preliminary packed GEMV remains slower than FP16 cuBLAS. The result is a validated scale-up baseline: model size improves robustness to aggressive post-training discretisation, actual serialisation is solved for the measured artefact, and direct execution is feasible, while broader seeds, calibration distributions, and kernel optimisation remain open.

</details>


### [160] [Distribution-Consistent Inference for Dynamic Sparse Mixture-of-Experts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09241)
*Dohyeon Kim,Bedionita Soro,Sung Ju Hwang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper addresses a key limitation in Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models: the fixed top-k expert selection policy that wastes computation when fewer experts suffice. The authors discover that reducing activated experts at inference time causes a distributional shift (increased RMS scale and variance) that degrades performance beyond just losing expert capacity. They propose Layer-wise Distribution Alignment (LDA), a lightweight correction method that aligns reduced-routing representations with the default configuration using layer-wise calibration statistics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current MoE models use a fixed top-k expert selection policy, assigning the same expert budget to every token even when fewer experts may be sufficient. While inference-time dynamic top-k routing can reduce computation, existing methods overlook the distributional shift caused by deviating from training-time routing configuration. This shift contributes to downstream performance degradation beyond the loss of expert capacity.

Method: The authors first analyze and identify the distributional shift problem: reducing the number of activated experts consistently increases the RMS scale and variance of SMoE outputs. To address this, they propose Layer-wise Distribution Alignment (LDA), a lightweight inference-time correction that uses layer-wise calibration statistics to align reduced-routing representations with the default configuration.

Result: Across multiple SMoE LLMs, benchmarks, and routing strategies, LDA recovers much of the performance lost induced by the distributional shift under reduced routing while preserving sparse-inference efficiency with negligible overhead.

Conclusion: The paper demonstrates that distributional shift is a correctable component of performance degradation in reduced-routing MoE models. LDA provides an effective solution that bridges the gap between training-time and inference-time routing configurations, enabling efficient sparse inference without significant performance loss.

Abstract: Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures have emerged as a powerful paradigm for scaling model capacity while preserving efficient inference in large foundation models. However, most MoE models use a fixed top-$k$ expert selection policy, assigning the same expert budget to every token even when fewer experts may be sufficient. Inference-time dynamic top-$k$ routing can reduce computation without retraining, but existing methods often overlook the distributional shift caused by deviating from the training-time routing configuration. We show that reducing the number of activated experts consistently increases the RMS scale and variance of SMoE outputs, inducing a representation mismatch that contributes to downstream performance degradation in addition to the loss of expert capacity. To address this correctable component, we propose Layer-wise Distribution Alignment (LDA), a lightweight inference-time correction that uses layer-wise calibration statistics to align reduced-routing representations with the default configuration. Across multiple SMoE LLMs, benchmarks, and routing strategies, LDA recovers much of the performance lost induced by the distributional shift under reduced routing while preserving sparse-inference efficiency with negligible overhead.

</details>


### [161] [DiffLUT-Net: Differentiable Training of FPGA LUT Networks with Learnable Connectivity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09254)
*Jiaqi Ye,Xinrui Gong,Jingcun Wang,Olga Kondrateva,Bing Li,Grace Li Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DiffLUT-Net is an FPGA-native neural network that uses six-input LUTs trained from scratch, jointly learning truth-table entries and input connections through differentiable relaxation, achieving favorable accuracy-resource trade-offs on FPGAs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current FPGA deployment flows either accelerate multiply-accumulate operations or convert quantized models into LUTs, but lack a unified approach for learning LUT functions and connectivity directly for efficient FPGA inference.

Method: The paper proposes DiffLUT-Net, which uses a differentiable LUT function relaxation and hardware source selection to jointly learn the 64 truth-table entries of each LUT and the source to each of its six input ports. After training, truth tables and connections are discretized, unused logic is pruned, and the network is exported directly as synthesizable Verilog.

Result: Across five benchmarks, DiffLUT-Net achieves favorable accuracy-resource trade-offs, demonstrating the effectiveness of jointly learning LUT functions and sparse connectivity for compact FPGA-native inference.
