Conclusion: The proposed DiffLUT-Net successfully demonstrates that jointly learning LUT functions and sparse connectivity enables efficient and compact FPGA-native neural network inference, with the code available at https://github.com/TUDa-HWAI/DiffLUT-Network.

Abstract: Field-programmable gate arrays (FPGAs) enable efficient neural-network inference, but most deployment flows either accelerate multiply-accumulate operations or convert pretrained quantized models into lookup tables (LUTs). We present DiffLUT-Net, an FPGA-native network connected by six-input LUTs that are trained from scratch. We jointly learn the 64 truth-table entries of a LUT and the source to each of its six input ports using a differentiable LUT function relaxation and hardware source selection. After training, the truth tables and connections are discretized, unused logic can be pruned, and the network is exported directly as synthesizable Verilog. Across five benchmarks, DiffLUT-Net achieves favorable accuracy-resource trade-offs. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of jointly learning LUT functions and sparse connectivity for compact FPGA-native inference. The code is available at https://github.com/TUDa-HWAI/DiffLUT-Network.

</details>


### [162] [Accountable and uncertainty-aware evaluation of sensor-based AI under distribution shift: devices, subjects, and nearly three years underground](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09257)
*Benny Platte,Rico Thomanek,Christian Roschke,Marc Ritter*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes a staged evaluation protocol for sensor-based AI systems deployed in real-world conditions, accounting for distribution shifts across devices, subjects, and time. The protocol uses quantile-based evaluation (5% quantile) rather than means, includes an out-of-present-scope rate to detect silent misdirection, and is demonstrated on smartphone-based geomagnetic localization in underground mines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sensor-based AI systems often degrade when deployed under conditions different from training (different devices, personnel, time periods). Standard train-test splits cannot reveal these generalization failures. The authors argue for treating model evaluation as a measurement with declared reference levels and quantified uncertainty.

Method: Four cumulative generalization stages that hold out: (1) devices, (2) subjects, (3) time. Evaluation uses quantiles of repeated trainings against chance references, an out-of-present-scope rate to detect misdirection toward classes absent in deployment, and explicit decision rules based on 5% quantiles rather than means.

Result: On smartphone-based geomagnetic localization in two underground mines (including 34-month temporal gap and unknown device generation): the 5% quantile of present-conditioned precision is 0.39 over 299 repeated trainings (16.5× chance level). Across 42 location classes, precision varies by ±0.08. A bimodal configuration passes mean-based tests but fails catastrophically (5% quantile below chance), demonstrating why means mislead.

Conclusion: The staged evaluation protocol with quantile-based decision rules provides a more rigorous and accountable approach to evaluating deployed AI systems. Using 5% quantiles rather than means reveals failures that average metrics miss. The protocol enables informed roll-out decisions by accounting for uncertainty across devices, subjects, and temporal shifts.

Abstract: Sensor-based AI systems are rarely operated under the conditions under which they were trained: devices, personnel and recording epochs change, and each change degrades performance in ways a random train-test split cannot reveal. We propose a staged, accountable evaluation protocol that treats the evaluation of a deployed model as a measurement with declared reference levels and a quantified uncertainty. Four cumulative generalisation stages hold out devices, subjects and time. Each stage is judged on quantiles of repeated trainings against chance references with the correct class count, an out-of-present-scope rate exposes silent misdirection towards classes that are no longer present in deployment relative to training, and an explicit decision rule ties roll-out decisions not to means but to 5% quantiles. We demonstrate the protocol on infrastructure-free geomagnetic localisation with smartphone-based recurrent classifiers in two real underground mines, including a replication of the scheme's training stages at the second site. Unchanged models are re-evaluated on data recorded 34 months after the training campaign, on a device generation unknown at training time and with a held-out surveyor. The 5% quantile of their present-conditioned precision there is 0.39 over 299 repeated trainings, 16.5 times the chance level; across the composition of the 42 reachable location classes the figure varies by +/-0.08, several times the spread between repeated runs. Repeated trainings of a single configuration show why means mislead: a bimodal configuration passes a mean-based test decisively while its 5% quantile lies more than an order of magnitude below chance.

</details>


### [163] [Literati: Towards Anytime Optimal Shape Generalized Trees via AO*](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09299)
*Nakul Upadhya,Eldan Cohen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Literati is the first optimal decision tree algorithm for Shape Generalized Trees (SGTs), which generalizes traditional axis-aligned threshold splits to learnable univariate shape functions. It uses a novel AND/OR graph formulation solved by an AO*-based algorithm with enhanced heuristics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Greedy decision tree algorithms produce suboptimal and unnecessarily complex structures. While optimal decision tree methods exist, they are restricted to axis-aligned threshold splits that limit expressivity and force deep, complex trees. Shape Generalized Trees (SGTs) improve expressivity but lack optimality guarantees.

Method: The paper proposes a novel AND/OR graph formulation that jointly optimizes tree structure and shape function complexity. An AO*-based algorithm is developed with two enhancements: (1) a secondary heuristic for OR-node selection and (2) a round-robin policy for AND-node exploration to improve anytime performance while preserving optimality.

Result: Across 24 real-world datasets, Literati achieves higher training and test accuracy than state-of-the-art tree approaches.

Conclusion: Literati successfully combines the expressivity benefits of SGTs with optimality guarantees, outperforming existing tree-based methods on real-world datasets while maintaining compact tree structures.

Abstract: Decision trees are prized for their interpretability and strong performance on tabular data, but popular greedy top-down induction algorithms can yield suboptimal and unnecessarily complex structures. Optimal decision tree methods address this through global optimization, yet remain restricted to axis-aligned threshold splits, which limit the expressivity of each node and often force deep, complex trees to capture non-linear feature effects. Shape Generalized Trees (SGTs) generalize threshold splits to learnable univariate shape functions, improving expressivity and enabling more compact trees. However, existing SGT induction algorithms are greedy and offer no optimality guarantees. In this work, we introduce Literati, the first algorithm for optimal SGT induction. We propose a novel AND/OR graph formulation of the problem that jointly optimizes tree structure and shape function complexity. To solve this AND/OR graph, we develop an AO*-based algorithm with two enhancements that improve anytime performance while preserving optimality: a secondary heuristic for OR-node selection and a round-robin policy for AND-node exploration. Across 24 real-world datasets, Literati achieves higher training and test accuracy than state-of-the-art tree approaches.

</details>


### [164] [Explaining f-Divergence-Based Regularization via Local Curvature and Sharpness-Aware Minimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09367)
*Nour Jamoussi,Marios Kountouris*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper establishes a theoretical connection between Divergence-based regularization and Sharpness-Aware Minimization (SAM) in deep learning, showing both methods are locally consistent under parameter-space perturbations and induce curvature-sensitive penalties. The relationship is particularly transparent for negative log-likelihood objectives with exponential-family distributions where Fisher and Gauss-Newton matrices coincide.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The relationship between divergence-based regularization and SAM has remained largely unexplored, despite both being prominent approaches for improving generalization in deep learning motivated by robustness to perturbations. This work aims to bridge this gap by examining their local geometric properties.

Method: The analysis uses classical second-order expansions of f-divergences to examine local behavior under parameter-space perturbations. For empirical validation, the asymmetric α-skew Jensen-Shannon divergence (JSD) family is used as a controlled testbed, with experiments on four benchmark datasets.

Result: Both methods induce curvature-sensitive penalties: divergence regularization yields a Fisher-weighted quadratic form while SAM penalizes sharpness through the dominant Hessian eigenvalue. The local curvature coefficient scales as α(1-α) and is maximized at α=½ (standard JSD). Loss-landscape visualizations show stronger induced curvature penalization is associated with flatter local minima. Experiments demonstrate both accuracy and negative log-likelihood are best near maximal curvature penalization.

Conclusion: The local geometric perspective extends to input-space perturbations, where divergence-based regularization through input transformations induces a pullback quadratic form, providing a more general perturbation framework than standard SAM while preserving the same local sensitivity interpretation. The two methods are locally consistent under parameter-space perturbations.

Abstract: Divergence-based regularization and Sharpness-Aware Minimization (SAM) are two prominent approaches for improving generalization in deep learning, both motivated by robustness to perturbations. However, their relationship has remained largely unexplored. Building on classical second-order expansions of $f$-divergences, we show that the two methods are locally consistent under parameter-space perturbations: both induce curvature-sensitive penalties, with divergence regularization yielding a Fisher-weighted quadratic form and SAM penalizing sharpness through the dominant Hessian eigenvalue. For negative log-likelihood objectives with exponential-family output distributions, this correspondence becomes especially transparent, since the Fisher and Gauss-Newton matrices coincide. We further show that the same local geometric perspective extends to input-space perturbations, where divergence-based regularization is defined through transformations of the input. In this setting, the regularizer induces a pullback quadratic form on the input space, providing a more general perturbation framework than standard SAM while preserving the same local sensitivity interpretation. To validate the analysis empirically, we use the asymmetric $α$-skew Jensen-Shannon divergence (JSD) family as a controlled testbed. Its local curvature coefficient scales as $α(1-α)$ and is maximized at the symmetric point $α=\tfrac12$, which recovers the standard JSD. Loss-landscape visualizations in the input-perturbation regime show that stronger induced curvature penalization is associated with flatter local minima. Experiments on four benchmark datasets further demonstrate that both accuracy and negative log-likelihood are consistently best near this regime of maximal curvature penalization.

</details>


### [165] [Constraint-Aware Discrete Black-Box Optimization Using Tensor Decomposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09370)
*Keisuke Onoue,Ryosuke Kojima*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes a tensor decomposition-based surrogate modeling approach for discrete black-box optimization that integrates feasibility information by formulating surrogate model training as a constrained polynomial optimization problem solved via differentiable T-norm penalties.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing surrogate modeling techniques in Sequential Model-Based Optimization (SMBO) fail to capture symbolic rules governing feasibility in discrete input spaces, leading to inefficient search that explores infeasible regions.

Method: The proposed approach uses tensor decomposition to capture discrete search space structure, integrates feasibility information directly into the model, and formulates surrogate model training as a constrained polynomial optimization problem solved using a differentiable penalty term derived from T-norms.

Result: Experiments on synthetic benchmarks and real-world problems (including pressure vessel design) demonstrate improved sample efficiency by effectively guiding the search away from infeasible regions.

Conclusion: The tensor decomposition-based surrogate modeling approach with T-norm penalty successfully integrates feasibility constraints into SMBO, improving sample efficiency in discrete black-box optimization with logical feasibility rules.

Abstract: Discrete black-box optimization is often addressed using approaches such as Sequential Model-Based Optimization (SMBO), which aims to improve sample efficiency by fitting surrogate models that approximate a costly objective function over a discrete search space. In many real-world problems, the set of feasible inputs is often given by logical constraints known in advance. However, existing surrogate modeling techniques generally fail to capture the symbolic rules governing feasibility in discrete input spaces. In this paper, we propose a surrogate modeling approach based on tensor decomposition that captures the structure of discrete search spaces while directly integrating feasibility information. To implement this approach, we formulate surrogate model training as a constrained polynomial optimization problem and solve a relaxed formulation using a differentiable penalty term derived from T-norms. Our experiments on both synthetic and real-world benchmarks, including a pressure vessel design task, demonstrate that the proposed method improves sample efficiency by effectively guiding the search away from infeasible regions.

</details>


### [166] [XAI-Refine: An Automated Explanation-Knowledge Loop for Brain-Age Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09388)
*Yang Qiao,Junjie Wu,Deqiang Qiu,James J. Lah,Liang Zhao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: XAI-Refine is an automated explanation-knowledge loop for brain-age prediction from resting-state functional connectivity that integrates post-hoc explanations with verified neurobiological literature to refine models while ensuring predictive performance and explanatory validity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Brain-age prediction models are typically evaluated only by predictive accuracy, which does not guarantee that models rely on reproducible or neurobiologically supported mechanisms. Existing post-hoc explanation methods either stop at diagnosis or require correction targets to be specified before model analysis, limiting their utility for evidence-guided model refinement.

Method: XAI-Refine operates through an iterative loop: (1) consolidates post-hoc analyses across repeated training runs into reliable model explanations; (2) converts explanations into neutral neurobiological questions and retrieves verified literature; (3) compiles verified evidence into an admissible set in the typed explanation space; (4) defines refinement targets as minimal projections of current explanations onto admissible sets; (5) translates revised explanations into differentiable constraints while preserving model variables and measurement operators; (6) promotes candidate updates only when multi-seed validation confirms target-directed explanatory movement, predictive performance stays within guardrails, and non-target explanatory drift remains bounded.

Result: Experiments on functional-connectivity-based brain-age prediction evaluated predictive performance, explanation reliability, literature alignment, and target-specific model revision, demonstrating a structured route from post-hoc analysis to evidence-guided model refinement.

Conclusion: XAI-Refine provides a systematic framework for bridging post-hoc explainability with neurobiological knowledge, enabling brain-age models to be refined based on verified scientific evidence while maintaining predictive performance and ensuring explanatory movement toward biologically supported mechanisms.

Abstract: Brain-age prediction models are commonly evaluated by predictive accuracy, yet accurate predictions alone do not establish that a model relies on reproducible or neurobiologically supported mechanisms. Post-hoc explanation methods can expose these mechanisms, but existing workflows typically stop at diagnosis or require correction targets to be specified before model analysis. We propose XAI-Refine, an automated explanation-knowledge loop for brain-age prediction from resting-state functional connectivity. At each iteration, XAI-Refine consolidates complementary post-hoc analyses across repeated training runs into reliable, structured model explanations. It converts each reliable explanation into a neutral neurobiological question, retrieves and verifies relevant literature, and compiles the verified evidence into an admissible set in the same typed explanation space. The target for refinement is defined as the minimal projection of the current model explanation onto the admissible set induced by applicable verified knowledge. This revised explanation is then translated into a differentiable constraint while preserving the originating model variable, measurement operator, and applicable scope. Candidate updates are promoted only when multi-seed validation confirms target-directed explanatory movement, predictive performance remains within a prespecified guardrail, and non-target explanatory drift remains bounded. Experiments on functional-connectivity-based brain-age prediction evaluate predictive performance, explanation reliability, literature alignment, and target-specific model revision, illustrating a structured route from post-hoc analysis to evidence-guided model refinement.

</details>


### [167] [Applying foundation model embeddings towards urban livability evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09429)
*Ayush Khot,Wen Zhou,Shaowen Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper develops a framework for using foundation model embeddings (AlphaEarth, AnySat, TerraMind) from satellite imagery to predict urban livability in data-scarce regions. The approach identifies which physical features in embeddings are most predictive of socioeconomic outcomes and demonstrates how to enhance prediction performance while accounting for spatial dependencies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate measurement of socioeconomic indicators remains challenging in data-scarce regions, limiting policy interventions and resource allocation. Meanwhile, high-resolution geospatial data from satellite imagery is widely available but underutilized for predicting livability outcomes.

Method: The authors systematically analyze which physical features are encoded within various foundation model embeddings (AlphaEarth, AnySat, TerraMind). They develop a framework to identify the most predictive geospatial indicators and examine how different types of geospatial data influence urban livability predictions. The approach accounts for complex spatial dependencies.

Result: The framework enables identification of the most informative features for specific applications and demonstrates enhanced prediction performance for urban livability outcomes using foundation model embeddings.

Conclusion: This work provides a principled methodology for extracting actionable information from satellite imagery, with practical applications for predicting urban livability in regions with limited observation data. The approach can help policymakers make better-informed decisions in data-scarce environments.

Abstract: While accurate measurement of socioeconomic indicators remains challenging in data-scarce regions, which limits policy interventions and resource allocation, high-resolution geospatial data is widely available and can contain information on various livability statistics. We investigate which physical features are encoded within foundation model embeddings, such as AlphaEarth, AnySat, and TerraMind, and provide a systematic framework for identifying the most predictive geospatial indicators. By analyzing how different types of geospatial data influence urban livability predictions, our approach enables researchers to prioritize the most informative features for their specific applications. Additionally, we demonstrate how to leverage foundation model embeddings to enhance prediction performance for these outcomes. This work contributes a principled methodology for extracting actionable information from satellite imagery while accounting for complex spatial dependencies, with applications in predicting urban livability in regions with limited observation data.

</details>


### [168] [SCCM : Stream Cruise Control Method for Automated Drift Detection and Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09432)
*Mohammad Abu-Shaira,Weishi Shi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The Stream Cruise Control Method (SCCM) is a comprehensive framework for drift detection and adaptation in online regression that addresses concept drift through early-response pre-update detection, dynamic thresholding, and automated hyperparameter tuning, outperforming eight baseline methods across 18 synthetic and 8 real-world datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world datasets exhibit evolving distributions (concept drift), which degrades predictive performance. Traditional methods rely on fixed hyperparameters and reactive adaptation (only after performance degradation), limiting model adaptability under changing conditions.

Method: SCCM integrates five key mechanisms: (1) early-response pre-update drift detection, (2) drift magnitude quantification, (3) KPI-window-based thresholding for local false-alarm mitigation, (4) dynamic hyperparameter tuning, and (5) model recalibration. It uses an in-memory design for real-time adaptability and remains agnostic to data distributions. Integrated with four online regression models, evaluated using R2 and MSE metrics.

Result: SCCM demonstrates improved predictive performance and effective drift handling across evaluated online regression settings, outperforming eight detector-adaptation baselines on 18 synthetic datasets (covering abrupt, incremental, and alternating gradual drift) and eight real-world datasets.

Conclusion: SCCM provides a comprehensive solution for drift handling in online regression through its proactive (early-response) detection approach, dynamic thresholding, and distribution-agnostic design, making it suitable for high-dimensional and large-scale data stream settings.

Abstract: Real-world datasets often exhibit evolving distributions, known as concept drift. Ignoring drift degrades predictive performance, while reliance on fixed hyperparameters further limits model adaptability under changing conditions. Adaptive learning addresses this challenge by continuously updating models online, allowing them to incrementally adjust and remain effective as data distributions evolve. This paper presents the Stream Cruise Control Method (SCCM), a comprehensive framework for drift detection and adaptation in online regression. SCCM enables automated adaptation through early-response, pre-update drift detection, drift magnitude quantification, KPI-window-based thresholding for local false-alarm mitigation, dynamic hyperparameter tuning, and model recalibration. SCCM also adopts an in-memory design for real-time adaptability, unlike purely reactive methods that typically activate adaptation only after performance degradation is observed. By using dynamic thresholding and remaining agnostic to data distributions, SCCM supports KPI-based monitoring across varying data streams, including high-dimensional and large-scale settings. SCCM is integrated with four online regression models and evaluated on 18 synthetic datasets covering abrupt, incremental, and alternating gradual drift, together with eight real-world datasets. The evaluation uses both R2 and MSE and compares against eight detector--adaptation baselines. Results show improved predictive performance and effective drift handling across the evaluated online regression settings.

</details>


### [169] [Efficient Leakage-Free Neural Architecture Search under Leave-One-Subject-Out Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09433)
*Heinke Hihn*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A computationally efficient LOSO-NAS approach that shares architecture searches across subjects, achieving 83.39% accuracy on BioVid Heat Pain while reducing parameters by 99.2%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Leave-One-Subject-Out (LOSO) evaluation for subject-based classification makes Neural Architecture Search (NAS) computationally prohibitive, scaling as O(N²) with N subjects. This creates a need for more efficient LOSO-NAS methods.

Method: Proposed a leakage-free, block-based approach that shares NAS runs across subjects, avoiding the need for N independent architecture searches required in fully nested LOSO-NAS implementation.

Result: On the BioVid Heat Pain dataset: mean accuracy increased from 82.79% to 83.39%, with parameter reduction up to 99.2%.

Conclusion: The block-based LOSO-NAS approach successfully reduces computational cost while improving accuracy and significantly compressing model parameters, making LOSO evaluation practical for NAS in subject-based classification tasks.

Abstract: Leave-One-Subject-Out (LOSO) evaluation estimates generalisation performance for subject-based classification but makes Neural Architecture Search (NAS) computationally expensive because a fully nested implementation requires N independent architecture searches and, assuming approximately linear training cost, scales as O(N^2). We propose a leakage-free, block-based approach that shares NAS runs across subjects. On the BioVid Heat Pain dataset, our approach increased the mean accuracy from 82.79% to 83.39% while reducing the number of parameters by up to 99.2%.

</details>


### [170] [Tensor-Train Weak SINDy: Identifying High-Dimensional Nonlinear Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09434)
*Will Houser,Vanja Dukic,David M. Bortz*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces TT-WSINDy, a method that combines MANDy and WSINDy techniques with tensor-train (TT) format to enable efficient data-driven discovery of dynamical systems in high-dimensional settings without suffering from the curse of dimensionality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Weak-form methods have advanced data-driven discovery of dynamical systems, but existing techniques are computationally expensive and memory-intensive in high-dimensional settings.

Method: The authors combine Multidimensional Approximation of Nonlinear Dynamics (MANDy) and Weak Sparse Identification of Nonlinear Dynamics (WSINDy) methods, implementing requisite computations in the tensor-train (TT) format to handle exponentially-growing spaces of candidate functions.

Result: TT-WSINDy can search an exponentially-growing space of candidate functions -- performing weak-form transformation, regression, and sparsification -- without suffering from the curse of dimensionality.

Conclusion: The method successfully integrates TT format with weak-form sparse identification, enabling efficient discovery of dynamical systems in high-dimensional settings.

Abstract: In recent years, weak-form methods have made significant advances in data-driven discovery of dynamical systems. However, in high-dimensional settings, current techniques can prove expensive in both computation and memory. In this work, we introduce TT-WSINDy, which combines techniques of the Multidimensional Approximation of Nonlinear Dynamics (MANDy) and Weak Sparse Identification of Nonlinear Dynamics (WSINDy) methods, implementing requisite computations in the tensor-train (TT) format. We demonstrate that this method is able to search an exponentially-growing space of candidate functions -- performing weak-form transformation, regression, and sparsification -- without suffering from the curse of dimensionality.

</details>


### [171] [Uncertainty-Aware Sea-Ice Type Mapping with Multiple Ice Charts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09451)
*Samira Alkaee Taleghan,Younghyun Koo,Andrew P. Barrett,Farnoush Banaei-Kashani*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses uncertainty quantification in sea-ice stage of development (SoD) mapping using deep learning. It examines two types of uncertainty: annotation uncertainty (disagreement among independent ice-service charts) and model uncertainty (from predictive models). The study tests whether model uncertainty correlates with multi-annotator disagreement, finding that soft supervision improves correlation, especially near the ice edge (r=0.704 within 0-10 km). Monte Carlo dropout provides the best-calibrated confidence estimates (ECE=0.050).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sea-ice stage of development (SoD) is important for navigation and operational ice monitoring. Current deep learning methods use operational ice charts as training labels, but these charts contain annotation uncertainty because different ice services may assign different SoD labels to the same conditions. Additionally, learned models have their own model uncertainty. The motivation is to quantify both sources of uncertainty and evaluate whether model uncertainty can track annotation disagreement.

Method: The authors quantify two sources of uncertainty: (1) annotation uncertainty from disagreement among independent ice-service charts (multi-annotator label uncertainty), and (2) model uncertainty from learned predictive models. They evaluate different supervision approaches (standard vs. soft supervision) and test uncertainty-estimation methods including Monte Carlo dropout. They analyze the correlation between model uncertainty and annotation disagreement, particularly near the ice edge.

Result: Soft supervision achieved the highest overall correlation of 0.256 between model uncertainty and annotation disagreement. The relationship is substantially stronger near the ice edge, with correlation reaching 0.704 within 0-10 km. Among uncertainty-estimation approaches, Monte Carlo dropout provides the best-calibrated confidence estimates with an expected calibration error (ECE) of 0.050.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates that model uncertainty can track multi-annotator disagreement, particularly near the ice edge where ice conditions are most variable. Incorporating information from multiple annotators through soft supervision improves this correspondence. Monte Carlo dropout is recommended for well-calibrated uncertainty estimates in sea-ice SoD mapping applications.

Abstract: Sea-ice stage of development (SoD) describes the age and associated thickness of sea ice and provides important information for navigation, and operational ice monitoring. SoD labels are obtained from operational ice charts, where trained analysts interpret satellite observations and assign standardized stage codes to regions with similar ice conditions. These codes often represent ranges of compatible ice thicknesses rather than exact physical values. Deep-learning methods can automate SoD mapping and commonly adopt operational ice charts as reference labels for training. These annotations are not exact, however; this is because chart interpretation relies on analyst judgement and on the observations available at the time, so different ice services may assign different SoD labels to the same conditions. We term this variation across independently produced expert annotations multi-annotator label uncertainty; collapsing the annotations into a single deterministic target discards this variation. A second source of uncertainty originates in the learned model itself. In this paper, we quantify both sources: annotation uncertainty from disagreement among independent ice-service charts and model uncertainty from the learned predictive models. We then evaluate their relationship by testing whether model uncertainty is higher where ice services disagree. We observe that supervision incorporating information from multiple annotators can improve this correspondence, with soft supervision achieving the highest overall correlation of 0.256. The relationship becomes substantially stronger near the ice edge, where model predictive uncertainty closely tracks multi-annotator disagreement, reaching a correlation of 0.704 within 0--10 km. Among the uncertainty-estimation approaches, Monte Carlo dropout provides the best-calibrated confidence estimates, with an expected calibration error of 0.050.

</details>


### [172] [Exact-Form Regret for Gradient Descent, Mirror Descent and Follow-the-Regularized-Leader](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09466)
*Ashkan Soleymani,Gabriele Farina,Patrick Jaillet*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper provides a unified geometric framework for understanding what classes of deviations first-order online learning algorithms (gradient descent, mirror descent, and FTRL) can control with sublinear regret. The key insight is "exactness" - the property that the relevant displacement field is generated by a scalar potential. The geometry varies by algorithm: Euclidean for gradient descent, regularizer-induced for mirror descent, and dual state-based for FTRL. The framework is applied to game theory, introducing "conservative correlated equilibrium" as a new equilibrium notion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous work on online learning studied external regret (competing with fixed alternatives). Recent work showed that first-order methods control richer action-dependent deviations. The authors seek a geometric characterization of exactly which deviations these algorithms can handle with no regret, and whether different algorithms control different classes of deviations.

Method: The paper uses a geometric approach, analyzing the structure of displacement fields and one-forms in different geometries (Euclidean, regularizer-induced, and dual state). The authors identify "exactness" as the key property: a displacement field is exact if it can be generated by a scalar potential (the one-form is exact). They analyze the complementary case of nonzero circulation and its consequences for regret. For the game theory application, they study equilibrium notions induced by exact-form deviations.

Result: The authors show that: (1) Under mild regularity conditions, exactness yields sublinear regret; (2) Nonzero circulation provides an obstruction and leads to linear regret; (3) Different first-order methods can control genuinely different classes of deviations; (4) They introduce "conservative correlated equilibrium" and characterize its relationship with correlated equilibrium, determining when they coincide and when they separate.

Conclusion: This work provides a unified geometric account of what first-order online learning algorithms are no-regret with respect to, beyond fixed comparators. It reveals that different algorithms control different deviation classes, with the geometry (Euclidean, regularizer-induced, or dual state-based) being the determining factor. The game theory applications show that these theoretical results have practical implications for understanding equilibrium behavior in multi-agent learning settings.

Abstract: Online gradient descent is usually studied through external regret, where the learner competes with fixed alternatives. Recent work shows that first-order methods control richer action-dependent deviations. We ask for a geometric characterization of the deviations with respect to which online gradient descent, mirror descent, and follow-the-regularized-leader (FTRL) achieve no regret. We identify exactness as the common principle. Exactness means that the relevant displacement field is generated by a scalar potential, or equivalently that the associated one-form is exact in the geometry used by the algorithm. This geometry depends on the algorithm. For gradient descent it is Euclidean geometry, for mirror descent it is the geometry induced by the regularizer, and for FTRL it is the cumulative dual state. Under mild regularity conditions, exactness yields sublinear regret, while nonzero circulation provides the complementary obstruction and leads to linear regret. This gives a unified geometric framework for understanding the deviation classes controlled by these algorithms and reveals that different first-order methods can control genuinely different classes of deviations. These deviation classes have direct consequences for learning, particularly in games. We study the equilibrium notions induced by exact-form deviations and introduce conservative correlated equilibrium, reflecting both the conservative geometry of the underlying displacement fields and the restricted family of deviations available to the players. We characterize its relation to correlated equilibrium, determine when the resulting equilibrium notions coincide and when they separate, and show how these relationships depend on the geometry and the learning algorithm. Overall, this work gives a unified geometric account of what first-order online learning algorithms are no-regret with respect to, beyond fixed comparators.

</details>


### [173] [Building the Harness Automatically: Self-Play in Code Distills a Text Harness for Black-Box Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09468)
*Yi Wu,Zheng Ren,Zhiyu Hu,Haochen Wang,Daryl Chang,Li Wei,Ting Wang,Zhen Li,Pooja Gupta,Nitin Jindal,Lukasz Heldt*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether an agent can learn a numerical search strategy through executable practice and transfer it as text for deployment. The method involves an agent repeatedly writing and evaluating optimizer programs, then distilling the learned strategy into a 197-word text (Harness A). The approach shows significant improvements across multiple language models and benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Unaided language models perform poorly in low-budget black-box optimization compared to strong classical optimizers. This work seeks to bridge this gap by using executable practice to discover a search policy that can be transferred via language.

Method: An agent repeatedly writes and evaluates optimizer programs during development (executable practice). The resulting program and practice record are distilled into a 197-word text (Harness A), which is frozen before evaluation. The method is tested on BBOB (Black-Box Optimization Benchmark) landscapes and a YouTube reward-tuning production benchmark.

Result: Harness A reduces Gemini Flash regret by 48% (p<.001), enters the GP-BO performance range on the practice family, and lowers mean regret on all three held-out BBOB landscapes. The same text improves every tested Gemini executor and transfers to Claude Sonnet (43% and 49% reduction, p≤.005). An independent replication produces Harness B at the same performance tier. The framework achieves the lowest regret on a sealed YouTube reward-tuning production benchmark.

Conclusion: Executable practice is a viable way to discover a search policy, and language serves as a portable medium for deploying it. The approach generalizes across different language models and holds up in real-world production settings.

Abstract: Can an agent learn a numerical search strategy through executable practice and then transfer that strategy as text? We study low-budget black-box optimization, where unaided language models remain well below strong classical optimizers. During development, an agent repeatedly writes and evaluates optimizer programs. It then distills the resulting program and practice record once into a 197-word primary Harness A, which is frozen before evaluation. Harness A reduces Gemini Flash regret by 48\% in an independent $N=30$ study ($p<.001$), enters the GP-BO performance range on the practice family, and lowers mean regret on all three held-out BBOB landscapes. The same text improves every tested Gemini executor and transfers to Claude Sonnet, reducing regret by 43\% and 49\% ($p\leq.005$). An independent end-to-end replication produces Harness B, a different program and text at the same performance tier. The same framework also attains the lowest regret on a sealed YouTube reward-tuning production benchmark. Executable practice is thus a viable way to discover a search policy, and language a portable medium for deploying it.

</details>


### [174] [From Fixed Keys to Readable Schemas: Small Language Models for Vehicle Agent Function Calls](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09476)
*Hamed Jafarzadeh Asl,Yuanhao Yu,Vahid Partovi Nia*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper compares two approaches for representing vehicle functions in small language models (SLMs) for on-device in-vehicle assistants: Functional Tokens (FT) vs Schema-in-Prompt (SIP). Using a benchmark of 9,822 examples across 79 Android Automotive functions, FT provides compact inference but cannot generalize to unseen functions, while SIP can generalize but has higher memory and latency costs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In-vehicle assistants must translate natural-language requests into accurate vehicle function calls under strict memory and latency constraints. Small language models (SLMs) are attractive for on-device deployment, but a key design choice is how to represent the available function surface - either through dedicated Functional Tokens (FT) or by including function schemas directly in the prompt (SIP).

Method: The authors created a benchmark of 9,822 single-turn examples spanning 79 vehicle functions derived from Android Automotive, including held-out functions and requests requiring refusal. They compared both approaches (FT and SIP) under matched fine-tuning across four SLMs ranging from 270M to 1.7B parameters.

Result: On functions seen during training, scaling provides limited benefit: the 270M model can match the 1.7B model, while the strongest overall performance occurs at 0.6B. On held-out functions, FT achieves zero accuracy by construction, whereas SIP generalizes and improves substantially with scale. On out-of-scope requests, FT can invoke an unavailable function it was trained to emit, while SIP more reliably refuses based on the functions offered. This flexibility comes with higher memory use and latency.

Conclusion: Function-surface representation, rather than model scale alone, determines the capabilities and failure modes of SLM-based vehicle function calling. SIP enables generalization but at higher computational cost, while FT offers compact inference but is restricted to functions learned during training.

Abstract: In-vehicle assistants must translate natural-language requests into accurate vehicle function calls under strict memory and latency constraints, making small language models (SLMs) attractive for on-device deployment. For such models, a key design choice is how the available function surface is presented. Two approaches are to represent each function with a dedicated Functional Token (FT) or provide function schemas directly in the prompt. FTs enable compact inference but are restricted to functions learned during training, whereas Schema-in-Prompt (SIP) can generalize to unseen functions at the cost of longer prompts and higher inference overhead. We introduce a benchmark of 9,822 single-turn examples spanning 79 vehicle functions derived from Android Automotive, including held-out functions and requests requiring refusal. We compare both approaches under matched fine-tuning across four SLMs from 270M to 1.7B parameters. On functions seen during training, scaling provides limited benefit: the 270M model can match the 1.7B model, while the strongest overall performance occurs at 0.6B. On held-out functions, FT achieves zero accuracy by construction, whereas SIP generalizes and improves substantially with scale. On out-of-scope requests, FT can invoke an unavailable function it was trained to emit, while SIP more reliably refuses based on the functions offered. This flexibility comes with higher memory use and latency. Our theoretical analysis explains how SIP enables generalization and why longer schema contexts increase inference cost. Overall, function-surface representation, rather than model scale alone, determines the capabilities and failure modes of SLM-based vehicle function calling.

</details>


### [175] [Unthrottling the Tanh Jacobian in SAC: A Negative Result on Bang-Bang Control and MetaDrive](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09478)
*Faiq Shamass*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper investigates whether the Jacobian vanishing problem in SAC (where ∂a/∂u = 1-a² approaches zero as actions saturate) is harmful in practice. The authors test interventions (bypass mechanisms) to restore critic signals at action bounds, finding that vanilla SAC already performs near-optimally on their test tasks, and the proposed interventions either fail or harm performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The concern is that the vanishing Jacobian at action bounds (full throttle/brake) could starve the actor of critic gradient signals exactly where extreme actions are optimal (e.g., in minimum-time control problems). The authors investigate whether this is actually a problem that needs fixing.

Method: The authors tested: (1) vanilla SAC on a minimum-time double integrator task (bang-bang optimal control); (2) an ungated bypass that restores gradient signal; (3) a gated bypass that only activates when |a|∈[0.9,0.999]; (4) warm-started MetaDrive fine-tuning. They measured return, action saturation rates, collision rates, and entropy coefficients.

Result: Vanilla SAC achieves near-optimal return (-31.6 vs -30.3 optimum) across 10 seeds. The ungated bypass saturates the policy (99% of steps with |a|≥0.9) and collapses return to -195.5. The gated bypass also fails without leaving a saturated policy. Auto-tuned entropy coefficient rises against the bypass, pushing probability mass toward tails.

Conclusion: The Jacobian effect is real but treating it as a bug to fix is not free and not helpful on the tasks studied. Saturating a bound is not the same as solving a problem whose optimum lives on that bound - the policy can achieve near-optimal returns without requiring extreme action signals.

Abstract: Soft Actor-Critic (SAC) represents a continuous policy as an unbounded Gaussian that is squashed by tanh. The Jacobian of that map is $\partial a/\partial u = 1-a^2$, which vanishes as $|a|\to 1$. A natural concern is that this throttle starves the actor of critic signal exactly where extreme actions (full brake, full throttle) are optimal. We test a minimal intervention that restores the missing signal: one extra term in the actor loss whose gradient on the pre-tanh mean is the detached action-gradient of $Q$, with no gain parameter.
  On a minimum-time double integrator whose optimum is bang-bang at the action bounds, vanilla SAC already reaches near-optimal return ($-31.6$ vs. a calibrated optimum of $-30.3$) across ten paired seeds. An ungated bypass does saturate the policy (99% of eval steps with $|a|\ge 0.9$) and collapses return to $-195.5$. A gated bypass that fires only on the flat shoulder $|a|\in[0.9,0.999]$ also fails, and does so without leaving a saturated policy. Warm-started MetaDrive fine-tuning shows the same pattern: the bypass does not improve return, and where collision rate falls it is typically traded for out-of-road departures. Auto-tuned entropy coefficient rises against the bypass, which is a push toward the tails.
  The Jacobian effect is real. Treating it as a bug to be undone is not free, and on the tasks studied here it is not helpful. Saturating a bound is not the same as solving a problem whose optimum lives on that bound.

</details>


### [176] [Efficient Fairness Auditing Across Guidance Scales in Text-to-Image Diffusion Models via Causal Abstraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09486)
*Nabila Tasfiha Rahman,Rajatsubhra Chakraborty,Depeng Xu,Lu Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes a computationally efficient method for auditing fairness in text-to-image diffusion models by using causal abstraction to evaluate model behavior under interventions on the classifier-free guidance scale, replacing expensive image generation with an amortized predictor.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fairness auditing of text-to-image diffusion models requires generating large numbers of images across sampling configurations, which is computationally expensive. The authors aim to develop a more efficient auditing instrument.

Method: The authors represent the diffusion process as a low-level structural causal model and construct a corresponding high-level model over abstract denoising states. They use a probabilistic transformer as an amortized predictor of target-feature distributions across guidance scales, implementing the high-level model.

Result: The proposed method was evaluated on distributional fidelity, fairness-query accuracy, and computational efficiency. Demonstrations were conducted using Stable Diffusion 1.5 and StayFair (a fairness-enhanced model) to examine their behavior across guidance scales.

Conclusion: The causal-abstraction-based audit instrument provides an efficient alternative to traditional fairness auditing by enabling the evaluation of fairness-relevant interventional queries without requiring full image generation, potentially reducing computational costs while maintaining accuracy.

Abstract: Fairness auditing of text-to-image diffusion models often requires generating large numbers of images across sampling configurations, making comprehensive evaluation computationally expensive. We propose a causal-abstraction-based audit instrument for efficiently evaluating fairness under interventions on the classifier-free guidance scale. Given a fixed prompt and a target feature function, we represent the diffusion process as a low-level structural causal model and construct a corresponding high-level model over abstract denoising states. We characterize the projected causal structure, establish identifiability of the fairness-relevant interventional query, and provide sufficient conditions under which the high-level model preserves this query. A probabilistic transformer implements the high-level model as an amortized predictor of target-feature distributions across guidance scales. Experiments evaluate distributional fidelity, fairness-query accuracy, and computational efficiency. We present two auditing demonstrations: one using standard Stable Diffusion 1.5 and another using StayFair, a fairness-enhanced Stable Diffusion model, to examine their behavior across guidance scales.

</details>


### [177] [A Statistical Approach to Estimating Sample Size of Machine Learning Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09547)
*Dat Phan-Trong,Sunil Gupta,Svetha Venkatesh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A framework for determining sample size requirements for machine learning prediction models by approximating nonlinear ML models with localized linear representations and evaluating statistical power across local regions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional power analysis requires specifying predictor-outcome relationships a priori, which is challenging for nonlinear ML models that learn complex prediction surfaces without straightforward analytical power calculations.

Method: Approximates nonlinear ML models with localized linear representations and estimates sample size requirements by evaluating statistical power across these local regions.

Result: The framework provides a method to estimate sample size requirements for ML prediction models by leveraging local linear approximations.

Conclusion: This approach enables practitioners to determine appropriate sample sizes for ML models when traditional power analysis methods are not applicable.

Abstract: Sample size determination for machine learning (ML) prediction models is challenging because conventional power analysis typically requires the predictor-outcome relationship and effect structure to be specified a priori. Nonlinear ML models learn complex prediction surfaces that do not admit straightforward analytical power calculations. We propose a framework that approximates nonlinear ML models with localized linear representations and estimates sample size requirements by evaluating statistical power across these local regions.

</details>


### [178] [Robust Industrial Cyber Physical Classification Using Neuromorphic Temporal Embeddings and Hybrid SNN XGBoost Under Machine Unlearning Attacks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09564)
*Ammar Kamoona,Sajad Koushkbaghi,Mahdi Jalili,Peter McTaggart,Xinghuo Yu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A hybrid Spiking Neural Network (SNN) and XGBoost architecture achieves 99.9% accuracy on power-grid intrusion detection while providing structural resilience to machine unlearning attacks, with only 0.9% F1-macro degradation at 10% poisoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing IDS rely on computationally expensive deep learning models difficult to deploy at edge, and periodic retraining exposes systems to machine unlearning attacks where selective data removal degrades detection performance.

Method: SNN is trained once on clean data and used as a fixed feature extractor, while only the XGBoost classifier is retrained during model updates, combining efficient temporal encoding with a lightweight classifier.

Result: 99.9% accuracy (F1-macro 0.999) on Synchrophasor dataset and 95.0% accuracy (F1-macro 0.943) on MSU/ORNL dataset; under selective label-flipping attacks, hybrid model loses only 0.9% F1-macro at 10% poisoning and delays target-class collapse from 60% to 70% poisoning.

Conclusion: Neuromorphic temporal encoding can provide both accurate cyber-attack detection and improved resilience to data poisoning in cyber-physical systems.

Abstract: The digitalisation of electrical distribution networks has increased the exposure of power-grid infrastructure to cyber attacks. Existing intrusion detection systems (IDSs), however, often rely on computationally expensive deep learning models that are difficult to deploy at the edge. Periodic retraining also exposes these systems to machine unlearning attacks, where selective data removal can degrade detection performance. We propose a hybrid Spiking Neural Network (SNN) and XGBoost architecture that combines efficient temporal encoding with a lightweight classifier and provides structural resilience to such attacks. The SNN is trained once on clean data and used as a fixed feature extractor, while only the XGBoost classifier is retrained during model updates. Evaluated on two real-world public power-system datasets, the proposed method achieves 99.9\% accuracy (F1-macro 0.999) on the Synchrophasor dataset and 95.0\% accuracy (F1-macro 0.943) on the MSU/ORNL dataset, outperforming standalone baselines. Under selective label-flipping attacks, the hybrid model loses only 0.9\% F1-macro at 10\% poisoning and delays target-class collapse from 60\% to 70\% poisoning compared with raw models. These results demonstrate that neuromorphic temporal encoding can provide both accurate cyber-attack detection and improved resilience to data poisoning in cyber-physical systems.

</details>


### [179] [Positional task conditioning for scalable defect detection across product families in large product catalogs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09567)
*Soham Satyadharma,Gabriel Roccabruna,Suleiman A. Khan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses product catalog inconsistencies (duplicates, unit mismatches) that degrade customer experience. The authors decompose error detection into focused sub-tasks to overcome LLM long-context limitations, improving F1 from 52% to 87%. They introduce Positional Task Conditioning (PTC) for efficient model distillation, achieving within 1.79% F1 of frontier models at 98% lower cost, deployed across multiple countries processing 10+ million product families.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Product families in large e-commerce catalogs suffer from inconsistencies like duplicates and unit mismatches, which degrade customer experience. Detecting these errors requires reasoning over multiple error types across lengthy product listings, but LLM classification quality degrades significantly due to long-context limitations.

Method: The authors address the long-context limitation by decomposing detection into focused sub-tasks that reduce context length and isolate error types. For scalable deployment, they introduce Positional Task Conditioning (PTC), which distills the capability into a smaller single model by reinforcing task identity at structural prompt boundaries.

Result: Task decomposition improved F1 from 52% to 87%. PTC outperforms rationale-based distillation across five models and two architecture families, achieving within 1.79% F1 of the frontier model at up to 98% lower cost. The system is deployed across multiple countries processing 10+ million product families.

Conclusion: Positional Task Conditioning (PTC) effectively enables scalable deployment of high-quality product error detection, achieving near-frontier performance at a fraction of the computational cost. The system processes 10+ million product families across multiple countries, demonstrating practical real-world viability.

Abstract: Product families in large product catalogs suffer from inconsistencies such as duplicates and unit mismatches that degrade customer experience. Detecting these requires reasoning over multiple error types across lengthy product listings, where LLM classification quality degrades due to long-context limitations. We address this by decomposing detection into focused sub-tasks that reduce context and isolate error types, improving F1 from 52\% to 87\%. For scalable deployment, we introduce Positional Task Conditioning (PTC), which distills this capability into a single smaller model by reinforcing task identity at structural prompt boundaries. PTC outperforms rationale-based distillation across five models and two architecture families, achieving within 1.79\% F1 of the frontier at upto 98\% lower cost. Our system is deployed across multiple countries processing 10+ million product families.

</details>


### [180] [Teacher Geometry Shapes Learnability in Teacher-Student Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09595)
*Kai J. Sandbrink,Flavio Martinelli,Alexander van Meegen,Wulfram Gerstner,Johanni Brea*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper studies how the structure of teacher neural networks affects the learnability of student networks in teacher-student systems. The authors demonstrate that teacher geometry significantly impacts success rates of training, and explain this phenomenon through analysis of loss landscapes containing two types of suboptimal minima: out-of-bounds (OOB) and interior minima.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The structure of teachers in teacher-student systems is often overlooked by assuming randomly-generated, normally-distributed parameters, which hides substantial variation in how learnable different teachers are. This creates a gap between idealized laboratory conditions and more structured functions that arise in practice.

Method: The authors formalize learnability as the success rate of converging to the global minimum as a function of overparameterization, learning algorithm, student initialization distribution, and teacher geometry. They identify easy (maximally dissimilar) and hard (minimally dissimilar) teacher distributions. For small networks, they analytically reduce the loss landscape to two dimensions assuming infinite data and a fast readout layer. They also propose a differentially increasing learning rate strategy for the readout layer.

Result: The easy and hard teacher distributions induce markedly different success rates across a large range of settings and for different activation functions. Maximally dissimilar teachers induce more interior minima while minimally dissimilar teachers induce more OOB minima. The proposed learning rate strategy increases success rates.

Conclusion: Teacher structure significantly impacts learnability in teacher-student systems. The findings help narrow the gap between idealized teacher-student networks and more structured functions that arise in practice. The differential learning rate strategy provides a practical method to improve training success.

Abstract: Teacher-student systems, in which a teacher neural network generates training labels so that a student neural network can learn to implement the same function, are widely used as an abstract setting to study learning. However, the structure of the teachers is often overlooked by assuming randomly-generated, normally-distributed parameters. This hides substantial variation in how learnable different teachers are. We formalize learnability as the success rate of converging to the global minimum, as a function of overparameterization, learning algorithm, student initialization distribution, and teacher geometry. We both identify an easy distribution that maximizes node dissimilarity and a hard distribution that minimizes it, and show that these two distributions induce markedly different success rates across a large range of settings and for different activation functions. To explain the gap, we study the loss landscape of small neural networks that contain two distinct kinds of suboptimal local minima, out-of-bounds (OOB) minima at the edge of the data distribution and interior minima within. Assuming infinite data and a fast readout layer, we analytically reduce the loss landscape of small networks to two dimensions, showing that the region of attraction of interior minima changes as a function of teacher structure. In larger networks, maximally dissimilar teachers induce more interior minima, while minimally dissimilar teachers induce more OOB minima. Motivated by these analyses, we show that differentially increasing the learning rate of the readout layer and decreasing the learning rate of the inner biases increases success rates. These findings provide an important step in narrowing the gap between the study of teacher-student networks and more structured functions that arise in practice.

</details>


### [181] [Cascading Gradient Inversion via LT-Code Inspired Peeling in Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09659)
*Saeed Shariati,Mohsen Alambardar Meybodi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces a new gradient inversion attack for federated learning that leverages erasure-correcting codes theory to recover client training data from model updates. The attack significantly outperforms prior methods, achieving 94-100% recovery rates on ImageNet batches even for passive attackers, revealing that privacy leakage in federated learning has been severely underestimated.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior analytic reconstruction attacks degrade significantly as batch size increases, with existing methods recovering only ~50% of batches of size 100. The authors identify theoretical upper bounds that limit what prior single-round attacks can achieve, motivating a new approach that breaks these limits.

Method: The authors establish a connection between gradient inversion and erasure-correcting codes theory. They construct attacks that treat the gradient reconstruction problem as a coding/decoding problem, enabling exact recovery of batches and sample labels from a single FedSGD round. The attack includes a certification mechanism that validates recovery without requiring ground-truth data.

Result: The proposed attacks exceed prior theoretical bounds and recover batches exactly. On eight image and tabular benchmarks, they outperform prior single-round attacks by a wide margin. A passive attacker (observing only an honestly trained network) achieves 94-100% recovery on ImageNet batches up to size 128. In active settings, over 90% recovery is achieved at batch sizes of several hundred.

Conclusion: The privacy leakage in federated learning has been significantly underestimated. Even passive attackers can recover most training data, and active attackers can achieve even higher recovery rates. These results challenge the fundamental privacy assumptions of federated learning and highlight the need for stronger privacy protections.

Abstract: Federated learning shares model updates rather than raw data, yet these updates can be inverted to reconstruct the clients' training data. Analytic reconstruction attacks, which invert a gradient in closed form, degrade as the batch grows: prior single-round attacks recover only about half of a batch of size $100$ even when the attacker fully controls the network parameters, and known upper bounds limit what any such method can recover. We establish a connection between gradient inversion and the theory of erasure-correcting codes, and use it to construct attacks that exceed these bounds. Our attacks recover batches exactly, together with every sample's label, from a single FedSGD round, and certify each recovery without ground-truth data. On eight image and tabular benchmarks they outperform prior single-round attacks by a wide margin. Even a passive attacker who only observes an honestly trained network recovers $94$--$100\%$ of ImageNet batches at sizes up to $128$, more than prior single-round attacks achieve even with active manipulation of the model, and in the active setting more than $90\%$ is recovered at batch sizes of several hundred. These results show that the privacy leakage of federated learning has been underestimated.

</details>


### [182] [PELM: Power Efficient On-Device LLM Inference with Speculative Decoding and Dynamic Voltage Frequency Scaling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09662)
*Weisi Yang,Stephen Xia*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PELM is a power-efficient on-device LLM inference solution that augments traditional DVFS with speculative decoding and variable verification depth to optimize LLM deployment on resource-constrained mobile/edge devices under thermal constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mobile and edge platforms face challenges deploying LLMs due to limited compute resources, compact form factor, and thermal constraints that cause throttling. Existing DVFS methods focus mainly on hardware parameters and processor frequencies, which are insufficient under thermally constrained scenarios.

Method: PELM expands the optimization space beyond traditional DVFS by incorporating two workload-specific knobs: 1) speculative decoding and 2) variable verification depth. The key insight is that not all tokens require full-depth inference to maintain high-quality generation.

Result: PELM demonstrates superior performance compared to state-of-the-art power governing methods, achieving up to 23.1% speedup and 52.4% reduction in energy consumption while maintaining comparable task performance.

Conclusion: The multi-dimensional optimization approach combining DVFS with speculative decoding and variable verification depth effectively addresses the power and thermal challenges of on-device LLM inference, enabling efficient deployment of LLMs on mobile and edge platforms.

Abstract: Deploying Large Language Models (LLMs) directly on mobile platforms at the edge is gaining traction due to a myriad of benefits, such as increased privacy, personalization, and reduced latency. However, LLMs have heavy computational requirements, which are difficult for resource-constrained mobile and edge platforms to fulfill. In addition to limited compute resources, mobile and edge systems often have a compact form factor and lack physical mechanisms to dissipate heat generated from high processor usage rates (e.g., fans) to prevent throttling and reduced processing power, which LLMs can easily cause. To mitigate these effects, prior works have proposed various power governing strategies, such as dynamic voltage and frequency scaling (DVFS), for reducing power and heat generation for heavy computational tasks on mobile platforms. Recently, DVFS methods tailored for mobile LLMs have also been proposed. However, these methods mostly focus on optimizing hardware parameters and processor frequencies, and they fall short under some thermally constrained scenarios. Drawing from recent advances in machine learning, we identify and take advantage of the key insight that not all tokens require full-depth inference to maintain high-quality generation. Motivated by this, we present PELM, a solution that augments traditional DVFS processor frequency tuning with two additional workload-specific knobs: 1) speculative decoding and 2) variable verification depth to expand the optimization space to multiple dimensions for more power efficient on-device LLM inference. In extensive evaluations across hardware platforms and datasets, PELM demonstrates superior performance compared to state-of-the-art power governing methods, with up to 23.1% speedup and 52.4% reduction in energy consumption, while maintaining comparable task performance. The source code is available at https://github.com/imec-nu/PELM.

</details>


### [183] [Muon-C: Operator-Aligned Muon for Convolutional Kernels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09676)
*Jiaxin Qing,Lexin Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Muon-C is an operator-aligned optimizer that fixes geometry issues in the original Muon optimizer for convolutional neural networks. It represents kernel momentum as frequency-wise channel-transfer matrices, polarizes blocks independently, and uses a critical Fourier grid to return updates to the original finite kernel support.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The original Muon optimizer replaces matrix momentum with an approximately orthogonal polar direction, but its geometry depends on the matrix representation. For convolution operations, standard unfolding describes a local patch map rather than the convolution operator itself, creating a mismatch between the optimizer's geometry and the actual operator.

Method: Muon-C represents kernel momentum as frequency-wise channel-transfer matrices, polarizes these blocks independently, and uses a critical Fourier grid to return updates exactly to the original finite kernel support. The exact-polar direction is a linear minimization oracle under the critically sampled convolution norm.

Result: On CIFAR-10 flow matching with matched applied-update RMS, Muon-C achieves 9.87 FID at 40k iterations, compared with 22.26 for unfolded Muon and 51.31 for Adam. It reaches comparable final quality using 0.62x and 0.64x the model FLOPs respectively. Under equal tuning budgets, Muon-C achieves 3.42 FID. Gains persist across data scales and transfer to classification across convolutional architectures.

Conclusion: Muon-C provides a strictly stronger worst-case guarantee than unfolding for 3×3 kernels and is never weaker than unfolding for other kernel sizes. The optimizer achieves state-of-the-art performance on image generation (flow matching) and classification tasks, with improved efficiency across various convolutional architectures and data scales.

Abstract: Muon replaces matrix momentum with an approximately orthogonal polar direction, but its geometry depends on the matrix representation. For convolution, standard unfolding describes a local patch map rather than the convolution operator. We introduce Muon-C, an operator-aligned optimizer that represents kernel momentum as frequency-wise channel-transfer matrices, polarizes these blocks independently, and uses a critical Fourier grid to return updates exactly to the original finite kernel support. We show that the new geometry arises from combining the block partition and Fourier coordinates. The exact-polar direction is a linear minimization oracle under the critically sampled convolution norm. Its worst-case guarantee relative to the continuous convolution-operator norm is never weaker than unfolding and is strictly stronger for $3\times3$ kernels. On CIFAR-10 flow matching with matched applied-update RMS, Muon-C reaches 9.87 FID at 40k iterations, compared with 22.26 for unfolded Muon and 51.31 for Adam. It reaches their final quality using $0.62\times$ and $0.64\times$ their model FLOPs, respectively. Under equal tuning budgets, Muon-C achieves 3.42 FID. Gains persist across data scales and transfer to classification across convolutional architectures.

</details>


### [184] [Settling: Equilibrium Inference for Non-Convex Validity Sets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09682)
*Lyes Saad Saoud*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses "conditional mean collapse" - a failure mode where learning systems return invalid Bayes-optimal conditional means when admissible outputs form disconnected or non-convex sets. The authors introduce "Settling," an equilibrium-based inference operator that separates proposal generation, consistency evaluation, and test-time equilibrium selection to resolve this issue.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The core problem is that many learning systems return single point estimates, but when admissible outputs form disconnected or non-convex sets, the conditional mean can be an invalid output. Under squared loss, this "conditional mean collapse" produces incorrect predictions. The authors seek an inference operator that can handle these complex output spaces.

Method: The proposed method "Settling" is an equilibrium-based inference operator with three distinct components: (1) proposal generation - uses a mean-seeking proposal as initialization, (2) consistency evaluation - checks whether proposals satisfy constraints, and (3) test-time equilibrium selection - refines the initialization toward a locally stable configuration. The refinement process is deterministic given the initialization. The paper establishes theoretical guarantees including exact-gradient descent, local convergence, and an inexact-gradient robustness condition.

Result: In a reproducible 100-context geometric diagnostic: mean-seeking baseline succeeded in 0/100 contexts, stochastic denoising in 100/100, and Settling in 99/100 while producing substantially lower trajectory roughness. A 1,200-run sensitivity study showed 97-100% success across obstacle-jitter ranges up to 0.20 and 94-100% across one-time initialization perturbations from 0.05 to 0.50.

Conclusion: Settling effectively addresses conditional mean collapse in geometric diagnostic tasks, achieving 99% success rate compared to 0% for mean-seeking baselines. The method shows robust performance across varying obstacle jitter (0-0.20) and initialization perturbations (0.05-0.50). Cross-domain panels remain mechanism illustrations, and learned high-dimensional validation remains an open empirical test.

Abstract: Many learning systems return a single point estimate even when admissible outputs form disconnected or non-convex sets. Under squared loss, an ambiguous conditional distribution can therefore have a Bayes-optimal conditional mean that is invalid. We formalize this failure as conditional mean collapse and introduce Settling, an equilibrium-based inference operator that separates proposal generation, consistency evaluation, and test-time equilibrium selection. The operator treats a mean-seeking proposal as an initialization and refines it toward a locally stable configuration; conditional on initialization, refinement is deterministic. We establish exact-gradient descent, local convergence, and an inexact-gradient robustness condition relevant to learned consistency critics. In a reproducible 100-context geometric diagnostic, the mean-seeking baseline succeeds in 0/100 contexts, stochastic denoising in 100/100, and Settling in 99/100 while producing substantially lower trajectory roughness. A 1,200-run sensitivity study yields 97-100% success across obstacle-jitter ranges up to 0.20 and 94-100% across one-time initialization perturbations from 0.05 to 0.50. Cross-domain panels remain mechanism illustrations; learned high-dimensional validation remains an open empirical test.

</details>


### [185] [ALIGN-HOLD: Experience Alignment for Real-Time Hold Control in Large-Scale Ride-Hailing Matching at DiDi](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09685)
*Zuhao Zhang,Xu Liu,Kai Wan,Zihao Lu,Li Ma,Shuai Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ALIGN-HOLD is a production-scale framework for real-time hold control in ride-hailing systems that learns hold policies from implicit marketplace preferences rather than handcrafted rewards. It uses experience-based reward modeling with balanced multi-view sampling and has been deployed on DiDi's Brazil platform.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing hold control systems like EXHOLD rely on handcrafted reward combinations (trip completion, cancellations, waiting time, driver effort), which becomes increasingly difficult as marketplace preferences are heterogeneous and observed behavior is sparse, noisy, and affected by dynamic supply-demand conditions.

Method: ALIGN-HOLD constructs complementary preference pairs from order trajectories, driver trajectories, and contemporaneous local matching graphs. It trains an experience Reward Model using balanced multi-view sampling and model-adaptive hard preference sampling. During simulator-based policy learning, the frozen RM provides dense, context-dependent rewards and supports label-free filtering of low-identifiability interactions.

Result: In a 28-day randomized A/B experiment covering approximately 100,000 passenger requests per day, ALIGN-HOLD achieved statistically significant improvements in trip completion rate and driver income, while significantly reducing passenger cancellations before and after driver acceptance, compared to the deployed production policy.

Conclusion: ALIGN-HOLD has been fully ramped up and is currently serving DiDi's Brazil marketplace. The complementary ablations, RM diagnostics, and behavioral analyses validate the contributions of the proposed components.

Abstract: Real-time hold control is a high-leverage mechanism in large-scale ride-hailing systems: by selectively deferring driver-order pairs, the platform can wait for better matching opportunities and improve end-to-end passenger-driver experience. Existing production systems such as EXHOLD learn bandit-based hold policies from handcrafted combinations of trip completion, cancellations, waiting time, and driver effort.
