However, designing such rewards becomes increasingly difficult as marketplace preferences are heterogeneous and observed passenger-driver behavior can be sparse, noisy, and affected by dynamic supply-demand conditions.
  We present ALIGN-HOLD, a production-scale experience alignment framework that learns hold policy from implicit marketplace preferences. ALIGN-HOLD constructs complementary preference pairs from order trajectories, driver trajectories, and contemporaneous local matching graphs, and trains an experience Reward Model (RM) using balanced multi-view sampling and model-adaptive hard preference sampling. During simulator-based policy learning, the frozen RM provides a dense, context-dependent reward and supports label-free filtering of low-identifiability interactions whose behavioral feedback is difficult to attribute to matching quality.
  We deploy ALIGN-HOLD on DiDi's ride-hailing platform and evaluate it in a 28-day randomized A/B experiment, covering approximately 100,000 passenger requests per day. Compared with the deployed production policy, ALIGN-HOLD achieves statistically significant improvements in trip completion rate and driver income, while significantly reducing passenger cancellations before and after driver acceptance. Complementary ablations, RM diagnostics, and behavioral analyses validate the contributions of the proposed components. ALIGN-HOLD has been fully ramped up and is currently serving DiDi's Brazil marketplace.

</details>


### [186] [Kernel-Complexity Edge Sanitization for Training-Free Defense against Structural Graph Attacks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09698)
*Yaning Jia,Shenyang Deng,Yaoqing Yang,Chiyu Ma,Wenxuan Xu,Soroush Vosoughi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: KCES is a training-free, model-agnostic framework for defending GNNs against structural adversarial attacks. It uses Graph Kernel Complexity (GKC) derived from generalization bounds to compute edge-specific KC scores, then prunes high-KC edges that are enriched with adversarial perturbations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing GNN defenses against adversarial attacks lack rigorous theoretical grounding, rely on attack-specific heuristics, or require costly retraining (e.g., adversarial training). There is a need for a principled, efficient, and通用的 defense mechanism.

Method: KCES is built upon Graph Kernel Complexity (GKC), a metric derived from the graph Gram matrix appearing in a GNN generalization upper bound. The method defines an edge-specific KC score quantifying each edge's structural influence via its induced change in GKC. High-KC edges (empirically enriched with adversarial perturbations) are identified and pruned as a preprocessing step.

Result: KCES consistently outperforms representative robust baselines across diverse attack settings and scales effectively to large graphs. The framework is computationally efficient, requires no retraining, and can be integrated with existing defenses.

Conclusion: KCES provides a principled and efficient framework for securing GNNs against structural attacks, supported by theoretical analysis (generalization bound foundation) and extensive empirical validation. It successfully addresses the limitations of prior defenses while maintaining scalability and compatibility.

Abstract: Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) have achieved remarkable success across diverse applications, yet they remain highly vulnerable to adversarial attacks that maliciously perturb graph structure. Existing defenses often lack rigorous theoretical grounding, rely on attack-specific heuristics, or require costly retraining procedures such as adversarial training. To address these limitations, we propose Kernel-Complexity Edge Sanitization (KCES), a training-free and model-agnostic framework for defending against structural attacks. KCES is built upon Graph Kernel Complexity (GKC), a principled metric derived from the graph Gram matrix that appears in a generalization upper bound on the GNN test error. From this bound, we define an edge-specific KC score that quantifies each edge's structural influence via its induced change in GKC. KCES then identifies and prunes high-KC edges, which are empirically enriched with adversarial perturbations under structural attacks, to mitigate their harmful impact. Computationally efficient and scalable, KCES operates as a lightweight preprocessing step without retraining and can be seamlessly integrated with existing defenses. Extensive experiments demonstrate that KCES consistently outperforms representative robust baselines across diverse attack settings and scales effectively to large graphs. Supported by theoretical analysis and extensive empirical validation, KCES provides a principled and efficient framework for securing GNNs. Our code is available at https://github.com/karpning/KCScore.

</details>


### [187] [EFQ-Softmax: Exp-Free Quantization for Softmax](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09721)
*Haohui Han,Yuming Wan,Hongni Wang,Pengcheng Xie,Xiaodong Yan,Runqi You,Wencong Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: EFQ-Softmax proposes exp-free quantization for softmax, directly mapping shifted attention scores to block-scaled E2M1 operands to eliminate the mismatch between high-precision probability generation and low-bit matrix consumption in Transformer inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The conventional exp-then-quantize path creates a mismatch between a high-precision probability producer (softmax evaluates shifted-score exponentials in higher precision, forms temporary probability block, then quantizes) and a low-bit matrix consumer (low-bit PV multiplication). This inefficiency limits low-bit attention acceleration.

Method: EFQ-Softmax selects an exponent-only scale from local maximum for each microscaling block, maps shifted scores to a normalized residual domain, and generates nonnegative E2M1 probability codes using a single affine rule. The resulting operand is used consistently in both the $\widetilde{P}V$ numerator update and the $\widetilde{P}\mathbf{1}$ denominator update. FlashAttention-style row-maximum update, historical rescaling, high-precision accumulation, and final normalization remain unchanged.

Result: EFQ-Softmax improves Qwen3-8B seven-task mean from 0.6749 (MXFP4) to 0.6773, and Qwen3-VL nine-task mean from 0.7826 to 0.8000. On WAN2.2, it maintains temporal consistency and visual quality comparable to FP16 and MXFP4 baselines under VBench. On A5 vector unit, it reduces vector-stage latency by 40.33% on average across sequence lengths from 16K to 128K.

Conclusion: Direct low-bit probability generation can replace the conventional exp-then-quantize path while preserving end-to-end model quality, achieving significant latency reduction without quality degradation.

Abstract: Low-bit attention accelerates Transformer inference by moving the $QK^\top$ and $PV$ matrix multiplications to FP8 or FP4 matrix engines. However, the softmax path often evaluates shifted-score exponentials in higher precision, forms a temporary probability block, and quantizes it before low-bit $PV$ multiplication. This exp-then-quantize path creates a mismatch between a high-precision probability producer and a low-bit matrix consumer. We propose EFQ-Softmax (Exp-Free Quantization for Softmax), a low-bit probability-generation method that directly maps shifted attention scores to block-scaled E2M1 operands. For each microscaling block, EFQ-Softmax selects an exponent-only scale from the local maximum, maps the shifted scores to a normalized residual domain, and generates nonnegative E2M1 probability codes using a single affine rule. The resulting operand is used consistently in both the $\widetilde{P}V$ numerator update and the $\widetilde{P}\mathbf{1}$ denominator update. The FlashAttention-style row-maximum update, historical rescaling, high-precision accumulation, and final normalization remain unchanged. We evaluate end-to-end quality on Qwen3-8B, Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct, and WAN2.2-TI2V-5B, and separately measure kernel-level performance on the A5 vector unit. EFQ-Softmax improves the Qwen3-8B seven-task mean from 0.6749 with MXFP4 to 0.6773 and the Qwen3-VL nine-task mean from 0.7826 to 0.8000. On WAN2.2, it maintains temporal consistency and visual quality comparable to the FP16 and MXFP4 baselines under VBench. On the A5 vector unit, EFQ-Softmax reduces the vector-stage latency of the fused probability-generation kernel by 40.33% on average across sequence lengths from 16K to 128K. These results show that direct low-bit probability generation can replace the conventional exp-then-quantize path while preserving end-to-end model quality.

</details>


### [188] [Fine-Tuning a KV Cache Concatenation-Aware Model or Recomputing KV Caches? Why Not Both?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09768)
*Fumihiko Tachibana,Daisuke Miyashita,Jun Deguchi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses the challenge of long input contexts in Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems that cause increased Time to First Token (TTFT). The authors propose combining KV cache-aware fine-tuning with selective KV cache recomputation to maintain response quality while reducing TTFT.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In RAG systems, concatenating many retrieved chunks forms very long input contexts, leading to substantial prefill workload and longer TTFT. While KV cache reuse can reduce TTFT, it remains unclear whether response quality is preserved for very long input contexts.

Method: The proposed method combines two techniques: (i) fine-tuning the model while accounting for KV cache concatenation, and (ii) selectively recomputing a subset of KV caches.

Result: On the RULER benchmark with a 124k-token input, the method improves RULER score by 9.7 points over the baseline that only recomputes KV caches, while reducing TTFT by 80% compared to full attention.

Conclusion: The combined approach of KV cache-aware fine-tuning and selective KV cache recomputation successfully preserves accuracy for long-context inputs while significantly reducing TTFT, demonstrating the effectiveness of addressing both training and inference challenges in RAG systems.

Abstract: In Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems, a large number of retrieved chunks are concatenated to form the input context so that users can receive high-quality responses based on external knowledge. As a result, the input context length increases substantially, leading to a larger prefill workload and, in turn, a longer time to first token (TTFT). While previous works that reuse precomputed key-value (KV) caches effectively reduce TTFT for long-context inputs, it remains unclear whether response quality is preserved when the input context becomes very long. In this paper, we propose a combined approach that (i) fine-tunes the model while taking KV cache concatenation into account and (ii) selectively recomputes a subset of the KV caches. By applying both techniques, we demonstrate improved accuracy for long-context inputs. Experiments on the RULER benchmark show that, for a 124k-token input, our method improves the RULER score by 9.7 point over the baseline that recomputes KV caches only. Moreover, TTFT is reduced by 80% compared with full attention.

</details>


### [189] [BRACE: Anchored Bellman-Residual Correction for Stale Critics in Asynchronous RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09783)
*Guanqun Zhao,Zijun Xie,Binbin Zheng,Jiafeng Lu,Enlei Gong,Zeyu Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: BRACE addresses the critic bias problem in asynchronous reinforcement learning for language models by using an anchored Bellman-residual correction that bounds the correction horizon to policy token prefixes and adds a constant-weight Monte-Carlo tail for reward propagation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Asynchronous reinforcement learning has become standard for scaling LLM training, but it introduces policy lag that biases the critic toward stale behavior policy. Existing methods only correct the actor while leaving the critic bias unaddressed, and classical off-policy value correction fails for long-horizon agentic tasks due to exponential importance ratio drift.

Method: BRACE proposes an anchored Bellman-residual correction that: (1) bounds the correction horizon to a prefix of policy tokens, (2) anchors a constant-weight Monte-Carlo tail beyond the horizon, and (3) separates policy correction from reward propagation.

Result: BRACE improves mean@1 on BrowseComp-Plus by 2.4% over the strongest baseline, runs 2.46× faster per step than synchronous training, and remains stable even 50 updates off-policy.

Conclusion: BRACE successfully addresses the critic bias problem in asynchronous LLM training, achieving better performance while maintaining computational efficiency and stability even with significant off-policy data.

Abstract: Asynchronous reinforcement learning has become the standard way to scale training for language models, but the resulting policy lag biases the critic toward the stale behavior policy. Existing work on asynchronous LLM training corrects the actor and leaves this bias unaddressed, while the off-policy value correction of classical RL does not carry over to long-horizon agentic tasks, since a short correction horizon leaves the regression target free of the reward and a long one lets the product of importance ratios drift exponentially with the trajectory length. We propose BRACE, an anchored Bellman-residual correction for stale value models. BRACE bounds the correction horizon to a prefix of policy tokens and anchors a constant-weight Monte-Carlo tail beyond it, which separates policy correction from reward propagation. BRACE improves mean@1 on BrowseComp-Plus by $2.4\%$ over the strongest baseline, runs $2.46\times$ faster per step than synchronous training, and remains stable $50$ updates off-policy.

</details>


### [190] [NEXUS-MI: Communication-Aware Federated Personalization for Gateway-Coordinated Motor-Imagery Brain-Computer Interfaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09786)
*Daniel Adu Worae,Aarthy Nagarajan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: NEXUS-MI is a gateway-coordinated federated personalization framework for EEG-based motor-imagery BCI that treats synchronization as a coupled learning-and-communication control problem. Raw EEG and classifier heads remain local while an edge coordinator maintains the shared backbone.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: EEG-based motor-imagery BCIs vary across subjects and sessions, complicating personalization from limited calibration data. Existing federated MI studies largely assume regular synchronization, but real-world scenarios involve heterogeneous network conditions.

Method: Gateway-coordinated federated personalization framework where raw EEG and classifier heads remain local, while an edge coordinator maintains the shared backbone. Evaluated on BCICIV-2a (9 subjects, 4 classes) and OpenBMI (54 subjects, 2 classes) with session 1 for backbone learning and session 2 for limited-calibration personalization and testing. Compared communication-aware vs non-adaptive synchronization with various gateway policies.

Result: Communication-aware coordination reduced server-to-client backbone traffic by approximately 42% on both datasets. Cohort-level accuracy differences were small and realization-dependent, but subject-level vulnerability was observed with losses reaching approximately 12 percentage points on BCICIV-2a relative to ideal-link reference.

Conclusion: Gateway synchronization should be treated as an explicit design variable in federated MI personalization. Joint evaluation of personalized accuracy, communication cost, update freshness, and subject-level reliability is necessary. The work motivates considering communication-aware synchronization strategies in practical federated BCI deployment.

Abstract: Electroencephalography (EEG)-based motor-imagery brain-computer interfaces (MI-BCIs) vary across subjects and sessions, complicating personalization from limited calibration data. Federated learning can exploit shared representations without centralizing raw EEG, but existing federated MI studies largely assume regular synchronization. We introduce NEXUS-MI, a gateway-coordinated federated personalization framework that treats synchronization as a coupled learning-and-communication control problem. Raw EEG and classifier heads remain local, while an edge coordinator maintains the shared backbone. We evaluate NEXUS-MI through offline replay using BCI Competition IV Dataset 2a (BCICIV-2a; 9 subjects, 4 classes) and OpenBMI (54 subjects, 2 classes). Session 1 supports backbone learning, and Session 2 provides limited-calibration personalization and held-out testing. An ideal-link reference and six heterogeneous-link policies characterize gateway participation, buffering, stale-update admission, and backbone-download control. The principal comparison holds delayed-update handling fixed while contrasting non-adaptive and communication-aware synchronization. Paired subject-level comparisons use Holm adjustment, and robustness across five matched realizations is assessed by hierarchical bootstrap. Communication-aware coordination reduced server-to-client backbone traffic by approximately 42% on both datasets, while cohort-level accuracy differences were small and realization-dependent. Cohort averages also concealed subject-level vulnerability, with losses reaching approximately 12 percentage points on BCICIV-2a relative to the ideal-link reference. These findings establish gateway synchronization as an explicit design variable in federated MI personalization and motivate joint evaluation of personalized accuracy, communication cost, update freshness, and subject-level reliability.

</details>


### [191] [Evaluating Model Retraining under Drift: Paired Comparisons of Cumulative Subgroup Disparity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09788)
*Aaron Ceross*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper compares three retraining policies (scheduled, loss-triggered, and subgroup-gap-triggered) for deployed classifiers against retaining the initial model, evaluating their impact on subgroup error rate disparities across deployment windows.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to address when and how to retrain deployed classifiers to minimize subgroup disparities (gaps in true-positive and false-positive rates across demographic groups), which is critical for fairness in machine learning systems deployed in high-stakes domains.

Method: The study compares complete scheduled, loss-triggered, and subgroup-gap-triggered retraining policies against retaining the initial model. Evaluation uses paired differences in absolute subgroup gaps summed over deployment windows, tested via simulation (400 trajectories per condition across two drift regimes), action records, and alternative schedules. An American Community Survey replay validates findings with person weighting.

Result: All three active policies reduced mean cumulative disparity by 0.04-0.88 percentage points per window. Under subgroup-specific drift, smaller TPR gaps correlated with lower sensitivity in both groups. Population evaluation preserved mean directions in 69-92% of trajectories. Person weighting reversed race FPR comparisons without changing predictions/actions, with all weighted intervals including zero.

Conclusion: Policy comparisons for retraining require group-specific rates, action distributions, and explicit evaluation populations. Under subgroup-specific drift, reducing TPR gaps may require sacrificing overall sensitivity. The study highlights the importance of careful population specification and weighting in fairness evaluations, and notes these analyses are non-confirmatory.

Abstract: Choosing when to retrain a deployed classifier requires assessing subgroup error rates across the sequence of models used, including periods between updates. We compare complete scheduled, loss-triggered, and subgroup-gap-triggered policies with retaining the initial model on the same observations and delayed labels. For true-positive and false-positive rates separately, the outcome is the paired difference in absolute subgroup gaps summed over deployment windows. Population evaluation in simulation, action records, and alternative schedules assess how measurement and retraining behaviour affect these comparisons. In a follow-up sample of 400 new trajectories per condition across two simulated drift regimes, all three policies had lower mean cumulative disparity, equivalent to reductions of 0.04 to 0.88 percentage points in the average gap per window. Evaluating the unchanged models against the known generating distributions preserved all mean directions, but finite-window and population comparisons agreed on whether updating increased, reduced or left cumulative disparity unchanged in 69 to 92 percent of trajectories. Under subgroup-specific drift, smaller true-positive-rate gaps accompanied lower sensitivity in both groups. In an exploratory American Community Survey replay, person weighting reversed all three race false-positive-rate mean comparisons without changing predictions or actions; all three weighted intervals included zero. Policy comparisons require group-specific rates, action distributions, and an explicit evaluation population alongside mean disparity. These analyses are non-confirmatory. Shared replay requires policy-independent observations and complete labels after the specified delay.

</details>


### [192] [Privacy-Preserving Split Learning for Federated LLM Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09794)
*Heng Jin,Chaoyu Zhang,Hexuan Yu,Wenjing Lou,Y. Thomas Hou*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses privacy leakage in split-based federated LLM fine-tuning through a learned obfuscate-and-recover scheme. The approach protects participants' private datasets while enabling independently deployable model training on the server side.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The autoregressive nature of LLMs causes intermediate activations transmitted during split learning to leak input information, creating an inherent privacy paradox. Existing perturbation-based defenses are fundamentally ineffective in this setting.

Method: The authors propose a learned obfuscate-and-recover scheme that protects participants' private datasets while still allowing independently deployable model training on the server side.

Result: The approach achieves strong privacy protection with modest utility loss and system overhead, making split-based federated LLM fine-tuning practically viable.

Conclusion: The proposed method successfully balances privacy preservation and model utility, enabling practical deployment of split-based federated LLM fine-tuning across multiple institutions with privacy requirements.

Abstract: Fine-tuning large language models (LLMs) on domain-specific data is essential for downstream adaptation. In many deployments, a participant cannot hold the complete model locally. This happens because the model owner keeps the full model proprietary, or because the participant lacks sufficient compute resources. Split Learning (SL) addresses this by partitioning the model between the participant and a server so that only a small portion runs locally. When the underlying data is additionally distributed across multiple institutions with privacy requirements, Federated Learning (FL) further enables collaborative training across participants by sharing only model updates instead of raw data. In this combined setting, each client transmits intermediate activations to the server, and for LLM fine-tuning, this exchange poses an inherent privacy paradox. The autoregressive nature of LLMs causes the transmitted activations to leak the input, and existing perturbation-based defenses are fundamentally ineffective in this setting. We address this leakage through a learned obfuscate-and-recover scheme that protects participants' private datasets while still allowing an independently deployable model to be trained on the server side. Experiments demonstrate that our approach achieves strong privacy protection with modest utility loss and system overhead, making split-based federated LLM fine-tuning practically viable.

</details>


### [193] [A practical DIRECT-type algorithm for medium-scale black-box global optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09796)
*Linas Stripinis,Remigijus Paulavičius*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: X-DTC-GL is a novel DIRECT-type global optimization algorithm that uses dynamic partitioning with surrogate models and hill-climbing hybridization to overcome traditional limitations of slow convergence and poor performance in high-dimensional spaces, achieving ~12% better solvability and ~27% better solution quality than existing DIRECT-type methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional DIRECT algorithms, while versatile for global optimization, suffer from two critical limitations: (1) effectiveness degrades significantly as problem dimensionality increases; (2) slow convergence in high-dimensional spaces, limiting applicability to complex optimization tasks.

Method: X-DICT-GL incorporates two key techniques: (1) Dynamic partitioning - adaptively refines the search space using local one-dimensional surrogate models, enabling rapid subdivision of promising hyper-rectangles; (2) Hybridization - selectively employs hill-climbing to exploit promising regions identified by the surrogate models.

Result: X-DTC-GL significantly outperforms existing DIRECT-type baselines: ~12% improvement in solvability and ~27% improvement in solution quality across four benchmark suites. Performance-profile analyses show: fastest convergence on ~40% of instances, best runtime performance on ~17% of problems, with competitive overall execution times.

Conclusion: These advances strengthen X-DTC-GL's competitiveness in state-of-the-art black-box optimization by improving performance within the partition-based framework.

Abstract: The DIRECT algorithm is a deterministic global optimization method known for its versatility and balanced exploration-exploitation strategy. However, DIRECT-type algorithms are primarily effective for low-dimensional problems and often exhibit slow convergence as dimensionality increases, limiting their applicability to more complex optimization tasks. To address this limitation, this paper introduces X-DTC-GL, a novel DIRECT-type algorithm that incorporates dynamic partitioning and hybridization techniques. The dynamic partitioning approach adaptively refines the search space based on local one-dimensional surrogate models, enabling rapid subdivision of promising hyper-rectangles. The hybridization strategy selectively employs a hill-climbing method to exploit promising regions identified by the surrogate models. Extensive experiments on four diverse benchmark suites demonstrate that X-DTC-GL significantly outperforms existing DIRECT-type baselines, achieving improvements of ~12% in solvability and ~27% in solution quality. Performance-profile analyses indicate the fastest convergence on up to ~40% of instances, the best runtime performance on ~17% of problems, and competitive overall execution times. By improving performance within the partition-based framework, these advances strengthen the algorithm's competitiveness in state-of-the-art black-box optimization.

</details>


### [194] [Online Inverse Integer Linear Optimization via Small-Gradient Skipping: Constant Regret and Finite Mistakes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09809)
*Akira Kitaoka*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes Small-Gradient Skipping (SGS), a mechanism for online inverse linear optimization that skips updates when there's no mistake. Applied to online gradient descent, online Newton step, and MetaGrad, it removes the log T factor from regret bounds in certain settings and achieves O(d²) dimension dependence for integer linear programs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The gap between the regret upper bound O(d log T) and lower bound Ω(d) in online inverse linear optimization was unresolved. Additionally, existing methods for M-convex action sets required computing a center of gravity at every round, which is computationally expensive.

Method: The paper proposes Small-Gradient Skipping (SGS), which skips updates at rounds without a mistake when the correct action is uniformly separated from other candidates. This mechanism is applied to three algorithms: online gradient descent, online Newton step, and MetaGrad.

Result: The number of mistakes is bounded by a quantity independent of T for all three algorithms. For the online Newton step and MetaGrad with SGS, when the forward problem is an integer linear program, the dimension dependence becomes O(d²), removing the log T factor. Furthermore, when the action set is M-convex, regret is bounded efficiently without computing a center of gravity.

Conclusion: SGS successfully removes the log T factor from regret bounds in online inverse linear optimization, achieving O(d²) dimension dependence for integer linear programs, while avoiding computationally expensive center of gravity calculations for M-convex action sets.

Abstract: In online inverse linear optimization, the learner predicts a weight at each round, observes the optimal action of the agent, and updates its prediction. In the general setting, the gap of $\log T$ between the regret upper bound $O(d \log T)$ and the lower bound $Ω(d)$ is unresolved (here $T$ is the total number of rounds and $d$ is the dimension). When the action set is M-convex, the regret is known to be bounded by $O(d \log d)$, but the method attaining it computes a center of gravity at every round. This paper therefore proposes Small-Gradient Skipping (SGS), a mechanism that skips the update at rounds without a mistake in the case where the correct action is uniformly separated from the other candidates, and applies it to online gradient descent, the online Newton step, and MetaGrad. The number of mistakes is then bounded, for all three, by a quantity independent of $T$; and for the online Newton step and for MetaGrad with SGS, the dimension dependence of the regret becomes $O(d^2)$ when the forward problem is an integer linear program, that is, the factor $\log T$ is removed. Moreover, when the action set is M-convex, the regret is bounded efficiently without computing a center of gravity.

</details>


### [195] [In Medical Claims Data, Enhancing Predictive Performance for Major Adverse Cardiovascular Events Using Cross Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09824)
*Yuhei Fujioka,Daitaro Misawa,Tatsuyoshi Ikenoue,Shingo Fukuma*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This study proposes a cross-attention-based model to predict major adverse cardiovascular events (MACE) using medical claims data. The method effectively captures relationships between diagnoses and treatments to generate more representative features, achieving a ROC-AUC of 0.7720.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Medical claims data contain valuable clinical information but are often unstructured, limiting their use in comprehensive analyses. MACE is a leading cause of death worldwide, and improving its predictive models is crucial for clinical practice.

Method: The study employs a cross-attention mechanism to develop a model that effectively weights the relationships between diagnoses and treatments in medical claims data, generating more representative features for MACE prediction.

Result: The proposed cross-attention-based model achieved a ROC-AUC score of 0.7720, outperforming benchmark models including the conventional atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease model, light gradient boosting machine (LightGBM), and a self-attention-based model.

Conclusion: Integrating the clinical structure of medical claims data using a cross-attention mechanism significantly enhances the performance of predictive models for MACE.

Abstract: Medical claims data comprise the financial details, including the expenses and billing information, as well as the clinical information, such as the diagnoses and treatments, of patients visiting medical facilities. Recently, it has been acknowledged that large databases can be constructed from medical claims data for medical research purposes. However, the clinical information within these datasets is often medically unstructured, limiting its application in comprehensive analyses. This study enhances predictive model performance for major adverse cardiovascular events (MACE), a leading cause of death worldwide. Models that predict MACE are crucial to clinical practice guidelines. We utilize a cross-attention mechanism to develop a method that effectively weights the relationships between diagnoses and treatments. Effectively repre- senting the clinical information contained in medical claims data, this approach generates more representative features for predicting MACE. The ROC-AUC score of our proposed cross-attention-based model was 0.7720, higher than other benchmark models including the conventional atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease model, the light gradient boosting machine, and a self-attention-based model. These results indicate that integrating the clinical structure of medical claims data using a cross-attention mechanism significantly enhances the performance of predictive models.

</details>


### [196] [TempTPI: Informer-Based trajectory prediction for maritime vessels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09840)
*Kevin Ferneding,Veronika Lietavcova,Aleksandra M. Blachowiak,Peder Heiselberg*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TempTPI is a novel maritime vessel trajectory prediction framework that combines an Informer-based encoder with multi-channel temporal encoding to achieve accurate long-term predictions (1-5 hours) using AIS data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing deep learning models for maritime trajectory prediction using AIS data suffer from two key limitations: (1) quadratic computational complexity of self-attention mechanisms, and (2) accuracy degradation over extended forecasting horizons. These challenges hinder long-range maritime situational awareness needed for safety and logistical efficiency.

Method: TempTPI integrates an Informer-based encoder with a ProbSparse self-attention mechanism to reduce computational overhead and focus on significant dependencies. It also incorporates a multi-channel temporal encoding mechanism using Fourier-like frequency expansions to capture cyclic patterns (hourly, daily, seasonal) in vessel behavior. The model is evaluated against the state-of-the-art TPTrans architecture using AIS data from Danish waters.

Result: TempTPI consistently outperforms TPTrans across all prediction windows (1-5 hours). At the 5-hour horizon, TempTPI achieves a 55% improvement in Mean Squared Error (MSE) compared to the baseline.

Conclusion: TempTPI offers a robust solution for long-range maritime situational awareness, effectively addressing the computational complexity and accuracy challenges of existing Transformer-based approaches for AIS-based trajectory prediction.

Abstract: Accurate long-term trajectory prediction for maritime vessels is essential for safety and logistical efficiency. While deep learning models, particularly Transformers, have shown promise in processing Automatic Identification System (AIS) data, they often struggle with the quadratic computational complexity of self-attention and the loss of accuracy over extended forecasting horizons. This study proposes TempTPI, a novel prediction framework that integrates an Informer-based encoder with a multi-channel temporal encoding mechanism. The Informer architecture leverages a ProbSparse self-attention mechanism to reduce computational overhead and focus on the most significant dependencies, while the temporal encoder utilizes Fourier-like frequency expansions to capture cyclic patterns (hourly, daily, and seasonal) in vessel behavior. We evaluate our model against the state-of-the-art TPTrans architecture using AIS data from Danish waters. Experimental results demonstrate that TempTPI consistently outperforms existing methods across prediction windows of 1 to 5 hours. Notably, at a 5-hour horizon, the proposed model achieves a 55% improvement in Mean Squared Error (MSE), offering a robust solution for long-range maritime situational awareness.

</details>


### [197] [Exact Degeneracy Under Balanced k-Shot Sampling:Consequences for Small-Sample Discriminant Analysis on LLM Embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09860)
*Lingxiao Qu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper reveals a theoretical degeneracy in Kernelized Linear Principal Component Discriminant Analysis (KLPCDA) induced by balanced k-shot sampling, where within-class scatter becomes exactly a scaled orthogonal projector. The authors derive closed-form consequences for this degeneracy, propose a repair mechanism, and evaluate the framework on few-shot text classification with LLM embeddings, finding that simple logistic regression outperforms all KLPCDA variants.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors investigate a fundamental issue with balanced k-shot sampling (drawing exactly k labeled examples per class) in small-sample discriminant estimators, specifically KLPCDA. This sampling strategy is commonly used in few-shot learning but causes a provable degeneracy in discriminant analysis methods.

Method: The authors mathematically prove that balanced k-shot sampling causes the within-class scatter operator of KLPCDA to become a scaled orthogonal projector. They derive closed-form consequences for all seven KLPCDA variants, identify which are affected, and propose an in-formula tie-break repair. They validate findings on frozen sentence embeddings and residual-stream activations from a decoder-only model. They then evaluate the repaired framework on few-shot text classification across four datasets, three embedding sizes, and compare against SetFit, LoRA, and in-context learning baselines using cross-validated logistic regression probes.

Result: Two KLPCDA variants have every signal eigenvalue exactly equal (indifferent selection criterion), a third has a void objective, and the repair mechanism shows class-count-dependent recovery (5x cost difference between few-class and many-class datasets, p=0.000001). On few-shot text classification, logistic regression probe beats all KLPCDA variants on 3/4 datasets at every embedding size. The gap between high-dimensional decoder embeddings and smaller bidirectional encoders is largely an estimation-efficiency effect, closing by >80% when support set grows from k≤10 to k=30-50 (p=0.00195).

Conclusion: Balanced k-shot sampling induces exact theoretical degeneracy in discriminant estimators, and this guidance from other domains (pixel, vibration-signal, gene-expression data) does not directly generalize to LLM embedding feature spaces. Simple logistic regression remains superior for few-shot text classification on LLM embeddings, and the performance gap is substantially due to estimation efficiency rather than permanent anisotropy.

Abstract: Balanced k-shot sampling draws exactly k labeled examples per class. We show that it induces an exact, provable degeneracy in a family of small-sample discriminant estimators. Under balanced sampling, the within-class scatter operator of Kernelized Linear Principal Component Discriminant Analysis (KLPCDA) is not merely rank-deficient but exactly a scaled orthogonal projector. We derive the consequences in closed form: two of KLPCDA's seven variants have every signal eigenvalue exactly equal, so their eigenvector selection criterion is provably indifferent rather than ill-conditioned, and a third has a provably void objective. This follows from the estimators' construction, not any dataset; we confirm it on frozen sentence embeddings and, separately, on residual-stream activations from a decoder-only generative model. An in-formula tie-break repairs the two repairable variants, with recovery gated by class count: the residual subspace constraint costs 5x more on few-class than many-class datasets (p=0.000001).
  We then evaluate the repaired framework on few-shot text classification on frozen LLM embeddings (n much smaller than d, up to 4096), across four datasets, three embedding sizes, and three trained baselines (SetFit, LoRA, in-context learning). A properly cross-validated logistic-regression probe still beats every KLPCDA variant on three of four datasets, at every embedding size; guidance carried from pixel, vibration-signal, and gene-expression data does not directly generalize to this feature space. Three independent geometric separability metrics fail to explain why one high-dimensional decoder-based embedding model underperforms smaller bidirectional encoders, ruling out anisotropy; the gap is substantially an estimation-efficiency effect, not a permanent ceiling, closing by more than 80% when the support set grows from k<=10 to k=30-50 (p=0.00195, both many-class datasets).

</details>


### [198] [Forward-Free LLM Depth Pruning via Weight Redundancy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09883)
*Vincent-Daniel Yun,Woosang Lim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Weight-Redundancy Pruning (WRP) is a forward-free depth pruning method that estimates inter-layer redundancy from model checkpoint weights to identify and remove redundant Transformer blocks without requiring calibration data or forward passes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing depth pruning methods either require expensive forward passes on calibration data (activation-based) or score blocks separately without measuring inter-layer similarity (forward-free methods). A forward-free approach that can capture inter-layer redundancy without calibration data or model execution is needed.

Method: WRP compares attention output and MLP down-projection weights across layers, combines their pairwise similarities with relative projection-scale information to generate an all-pairs similarity matrix. This matrix guides layer grouping and block selection for pruning.

Result: WRP consistently outperforms existing forward-free magnitude pruning across multiple pruning settings, model families, and downstream tasks, while approaching the performance of activation-based methods.

Conclusion: Weight-based similarity metrics from checkpoint weights can effectively capture inter-layer redundancy, enabling efficient depth pruning without requiring model execution or calibration data, achieving competitive performance compared to activation-based approaches.

Abstract: Depth pruning reduces large language model (LLM) inference cost by removing complete Transformer blocks. Activation-based methods collect hidden states through forward passes on calibration data, while existing forward-free methods score each Transformer block separately without measuring similarity between blocks. We propose Weight-Redundancy Pruning (WRP), a forward-free depth-pruning method that estimates inter-layer redundancy from checkpoint weights to select blocks without calibration data or model forward passes. WRP compares attention output and MLP down-projection weights across layers and combines their pairwise similarities with relative projection-scale information. The resulting all-pairs similarity matrix guides layer grouping and block selection. Across multiple pruning settings, model families, and downstream tasks, WRP consistently outperforms existing forward-free magnitude pruning and approaches the performance of activation-based methods.

</details>


### [199] [ProMeta: Few-shot PROTAC-targeted degradation prediction across E3 ligases](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09891)
*Yuansheng Liu,Yufei Ye,Tao Tang,Jiawei Luo,Wen Tao,Xiao Luo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ProMeta is a few-shot meta-learning framework for predicting PROTAC degradation activity across different E3 ligases, addressing data scarcity and imbalance issues in traditional supervised approaches. It uses prototype-based graph neural networks with episodic training and support-conditioned inference, achieving AUROC of 0.796-0.883 on CRBN-to-VHL and VHL-to-CRBN transfer tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing supervised methods for PROTAC degradation activity prediction struggle with data scarcity and severe class imbalance across E3 ligases. Most labeled data concentrates on a few ligases (CRBN, VHL) while the majority remain underexplored. There is a need for methods that can generalize across ligases with minimal labeled data.

Method: The authors reformulate PROTAC degradation prediction as a few-shot meta-learning problem. ProMeta uses a prototype-based graph neural network trained through episodic meta-learning on source-E3 tasks. Inference is performed without updating the encoder by dynamically estimating class prototypes from minimal target-ligase support samples (K=2 support samples per class, Q=3 or 5 query samples).

Result: On CRBN-to-VHL benchmark: AUROC 0.796 (K=2, Q=3) and 0.883 (K=2, Q=5), improving 19.9% and 6.8% over supervised GNN baseline. Reverse VHL-to-CRBN transfer: AUROC 0.702 (K=2, Q=3) and 0.821 (K=2, Q=5). Results show bidirectional applicability with direction and data-regime dependence.

Conclusion: ProMeta successfully enables robust cross-ligase few-shot prediction for PROTAC degradation activity. The framework demonstrates practical utility for computational PROTAC discovery by generalizing to unseen E3 ligases with minimal labeled data, though performance varies depending on transfer direction and available support data.

Abstract: Proteolysis-targeting chimeras (PROTACs) have emerged as a transformative therapeutic strategy that selectively degrades historically ''undruggable'' targets via the ubiquitin-proteasome system. Despite growing efforts to develop computational predictors of PROTAC degradation activity, existing supervised approaches remain severely challenged by data scarcity and imbalance across E3 ligases, limiting their ability to generalize beyond well-studied ligase contexts. In practice, labeled data are heavily concentrated on a few ligases (e.g., CRBN and VHL), while the majority of E3 ligases remain underexplored yet are critical for expanding the design space of targeted degraders. Developing methods that enable robust cross-ligase generalization with minimal labeled data is therefore essential for improving the practical utility of computational PROTAC discovery. We reformulate PROTAC degradation activity prediction across E3 ligases as a few-shot meta-learning problem and present ProMeta, a prototype-based graph neural network trained through episodic meta-learning on source-E3 tasks and evaluated on held-out target-E3 tasks through support-conditioned inference. ProMeta performs inference without updating the encoder by dynamically estimating class prototypes from minimal target-ligase support samples. On the CRBN-to-VHL benchmark, ProMeta achieves AUROC values of 0.796 under K=2, Q=3 and 0.883 under K=2, Q=5, improving by 19.9% and 6.8%, respectively, over the corresponding supervised GNN baseline. Reverse VHL-to-CRBN transfer under the same protocol yielded AUROC values of 0.702 (K=2, Q=3) and 0.821 (K=2, Q=5), confirming bidirectional applicability while revealing direction and data-regime dependence. Together, these results support ProMeta as a practical framework for cross-ligase few-shot prediction under the evaluated support/query protocols.

</details>


### [200] [Strangers to Themselves: What Language Models Say About Themselves Is Generic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09899)
*Phil Blandfort,Urja Pawar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Language models cannot meaningfully predict their own behavioral tendencies; asking them what they would do primarily reveals general theories about AI assistants rather than self-knowledge, plus a favorable bias toward understating harmful behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To test whether language models' self-descriptions about their behavior (e.g., whether they would cave to pressure, misuse tools, or lie) actually reflect genuine self-knowledge or are just fluent but ungrounded predictions.

Method: Across nine behavioral evaluations, researchers measured how models behaved under different conditions, asked them to predict those rates, and compared predictions with controls that removed the self from the question (e.g., asking about "capable AI agents in general" instead).

Result: Direct self-report showed near-zero correlation (r = +0.04). Even showing exact items only raised it to +0.24. Surprisingly, the same item-informed question about "AI agents in general" performed equally well (+0.28), and other models' predictions about the target model were at least as accurate as its own. First-person framing reliably shifted reports in a flattering direction, understating harmful behavior. Finetuning on behavioral records could teach narrow self-predictions but also changed the behavior being predicted.

Conclusion: Asking a model what it would do mostly reveals a theory of how AI assistants behave in general, plus a favorable bias, rather than privileged self-knowledge. This has practical implications for using model self-reports as reliable indicators of actual behavior.

Abstract: Language models can fluently describe how they would behave: whether they would cave to pushback, misuse a tool, or lie under pressure. Is that description actually about the model speaking? We turn self-knowledge into a prediction test. Across nine behavioral evaluations, we measure how a model behaves under different conditions, ask it to predict those rates, and compare its predictions with controls that remove the self from the question. We find that: (i) Direct self-report is weak (r = +0.04), and even showing the model the exact items only raises prediction to +0.24. Crucially, the same item-informed question about "capable AI agents in general" does just as well (+0.28), while other models' answers about themselves predict the target model at least as well as its own. (ii) Frontier scale does not detectably change this pattern: any gains in prediction are not self-specific, and are consistent with a better theory of how AI assistants behave rather than better self-knowledge. (iii) First-person framing does have one robust effect: it shifts reports in the flattering direction, understating harmful behavior relative to the same question about a generic agent. (iv) Finetuning on a model's own behavioral record can teach narrow self-predictions, but it also changes the behavior being predicted and the gains do not transfer broadly. The practical implication is simple: asking a model what it would do mostly reveals a theory of AI assistants in general, plus a favorable bias, rather than privileged knowledge of that model.

</details>


### [201] [Beyond Conventional Federated Learning via High-Order Regularization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09904)
*Alireza Kabgani,Masoud Ahookhosh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HiFedProx replaces FedProx's quadratic regularization with a scale-matched power-type regularizer (p≥2) to better control client displacement magnitudes in federated learning. It combines this geometry with finite-budget stochastic optimization and Armijo backtracking, showing substantial gains under stress conditions with optimal exponent range p=5-7.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: FedProx's quadratic regularization grows linearly with displacement, offering limited control over the contrast between ordinary and unusually large client movements. There is a need for a regularizer that can more effectively compress relative displacement disparities.

Method: Replaces quadratic penalty with a scale-matched power-type regularizer indexed by p≥2. All powers have the same regularization-gradient magnitude at reference displacement R, while p>2 gives weaker response below R and stronger response above R. Combined with finite-budget stochastic client optimization and same-minibatch Armijo backtracking.

Result: On FEMNIST (60-writer subset): under moderate-stress, p=7 achieves lowest loss (11.44% improvement over p=2); under severe-stress, p=6 achieves lowest loss (23.16% improvement over p=2). While displacement-tail ratios continue decreasing through p=8, predictive performance peaks in intermediate range. Armijo trial cost increases with p.

Conclusion: The exponent p should be calibrated rather than maximized. In experiments, p=5-7 provides the most useful range, balancing predictive performance and computational cost. Very large powers approach fixed-radius behavior and increase local curvature.

Abstract: Federated clients that perform several local optimization steps can return parameter displacements with widely different magnitudes. The quadratic regularization of FedProx grows linearly with displacement and therefore offers limited control over the contrast between ordinary and unusually large client movements. We here introduce HiFedProx, which replaces the quadratic penalty with a scale-matched power-type regularizer indexed by $p\geq2$. All powers have the same regularization-gradient magnitude at a reference displacement $R$, while every $p>2$ gives a weaker response below $R$ and a stronger response above it. An exact affine reference calculation shows that increasing $p$ compresses relative displacement disparities, although very large powers approach fixed-radius behavior and increase local curvature. HiFedProx combines this geometry with finite-budget stochastic client optimization and same-minibatch Armijo backtracking. In paired five-seed experiments on a frozen 60-writer FEMNIST subset, a common-parameter study over $p\in\{2,3,4,5,6,7,8\}$ shows similar clean-training performance but substantial gains under composite stress. The lowest moderate- and severe-stress losses occur at $p=7$ and $p=6$, improving over $p=2$ by $11.44\%$ and $23.16\%$, respectively. Although displacement-tail ratios continue to decrease through $p=8$, predictive performance peaks in an intermediate range and Armijo trial cost increases with $p$. These results indicate that the exponent should be calibrated rather than maximized. In our experiments, $p=5$--$7$ provides the most useful range.

</details>


### [202] [Meta-LinEXP3: Online-within-Online Learning for Adversarial Linear Contextual Bandits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09907)
*Hao Li,Jie Xu,Zheng Xie*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper addresses meta-learning for adversarial linear contextual bandits (ALCBs) with random action sets, proposing Meta-LinEXP3 that constructs task-level priors to guide an inner LinEXP3 learner. It achieves O(√n) per-task regret for known context distributions and O(n^{2/3}) for unknown distributions, with theoretical analysis linking prior accuracy to transfer regret.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While meta-learning has been effective for stochastic bandits and non-contextual adversarial bandits, it remains largely unexplored for ALCBs with random action sets, which is a significant gap in the literature.

Method: The proposed Meta-LinEXP3 is an online-within-online algorithm that constructs predictable task-level priors from completed tasks to guide the inner LinEXP3 learner. Two estimators are developed: (1) a policy-centered estimator for known context distributions, and (2) a past-only regularized moment estimator for unknown distributions.

Result: For known context distributions, the policy-centered estimator achieves an intrinsic-dimension O(√n) per-task regret bound. For unknown distributions, the past-only regularized moment estimator achieves O(n^{2/3}) leading regret term with explicit finite-sample error. The paper establishes a direct connection showing that increasingly accurate priors yield sublinear transfer-dependent regret.

Conclusion: Meta-LinEXP3 effectively transfers knowledge across sequential bandit tasks, with theoretical guarantees demonstrating that better prior accuracy leads to improved transfer performance. Experiments validate the approach, including successful application to structured hyperspectral tensor sampling.

Abstract: Meta-learning has emerged as an effective paradigm for transferring knowledge across sequential bandit tasks. While substantial progress has been made for stochastic bandits and non-contextual adversarial bandits, meta-learning for adversarial linear contextual bandits (ALCBs) with random action sets remains largely unexplored. To address this problem, we propose Meta-LinEXP3, an online-within-online algorithm that constructs a predictable task-level prior from completed tasks to guide the inner LinEXP3 learner. For known context distributions, we develop a policy-centered estimator that achieves an intrinsic-dimension $\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{n})$ per-task regret bound. For unknown distributions, we introduce a past-only regularized moment estimator with an $\mathcal{O}(n^{2/3})$ leading regret term and explicit finite-sample error. We further establish a direct connection between prior accuracy and transfer regret, showing that increasingly accurate priors yield sublinear transfer-dependent regret across tasks. Experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of Meta-LinEXP3, including its application to structured hyperspectral tensor sampling.

</details>


### [203] [A Kernel-Based Modular Discriminant Analysis Framework for Small-Sample Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09910)
*Lingxiao Qu,Yan Pei*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper presents a systematic cross-domain study of KLPCDA (Kernelized Linear Principal Component Discriminant Analysis), analyzing seven variants across multiple small-sample-size (SSS) tasks including hyperspectral image classification, mechanical fault diagnosis, medical diagnosis, and face recognition. The study investigates how different objective combinations (variance, between-class, within-class terms) influence performance under conditions like noise, class imbalance, and high dimensionality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The small-sample-size (SSS) problem is a fundamental challenge in machine learning when labeled data are scarce due to cost, accessibility, or ethical constraints. Existing methods struggle to maintain stable and discriminative representations under high-dimensional and limited-data conditions. Although KLPCDA was proposed as a modular framework integrating variance preservation, inter-class separability, and intra-class compactness, a systematic understanding of how its components interact across diverse SSS scenarios was lacking.

Method: The researchers conducted a systematic cross-domain study of KLPCDA, analyzing seven variants across multiple real-world SSS tasks including hyperspectral image classification, mechanical fault diagnosis, medical diagnosis, and face recognition. They performed extensive experiments and ablation studies to investigate how different objective combinations influence performance under varying conditions such as noise, class imbalance, and high dimensionality.

Result: The analysis revealed consistent patterns in the interaction of the three core objectives (variance, between-class, and within-class terms), providing a unified and interpretable understanding of their roles in stabilizing representations and enhancing discrimination in SSS settings. KLPCDA achieved strong and robust performance across domains while maintaining low computational complexity suitable for resource-constrained environments.

Conclusion: The study provides practical guidelines for selecting appropriate KLPCDA variants under different data characteristics. The findings offer an interpretable understanding of how the core objectives interact to stabilize representations and enhance discrimination in small-sample-size scenarios, enabling practitioners to make informed choices based on their specific data characteristics and constraints.

Abstract: The small-sample-size (SSS) problem remains a fundamental challenge in machine learning when labeled data are scarce due to cost, accessibility, or ethical constraints. While numerous approaches have been proposed, existing methods often struggle to maintain stable and discriminative representations under high-dimensional and limited-data conditions. Kernelized Linear Principal Component Discriminant Analysis (KLPCDA), a recently proposed modular framework, integrates variance preservation, inter-class separability, and intra-class compactness within a unified kernel space. Although its formulation has shown promising initial results, a systematic understanding of how its components interact across diverse SSS scenarios remains lacking. In this paper, we present a systematic cross-domain study of KLPCDA to characterize the interaction mechanisms among its core objectives. We analyze the behavior of its seven variants across multiple real-world SSS tasks, including hyperspectral image classification, mechanical fault diagnosis, medical diagnosis, and face recognition. Through extensive experiments and ablation studies, we investigate how different objective combinations influence performance under varying conditions such as noise, class imbalance, and high dimensionality. Our analysis reveals consistent patterns in the interaction of the three core objectives variance, between-class, and within-class terms, providing a unified and interpretable understanding of their roles in stabilizing representations and enhancing discrimination in SSS settings. Based on these findings, we further derive practical guidelines for selecting appropriate KLPCDA variants under different data characteristics. Experimental results demonstrate that KLPCDA achieves strong and robust performance across domains, while maintaining low computational complexity suitable for resource-constrained environments.

</details>


### [204] [Development and Validation of a Physics-Guided Machine Learning Extrapolation Framework Using a Classical Transient Diffusion Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09912)
*Ashutosh Yadav,Alok Dubey,Prodyut Ranjan Chakraborty,Harshal Akolekar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper presents a novel extrapolation framework integrated with physics-guided machine learning architectures (BiLSTM and PINN) to enable accurate predictions beyond training data ranges in engineering applications. The method is validated on a 1D transient diffusion problem using exact analytical solutions for rigorous testing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Machine learning models in engineering are trained within limited operating ranges, but reliable predictions are often needed beyond these domains. The primary challenge is extrapolation rather than interpolation, and rigorous validation is hindered by scarce data outside the training range.

Method: The framework integrates physics-guided architectures (BiLSTM and PINN) with coordinate transformations, boundary-aware learning strategies, and stability-enhancing temporal marching. A train-predict-validate-extend strategy is used, where validated predictions are recursively added to training to extend prediction horizon. The benchmark is a 1D transient diffusion problem with exact analytical solution.

Result: The results demonstrate accurate and physically consistent predictions beyond the training domain. The method successfully handles the challenging scenario of evolving from an initial singularity through a strongly nonlinear transient regime to steady-state linear profile, with backward extrapolation toward the singularity being particularly demanding.

Conclusion: The framework shows potential for engineering applications where data availability is limited, enabling reliable extrapolation beyond training domains while maintaining physical consistency.

Abstract: Machine learning models used in engineering are typically trained within limited operating ranges, yet reliable predictions are often required beyond these domains. Consequently, the primary challenge is extrapolation rather than interpolation. Rigorous validation is hindered by the scarcity of data outside the training range. To address this limitation, a novel extrapolation framework is integrated with established machine learning architectures to enable accurate and physically consistent predictions beyond the training domain. The framework is established by systematically evaluating two physics-guided architectures: a Bidirectional Long Short-Term Memory (BiLSTM) network and a Physics-Informed Neural Network (PINN). A classical one-dimensional transient diffusion problem is adopted as a benchmark because its exact analytical solution provides unlimited, reliable data across the spatio-temporal domain, enabling rigorous quantitative validation. The problem is particularly challenging because the solution evolves from an initial singularity through a strongly nonlinear transient regime before approaching a steady-state linear profile. When training data are confined to an intermediate portion of this evolution, backward extrapolation toward the singularity becomes especially demanding. To improve reliability, physics-guided coordinate transformations, boundary-aware learning strategies, and stability-enhancing temporal marching are incorporated. Extrapolation is evaluated using a train-predict-validate-extend strategy, in which validated predictions are recursively added to the training set to progressively extend the prediction horizon. The results demonstrate accurate and physically consistent predictions beyond the training domain, highlighting the framework's potential for engineering applications where data availability is limited.

</details>


### [205] [Multi-Pass, Multi-View Blended Learning for High-Fidelity Volumetric CT Synthesis from Chest X-Rays](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09920)
*Ozer Can Devecioglu,Serkan Kiranyaz,Rashid Mazhar,Tahir Hamid,Muhammad Chowdhury,Moncef Gabbouj*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A deep learning framework that reconstructs 3D CT volumes from single 2D chest X-rays using a multi-stage blended learning approach, addressing the domain gap between synthetic DRRs and real CXRs through progressive domain adaptation and multi-view refinement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reconstructing volumetric CT from single 2D chest radiographs is clinically valuable but challenging due to: (1) the ill-posed nature of the inverse problem, (2) scarcity of paired CXR-CT training data, and (3) the domain gap between synthetic DRRs derived from CT and real clinical CXRs, which limits generalization.

Method: Multi-Pass Multi-View Blended Learning framework with two stages: Stage 1 performs unsupervised CXR-to-DRR domain adaptation to bridge the synthetic-to-real gap. Stage 2 comprises three progressive passes: (a) supervised DRR-to-CT transformation, (b) unsupervised multi-view slice refinement for anatomical consistency, and (c) progressive transfer learning to enhance anatomical details.

Result: On LIDC-IDRI dataset, the method achieves up to 14% improvement in PSNR and 7.6% improvement in SSIM compared to prior approaches. The framework generates structurally consistent and anatomically realistic high-fidelity CT volumes from real CXRs.

Conclusion: The proposed blended learning paradigm successfully mitigates the synthetic-to-real domain gap while enhancing structural integrity and anatomical detail. This represents a significant advancement toward clinical viability of CT reconstruction from standard radiographic images.

Abstract: Reconstructing volumetric Computed Tomography (CT) from a single 2D chest radiograph (CXR) is an ill-posed inverse problem, further complicated by the scarcity of paired CXR-CT training data. Prior approaches address this by training on Digitally Reconstructed Radiographs (DRRs), which are synthetic projections derived from CT volumes. However, the domain gap between DRRs and real CXRs limits generalization, often resulting in coarse or anatomically inconsistent reconstructions when applied to clinical images. To address this challenging problem, this study introduces a Multi-Pass Multi-View Blended Learning framework for synthesizing high-fidelity volumetric CT directly from real chest X-ray (CXR) images. The proposed approach progressively decomposes the synthesis task into two distinct, complementary learning stages. Stage 1 is an unsupervised CXR-to-DRR Domain Adaptation, while Stage 2 includes three passes, namely, (a) supervised DRR-to-CT Transformation, (b) unsupervised Multi-View Slice Refinement, followed by (c) Progressive Transfer Learning (PTL). With such a blended learning paradigm, the proposed approach mitigates the synthetic-to-real domain gap while enhancing both the structural integrity and anatomical detail of the final output. On the LIDC-IDRI dataset, where paired DRR-CT ground truth is available for quantitative evaluation, the proposed method improves upon prior methods by up to 14% in PSNR and 7.6% in SSIM. The framework successfully generates structurally consistent and anatomically realistic high-fidelity CT volumes from real CXRs, marking a significant advancement toward clinical viability of CT reconstruction from standard radiographic images.

</details>


### [206] [Adversarial Training for Tabular Credit Scoring: A Multi-Attack Robustness Evaluation in P2P Lending](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09945)
*Gijs A. F. Niewzwaag,Marijn G. S. Veth,Manuele Massei,Marcos R. Machado*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper evaluates adversarial robustness of machine learning credit scoring models in P2P lending against multiple attack types (FGSM, PGD, Salt-and-Pepper, DeepFool) on Lending Club data. It finds that adversarial training is attack-specific, gradient-based attacks transfer well among themselves, but single-attack defenses fail against heterogeneous attacks. Mixed training provides the most balanced robustness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Machine learning-based credit scoring in P2P lending is vulnerable to adversarial manipulation where applicants strategically alter self-reported inputs. However, existing adversarial robustness research focuses primarily on image/text domains and evaluates single attacks against matching defenses, providing limited guidance on cross-attack generalization in tabular credit data.

Method: Systematic train-test robustness benchmark on a large Lending Club subset using three model families (logistic regression, feed-forward neural network, and transformer for tabular data) and four attacks confined to applicant-mutable features: FGSM, PGD, Salt-and-Pepper noise, and DeepFool, plus a mixed-attack regime. Full grid evaluation with stratified cross-validation.

Result: Adversarial training sharply improves robustness against the attack it is trained on and transfers well within the gradient-based family (FGSM, PGD, DeepFool), but transfers weakly to non-gradient corruption (Salt-and-Pepper). Single-attack defenses overstate real-world resilience. Mixed training delivers the most balanced robustness across heterogeneous attacks while preserving clean-test performance.

Conclusion: Single-attack adversarial training is insufficient for real-world credit scoring deployments. Mixed training (training against multiple attack types) is recommended for credit model governance as it provides the most balanced robustness across heterogeneous attacks while maintaining clean-test performance. Multi-attack stress testing should be adopted in credit model governance.

Abstract: Machine learning-based credit scoring is increasingly central to Peer-to-Peer (P2P) lending, yet its resilience to adversarial manipulation, where applicants strategically alter self-reported inputs to secure favourable decisions, remains poorly understood. Most adversarial-robustness evidence comes from image and text domains and evaluates a single attack against a matching defence, offering little guidance on how defences generalise across attack types in tabular credit data. We address this with a systematic train-test robustness benchmark on a large Lending Club subset, spanning three model families (logistic regression, a feed-forward neural network, and a transformer for tabular data) and four attacks confined to applicant-mutable features: Fast Gradient Sign Method (FGSM), Projected Gradient Descent (PGD), Salt-and-Pepper (S&P) noise, and DeepFool, plus a mixed-attack regime. Across a full grid evaluated with stratified cross-validation, adversarial training sharply improves robustness against the attack it is trained on and transfers well within the gradient-based family, but transfers weakly to non-gradient corruption, so single-attack defences overstate real-world resilience. Mixed training delivers the most balanced robustness across heterogeneous attacks while preserving clean-test performance, supporting multi-attack stress testing in credit-model governance.

</details>


### [207] [Deep Neural Networks for Learning Intent from sEMG Signals to Support Hardware Devices for Post-Stroke Neurorehabilitation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09971)
*Zakariyya Brewster,Divy Wadhwani,Emily Yan,Aidan Wang,Karma Namgyal,Shuting Xie,Markiyan Konyk,Tala Abdelmaguid*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper presents a machine learning approach to decode five-finger motor intent from high-density surface electromyography (sEMG) in post-stroke patients. The study compares LSTM, CNN, and GNN architectures, identifies optimal models through architecture search, and develops a compact four-channel model for embedded hardware using knowledge distillation, achieving 0.5219 subset accuracy and 0.7612 finger accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Finger-specific motor intent is a clinically meaningful control signal for post-stroke neurorehabilitation, as residual muscle activity may remain measurable even when movements are weak or incomplete. Current methods need to work with limited channels to enable practical hardware implementation.

Method: The study uses PhysioMio, a bilateral longitudinal dataset from stroke patients. Processing includes: movement label alignment, 20-450 Hz Butterworth filtering, Symlet-4 wavelet denoising, 200 ms overlapping window segmentation, and extraction of twelve time- and frequency-domain descriptors per channel. Direct LSTM, CNN, and GNN baselines were compared, followed by architecture search. For hardware compatibility, knowledge distillation was applied to train a compact four-channel model (using ECRB, ECRL, FDS, FDP channels) matching a four-sensor design.

Result: LSTM achieved highest subset accuracy (0.545), while GNN achieved highest macro F1 (0.706) and macro AUPRC (0.776). CNN-Large was identified as the strongest single-split CNN with 0.593 subset accuracy and 0.714 macro F1. The final distilled four-channel model achieved 0.5219 ± 0.0114 subset accuracy, 0.7612 ± 0.0038 finger accuracy, and 0.6095 ± 0.0058 macro F1 across five seeds. The 123K-parameter model accepts nine windows of 48 features and has been exported to ONNX.

Conclusion: The study establishes a reproducible software path from post-stroke sEMG to compact five-finger intent prediction, with a hardware-ready model suitable for subsequent hardware-in-the-loop evaluation. The approach demonstrates complementary behavior between different neural network architectures and successfully transfers knowledge to a compact model for embedded inference.

Abstract: Finger-specific motor intent is a clinically meaningful control signal for post-stroke neurorehabilitation, where residual muscle activity may remain measurable despite weak or incomplete movement. We study five-finger multilabel intent decoding from impaired-arm high-density surface electromyography (sEMG) in PhysioMio, a bilateral longitudinal dataset collected from stroke patients. A common processing protocol aligns movement labels, applies 20--450 Hz Butterworth filtering and Symlet-4 wavelet denoising, segments overlapping 200 ms windows, and extracts twelve time- and frequency-domain descriptors per channel. Direct LSTM, CNN, and GNN baselines reveal complementary behavior: the LSTM attains the highest subset accuracy (0.545), whereas the GNN attains the highest macro F1 (0.706) and macro AUPRC (0.776). Architecture search then identifies CNN-Large as the strongest single-split CNN, with 0.593 subset accuracy and 0.714 macro F1, while CNN-Micro provides a compact architecture for embedded inference. To match a four-sensor hardware design, we retrain CNN-Micro using channels associated with ECRB, ECRL, FDS, and FDP and exclude the ground electrode from model input. Across five seeds, cross-channel knowledge distillation improves the four-channel student over direct training, reaching $0.5219 \pm 0.0114$ subset accuracy, $0.7612 \pm 0.0038$ finger accuracy, and $0.6095 \pm 0.0058$ macro F1. The selected 123K-parameter model accepts nine windows of 48 features and has been exported to ONNX. These results establish a reproducible software path from post-stroke sEMG to compact five-finger intent prediction for subsequent hardware-in-the-loop evaluation.

</details>


### [208] [An Explainable Machine Learning Framework for Predicting Blood-Brain Barrier Permeability Using Molecular Descriptors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10012)
*Fatemeh Mahmoudi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A machine learning framework was developed to predict blood-brain barrier (BBB) permeability using molecular descriptors. XGBoost achieved the best performance (88.97% accuracy, 0.9282 ROC-AUC) with TPSA, HBD, and LogP identified as the most important features through SHAP analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Blood-brain barrier (BBB) permeability is a critical factor in central nervous system drug development, as it determines whether drug candidates can reach their target sites in the brain. Traditional experimental methods for assessing BBB permeability are time-consuming and expensive, necessitating computational approaches for early-stage screening.

Method: The study used 15 physicochemical descriptors extracted from 2,039 compounds in the MoleculeNet BBBP dataset using RDKit. Four supervised machine learning algorithms were trained: Logistic Regression, SVM, Random Forest, and XGBoost. Hyperparameter optimization was performed using GridSearchCV, and model interpretability was investigated using SHAP analysis. Stratified five-fold cross-validation was used for validation.

Result: The optimized XGBoost classifier achieved the best performance with accuracy of 88.97%, precision of 88.92%, recall of 97.76%, F1-score of 93.13%, and ROC-AUC of 0.9282. Stratified five-fold cross-validation yielded a mean ROC-AUC of 0.8982 ± 0.0130. SHAP analysis identified TPSA, HBD, and LogP as the most influential molecular descriptors for BBB permeability prediction.

Conclusion: The proposed explainable machine learning framework provides an accurate, interpretable, and computationally efficient approach for BBB permeability prediction. It can serve as a valuable tool for early-stage screening of CNS drug candidates.

Abstract: Blood-brain barrier (BBB) permeability is a critical determinant in the development of central nervous system therapeutics because it directly influences the ability of drug candidates to reach their target sites within the brain.
