In this study, an explainable machine learning framework was developed to predict BBB permeability using molecular descriptors generated from the MoleculeNet BBBP dataset with the RDKit cheminformatics toolkit. Fifteen physicochemical descriptors extracted from 2,039 compounds were used to train four supervised machine learning algorithms, including Logistic Regression, Support Vector Machine (SVM), Random Forest, and Extreme Gradient Boosting (XGBoost). Hyperparameter optimization was performed using GridSearchCV, while model interpretability was investigated using SHapley Additive exPlanations (SHAP). Among the evaluated models, the optimized XGBoost classifier achieved the best predictive performance, with an accuracy of 88.97%, a precision of 88.92%, a recall of 97.76%, an F1-score of 93.13%, and a ROC-AUC of 0.9282. Stratified five-fold cross-validation further demonstrated the robustness of the proposed model, yielding a mean ROC-AUC of 0.8982 +/- 0.0130. Feature importance and SHAP analyses consistently identified TPSA, HBD, and LogP as the most influential molecular descriptors governing BBB permeability prediction. Overall, the proposed framework provides an accurate, interpretable, and computationally efficient approach for BBB permeability prediction and may serve as a valuable tool for the early-stage screening of CNS drug candidates.

</details>


### [209] [MetroLLM-Bench: Evaluating Language Models as Transit Kiosk Runtimes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10016)
*Remco Hendriks*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MetroLLM-Bench is a 955-case benchmark for evaluating language models as transit kiosk policy layers, covering six metro systems and eleven task categories. A 4B Qwen 3.5 model fine-tuned via PEFT outperforms GPT-5.6 on Tier 1 (91.3 vs 90.6/90.0) and matches GPT-5.4 at maximum reasoning effort, while PEFT effectiveness decreases with model size.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a need to systematically evaluate language models as policy layers in real-world transit kiosk applications, covering diverse scenarios including routing, fare calculation, disruptions, accessibility, and adversarial inputs.

Method: Created a benchmark with 955 cases across six metro systems (37-414 stations) and eleven categories. Two-tier scoring: 14 deterministic components (Tier 1) and 8 semantic-quality components (Tier 2). Evaluated 26 models using parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) on a 75/25 train/test split.

Result: The 4B Qwen 3.5 student exceeds GPT-5.6 tiers on Tier 1 (91.3 vs 90.6/90.0) and matches GPT-5.4 at maximum reasoning (91.4). PEFT gains decrease from +7.03 at 2B to -0.91 at 27B. Larger models (9B, 27B) provide no Tier 1 improvement over 4B. Rule-based baseline achieves 84.6. Serving configuration alone shifts Qwen 3.5-to-3.8 comparison by 2.7 points.

Conclusion: A small 4B PEFT model can outperform larger GPT models in transit kiosk policy tasks. PEFT effectiveness diminishes with model size, and serving configuration significantly impacts performance. The benchmark and fine-tuned models are publicly released.

Abstract: We introduce MetroLLM-Bench, a 955-case benchmark for testing language models as the policy layer of a transit kiosk. It covers six real metro systems, ranging from 37 to 414 stations, and eleven categories that include routing, fare calculation, disruptions, accessibility, and adversarial input. In each case, the model must call structured tools and submit a machine-renderable terminal state containing an outcome, a per-ticket fare quote when applicable, and a kiosk action. Fourteen deterministic scoring components form Tier 1; eight semantic-quality components form Tier 2, six of which use a language-model judge. We report Tier 1 and the combined score of both tiers. A stratified 75/25 split reserves 717 cases for training-data generation and 238 for held-out evaluation.
  We evaluate twenty-six models from six vendors, of which twenty-three are ranked. On the held-out partition, a 4B Qwen 3.5 student trained through parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) exceeds both GPT-5.6 tiers on Tier 1 (91.3 against 90.6 and 90.0) and matches GPT-5.4 full at maximum reasoning effort (91.4), with a 2.6 GB Q4_K_M footprint. Larger 9B and 27B students provide no further Tier 1 improvement over the 4B student at this training scale. Across the four Qwen sizes, the PEFT gain over the corresponding base model decreases from +7.03 points at 2B (three training seeds) to -0.91 at 27B; every seed shows the same direction at every size. A deterministic rule-based baseline reaches 84.6 on Tier 1, with the remaining language-model advantage concentrated in policy adaptation, compound scenarios, accessibility, and temporal reasoning. Muse Glimmer 30B leads the composite ranking, and serving configuration alone moves the Qwen 3.5-to-3.8 comparison by 2.7 Tier 1 points. The benchmark, harness, reproduction guide, and fine-tuned students are released at https://github.com/continker/metrollm-bench.

</details>


### [210] [Structure-Aware Unsupervised Anomaly Detection for Spacecraft Telemetry with Adaptive EVT Thresholding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10017)
*Óscar Alcarria,Rafael Sánchez,Javier Sempere,Pablo Torrijos,Juan C. Alfaro,Juan M. Auñón,José A. Gámez,José M. Puerta*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: An unsupervised framework for spacecraft telemetry anomaly detection that works from the second month of operation without labels, prior fault knowledge, or mission-specific tuning, using incremental retraining, statistical model selection, and adaptive EVT thresholding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current spacecraft anomaly detection methods require labeled historical anomalies or extended warm-up periods, which are rarely available in practice, creating a deployment gap.

Method: The framework combines three key components: (1) incremental monthly retraining for continuous learning, (2) statistical model selection to choose appropriate baseline models, and (3) adaptive Extreme Value Theory (EVT) thresholding for false alarm control. Evaluation uses strict chronological evaluation on the ESA Anomalies Dataset.

Result: The method achieves F0.5=0.700 on Mission 1 and F0.5=0.698 on Mission 2 on the ESA Anomalies Dataset under strict chronological evaluation.

Conclusion: The proposed unsupervised framework is deployment-ready from the second month of operation without requiring labels, prior fault knowledge, or mission-specific tuning, making it practical for real-world spacecraft operations.

Abstract: Operational anomaly detection in spacecraft telemetry typically requires labeled historical anomalies or extended warm-up periods. These requirements are rarely met in practice. We propose an unsupervised, deployment-ready framework that produces predictions from the second month of operation without any labels, prior fault knowledge, or mission-specific tuning. The approach combines incremental monthly retraining, statistical model selection, and adaptive Extreme Value Theory (EVT) thresholding for false alarm control. On the ESA Anomalies Dataset (ESA-AD), it achieves $F_{0.5}=0.700$ on Mission~1 and $F_{0.5}=0.698$ on Mission~2 under strict chronological evaluation.

</details>


### [211] [Beyond Contact Sensors: Deep learning with Pseudo-Labeling for remote Photoplethysmography](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10026)
*Bhargav Acharya,Barbara Hammer,Hanna Drimalla*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether pseudo-labels extracted from unsupervised signal-processing methods can replace contact sensor labels for training deep learning models for remote photoplethysmography (rPPG), reducing the need for labor-intensive synchronized dataset collection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep learning-based rPPG methods achieve state-of-the-art results but require precisely synchronized datasets with ground truth signals from contact sensors, which are labor-intensive to collect. The research aims to reduce this dependency by exploring alternative labeling approaches.

Method: Systematic evaluation comparing supervised training (using contact sensor labels) versus pseudo-label training (using unsupervised signal-processing methods) across datasets with different synchronization qualities. Evaluations included both within-dataset and cross-dataset scenarios, with outlier analysis.

Result: For imperfectly synchronized datasets, pseudo-label approach outperforms supervised training. For well-synchronized datasets, within-dataset evaluation shows no significant difference, while cross-dataset evaluation favors supervised training. However, removing a single outlier participant significantly improves pseudo-label cross-dataset performance, demonstrating the critical importance of label quality.

Conclusion: Signal-processing methods can generate valid training signals for deep learning rPPG models, reducing dependency on labor-intensive contact sensor dataset collection while maintaining competitive performance. Label quality matters more than the labeling method itself.

Abstract: Heart rate is a critical biomarker of health, and remote photoplethysmography (rPPG) enables its contactless estimation from video data for telemedicine applications. Recent advancements in deep learning based rPPG methods achieve state-of-the-art results, outperforming classical signal-processing methods in complex scenarios. However, deep learning methods depend on datasets with precise synchronization between videos and ground truth signals collected via contact sensors, whereas signal-processing-based methods do not. To address this dependence on labeled datasets, which are labor-intensive to collect, we investigate under which circumstances pseudo-labels extracted using unsupervised signal-processing methods can replace contact sensors labels for training deep learning methods. Our systematic evaluations found that for datasets with imperfect synchronization, the pseudo-label approach outperforms supervised training on contact sensors. For datasets with good synchronization, results are mixed: within-dataset evaluation shows no significant difference between training methods, while cross-dataset evaluation favors supervised training. However, removing a single outlier participant significantly improves the pseudo-label approach's cross-dataset performance, highlighting the importance of label quality. These results demonstrate that signal-processing methods can generate valid training signals for deep learning models, reducing dependency on labor-intensive dataset collection while maintaining competitive performance.

</details>


### [212] [Field-level prediction of mid-plane stress tensor fields in concrete target penetration: a cross-velocity graph neural operator surrogate](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10032)
*Wenpu Du,Peng Zhou,Yunlong Xia,Sinuo Xin,Congcong Zhang,Boyang Zhang,Yi Zhang,Wenzheng Xu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper presents a computational framework linking mesoscale concrete heterogeneity to full-field stress-tensor prediction under impact loading. Using LS-DYNA with aggregate-resolved modeling, 400 impact cases were simulated across 4 velocities and 100 aggregate seeds. A graph neural operator surrogate was trained to predict stress-field evolution, achieving ~3,600-4,300x speedup over LS-DYNA. Most cases exhibited rebound rather than arrest, with only one case fully arresting at 165 m/s.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The impact resistance of concrete has been extensively studied, but there was no existing framework linking mesoscale heterogeneity (aggregate distribution) to full-field stress-tensor prediction. This gap limits understanding of how material microstructure affects stress evolution during penetration events.

Method: 1. Full-scale aggregate-resolved LS-DYNA model (projectile: 45mm diameter, 2.13 kg; target: 500mm × 200mm, 10mm mesh)
2. 400 cases generated: 4 impact velocities × 100 aggregate seeds
3. Verified against published penetration experiments (Frew 2006, Hanchak 1992, Forrestal 1996) via configuration similarity
4. Field-level graph neural operator (GNO) surrogate model for time-varying stress-field prediction
5. Cross-velocity leave-one-out extrapolation validation

Result: 1. Most cases (100, 135, 200 m/s) exhibited rebound (negative velocity at window end); only one 165 m/s case arrested
2. Single arrested case: penetration depth 69.33 mm
3. Nose-node depth differences confirmed as numerical artifacts
4. Single-step relative L2 error: 0.6977
5. Autoregressive rollout (frame 11 to 39): ~144 ms, speedup of 3.6×10³ to 4.3×10³ relative to single-core LS-DYNA
6. Full-scale aggregate-resolved cross-velocity per-seed database established

Conclusion: The framework successfully links mesoscale heterogeneity to full-field stress-tensor prediction for concrete impact. The GNO surrogate achieves significant computational speedup (~3,600-4,300x), enabling rapid stress-field predictions. Validation is bounded by configuration similarity and field-level self-consistency. The framework serves as a simulation-trained decision-support method within the studied parameter space. The database provides a reproducible resource for future research.

Abstract: Although the impact resistance of concrete has been studied extensively, a framework linking mesoscale heterogeneity to full-field stress-tensor prediction has been lacking. Data were generated with a full-scale aggregate-resolved LS-DYNA model (projectile diameter 45 mm, mass 2.13 kg, target diameter 500 mm x thickness 200 mm, mesh 10 mm), verified against published penetration experiments (Frew 2006, Hanchak 1992, Forrestal 1996) by configuration similarity. The dataset contains six-component stress-tensor fields on the X-Z mid-plane for 400 cases (4 impact velocities x 100 aggregate seeds). Three contributions are reported. First, case-by-case verification of the terminal penetration state delimited the rest-state validity of penetration depth and anchored reliable observables to rigid-body motion and field-level stress evolution. Second, a field-level graph neural operator surrogate learned the time-varying stress-field evolution and evaluated cross-velocity leave-one-out extrapolation. Third, the full-scale, aggregate-resolved, cross-velocity, per-seed database was established as a reproducible resource. Cases at 100, 135 and 200 m/s still moved at window end (negative velocity, i.e. rebound), and only one 165 m/s case arrested. Penetration depth is therefore not reported as a rest-state scalar except for the single arrested case (69.33 mm); nose-node depth differences were confirmed as numerical artifacts of displacement integration after erosion. The single-step relative L2 error was 0.6977, reported honestly; autoregressive rollout from frame 11 to 39 took about 144 ms, a speedup of about 3.6x10^3 to 4.3x10^3 relative to single-core LS-DYNA, reported as application value. Validation is bounded by configuration similarity and field-level self-consistency; the framework is a simulation-trained decision-support method within the studied parameter space.

</details>


### [213] [Hybrid Quantum-Classical NLP Classification with Compact Semantic Representations: An Experimental Analysis of Representation Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10089)
*Ali Hassan,Zijia Zhao,Maha A. Metawei*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates a hybrid quantum-classical pipeline for transforming high-dimensional sentence embeddings into compact representations for variational quantum classification. The approach combines pretrained sentence embeddings, dimensionality reduction (PCA, NCA, LDA), angle encoding, and a variational quantum circuit (VQC) for question classification on the TREC dataset.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language and sentence-embedding models produce high-dimensional semantic representations (768 dimensions in this study), but near-term quantum machine learning (QML) devices can only process a limited number of input features (qubits). This creates a fundamental bottleneck for applying quantum methods to NLP tasks.

Method: The hybrid pipeline consists of: (1) Pretrained sentence-embedding model (384-dimensional output), (2) Dimensionality reduction using PCA (unsupervised), NCA (supervised), or LDA (supervised), (3) Angle encoding to map classical data to quantum states, (4) Variational Quantum Circuit (VQC) for classification, (5) Classical decision layer. The study uses the TREC question-classification dataset and compares different dimensionality reduction techniques across various reduced dimensions (3, 4, 5, 8) under a leakage-free cross-validation protocol.

Result: PCA results show significant information loss: reducing 768 dimensions to 3, 4, 5, and 8 dimensions retains only 8.2%, 10.2%, 11.9%, and 16.4% of variance respectively, with corresponding accuracies of 50.3%, 51.2%, 57.9%, and 63.4%. In contrast, supervised methods dramatically outperform PCA. LDA achieves 85.3% accuracy with only 5 dimensions, NCA achieves 83.1% with 5 dimensions, compared to a classical baseline of 85.1% using the full 384-dimensional embeddings. Notably, supervised methods retain task-relevant information far more effectively than variance-based compression.

Conclusion: Supervised dimensionality reduction (LDA, NCA) preserves task-relevant information far more effectively than variance-based compression (PCA) for quantum NLP classification. Using only 5 dimensions, supervised methods nearly match the performance of full 384-dimensional classical embeddings (85.3% vs 85.1%). This makes compact representations a promising route toward practical hybrid quantum-classical NLP models, as they significantly reduce qubit requirements while maintaining high accuracy.

Abstract: Large language and sentence-embedding models provide rich semantic representations, but their high dimensionality poses a challenge for near-term quantum machine learning (QML), where quantum circuits can process only a limited number of input features. We investigate a hybrid quantum-classical pipeline that transforms high-dimensional sentence embeddings into compact representations for variational quantum classification. The workflow combines a pretrained sentence-embedding model, dimensionality reduction, angle encoding, a variational quantum circuit (VQC), and a classical decision layer. We systematically compare principal component analysis (PCA), neighborhood components analysis (NCA), and linear discriminant analysis (LDA), covering both unsupervised and supervised dimensionality reduction. Using the TREC question-classification dataset, we study the relationship between representation dimensionality, information retention, qubit count, and classification performance. Preliminary PCA experiments reveal a strong information bottleneck: reducing 768-dimensional embeddings to 3, 4, 5, and 8 dimensions retains about 8.2%, 10.2%, 11.9%, and 16.4% of the variance, with corresponding classification accuracies of 50.3%, 51.2%, 57.9%, and 63.4%. In contrast, supervised reduction is substantially more efficient. LDA reaches 85.3% accuracy and NCA reaches 83.1% using only 5 dimensions, under a leakage-free cross-validation protocol, compared with 85.1% for a full 384-dimensional classical baseline. These results indicate that supervised dimensionality reduction can preserve task-relevant information far more effectively than variance-based compression, making compact representations a promising route toward practical hybrid quantum-classical NLP models.

</details>


### [214] [A Systematic Evaluation of Molecule Generation Models for De Novo Drug Design: From Benchmarks to Practical Insights](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10099)
*Xinrui Xu,Xueer Wang,Dan Luo,Sisi Yuan,Xuan Lin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A comprehensive review of 82 molecule generation methods for de novo drug design across five deep generative frameworks (RNN/Transformer, VAEs, GANs, Flow-based, Diffusion models), covering benchmarks, molecular representations, methodologies, performance evaluation, and future directions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing reviews address specific model families or application scenarios in isolation, lacking an integrated perspective on how components collectively form a coherent generation workflow for drug design.

Method: Systematic review covering: (1) benchmarks and molecular representations, (2) methodological principles for general and pocket-conditioned generation, (3) comparative performance analysis across benchmarks, (4) experimentally validated case studies.

Result: Comprehensive evaluation of 82 methods across five generative frameworks, synthesis of benchmark performance, summary of validated case studies, with all resources compiled in a public GitHub repository.

Conclusion: Future directions emphasize: standardized 3D data, interaction-aware generation, receptor flexibility, and multi-objective molecular design to improve reliability and experimental relevance of molecule generation.

Abstract: Molecule generation has emerged as a powerful computational tool for de novo drug design, enabling the exploration of chemical space beyond the limits of conventional virtual screening. The field has progressed rapidly, driven by advances in molecular representations, generative architectures, and target-aware modeling strategies. However, existing reviews typically address specific model families or application scenarios in isolation, rather than offering an integrated perspective on how these components collectively form a coherent generation workflow. In this review, we present a comprehensive evaluation of molecule generation models for de novo drug design, covering 82 methods across five deep generative frameworks, including recurrent neural network (RNN)- and Transformer-based models, variational autoencoders (VAEs), generative adversarial networks (GANs), flow-based models, and diffusion models. We first summarize widely used benchmarks and molecular representations, and then examine the methodological principles underlying both general and pocket-conditioned generation. A central contribution of this work is a systematic synthesis and comparative analysis of reported performance across commonly used benchmarks and evaluation metrics. We also summarize representative experimentally validated case studies. Looking ahead, we discuss future directions in standardized 3D data, interaction-aware generation, receptor flexibility, and multi-objective molecular design, with the aim of improving the reliability and experimental relevance of molecule generation. All collected benchmark resources, evaluation metrics, and model references are provided in a publicly accessible repository at https://github.com/JacklinGroup/molecule-generation-review.

</details>


### [215] [A Trust-Network-Based Federated Learning Framework for Multi-Center Aging Clock Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10108)
*Chunxu Zhang,Bo Li,Wenliang Wang,Yang Liu,Di Jiang,Yuan Huang,Yo-ichi Nabeshima,Akinori Yamamura,Bo Yang,Qiang Yang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes TNFL, a trust-network-based federated learning framework for building accurate aging clocks across distributed medical centers without sharing sensitive data. TNFL addresses challenges of limited local data, sparse trust networks, interpretability, and model drift through a novel combination of trust-based model propagation, mixture-of-experts modeling, and generative replay. Biological analysis reveals that protein interactions important for aging clocks form coordinated higher-order subnetworks spanning multiple aging-related biological systems, suggesting that higher-order interactions are biologically meaningful for aging.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The research aims to address two key questions: (1) what protein interactions are important for accurate aging clocks - are they zeroth-order (individual proteins) or higher-order (protein complexes/pathways)?, and (2) how to build accurate aging clocks using distributed molecular datasets across medical centers while preserving privacy? Federated learning is a natural solution but faces significant challenges in this medical context.

Method: TNFL framework combines: (1) Trust-network-based model propagation - models progressively propagate along directed pairwise trust relations without centralized aggregation, (2) Age-aware mixture-of-experts model for interpretable age prediction, and (3) Generative replay to preserve previously learned information and reduce forgetting/drift. Biological analysis involved functional and network analyses of identified protein interactions to examine higher-order organization.

Result: TNFL enables effective aging-clock prediction with limited local data, provides interpretable age-dependent prediction patterns, and maintains stable performance across interaction orders. Biological analysis reveals that identified protein interactions form coordinated higher-order subnetworks spanning multiple aging-related biological systems, with several proteins recurring across subnetworks. This suggests TNFL captures molecular relationships beyond isolated pairwise associations and reveals coherent higher-order biological organization associated with aging.

Conclusion: The findings indicate that higher-order protein interactions (not just individual proteins or pairwise associations) are important for accurate aging clocks. The proposed TNFL framework successfully addresses the challenges of federated learning in medical settings and reveals that aging-related proteins are organized into coherent higher-order biological modules, demonstrating the biological relevance of higher-order interactions for quantifying biological aging.

Abstract: Aging clocks quantify biological aging and help characterize individual health status. What protein interactions are important for accurate aging clocks, and are they zeroth-order or higher-order? Addressing these questions requires learning from large molecular datasets distributed across medical centers, where privacy constraints prevent centralized data sharing. Federated learning offers a natural solution but faces four challenges in this setting: limited local sample sizes, sparse and directional inter-center trust, the need to retain discriminative age prediction while supporting interpretation, and model drift and forgetting under heterogeneous cross-center data.
  We propose TNFL, a trust-network-based federated learning framework that progressively propagates models along directed pairwise trust relations without centralized aggregation. TNFL combines an age-aware mixture-of-experts model with generative replay to preserve previously learned information and reduce forgetting and drift. Experiments across multiple molecular datasets show that TNFL enables effective aging-clock prediction with limited local data, provides interpretable age-dependent prediction patterns, and maintains stable performance across interaction orders.
  To investigate the biological questions, we analyze TNFL-identified pairwise protein interactions and their higher-order organization through functional and network analyses. The identified interactions repeatedly form coordinated higher-order subnetworks spanning multiple aging-related biological systems, with several proteins recurring across subnetworks. These findings suggest that TNFL captures molecular relationships beyond isolated pairwise associations and reveals coherent higher-order biological organization associated with aging.

</details>


### [216] [Storage-Scalable Progressive Semantic Communication via Knowledge-Base Reuse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10112)
*Heng Zhu,Ye Liu,Kun Zhu,Feifei Song*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes SSKBQ (storage-scalable knowledge-base reuse quantization) to solve the storage scalability problem in knowledge-base-assisted semantic communication systems. Existing methods either have limited quantization capacity (SKBQ) or linearly increasing KB storage with transmission depth (MKBQ). The proposed approach reuses a compact set of knowledge bases across multiple residual refinement stages, decoupling the number of transmission stages from KB storage requirements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing knowledge-base-assisted semantic communication schemes face a trade-off: SKBQ has limited quantization capacity while MKBQ requires KB storage that grows linearly with transmission depth. This storage scalability problem limits the practical deployment of progressive refinement in semantic communication systems.

Method: The proposed SSKBQ method reuses a compact set of KBs across multiple residual refinement stages, decoupling the number of transmission stages from the number of maintained KBs. Additionally, a stage-aware residual supervision mechanism is introduced to regularize intermediate quantized representations and encourage progressive refinement.

Result: Experimental results demonstrate that KB reuse provides an effective solution to the storage scalability problem while maintaining competitive progressive reconstruction performance.

Conclusion: The SSKBQ approach successfully addresses the storage scalability issue in knowledge-base-assisted semantic communication, offering a practical solution that maintains competitive performance while significantly reducing KB storage requirements compared to existing methods.

Abstract: Existing knowledge-base-assisted semantic communication schemes commonly adopt either single knowledge-base quantization (SKBQ) or multi-knowledge-base residual quantization (MKBQ). SKBQ incurs limited storage overhead but has restricted quantization capacity, whereas MKBQ supports progressive refinement by assigning an independent knowledge base (KB) to each stage, causing the KB storage to grow linearly with the transmission depth. To address this problem, we propose storage-scalable knowledge-base reuse quantization (SSKBQ), which reuses a compact set of KBs across multiple residual refinement stages and thereby decouples the number of transmission stages from the number of maintained KBs. A stage-aware residual supervision mechanism is further introduced to regularize intermediate quantized representations and encourage progressive refinement. Experimental results demonstrate that KB reuse provides an effective solution to the storage scalability problem while maintaining competitive progressive reconstruction performance.

</details>


### [217] [CompassOPD: Cross-Family On-Policy Distillation via Within-Family Likelihood Shifts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10154)
*Naibin Gu,Qingyi Si,Chenxu Yang,Chuanyu Qin,Junhao Zhou,Peng Fu,Zheng Lin,Weiping Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Cross-family on-policy distillation (OPD) suffers from effectiveness degradation even with tokenizer alignment. This paper identifies that the offset between teacher-family reference and student dominates the update direction, obscuring capability improvements. The proposed CompassOPD removes this offset by using a frozen student reference and transfers only the within-family log-likelihood shift, achieving up to 5.50 points improvement in reasoning accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: OPD provides dense token-level supervision and works well within the same model family, but its effectiveness degrades in cross-family settings (different teacher and student model families) even after tokenizer alignment. Stronger external teachers offer little additional improvement, indicating a fundamental limitation in cross-family distillation.

Method: The authors decompose cross-family OPD into two components: (1) an offset between a low-capability teacher-family reference and the student, and (2) the within-family log-likelihood shift from the reference to the strong teacher. CompassOPD removes the offset by anchoring updates to a frozen student reference while transferring only the within-family shift. For MoE teachers, they construct the reference directly from the teacher checkpoint by reducing expert activation.

Result: CompassOPD consistently outperforms standard cross-family OPD across three student families and multiple teacher families, improving average reasoning accuracy by up to 5.50 points. For MoE teachers, the method retains a 3.43-point gain over OPD without needing a separate reference checkpoint.

Conclusion: CompassOPD successfully addresses the cross-family distillation problem by isolating the within-family capability improvement from the cross-family offset. This allows effective knowledge transfer regardless of model family differences, with particular benefit for MoE architectures where reference construction from the teacher checkpoint is possible.

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) provides dense token-level supervision on student-generated trajectories. Although OPD performs strongly when teacher and student belong to the same model family, we find that its effectiveness degrades in cross-family settings even after tokenizer alignment, with substantially stronger external teachers offering little additional improvement. To understand this disconnect, we decompose the cross-family OPD signal into two components: an offset between a low-capability teacher-family reference and the student, and the within-family log-likelihood shift from that reference to the strong teacher. Standard OPD transfers both components together, allowing the offset to dominate the update direction and obscure the changes associated with teacher capability improvements. We propose CompassOPD, which removes this offset and transfers the within-family shift, while a frozen student reference anchors updates to the student's initial policy. Thus, both teacher-side and student-side changes are measured within their respective model families. Experiments across three student families and multiple teacher families show that CompassOPD consistently outperforms standard cross-family OPD, improving average reasoning accuracy by up to 5.50 points. For an MoE teacher, we further construct the reference directly from the teacher checkpoint by reducing expert activation, eliminating the need for a separate reference checkpoint while retaining a 3.43-point gain over OPD.

</details>


### [218] [CoGe-GCD: Reframing Generalized Category Discovery with Compositional Generalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10158)
*Luyao Tang,Jiewei Zheng,Kunze Huang,Chaoqi Chen,Yue Huang,Cheng Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CoGe-GCD proposes a two-stage compositional generalization approach for Generalized Category Discovery. It introduces (i) Compositional Perception that structures patch tokens by mapping them to primitives with competitive assignment and information passing, and (ii) Generalizing Induction that applies structure-preserving calibration over spatial relations. The method works as an inductive-bias module between backbone and projection head, compatible with diverse GCD frameworks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing GCD methods operate on unstructured token features and struggle to extrapolate to novel compositions. The task requires human-like compositional reasoning: reusing primitives from known classes and determining when new combinations imply novel categories.

Method: CoGe-GCD uses two coupled stages: (1) Compositional Perception structures patch tokens by mapping them to a small vocabulary of primitives and refines token embeddings via competitive token-primitive assignment and information passing, yielding coherent groups for discovery. (2) Generalizing Induction exploits the induced geometric structure and applies structure-preserving calibration over spatial relations, maintaining probabilistic semantics while improving extrapolation to unseen primitive combinations. Implemented as an inductive-bias module between backbone and projection head without modifying heads or losses.

Result: On standard benchmarks, CoGe-GCD consistently improves all-class accuracy, unknown-class number estimation, and geometric quality, with marginal computational overhead.

Conclusion: CoGe-GCD successfully addresses compositional generalization in GCD through its two-stage approach. The method can be plugged into diverse GCD frameworks with minimal computational overhead while achieving improved performance across multiple metrics.

Abstract: Generalized Category Discovery (GCD) assigns unlabeled instances, mixed with labeled data, to known or novel categories, requiring human-like compositional reasoning: reusing primitives learned from known classes and deciding when new combinations imply new categories. Existing GCD methods operate on unstructured token features and struggle to extrapolate to novel compositions. We propose CoGe-GCD, which rethinks GCD through compositional generalization with two coupled stages. (i) Compositional Perception structures patch tokens by mapping them to a small vocabulary of primitives and refining token embeddings via competitive token-primitive assignment and information passing, yielding coherent groups for discovery. (ii) Generalizing Induction exploits the induced geometric structure and applies a structure-preserving calibration over spatial relations, maintaining probabilistic semantics while improving extrapolation to unseen primitive combinations. CoGe-GCD is implemented as an inductive-bias module between backbone and projection head, without modifying heads or losses, and can be plugged into diverse GCD frameworks. On standard benchmarks, it consistently improves all-class accuracy, unknown-class number estimation, and geometric quality, with marginal computational overhead. Code is available at https://github.com/lytang63/CoGe-GCD.

</details>


### [219] [An Exponential Deterministic--Randomized Gap in ERM-Oracle Complexity for Thresholds on an Unknown Order](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10196)
*Xuan Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proves a separation between deterministic and randomized learners in transductive online learning of thresholds, showing that under the minimal-prefix (or maximal-prefix) ERM oracle, any deterministic learner must make M mistakes and Q calls satisfying M+Q ≥ T-ε, while a randomized learner can achieve O(log T) expected calls and mistakes. The result demonstrates that randomization can provably reduce oracle complexity, and the separation depends on the oracle's selection rule rather than the hypothesis class alone.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation stems from an open question posed by Attias, Hanneke and Ramaswami (NeurIPS 2025) about whether randomization can provably reduce oracle calls needed for online learning when the class is accessible only through an oracle. This paper provides the first affirmative answer by constructing a concrete separation instance.

Method: The authors study transductive online learning of thresholds on an unknown total order of T instances. They consider a consistency-type ERM oracle that returns a full concept consistent with a queried labeled set (or reports non-realizability). The main technical approach involves analyzing the minimal-prefix rule and maximal-prefix rule as the oracle's selection mechanism, and constructing hard distributions to prove lower bounds against deterministic learners while showing randomized learners can succeed with logarithmic complexity.

Result: The main result shows that for the minimal-prefix rule (or maximal-prefix rule), every deterministic learner makes M mistakes and Q calls with M+Q ≥ T-ε on some instance (ε ∈ {0,1}), establishing an exact constant. This means O(log T) mistakes cost T-ε - O(log T) calls. In contrast, the randomized learner achieves O(log T) expected calls and mistakes under the same oracle. Additionally, the paper proves a lower bound showing that on an explicit hard distribution under the minimal-prefix rule, every learner has expected mistakes at least ((T+1-ε)·128^(-E[Q]) - 1)/2, implying Ω(log T) expected calls are necessary for polylogarithmic mistakes. The paper also shows that with a feasible-median ERM rule, deterministic learners can achieve O(log T) calls and mistakes, while a global-median rule forces linear total cost. With only a weak consistency oracle (returning just a realizability bit), both deterministic and randomized learners need Θ(T) calls.

Conclusion: This work establishes the first provable separation between deterministic and randomized learners in terms of oracle complexity for online learning. Key insights: (1) The separation is governed by the oracle's selection rule, not the hypothesis class alone—different ERM rules (minimal-prefix vs. feasible-median) yield dramatically different complexities. (2) Randomization provides exponential improvement from linear to logarithmic oracle calls when the oracle uses certain selection rules. (3) The weakness of the oracle matters significantly: with only a realizability bit, even randomization cannot help. This advances understanding of the role of randomization in interactive learning and oracle design.

Abstract: Attias, Hanneke and Ramaswami (NeurIPS 2025) asked whether randomization provably reduces the oracle calls needed for online learning when the class is accessible only through an oracle. We study the instance they singled out: transductive online learning of thresholds on an unknown total order of T instances, with a consistency-type ERM oracle that returns a full concept consistent with a queried labeled set (or reports non-realizability). Our main result is a separation for a fixed natural oracle. When the oracle is the minimal-prefix rule (or the maximal-prefix rule), every deterministic learner makes M mistakes and Q calls with $M+Q\ge T-\varepsilon$ on some instance ($\varepsilon\in\{0,1\}$, according to whether the empty prefix is a concept), and the constant is exact; hence $O(\log T)$ mistakes cost $T-\varepsilon-O(\log T)$ calls, whereas that paper's randomized learner achieves $O(\log T)$ expected calls and mistakes under the same rule. The randomized order is optimal: on an explicit hard distribution under the minimal-prefix rule, every learner has expected mistakes at least $((T+1-\varepsilon)\,128^{-\mathbb{E}[Q]}-1)/2$, so $Ω(\log T)$ expected calls are necessary for polylogarithmic mistakes. The separation is governed by the oracle's selection rule, not by the class alone: for a legal feasible-median ERM rule a deterministic learner achieves $O(\log T)$ calls and mistakes, while a global-median rule again forces linear total cost. The same linear bound holds when the oracle's answers are chosen adversarially and then frozen into a memoryless oracle. We add partial tradeoff results for fixed query budgets (the middle regime is open) and an interface contrast: with only a weak consistency oracle, returning a realizability bit, both deterministic and randomized learners need $Θ(T)$ calls.

</details>


### [220] [Robust Beam Prediction for V2X Networks with Multi-Modal Sensing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10200)
*Chen Shang,Dinh Thai Hoang,Diep N. Nguyen,Jiadong Yu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes BeamTransFuser, a multi-modal beam prediction framework for V2X networks that fuses camera, LiDAR, radar, and GPS data using a hierarchical Transformer architecture, with a generative module to handle missing modalities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing sensing-assisted beamforming for V2X networks relies heavily on radio-frequency (RF) sensing, which becomes unreliable in complex vehicular environments. The growing availability of heterogeneous sensors (cameras, LiDAR) offers opportunities for richer environmental perception to improve beam prediction.

Method: The paper develops BeamTransFuser, a hierarchical Transformer-based architecture that progressively fuses multi-modal sensor observations (camera, LiDAR, radar, GPS) for robust beam prediction. Additionally, a generative module is introduced to reconstruct missing modality features from available observations to handle incomplete sensing conditions.

Result: Experimental results on a real-world multi-modal V2X dataset show that the proposed framework consistently outperforms representative baselines, and the generative module further improves robustness under incomplete sensing conditions.

Conclusion: The multi-modal beam prediction framework with BeamTransFuser effectively addresses beam prediction challenges in V2X networks, demonstrating superior performance over baselines and enhanced robustness through the generative module for handling missing modalities.

Abstract: Integrated sensing and communication (ISAC) provides a promising foundation for beam prediction in future vehicle-to-everything (V2X) networks. However, existing sensing-assisted beamforming methods still rely heavily on radio-frequency sensing, which may become unreliable in complex vehicular environments. Meanwhile, the growing availability of heterogeneous sensors, such as cameras and LiDAR, offers new opportunities to improve beam prediction through richer environmental perception. Motivated by this, this paper proposes a multi-modal beam prediction framework for V2X networks. Specifically, we develop BeamTransFuser, a hierarchical Transformer-based architecture that progressively fuses camera, LiDAR, radar, and GPS observations for robust beam prediction. In addition, to handle possible missing modalities in practical deployment, we introduce a generative module that reconstructs missing modality features from the available observations. Experimental results on a real-world multi-modal V2X dataset show that the proposed framework consistently outperforms representative baselines, while the generative module further improves robustness under incomplete sensing conditions.

</details>


### [221] [Hierarchical and Permutation-Invariant Feature Transformation Learning via Policy-Guided Embedding Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10225)
*Rui Liu,Tao Zhe,Yanyong Huang,Sankha Narayan Guria,Xiao Luo,Wei Fan,Yanjie Fu,Dongjie Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes a framework to improve feature transformation for tabular data by addressing three key limitations in prior generative approaches: ignoring hierarchical feature relationships, introducing bias through order-sensitive embeddings, and using gradient-based search ill-suited for non-convex spaces. The solution combines a permutation-invariant hierarchical module with self-attention pooling and a policy-guided multi-objective reinforcement learning strategy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current generative approaches for feature transformation on tabular data suffer from three fundamental limitations: (1) they overlook hierarchical relationships between low-level features, operations, and high-level abstractions; (2) they enforce order-sensitive embeddings on inherently permutation-invariant transformation sequences, introducing systematic bias; and (3) they rely on gradient-based search, which is unsuitable for non-convex transformation spaces.

Method: The proposed framework has two complementary components: (1) A permutation-invariant hierarchical module that captures interactions across features, operations, and abstraction levels, using a self-attention pooling mechanism that maps semantically equivalent structures to consistent embeddings aligned with downstream performance; (2) A policy-guided multi-objective reinforcement learning strategy that initializes the search from empirically strong seeds and jointly optimizes predictive accuracy and transformation efficiency.

Result: Extensive experiments on diverse tabular benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness and robustness of the framework against strong baselines.

Conclusion: The proposed framework successfully addresses the limitations of prior generative approaches for feature transformation on tabular data through hierarchical modeling and reinforcement learning-based search, achieving superior performance on benchmark datasets.

Abstract: Feature transformation improves predictive performance on tabular data by constructing informative abstractions from raw features. Recent generative approaches encode transformation knowledge into continuous embedding spaces for efficient exploration of candidate strategies, but face three key limitations: (1) overlooking hierarchical relationships between low-level features, operations, and high-level abstractions; (2) enforcing order-sensitive embeddings on inherently permutation-invariant transformation sequences, thereby introducing systematic bias; and (3) relying on gradient-based search, which is ill-suited to non-convex transformation spaces. We propose a framework with two complementary components. First, a permutation-invariant hierarchical module captures interactions across features, operations, and abstraction levels, with a self-attention pooling mechanism that maps semantically equivalent structures to consistent embeddings aligned with downstream performance. Second, a policy-guided multi-objective reinforcement learning strategy initializes the search from empirically strong seeds and jointly optimizes predictive accuracy and transformation efficiency. Extensive experiments on diverse tabular benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness and robustness of our framework against strong baselines. Our code and data are publicly available at: https://github.com/RayLiu1103/PHER.

</details>


### [222] [Training Trajectories Determine Circuit Removability in Annealable Soft-Prior Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10287)
*Zonglin Yang,Ziming Zhao,Wei Tang,Xunyu Jiang,Yihong Liu,Tailin Chen,Zifu Yu,Jiayu Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether Transformer circuits learned with soft positional priors can remain functional after the prior is removed. Using an annealable soft-prior Transformer, the authors find that smooth fade-to-zero training preserves circuit functionality (0.734 ± 0.028 accuracy at zero gate), while other approaches fail. The study reveals that circuit removability depends on training trajectory, not just final architecture.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The research addresses a fundamental question in mechanistic interpretability: can learned circuits function independently of the inductive biases that helped form them? Specifically, the authors investigate whether attention biases (positional priors) that assist small Transformers in learning retrieval circuits are necessary for maintaining those circuits' functionality after training.

Method: The authors develop an annealable soft-prior Transformer where attention biases can be learned, faded, or zeroed during training and evaluation. They compare four training strategies: (1) unforced models (prior active), (2) smooth fade-to-zero training, (3) forced-zero training, and (4) hard switching. They test on associative recall and Markov induction tasks, using linear regression ICL as a boundary case. Mechanistic traces are used to analyze circuit consolidation.

Result: Key findings: (1) Unforced models achieve high accuracy with prior active (0.772 ± 0.020) but collapse at zero gate (0.095 ± 0.009). (2) Smooth fade-to-zero training preserves high zero-gate accuracy (0.734 ± 0.028). (3) Forced-zero training, hard switching, and post hoc continuation all fail to recover the same effect. (4) Circuit consolidation occurs after the gate reaches zero, with responsible heads varying across seeds.

Conclusion: Circuit removability in small discrete retrieval tasks depends critically on the training trajectory, not merely on the final model architecture. The gradual removal of positional priors through smooth fade-to-zero training allows circuits to consolidate and remain functional without the prior, whereas abrupt removal or post hoc intervention fails. This suggests that the learning process itself shapes whether learned mechanisms are independent of their initial inductive biases.

Abstract: Soft positional priors can help small Transformers learn retrieval circuits, but it is unclear whether the resulting circuits remain functional once the prior is removed. We test this with an annealable soft-prior Transformer whose attention biases can be learned, faded, or zeroed during training and evaluation. On associative recall, unforced models perform well with the prior active ($0.772 \pm 0.020$) but collapse at zero gate ($0.095 \pm 0.009$). Smooth fade-to-zero training preserves high zero-gate accuracy ($0.734 \pm 0.028$), whereas forced-zero training, hard switching, and post hoc continuation fail to recover the same effect. The pattern also appears on Markov induction. Linear regression ICL provides a boundary case because zero-gate training can learn that task directly. Mechanistic traces show that circuit consolidation occurs after the gate reaches zero, even though the responsible heads vary across seeds. These results suggest that circuit removability in small discrete retrieval tasks depends on the training trajectory, not just the final architecture.

</details>


### [223] [A Dominant Diffuse Phase in the Sparse Autoencoder Phase Diagram](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10299)
*Alexis D. Plascencia*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates sparse autoencoders (SAEs) in a controlled experimental setting (MAIS-O43) to determine when true dictionary recovery transitions to feature merging as parameters vary. Contrary to expectations, the study found no full recoveries or merges across 200+ experiments; instead, all runs converge to a "diffuse phase" with near-perfect reconstruction but poor feature recovery (median cosine 0.5-0.7 vs 0.95 criterion) and overly dense codes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The research addresses a fundamental challenge in interpretability: systematic feature co-occurrence causes distinct features to be absorbed or merged in SAE training. The goal was to characterize the parameter boundaries (nesting fraction γ, sparsity penalty λ, dictionary size M) where true dictionary recovery gives way to feature merging.

Method: Implemented the MAIS-O43 protocol: evaluated 200 independently initialized fits across 10 of 165 grid cells, then extended to full grid (3,300 additional fits) using standard minibatch Adam. Parameters varied were nesting fraction γ, sparsity penalty λ, and dictionary size M.

Result: Zero full-dictionary recoveries and zero merges observed. All runs converged to a reproducible "diffuse phase": nearly perfect reconstruction but learned atoms far from true features (median best cosine 0.5-0.7 against 0.95 recovery criterion) with learned codes an order of magnitude denser than ground truth. This behavior persisted under robustness checks and across the full 165-cell parameter grid.

Conclusion: Since the global optimum of the exact sparse-coding objective is known to merge nested features in the two-feature case, trained SAEs need not reach these minima. The phase diagram of trained models may differ fundamentally from that of objective minimizers, suggesting optimization dynamics prevent convergence to expected merged states.

Abstract: Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) are increasingly used to recover interpretable features from neural-network activations, yet systematic feature co-occurrence can cause distinct features to be absorbed or merged. The MAIS-O43 open problem proposes a controlled experiment to characterize when recovery of a true synthetic dictionary gives way to feature merging as the nesting fraction $γ$, sparsity penalty $λ$, and dictionary size $M$ vary. We implement the specified protocol and evaluate 200 independently initialized fits across ten of the 165 grid cells. We observe zero full-dictionary recoveries and zero merges. Instead, every run converges to a reproducible diffuse phase: reconstruction is nearly perfect, but learned atoms typically remain far from the true features (median best cosine 0.5-0.7 against a 0.95 recovery criterion) and learned codes are an order of magnitude denser than the ground truth. This behavior persists under robustness checks and across the full 165-cell grid using standard minibatch Adam (3,300 additional fits). Since the global optimum of the exact sparse-coding objective is known to merge nested features in the two-feature case, these results suggest that trained SAEs need not reach the corresponding minima, and that the phase diagram of trained models may differ fundamentally from that of objective minimizers.

</details>


### [224] [View-Structured Conformal Prediction for 3D Gaussian Splatting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10307)
*Junzheng Chu,Bin Pan,Zhenwei Shi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: VSCP introduces view-structured conformal prediction for 3D Gaussian Splatting, providing statistically valid uncertainty quantification for novel-view synthesis with significant width reductions over baseline methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 3D Gaussian Splatting enables real-time novel-view rendering but lacks certified uncertainty quantification—existing methods like pixel-pooled calibration fail to guarantee prediction coverage (e.g., 90% target achieving only 61.4% view-event coverage), necessitating a statistically rigorous approach to ensure with probability at least 1-α that RGB prediction boxes cover at least 1-β fraction of pixels in new views.

Method: View-Structured Conformal Prediction (VSCP) factorizes the pre-calibration scale into a spatial shape from the renderer and a transferable view-difficulty factor that predicts the smallest view-wise multiplier needed. A held-out quantile over views (View-CP) provides finite-sample validity when transferring to new scenes, with conformity score defined as the ratio of oracle to predicted view difficulty.

Result: On 13 real scenes, VSCP achieves 91.7-92.0% view-event coverage at 90% target (vs. 61.4% for pixel-pooled). At matched coverage, VSCP reduces width by 22.1% versus constant scale, matching a ten-model ensemble's 21.0% reduction using only one model per scene. VSCP outperforms 3DGS-U by 4.7 points (p=0.0225). The view predictor transfers successfully to all nine unbounded Mip-NeRF 360 scenes with 20.7% width saving, maintains 18.3% saving under different densification backbone, and runs at 216-280 FPS on RTX 4090.

Conclusion: VSCP provides statistically valid uncertainty quantification for 3DGS rendering by decoupling spatial shape and view difficulty, enabling transfer learning across scenes while achieving substantial width reductions and real-time performance. The approach bridges the gap between conformal prediction theory and practical novel-view synthesis uncertainty.

Abstract: 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) renders novel views in real time, but an uncertainty heatmap does not certify that a rendered view meets a certain prediction coverage. We treat novel-view synthesis as structured regression and ask that, with probability at least $1-α$, RGB prediction boxes cover at least a $1-β$ fraction of pixels in a new view. We propose View-Structured Conformal Prediction (VSCP). It splits the pre-calibration scale into a spatial shape from the renderer and a transferable view-difficulty factor, which predicts the smallest view-wise multiplier that shape needs. A held-out quantile over views (View-CP) then gives finite-sample validity even when transferring to new scenes. The same factorization makes the analysis exact: a conformity score is the ratio of oracle to predicted view difficulty, and excess width separates into a test-side and a calibration-side term. Across 13 real scenes, pixel-pooled calibration reaches 89.9\% marginal pixel coverage but only 61.4\% view-event coverage at a 90\% target, while View-CP reaches 91.7--92.0\%. At matched coverage VSCP cuts width by 22.1\% against a constant scale, and matches a ten-model ensemble's 21.0\% reduction using only one model per scene and four rather than ten rasterization passes per query. VSCP also improves on the closest single-model baseline, the 3DGS-U field, by 4.7 points ($p=0.0225$). The view predictor transfers from bounded source families to all nine unbounded Mip-NeRF~360 scenes. There the full scale beats the constant scale with 20.7\% width saving on all nine scenes. It also keeps an 18.3\% saving under a different densification backbone and runs at 216--280 FPS on an RTX~4090.

</details>


### [225] [One Loop, Two Gains: Can Active Learning win the Lottery for Free?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10311)
*Benedikt Tscheschner,Eduardo Veas,Marc Masana*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes Improve & Prune (I&P), a method that integrates iterative magnitude pruning into deep active learning cycles to discover sparse winning tickets at each active learning iteration with no additional computational cost. The method achieves up to 95% sparsity while matching dense model accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Both iterative magnitude pruning (for finding lottery tickets) and deep active learning (for data-efficient learning) rely on iterative retraining from scratch, yet they have been studied separately. The authors propose integrating these paradigms to obtain sparse deployable models as a byproduct of active learning, addressing computational bottlenecks in practical deployment.

Method: Improve & Prune (I&P) integrates magnitude pruning into each active learning retraining cycle. The method alternates between model training and pruning at each active learning iteration, exploiting the shared computational structure between iterative pruning and active learning retraining cycles.

Result: I&P yields sparse, deployable models at each active learning iteration that match the accuracy of dense counterparts at sparsities up to 95%. The method works across multiple acquisition functions, architecture families, and image classification datasets, including an active fine-tuning scenario.

Conclusion: Iterative magnitude pruning can produce winning tickets under the non-stationary data regime of active learning. The per-iteration sparse models can address two computational bottlenecks in deep active learning: per-round model retraining and acquisition scoring over large unlabeled pools, enabling practical adoption on large architectures.

Abstract: The lottery ticket hypothesis posits the existence of winning tickets: sparse subnetworks that, when trained in isolation from their original initialization, match the accuracy of the full dense network. The predominant method for discovering such tickets, iterative magnitude pruning, alternates pruning with full retraining from scratch until convergence over many cycles. Similarly, deep active learning also retrains a model from scratch after each acquisition round as new labels become available. Despite this shared reliance on iterative retraining with a substantial computational overhead, the two paradigms have been studied separately. We observe that the iterative training loop inherent to pool-based active learning already provides the exact computational structure that iterative magnitude pruning exploits, and propose Improve & Prune (I&P), a method that integrates magnitude pruning into each active learning retraining cycle at practically no additional cost. This raises a key empirical question: can iterative magnitude pruning produce winning tickets under the non-stationary data regime of active learning? We investigate this question across multiple acquisition functions, architecture families, and image classification datasets, including an active fine-tuning scenario. Our results demonstrate that I&P yields sparse, deployable models at each active learning iteration. Those match the accuracy of their dense counterparts at sparsities up to 95%, effectively obtaining winning tickets as a byproduct of the active learning pipeline. These per-iteration sparse models can address two computational bottlenecks - per-round model retraining and acquisition scoring over the unlabeled pool - that currently prevent the practical adoption of DAL on large architectures and large unlabeled pools.

</details>


### [226] [A Later Test Set Is Not a New Domain: Pretraining Familiarity Survives a Contamination-Free Hold-Out](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10357)
*Mahdi Naser Moghadasi,Faezeh Ghaderi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper reveals that time-series foundation models benefit from domain familiarity rather than general temporal knowledge. When evaluated on a temporal hold-out postdating model releases, pretrained models win 5/7 groups but lose to classical methods on others. The key advantage comes from corpus familiarity—TimesFM outperforms on Wikipedia data (its pretraining bulk) even within the pretrained model family.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Public time-series benchmarks predate the models being evaluated, causing evaluation scores to be inflated by memorization of test data during pretraining. The authors sought to create a proper hold-out dataset and understand what actually drives pretrained model performance.

Method: The authors built a temporal hold-out dataset with 13 forecasters (4 classical, 3 trained per dataset, 6 pretrained) evaluated on 7 groups from 5 domains, all with observations published after the last model release. They tested two intrinsic properties (seasonal strength and spectral entropy) and corpus familiarity as predictors of model performance.

Result: Pretrained models won 5/7 groups but lost one to Theta baseline and performed like seasonal naive on daily exchange rates. Intrinsic properties (seasonal strength, spectral entropy) did not explain performance patterns—seasonal strength was negatively associated with pretrained advantage. Corpus familiarity tracked performance: TimesFM family outperformed Chronos on Wikipedia (rank -0.53 vs -0.09 elsewhere, p < 1e-5), with 28% lower MASE than best classical method on weekly Wikipedia pageviews.

Conclusion: Temporal hold-outs remove window memorization but not domain familiarity. Benchmarks need domain hold-outs stated relative to disclosed corpora. For practitioners, the key question is not which model is better, but whether their specific domain was well-represented in the model's pretraining corpus.

Abstract: Time-series foundation models are evaluated almost exclusively on public archives that predate them, so a strong score cannot be separated from having seen the test set during pretraining. The obvious remedy is a hold-out that postdates the models. We build one: thirteen forecasters -- four classical, three trained per dataset, six pretrained -- on seven groups drawn from five domains, every observation published after the last model was released, and every dataset rebuildable without an API key. Under this protocol pretrained models win 5 of 7 groups, lose one to a Theta baseline, and on daily exchange rates are indistinguishable from a seasonal naive forecast, along with every other method tested.
  We then ask what separates the wins from the losses, and report a negative result: the two intrinsic properties one would reach for -- seasonal strength and spectral entropy, measured on the input window -- do not account for the pattern, and seasonal strength is if anything negatively associated with the advantage. What does track it is corpus familiarity. Our largest gain (28% lower MASE than the best classical method, on weekly Wikipedia pageviews) falls on Wikipedia pageviews, the domain TimesFM's authors describe as the bulk of its pretraining corpus, at the same granularities and differing only in time window. Within the pretrained family, where every model forecasts identical series so that series difficulty cancels, the TimesFM family outranks the Chronos family by -0.53 ranks on Wikipedia against -0.09 everywhere else (1,500 vs. 754 series, Mann-Whitney p < 1e-5). We conclude that a temporal hold-out removes memorisation of a window but not familiarity with a domain, that benchmarks therefore need domain hold-outs stated relative to disclosed corpora, and that the practitioner's question is less which model is better than whether their domain is one the model was raised on.

</details>


### [227] [OmniMed-FL: A Robust Multimodal Federated Learning Framework for Clinical Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10364)
*Ayush Debnath,Ruelia Saha,Sudip Misra*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: OmniMed-FL is a federated learning framework for multimodal clinical condition classification (Normal, Pneumonia, COVID-19, Pleural Effusion, Cardiomegaly) using chest radiographs and synthetic clinical notes. The study benchmarks multiple fusion strategies and federated algorithms under non-IID conditions, finding FedProx performs best (0.737 macro-F1) and multimodal fusion outperforms unimodal approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard machine learning cannot analyze medical imaging and patient records together, while HIPAA and GDPR regulations constrain centralized aggregation of sensitive patient data. This creates a need for secure fusion of visual and textual context across distant networks without centralizing data.

Method: The framework benchmarks eight fusion strategies, three initializations, four missing-text imputation rules, and matched federated baselines (FedAvg, FedProx, FedMME-style one-shot ensemble, SCAFFOLD-AdamW adaptation) under non-IID Dirichlet partitioning across 3 to 20 hospital clients. Uses 3,000 public chest radiographs paired with 3,000 class-conditioned synthetic notes (matched by class, not patient).

Result: With K=5 clients and severe skew (α=0.1): local-only achieves 0.297 macro-F1, FedAvg achieves 0.662±0.074, FedProx achieves 0.737±0.085, FedMME-style ensemble achieves 0.647±0.080, and SCAFFOLD-AdamW achieves 0.070±0.015. Label skew costs up to 0.27 F1 while a sevenfold client increase costs at most 0.10. Bidirectional volume grows linearly to 183.5 GiB at K=20. Multimodal fusion scores 0.956 vs 0.934 for text-only and 0.664 for image-only on synthetic corpus, and 0.906 vs 0.880 and 0.737 on radiograph corpus.

Conclusion: The paper presents OmniMed-FL, a controlled systems study demonstrating the feasibility of multimodal federated learning for clinical diagnosis. The study shows that FedProx outperforms other federated methods (macro-F1 0.737 vs 0.662 for FedAvg), that label skew has greater impact than client scaling on performance, and that multimodal fusion consistently outperforms unimodal approaches (0.956 vs 0.934 for text and 0.664 for images). The authors emphasize these are proxy comparisons using synthetic data, not diagnostic performance estimates.

Abstract: Simultaneous assessment of medical imaging and patient records is often required in clinical diagnosis. However, standard machine learning algorithms cannot analyze these data types together. Meanwhile, compliance with HIPAA and GDPR can constrain centralized aggregation of sensitive patient data. This leaves a crucial void of secure fusion of visual and textual context across distant networks. Thus, we present OmniMed-FL, a controlled systems study of multimodal federated learning for five-class clinical condition classification (Normal, Pneumonia, COVID-19, Pleural Effusion, Cardiomegaly). Our proxy corpus pairs 3,000 public chest radiographs with 3,000 class-conditioned synthetic notes, matched by class, not by patient. The framework benchmarks eight fusion strategies, three initializations, four missing-text imputation rules, and matched federated baselines under non-IID Dirichlet partitioning across 3 to 20 hospital clients. As all notes are synthetic and pairing is not patient-level, these are descriptive proxy comparisons, not estimates of diagnostic performance or deployment readiness. Within those limits with clients ($K=5$) and severe skew ($α=0.1$), local-only training achieves a macro-F1 score of 0.297, FedAvg achieves $0.662\pm0.074$, FedProx $0.737\pm0.085$, a matched FedMME-style one-shot ensemble $0.647\pm0.080$, and our SCAFFOLD-AdamW adaptation $0.070\pm0.015$, the 0.075 FedProx-FedAvg gap falling inside the wider of the two two-seed standard deviations. Over a $4\times3$ grid, label skew costs up to 0.27 F1 whereas a near-sevenfold client increase costs at most 0.10, while bidirectional volume grows linearly to 183.5 GiB at $K=20$. Multimodal fusion leads on both corpora, scoring 0.956 against 0.934 for text and 0.664 for images on the synthetic corpus and 0.906 against 0.880 and 0.737 on the radiograph corpus, for $2.3\times$ the model state of text alone.

</details>


### [228] [Forgetting Only What Matters: Layer-Selective Unlearning toward Robust LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10439)
*Ravi Ranjan,Olivera Kotevska,Agoritsa Polyzou*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FOM-UL is a layer-level machine unlearning framework for LLMs that selects transformer layers using a forget-to-retain significance score to concentrate updates where most effective, while preserving most of the model unchanged. It aims to improve forgetting-utility trade-off and provide quantization-resilient unlearning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs can memorize and reproduce sensitive, copyrighted, or undesirable training content, creating privacy, safety, and regulatory concerns. Existing machine unlearning methods apply broad/fixed parameter updates that can degrade utility and remain brittle under deployment changes like post-training quantization, where forgotten knowledge may partially re-emerge.

Method: FOM-UL selects transformer layers using a forget-to-retain significance score that identifies layers with high influence on the forget set and low sensitivity to the retain set. This targeted update strategy concentrates updates where most effective while leaving most of the model unchanged.

Result: Across TOFU, KnowUnDo, and MUSE-style evaluations, FOM-UL reduces residual memorization compared with strong baselines (GA, NPO, KLD, SURE, ReLearn, LUNAR) while preserving retain-set utility close to the vanilla model. Under 8-bit and 4-bit post-training quantization, FOM-UL maintains stronger memorization suppression and utility preservation than competing methods, with lower recovery of forgotten content under adversarial prompts.

Conclusion: FOM-UL provides an efficient unlearning strategy that improves targeted forgetting, utility preservation, and deployment robustness without claiming formal guarantees of erasure.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) can memorize and reproduce sensitive, copyrighted, or otherwise undesirable training content, creating privacy, safety, and regulatory concerns. Machine unlearning offers a practical alternative to full retraining, but many existing methods apply broad or fixed parameter updates that can degrade utility and remain brittle under deployment changes such as post-training quantization, where forgotten knowledge may partially re-emerge. We propose Forgetting Only What Matters via Unlearning Layers (FOM-UL), a layer-level unlearning framework that selects transformer layers using a forget-to-retain significance score. This score identifies layers with high influence on the forget set and low sensitivity to the retain set, allowing FOM-UL to concentrate updates where they are most effective while leaving most of the model unchanged. This targeted update strategy improves the forgetting-utility trade-off and provides an empirical path toward quantization-resilient unlearning by reducing the chance that small, diffuse updates are erased by low-bit rounding. Across TOFU, KnowUnDo, and MUSE-style evaluations, FOM-UL reduces residual memorization compared with strong GA, NPO, KLD, SURE, ReLearn, and LUNAR-based baselines while preserving retain-set utility close to the vanilla model. Under 8-bit and 4-bit post-training quantization, FOM-UL maintains stronger memorization suppression and utility preservation than competing methods, and adversarial prompt evaluations show lower recovery of forgotten content. Overall, FOM-UL provides an efficient unlearning strategy that improves targeted forgetting, utility preservation, and deployment robustness without claiming formal guarantees of erasure.

</details>


### [229] [Semigroup-JEPA: Latent Dynamics Consistency for Zero-Shot Physics Generalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10464)
*Andy Zeyi Liu,Haoran Sun,Lucas Baker,Randall Balestriero,John Sous*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SG-JEPA extends JEPA world models to learn physics by supplying physics parameters (gravity) to the temporal model via action-conditioning and jointly training encoder/predictor through autoregressive latent rollout. It outperforms DINO-WM by reducing open-loop prediction error by 2x on 2D datasets and increasing control success rate by 2.5x on 3D robotic datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Joint-Embedding Predictive Architecture (JEPA) world models learn compact latent representations for prediction and planning, but their ability to learn physics and generate physically realistic dynamics remains untested. The work aims to evaluate generalization to out-of-distribution dynamical tasks under different gravitational fields.

Method: Three key methods: (1) Action-conditioning supplies physics parameters (gravity) to the temporal model; (2) Joint training of encoder and predictor through autoregressive latent rollout; (3) Independent diffusion policies for control. Additionally, a linear feature model was developed to analyze local law-conditioned error and its recursive amplification under rollout.

Result: SG-JEPA reduces open-loop prediction error by up to 2 times on 2D datasets and increases control success rate up to 2.5 times for 3D robotic datasets compared to DINO-WM. Analysis reveals most performance gain comes from the encoder learning better features (that the predictor can carry forward) rather than the predictor learning better dynamics.

Conclusion: The key insight is that back-propagating multi-step rollout loss trains the encoder to keep features that the predictor can carry forward, and those are precisely the features the dynamics depend on. Thus, the advantage of SG-JEPA stems primarily from the encoder learning better representations rather than improved predictor dynamics.

Abstract: Joint-Embedding Predictive Architecture (JEPA) world models learn a compact latent representation of the world that supports prediction and planning, but their capability to learn physics and generate physically realistic dynamics remains hitherto untested. In this work, we introduce SemiGroup-JEPA (SG-JEPA), which extends the LeWorldModel framework by supplying the parameter governing the physics to the temporal model via action-conditioning and jointly training an encoder and predictor through an autoregressive latent rollout. To evaluate the model's ability to generalize out of distribution, we design dynamical tasks under different gravitational fields that, despite obeying the same physical law, exhibit qualitatively different dynamics, ranging from floating motion in weak gravitational fields to rapid bouncing in strong ones. In contrast to DINO-WM, SG-JEPA reduces open-loop prediction error by up to 2 times on two-dimensional datasets, and increases control success rate up to 2.5 times for three-dimensional robotic datasets, for which we train independent diffusion policies.
