To explain this advantage, we develop a linear feature model that separates local law-conditioned error from its recursive amplification under rollout. Guided by this model, we find that back-propagating the multi-step rollout loss into the representation trains the encoder to keep the features that the predictor can carry forward, and that those are the features the dynamics depend on, so most of the gain comes from the encoder learning better features rather than from the predictor learning better dynamics. See project page at https://sg-jepa.github.io.

</details>


### [230] [Nonmaximal sums of maximally monotone operators under Rockafellar's constraint qualification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10487)
*Weifeng Yang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper provides counterexamples to Rockafellar's sum conjecture, which posits that the sum of two maximally monotone operators satisfying the interior-domain condition should also be maximally monotone. The authors construct counterexamples on $c_0$ and $\ell^1$ spaces, and develop a general construction theorem for analyzing monotone polar graphs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Rockafellar's sum conjecture is a fundamental question in monotone operator theory and convex analysis. The conjecture suggests that the sum of two maximally monotone operators should remain maximally monotone if certain conditions (like the interior-domain condition) are satisfied. Proving or disproving this conjecture has significant implications for the theory of monotone operators and its applications in optimization.

Method: The authors develop a general construction theorem that: (1) computes the entire monotone polar of a class of graphs; (2) provides necessary and sufficient conditions for maximal monotonicity; (3) shows how positive rank-one perturbations yield non-maximal sums under specific conditions. They verify the theorem on $c_0$ and construct a bounded linear surjection from $\ell^1$ onto $c_0$ to obtain the counterexample on $\ell^1$.

Result: The authors successfully construct counterexamples to Rockafellar's sum conjecture on both $c_0$ and $\ell^1$ spaces. They demonstrate that even when two maximally monotone operators satisfy the interior-domain condition, their sum can fail to be maximally monotone. The general construction theorem provides a framework for understanding when such failures occur.

Conclusion: Rockafellar's sum conjecture is false in general. The sum of two maximally monotone operators satisfying the interior-domain condition need not be maximally monotone. The paper provides explicit counterexamples and a theoretical framework for understanding this phenomenon through the analysis of monotone polar graphs and rank-one perturbations.

Abstract: We construct counterexamples to Rockafellar's sum conjecture in which two maximally monotone operators satisfy the interior-domain condition but their sum is not maximally monotone. We give one counterexample on $c_0$ and another on $\ell^1$ with its usual norm. We establish a general construction theorem that computes the entire monotone polar of a class of graphs, gives a necessary and sufficient condition for their maximal monotonicity, and shows how a positive rank-one perturbation yields a nonmaximal sum under this condition. We verify the theorem's hypotheses and its maximality criterion on $c_0$, thereby obtaining a counterexample to the conjecture. Furthermore, we construct a bounded linear surjection from $\ell^1$ onto $c_0$ and use it to obtain the counterexample on $\ell^1$.

</details>


### [231] [Learning with Covariance Matrices: Principal Component Analysis Meets Learning with Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10490)
*Saurabh Sihag,Andrea Cavallo,Elvin Isufi,Gonzalo Mateos,Alejandro Ribeiro*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This tutorial paper presents theoretical foundations for CoVariance Neural Networks (VNNs), which are Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) designed to operate on covariance matrices as graphs. The work addresses the gap between existing GNN theory and the unique characteristics of covariance matrix data, providing mathematical analyses with implications for signal processing, including equivalence to PCA, stability bounds under finite sample perturbations, and transferability across multiscale datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Covariance matrices are ubiquitous across domains, and GNNs often leverage graphs of pairwise statistical dependencies. However, existing theoretical contributions on GNNs consider abstract graph representations and cannot accommodate the data-driven nuances associated with covariance matrices. This creates a need for specialized theoretical frameworks for VNNs.

Method: Mathematical analysis of VNNs through theoretical derivations and proofs, examining: (i) conceptual equivalence between VNNs and PCA-based information processing; (ii) stability analysis under finite sample-induced covariance matrix perturbations; and (iii) characterization of transferability across multiscale datasets.

Result: Three novel theoretical insights: (1) VNNs are conceptually equivalent to PCA-based information processing; (2) refined stability bounds on predictive outcomes in the presence of finite sample-induced perturbations; and (3) refined characterization of transferability across multiscale datasets. Application to neuroimaging for brain age gap characterization in neurodegenerative conditions is demonstrated.

Conclusion: The theoretical insights provide underlying principles and justification for adopting VNNs over PCA-based learning pipelines in applications where covariance matrices are useful descriptors of data structure. These advances enable principled designs and applications across broad domains where covariance matrices emerge, with notable impact in computational neuroscience.

Abstract: This feature article provides an overview of the theoretical foundations for coVariance neural networks (VNNs), i.e., graph neural networks (GNNs) operating on covariance matrices as graphs. Covariance matrices are ubiquitous across domains, and hence, the deployment of GNNs often leverages graphs of pairwise statistical dependencies. Existing theoretical contributions on GNNs consider abstract graph representations and cannot accommodate the data-driven nuances associated with covariance matrices. This tutorial brings into focus various novel theoretical insights via mathematical analyses of VNNs that have broad signal processing implications, including: (i) a conceptual equivalence between VNNs and principal component analysis (PCA)-based information processing; (ii) refined stability bounds on predictive outcomes in the presence of finite sample-induced covariance matrix perturbations; and (iii) refined characterization of transferability of VNNs across multiscale datasets. The theoretical insights discussed herein provide the underlying principles and justification towards adopting VNNs over workhorse PCA-based learning pipelines, in applications where covariance matrices are useful descriptors of data structure. We also convey how impact of these foundational advances permeates to \textit{principled} designs and applications of learning methods across broad domains where covariance matrices emerge. Notably, we elucidate the conceptual insights facilitated by VNNs to the specific task of characterizing brain age gap for neurodegenerative conditions using neuroimaging datasets, a timely problem in computational neuroscience. Broader impacts to other application domains are discussed as well.

</details>


### [232] [Quantum Feature Engineering for Credit Default Prediction: When and Why IQP Circuits Help Linear Classifiers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10505)
*Menachem Finkelstein,Diana Legziel Levy,Zohar Yakhini,Sarel Cohen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether IQP (Instantaneous Quantum Polynomial-time) quantum circuits can generate features that improve credit default prediction over classical methods. Using the UCI Default of Credit Card Clients dataset with 23 financial attributes, the study finds that adding 16 IQP features (n=8 qubits) to Logistic Regression improves F1 from 0.462 to 0.517, outperforming Kernel PCA (0.493) at the same feature count.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Credit default prediction is a high-stakes tabular classification problem where modest F1 improvements translate directly to reduced financial exposure. The authors explore quantum computing's potential because IQP circuits run in constant depth, encode feature correlations in a 2^n-dimensional Hilbert space, and resist classical efficient simulation - potentially capturing patterns inaccessible to classical methods.

Method: Using the UCI Default of Credit Card Clients dataset with five-fold cross-validation, the study encoded 8 selected financial attributes as rotation angles into an 8-qubit IQP circuit, extracting 2n=16 expectation values as new features. They compared IQP-augmented Logistic Regression against raw classical baseline, Kernel PCA, Random Forest, SVM, XGBoost, and k-NN at equal feature budgets. They also tested feature selection strategies (Random Forest importance-guided vs. maximally uncorrelated).

Result: Appending 16 IQP features to Logistic Regression raised F1 from 0.462 to 0.517 (+0.055, p < 0.0001). Kernel PCA reached only 0.493 at the same feature count, with the gap significant after Benjamini-Hochberg correction (p = 0.00007). No other classifier benefited from IQP features. Random Forest importance-guided feature selection achieved F1=0.523, while maximally uncorrelated features yielded only 0.496, demonstrating the circuit amplifies informative structure rather than creating it.

Conclusion: IQP circuits can produce useful features for credit default prediction when combined with Logistic Regression, outperforming both raw baseline and Kernel PCA. The benefit is specific to linear-expressivity classifiers, not universal. Feature selection matters significantly - the circuit amplifies pre-existing informative correlations rather than generating information from nothing. This suggests quantum circuits may offer advantages for specific linear-classifier tasks ontabular financial data.

Abstract: Credit default prediction is a tabular classification problem in which modest gains in F1 translate directly into reduced financial exposure. We ask whether Instantaneous Quantum Polynomial-time (IQP) circuits can produce features that improve a classifier over both its raw classical baseline and Kernel PCA - the strongest unsupervised classical non-linear alternative - at an equal feature budget. The dataset provides 23 financial attributes per client; for an n-qubit circuit we select n of them, encode each as a rotation angle, and read 2n expectation values back out as new features. The motivation for using a quantum circuit is computational: an n-qubit IQP circuit runs in constant depth and encodes feature correlations in a 2^n-dimensional Hilbert space, whereas classical simulation of its exact output statistics scales exponentially in n. Using the UCI Default of Credit Card Clients dataset and five-fold cross-validation, we find that appending 16 IQP features (n = 8 qubits) to a Logistic Regression model raises F1 from 0.462 to 0.517 (+0.055, p < 0.0001). Kernel PCA, the next-best method, reaches only 0.493 at the same feature count; the gap survives Benjamini-Hochberg correction across 12 tests (p = 0.00007). No other classifier - Random Forest, SVM, XGBoost, or k-NN - benefits, which points to a linear-expressivity mechanism rather than a generic improvement. We also show that how the 8 input features are chosen matters: Random Forest importance-guided selection reaches F1 = 0.523, while encoding maximally uncorrelated features drops it to 0.496, demonstrating that the circuit amplifies informative structure rather than creating it from scratch.

</details>


### [233] [A positive resolution of the gap-entropy conjecture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10529)
*P. M. Aronow,Nathan Kallus,Patrick Lopatto*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proves the gap-entropy conjecture for fixed-confidence best-arm identification with Gaussian arms. It establishes that the optimal expected sample complexity is within constant factors of H(log(1/δ) + Ent(I)), where H is the sum of inverse squared gaps, δ is the failure probability, and Ent(I) is an entropy term based on gap distribution. An instance-independent algorithm is also provided with an additional additive term.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The gap-entropy conjecture in best-arm identification proposes a specific form for the optimal sample complexity that balances the gaps to suboptimal arms with an entropy term. Understanding this optimal sample complexity is crucial for designing efficient adaptive experiments in multi-armed bandit settings, which have applications in clinical trials, recommendation systems, and adaptive learning.

Method: The proof involves analyzing the information-theoretic lower bounds and constructing an algorithm that achieves the stated sample complexity. The approach uses the structure of Gaussian distributions with unit variance, the definition of gap parameters Δ_i, and the construction of an entropy measure Ent(I) based on the distribution of gaps across different scales (powers of 2).

Result: The paper proves that among all algorithms that identify the optimal arm with probability at least 1-δ, the optimal expected number of samples is within absolute constant factors of H(log(1/δ) + Ent(I)), where H = Σ_{i≠*} Δ_i^{-2}. Additionally, an instance-independent algorithm is provided with expected sample complexity bounded by a constant multiple of this quantity plus g^{-2}log log(e^e/g), where g = min_{i≠*} Δ_i is the minimum gap.

Conclusion: The gap-entropy conjecture is proven correct for Gaussian arms with unique optimal arms. This establishes the precise form of the optimal sample complexity, showing it depends not only on the sum of inverse squared gaps (as in prior results) but also on an entropy term capturing the distribution of gaps across scale levels. The result provides both lower and upper bounds that match up to constant factors, fully characterizing the problem.

Abstract: We prove the gap-entropy conjecture for fixed-confidence best-arm identification with independent unit-variance Gaussian arms, means in $[0,1]$, and a unique optimal arm. For each suboptimal arm $i$, let $Δ_i=μ_*-μ_i$ be its gap from the optimal mean, and write $H=\sum_{i\ne *}Δ_i^{-2}$. Let $p_r$ be the fraction of $H$ contributed by arms with $2^{-(r+1)}<Δ_i\le2^{-r}$, and let $\mathrm{Ent}(I)=\sum_{r:p_r>0} p_r\log(1/p_r)$. Among all algorithms that identify the optimal arm with probability at least $1-δ$ on every Gaussian instance, the optimal expected number of samples on a given instance, averaged over all permutations of the arm labels, is within absolute constant factors of $H(\log(1/δ)+\mathrm{Ent}(I))$. Moreover, there is an algorithm, independent of the instance, whose expected number of samples is bounded by a constant multiple of this quantity plus $g^{-2}\log\log(e^e/g)$, where $g=\min_{i\ne *}Δ_i$ is the gap to the closest competitor.

</details>


<div id='cs.SD'></div>

# cs.SD [[Back]](#toc)

### [234] [Voice or Stereotype? Disentangling Acoustic and Content-Based Gender in Speech-to-Speech Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09263)
*Xiaoqun Liu,Tanu Mitra,Harshit Rajgarhia,Abhishek Mukherji*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether speech-to-speech (S2S) models determine speaker gender from voice or from stereotypical content in speech. The study tests 5 models across English, Spanish, and Mandarin using a controlled experiment with male/female voices crossed with masculine/neutral/feminine-stereotyped passages.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: S2S models are increasingly used in dubbing, translation, and voice agents. These models hear the speaker's voice (which carries gender information), but existing evaluation methods use fixed output voices that cannot detect bias. The authors aim to expose hidden gender bias in S2S systems that current audits miss.

Method: Controlled experiment crossing male and female voices with masculine-, neutral-, and feminine-stereotyped passages, tested on five open- and closed-source models across three languages (English, Spanish, Mandarin). Two questions were addressed: (1) Voice rendering - does stereotype in words shift perceived gender of output voice? (2) Gender attribution - does the model state speaker's gender based on voice or content?

Result: Rendered voice shows no stereotype drift. However, every model determines speaker gender from content, not voice. Making content one step more feminine (masculine → neutral → feminine) multiplies odds of "female" judgment by 1.7-24x. When content clashes with voice, worst model misgenders 90% of speakers. When they agree, misgendering drops to 2%.

Conclusion: Gender bias in S2S models hides in gender attribution (where fixed-voice evaluation cannot detect it), not in voice rendering. Current audits using fixed output voices will miss this bias entirely. As S2S systems increasingly speak for real people, audits must incorporate gender attribution testing to catch misgendering when content and voice disagree.

Abstract: Speech-to-speech (S2S) models now run inside dubbing, translation, and voice agents. Unlike text models, they hear the speaker's voice, which carries the speaker's gender. A faithful system should treat a speaker as who they sound like, not as whoever usually says what they said. Testing this is harder than it looks, since most S2S models answer in a single, fixed output voice, hard-coded so it cannot drift toward a stereotype. Checking the output voice comes back clean even when the model is biased. We therefore ask two questions. When a model re-speaks the input, does the stereotype in the words shift the perceived gender of the output voice (voice rendering)? And when the model states the speaker's gender, does it follow the voice or the content (gender attribution)? We answer both with one controlled experiment crossing male and female voices with masculine-, neutral-, and feminine-stereotyped passages, on five open- and closed-source models in English, Spanish, and Mandarin. The rendered voice shows no stereotype drift. But every model decides the speaker's gender from the content, not the voice. Making the content one step more feminine (masculine -> neutral -> feminine) multiplies the odds of a "female" judgment by 1.7-24. When the content clashes with the voice, the worst model misgenders the speaker in 90% of cases. When they agree, it misgenders in only 2%. The bias thus hides in gender attribution, where fixed-voice evaluation cannot see, and where audits must look as S2S systems increasingly speak for real people.

</details>


### [235] [Beyond Accuracy: ARIA-Rubrics for Evaluating Audio Reasoning in Large Audio Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09681)
*Yupei Li,Qiyang Sun,Mohamed Mady,Chenxi Wang,Zhengwei Gong,Berrak Sisman,Björn Schller*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: ARIA-Rubrics is a lightweight, annotation-free framework for evaluating audio reasoning quality in Large Audio Language Models (LALMs). It uses Chain-of-Thought prompting to externalize reasoning processes and comprises six complementary metrics across three dimensions: perceptual grounding, reasoning coherence, and answer consistency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current audio reasoning benchmarks only measure accuracy, which cannot distinguish true reasoning from superficial pattern matching. High scores may result from guessing rather than genuine audio understanding. Existing evaluation methods rely on costly human annotation or opaque LLM-as-judge approaches, making them impractical, biased, and lacking transparency. Additionally, audio reasoning introduces unique challenges (perceptual hallucination and cross-modal alignment) that text-based evaluation frameworks cannot address.

Method: The proposed ARIA-Rubrics framework uses Chain-of-Thought prompting as an externalization mechanism to make reasoning processes observable. It comprises six complementary metrics evaluating audio reasoning across three dimensions: (1) perceptual grounding, (2) reasoning coherence, and (3) answer consistency. The framework is automatic, transparent, and does not require human annotation.

Result: Experiments on 9 models across 2 benchmarks identified three reasoning modes of current LALMs with actionable directions for future development. ARIA-Rubrics achieves high correlation with human judgments, demonstrating its effectiveness as an evaluation metric.

Conclusion: ARIA-Rubrics provides a practical, unbiased, and transparent solution for evaluating audio reasoning in LALMs. It addresses the unique challenges of audio reasoning that text-based frameworks cannot handle. The framework offers actionable insights for improving LALMs' reasoning abilities through the identification of three distinct reasoning modes.

Abstract: Large Audio Language Models (LALMs) have shown strong performance on audio reasoning benchmarks, but accuracy alone cannot distinguish true reasoning from superficial pattern matching, often overestimating reasoning ability since high scores may result from guessing rather than genuine audio understanding. Evaluating the reasoning process itself is essential for improving LALMs' reasoning ability, yet remains challenging. Existing methods either rely on costly human annotation or opaque LLM-as-judge approaches, making them impractical, biased, and lacking transparency. Moreover, audio reasoning introduces unique challenges absent in text-based settings, perceptual hallucination and cross-modal alignment between audio understanding and textual inference, hence text-based evaluation frameworks cannot be directly applied. Therefore, we propose ARIA-Rubrics (Audio Reasoning Integrity Assessment), a lightweight, annotation-free gold reasoning chains, automatic and transparent framework comprising six complementary metrics that evaluate audio reasoning quality across perceptual grounding, reasoning coherence, and answer consistency. We use Chain-of-Thought prompting as an externalization mechanism to make the reasoning process observable. Experiments on 9 models across 2 benchmarks identify three reasoning modes of current LALMs with actionable directions for future development, with ARIA-Rubrics achieving high correlation with human judgments. The code is available at the Github Repository.

</details>


### [236] [Arti-JEPA: Adapting Video World Model to Real-Time MRI of the Vocal Tract for Speech-Production Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09757)
*Hong Nguyen,Sean Foley,Christina Hagedorn,Yijing Lu,Sudarsana Reddy Kadiri,Dani Byrd,Shrikanth Narayanan*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: Arti-JEPA is a self-supervised model trained on 62 hours of unlabeled real-time MRI (rtMRI) vocal tract videos to learn representations, which are then evaluated on three downstream tasks: cross-domain phoneme prediction, stuttering classification, and pre/post-operative transfer analysis after partial glossectomy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-time MRI captures vocal tract dynamics during speech, but labeled data are scarce and the modality (single-slice, grayscale, low-resolution) differs significantly from natural videos that existing video foundation models are trained on. This domain gap limits the applicability of pre-trained models to rtMRI speech analysis.

Method: The researchers used a Joint Embedding Predictive Architecture (JEPA) approach, training self-supervised representations on approximately 62 hours of unlabeled vocal tract rtMRI videos. They compared temporal video priors against per-frame image encoders, and latent prediction (V-JEPA) against pixel reconstruction (VideoMAE). Domain adaptation was also evaluated.

Result: (1) Temporal video priors significantly outperform per-frame image encoders, with latent prediction (V-JEPA) matching or exceeding pixel reconstruction (VideoMAE), especially for fine-grained phonemes. (2) Domain adaptation is task-dependent: it roughly doubles cross-domain phoneme prediction κ (to 0.352) but provides no benefit for binary stuttering classification. (3) Arti-JEPA can recover phoneme signals from pre/post glossectomy speech, with in-domain probes decoding patients at least as well as typical speakers, indicating the transfer gap is due to cross-speaker/domain misalignment rather than surgical signal loss.

Conclusion: A frozen, domain-adapted rtMRI encoder can serve as a reusable measurement tool for articulatory and clinical speech science. The residual transfer gap in cross-domain scenarios is attributed to speaker/domain misalignment rather than actual signal loss, enabling applications in post-operative speech assessment.

Abstract: Real-time MRI (rtMRI) captures the dynamics of the entire vocal tract during speech, but labeled data are scarce and the modality - single-slice, grayscale, low-resolution - differs substantially from the natural videos that video foundation models are trained on. We introduce Arti-JEPA, a joint embedding predictive architecture to model vocal tract rtMRI by continuing its self-supervised objective on about 62h of unlabelled vocal-tract videos, and evaluate the frozen representation on three tasks: cross-domain phoneme prediction (on typical speakers), fluent-vs-disfluent classification (a corpus containing stuttered speech), and characterizing pre/post-operative transfer (after partial glossectomy). Three key findings emerge. (1) A temporal video prior decisively outperforms per-frame image encoders, and latent prediction (V-JEPA) is at least as strong as pixel reconstruction (VideoMAE), with the edge on fine-grained phonemes. (2) Domain adaptation is \emph{task-dependent}: it roughly doubles cross-domain phoneme prediction $κ$ (to 0.352) but does not help binary stuttering classification. (3) Arti-JEPA was able to recover phoneme signal from pre/post glossectomy speech --- an in-domain probe decodes patients at least as well as a typical speaker, indicating that the residual transfer gap is cross-speaker/domain misalignment, not surgical signal loss, and post-operative decoding does not fall below performance on pre-operative speech. Together, these position a frozen, domain-adapted rtMRI encoder as a reusable measurement tool for articulatory and clinical speech science.

</details>


### [237] [Robust Rank Aggregation for Multimodal Speech-Based Alzheimer's Disease Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09948)
*Zemin Jin,Tomoko Matsui*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper proposes a robust rank aggregation framework for speech-based Alzheimer's disease detection that addresses the problem of mismatched posterior probability scales across multimodal foundation models. The method uses normalized prediction ranks instead of probabilities, combined with a confidence-gated Random Forest for error correction, achieving 95.83% accuracy on ADReSS2020 and 90.14% on ADReSSo2021.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to solve the problem of unreliable probability averaging in multimodal AD detection. Conventional probability averaging fails because posterior probabilities from different classifiers have mismatched scales - identical values may reflect different confidence levels across models. This makes direct probability aggregation unreliable.

Method: The proposed method consists of two main components: (1) A robust rank aggregation framework that aggregates normalized prediction ranks instead of posterior probabilities. Each subject is scored by its percentile within a fixed training-cohort distribution of out-of-fold predictions. Since rank ordering is invariant to monotonic transformations, this avoids probability-scale mismatch while preserving classifier confidence ordering. (2) A confidence-gated Random Forest that uses clinically interpretable linguistic features to correct residual errors, overriding the rank prediction only when the two disagree and the RF is highly confident.

Result: The method achieves accuracies of 95.83% on ADReSS2020 and 90.14% on ADReSSo2021, which compares favorably with previously reported results.

Conclusion: The rank aggregation approach successfully addresses the probability-scale mismatch problem in multimodal AD detection without requiring additional deep model training or explicit posterior-probability calibration. The confidence-gated Random Forest provides interpretable error correction while maintaining high accuracy.

Abstract: Speech-based Alzheimer's disease (AD) detection has recently benefited from multimodal foundation-model representations that integrate complementary acoustic and linguistic information. However, conventional probability averaging over these complementary classifiers is unreliable, because their posterior probabilities exhibit mismatched scales: identical values may reflect different confidence levels across models. We propose a robust rank aggregation framework that aggregates normalized prediction ranks instead of posterior probabilities. Each subject is scored by its percentile within a fixed training-cohort distribution of out-of-fold predictions; since rank ordering is invariant to monotonic transformations, this avoids probability-scale mismatch while preserving classifier confidence ordering. A confidence-gated Random Forest further corrects residual errors using clinically interpretable linguistic features, overriding the rank prediction only when the two disagree and the RF is highly confident, without additional deep model training or explicit posterior-probability calibration. On ADReSS2020 and ADReSSo2021, the method achieves accuracies of 95.83% and 90.14%, respectively, comparing favorably with previously reported results.

</details>


### [238] [TimeCues Studio: A Workspace for Music Annotation and Algorithm Prototyping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10338)
*Sapir Caduri,Yoav Goldberg*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: TimeCues Studio is an open-source, team-oriented workspace for music annotation, algorithm comparison, and prototyping. It supports ambiguity-aware labeling on a feature-rich timeline, includes a Python sandbox for new models, and deploys via Docker Compose.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multimedia applications require precise music annotations (positions, segments, loops), but annotated training data is scarce. Existing tools are designed for single-track annotation and lack tight integration with algorithm development workflows.

Method: TimeCues Studio provides a collaborative annotation environment where teams annotate entire music collections on a grid-locked timeline supporting multiple marker types with ambiguity-aware labeling. It integrates an algorithm-comparison engine with baseline algorithms, a Python sandbox for prototyping new models, and an ambiguity-aware evaluator.

Result: TimeCues enables teams to annotate whole collections, compare detection algorithms against annotations, and prototype new models within the same workspace. It visualizes many music features including separated audio stems and supports solo annotators for music-sync projects.

Conclusion: TimeCues Studio fills a gap in the music annotation ecosystem by providing a tightly integrated tool for team-based annotation and algorithm development. It is MIT-licensed and deploys with a single Docker Compose command, making it accessible for research teams.

Abstract: Multimedia applications require precise music annotation-labeled positions, segments, or loops-placed by hand or algorithmically. Machine-learning algorithms are scalable and effective but need annotated training data, scarce for many tasks. TimeCues Studio is an open-source workspace where algorithm-development teams annotate a music corpus, compare detection algorithms against those annotations, and prototype new ones. Unlike existing tools built for a single track at a time, TimeCues targets teams annotating whole collections, tightly integrated with algorithm development. Annotators place several marker types-each supporting ambiguity-aware labeling-on a grid-locked timeline that visualizes many music features, including separated audio stems. The same timeline drives an algorithm-comparison engine with bundled baselines, a Python sandbox for prototyping new models, and an ambiguity-aware evaluator that honors the structured fields. The same visualization suits solo annotators on music-sync projects. TimeCues is MIT-licensed and deploys via one Docker Compose command.

</details>


### [239] [Deterministic Prompting for Speaker-Stable Low-Resource Greek TTS](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10022)
*Georgios Syllas,Efthymios Georgiou,Kosmas Kritsis,Alexandros Potamianos*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper presents a data curation and fine-tuning approach to build a Greek TTS system using limited data. The method transforms audiobook recordings into TTS-ready data via WhisperX, fine-tunes Parler-TTS (880M), and uses speaker-specific LoRA to anchor speaker identity. The system achieves WER 10.7%, MOS-I 4.00, and MOS-C 4.24, demonstrating that robust single-speaker Greek TTS is achievable with limited curated data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern TTS systems achieve human-quality synthesis for high-resource languages but degrade significantly when clean speech data is scarce. Modern Greek exemplifies this challenge, lacking the curated corpora that underpin state-of-the-art synthesis systems. The authors aim to address this data scarcity problem for Greek TTS.

Method: The authors propose a three-stage approach: (1) Data curation using WhisperX alignment and filtering to transform audiobook recordings into TTS-ready data; (2) Fine-tuning Parler-TTS (880M), a prompt-based multilingual model whose pre-training encodes phonetic priors transferable to Greek; (3) Addressing speaker drift by replacing LLM-generated style prompts with deterministic prompts, and using a speaker-specific LoRA stage trained on 3.5 hours of single-speaker data to anchor speaker identity while updating ~5% of parameters.

Result: The system achieves WER of 10.7% (only 2.9 above the ASR floor), MOS-I (intelligibility) of 4.00 compared to 4.36 for human speech, and near-human speaker consistency with MOS-C of 4.24 versus 4.30 for human speech.

Conclusion: Robust single-speaker Greek TTS is achievable with limited curated data. The combination of proper data curation, leveraging multilingual pretrained models with phonetic priors, and speaker-specific LoRA adaptation provides an effective solution for low-resource language TTS development.

Abstract: Modern TTS systems approach human quality for high-resource languages but degrade when clean speech data is scarce. Modern Greek exemplifies this, lacking the curated corpora behind state-of-the-art synthesis. We propose a data curation recipe that transforms audiobook recordings into TTS-ready data via WhisperX alignment and filtering. Then we fine-tune Parler-TTS (880M), a prompt-based multilingual model whose pre-training encodes phonetic priors transferable to Greek. During development, we find that LLM-generated style prompts introduce speaker drift at inference. Replacing them with deterministic prompts resolves this, and a speaker-specific LoRA stage trained on 3.5 h of single-speaker data anchors identity while updating ~5% of parameters. Our system achieves WER 10.7% (2.9 above the ASR floor), MOS-I 4.00 (vs. 4.36 human speech), and near-human speaker consistency (MOS-C 4.24 vs. 4.30), showing that robust single-speaker Greek TTS is achievable with limited curated data.

</details>


### [240] [Zero-Shot Temporal Localisation of Audio Deepfakes in Multi-Speaker Conversations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10051)
*Soumyadeep Roy*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper addresses voice-cloning fraud via "surgical injection" in multi-speaker conversations, where only 1-2 sentences are replaced by synthetic speech. Current utterance-level detectors cannot localize synthetic segments, so the authors formalize Temporal Deepfake Localisation in Multi-Speaker Conversations (TDLMC) and propose a training-free five-stage pipeline using a frozen binary detector with a two-threshold hysteresis finite-state-machine decoder to identify synthetic speech intervals without retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Voice-cloning fraud increasingly uses surgical injection - replacing only 1-2 sentences in a genuine conversation with synthetic speech. Existing utterance-level deepfake detectors only emit a single real/fake label per clip and cannot identify where synthetic speech occurs. Additionally, standard metrics like EER and min-DCF become ill-posed when a file contains both genuine and synthetic speech segments.

Method: A training-free five-stage pipeline that: (1) wraps a frozen binary detector, (2) adds segment-level output without retraining, (3) uses a two-threshold hysteresis finite-state-machine decoder to convert noisy window scores into coherent temporal intervals. The pipeline was evaluated on 180 constructed multi-speaker conversations from ASVspoof 5, testing across three frozen detectors with constants selected on a held-out calibration split.

Result: With a strong backbone, the system achieves temporal IoU 0.90, temporal detection rate 0.95, and MS-DCF 0.26. False-alarm rate on genuine speech is below 6%, falling under 2% on genuine real multi-speaker dialogue (AMI). A trained localiser improves temporal IoU by only ~0.04, demonstrating the effectiveness of the training-free approach.

Conclusion: This work provides the first zero-shot baseline and reusable benchmark for TDLMC. The training-free pipeline achieves strong performance with minimal supervision cost, and the residual false-alarm rate is attributed to backbone domain gap rather than the decoder. The approach bounds the cost of forgoing supervision and offers a practical solution for temporal deepfake localization in multi-speaker conversations.

Abstract: Voice-cloning fraud increasingly relies on surgical injection: a genuine conversation in which only one or two sentences are replaced by synthetic speech. Utterance-level deepfake detectors emit a single real/fake label per clip and cannot report where the synthetic speech lies. We formalise this as Temporal Deepfake Localisation in Multi-Speaker Conversations (TDLMC), show that equal error rate and min-DCF are ill-posed once a file contains both classes, and propose temporal metrics for this regime. Our contribution is a training-free five-stage pipeline that wraps a frozen binary detector and adds segment-level output with no retraining, using a two-threshold hysteresis finitestate-machine decoder to turn noisy window scores into coherent intervals. On 180 constructed multi-speaker conversations from ASVspoof 5, the system attains temporal intersection-over-union 0.90, temporal detection rate 0.95, and MS-DCF 0.26 with a strong backbone, and its false-alarm rate on genuine speech is below 6%, falling under 2% on genuine real multi-speaker dialogue (AMI). Under an identical pipeline, a trained localiser improves temporal IoU by only about 0.04, bounding the cost of forgoing supervision. Evaluated across three frozen detectors under one decoder whose constants are selected on a held-out calibration split, and with a controlled analysis attributing the residual false-alarm rate to a backbone domain gap rather than to the decoder, this provides the first zero-shot baseline and a reusable benchmark for TDLMC.

</details>


### [241] [Orukeet: Multilingual ASR with Frozen Gabor Kernels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10054)
*Nathan Roll,Irene Yi,Büşra Marşan,Vianney Grenez,Gabriel Stein,Momcilo Mrkaic,Pavle Padjin,Vladimir Zeljkovic,Calbert Graham*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: Orukeet improves ASR performance by replacing half of Parakeet encoder's temporal filters with 12,288 fitted Gabor kernels, achieving 10.6% relative WER reduction on FLEURS (25 languages) and outperforming Parakeet on 61/74 test splits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to improve multilingual and multi-accent speech recognition by introducing Gabor kernels into the Parakeet encoder architecture, which can better capture temporal frequency characteristics in speech signals.

Method: 1) Replace half of adapted Parakeet encoder's temporal filters with 12,288 fitted Gabor kernels; 2) Freeze the Gabor kernel replacements; 3) Train remaining parameters on multilingual and multi-accent data; 4) Use LibriSpeech test-other for final adaptation and checkpoint selection.

Result: Pooled WER on 20,146 FLEURS recordings (25 languages): 11.01% → 9.85% (10.6% relative reduction). Lower WER on 23/25 languages. Outperforms Parakeet on 61/74 tested splits. LibriSpeech test-clean: 1.46% vs 1.53% WER; test-other: 2.86% vs 3.14%; FLEURS English: 3.82% vs 4.28%.

Conclusion: Fitted Gabor kernels stored as ordinary convolution weights enable architecture retention and inference compatibility while significantly improving ASR performance across multilingual and multi-accent benchmarks.

Abstract: Orukeet replaces half of an adapted Parakeet encoder's temporal filters with 12,288 fitted Gabor kernels, freezes these replacements, and trains the remaining parameters on multilingual and multi-accent data. Final adaptation and checkpoint selection use LibriSpeech test-other. Across 20,146 FLEURS recordings in 25 languages, pooled word error rate (WER) falls from Parakeet's 11.01% to Orukeet's 9.85%, a 10.6% relative reduction. Orukeet has lower WER on 23 of the 25 languages. Orukeet outperforms Parakeet on 61 out of 74 tested splits, including LibriSpeech test-clean (1.46% vs. 1.53% WER), test-other (2.86% vs. 3.14%), and FLEURS English (3.82% vs. 4.28%). All comparisons decode the same audio with matched NeMo settings. The fitted kernels are stored as ordinary convolution weights, retaining Parakeet's architecture and inference operators.

</details>


### [242] [NOPE-HYPE: A Structured Simulation Workflow for Robust Speech-to-Text Across Diverse Acoustic Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10058)
*Niramay M. Patel,Bibek Behera,Raksha Sharma*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: NOPEHYPE is a structured training workflow for robust speech-to-text translation that combines a controllable environment simulator, coverage-optimal environment reduction on PSD templates, and hyperparameter search over simulator knobs. It achieves comparable performance to real-noise training on Whisper and SeamlessM4T models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large speech models (e.g., Whisper, SeamlessM4T) remain sensitive to unseen acoustic conditions because training data rarely cover the full range of real environments. Practical pipelines lack controllable tools for systematic environment exploration.

Method: NOPEHYPE combines three components: (1) a controllable environment simulator, (2) coverage-optimal environment reduction on Power Spectral Density (PSD) templates, and (3) a small, interpretable hyperparameter search over simulator knobs. A structured 27-run hyperparameter sweep was conducted.

Result: Simulator-generated noise achieves performance comparable to balanced real-noise training across Whisper and SeamlessM4T models. The method provides principled environment prototype sets and identifies practical default simulator configurations.

Conclusion: NOPEHYPE enables systematic environment exploration for speech translation models, offering a practical solution for improving robustness across diverse acoustic conditions without requiring extensive real-world noise data.

Abstract: Robust speech-to-text translation systems should perform reliably across diverse acoustic conditions, yet practical pipelines lack controllable tools for systematic environment exploration. Large speech models remain sensitive to unseen acoustic conditions, as training data rarely cover the full range of real environments.We present NOPEHYPE, a structured training workflow that combines a controllable environment simulator, coverage-optimal environment reduction on Power Spectral Density (PSD) templates, and a small, interpretable hyperparameter search over simulator knobs. We show that simulator-generated noise achieves performance comparable to balanced realnoise training across Whisper and SeamlessM4T models, provide principled environment prototype sets, and identify practical default simulator configurations from a structured 27-run hyperparameter sweep.

</details>


### [243] [Unifying Score and Performance for Fine-Grained Music Understanding in Audio-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10351)
*Milan Liessens Dujardin,Song-Ze Yu,Kevin Miao*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: MuNo-SP is a text-based representation that jointly encodes musical score and performance information, enabling better supervision for large audio language models (LALMs) in music understanding tasks. The authors develop an automatic training-data generation pipeline to create MAESTROCaps, a classical piano dataset with 148 long-form analyses and 31,080 QA pairs. Human evaluation shows MuNo-SP analyses are preferred over MIDI-only baselines for 8 of 9 excerpts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing audio-language model training pipelines rely on coarse, weakly grounded captions that provide little support for learning subtle nuances in music (dynamics, phrasing, articulation, timing, performance techniques). This limits their ability to serve real-world applications in education or artistic practice. The field needs finer-grained music understanding that captures both score content and performance realization.

Method: 1) Propose MuNo-SP, a text-based representation that jointly encodes score content and performance information. 2) Develop an automatic training-data generation pipeline using aligned scores and performances to produce long-form auditory analyses and musically-informed QA pairs. 3) Construct MAESTROCaps dataset (148 aligned score-performance pairs → 148 analyses + 31,080 QA pairs). 4) Conduct human evaluation comparing MuNo-SP analyses vs MIDI-only analyses.

Result: Human evaluation: MuNo-SP analyses were preferred by majority vote over MIDI-only analyses for 8 of 9 excerpts. MuNo-SP also performed strongly on a benchmark of score-performance understanding, suggesting that integrating score and performance information enables more reliable and musically informative LALM supervision than MIDI-only baseline.

Conclusion: Integrating score and performance information via MuNo-SP enables more reliable and musically informative LALM supervision than MIDI-only approaches. This addresses the limitation of coarse captions in existing ALM training pipelines and opens possibilities for real-world applications in music education and artistic practice.

Abstract: Large audio language models (LALMs) have shown promising progress in broad music-understanding tasks such as tagging, retrieval, and captioning. Music understanding that requires finer hearing over both the content and how it is realized within a performance through dynamics, phrasing, articulation, time, and other performance techniques, however, remains at an earlier stage. Existing audio-language model (ALM) training pipelines typically rely on coarse, weakly grounded captions and therefore provide little support for learning these subtle nuances in music, limiting their ability to serve real-world applications in education or artistic practice.
  We therefore introduce MuNo-SP (Music Notation unifying Score and Performance), a text-based representation that jointly encodes score content and performance information. Building on MuNo-SP, we develop an automatic training-data generation pipeline that uses aligned scores and performances to produce long-form auditory analyses and musically informed question-answer pairs. We use this pipeline to construct MAESTROCaps, a classical piano dataset comprising 148 long-form performance analyses and 31,080 question-answer pairs derived from 148 aligned score-performance pairs. In a human evaluation, MuNo-SP analyses were preferred by majority vote over MIDI-only analyses for eight of nine excerpts. MuNo-SP also performed strongly on a benchmark of score-performance understanding, suggesting that integrating score and performance information enables more reliable and musically informative LALM supervision than a MIDI-only baseline.

</details>

# cs.MM [[Back]](#toc)

### [244] [MotionCanvas: Learning Implicit Motion Planning from Composable Kinematic Cues](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10457)
*Zeyu Ling,Di Kang,Qing Shuai,Yuxin Wen,Jing Li,Zhanke Wang,Heng Li,Changqing Zou,Chunchao Guo,Linchao Bao*

Main category: cs.MM

TL;DR: MotionCanvas is a character animation model that generates natural full-body motion from heterogeneous kinematic cues (position, rotation across body joints and time). It uses a flow-matching model conditioned on a shared "motion canvas" with cue imputation to preserve specified values, achieving state-of-the-art results in controlled motion generation, mixed-cue adherence, sequential generation, instruction editing, and motion repair.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Professional character animation requires both natural motion and precise, versatile control. Artists need to define timing, motion range, character paths, and other kinematic cues - similar to a "motion canvas" where various motion parameters can be specified across body joints and time. Existing methods struggle to handle heterogeneous, dense/sparse, full/partial cues coherently.

Method: 1) Represent heterogeneous kinematic cues on a shared motion canvas (position/rotation values across body joints and time). 2) Use a shared flow-matching model to generate motion conditioned on this canvas, with optional language and input motion conditioning. 3) Apply cue imputation to keep specified canvas values fixed during both training and sampling. 4) Train with a compositional cue sampler that varies: when cues are applied, which positions/rotations are specified, and how they are combined.

Result: MotionCanvas establishes state-of-the-art results in: controlled-motion quality, mixed-cue adherence, sequential generation, instruction editing, and motion repair, while preserving text-to-motion capability. Tested with temporal, root, and body-part cues alone and in combination, plus language-guided editing.

Conclusion: MotionCanvas successfully enables a single generator to synthesize globally coherent actions that jointly satisfy compatible heterogeneous kinematic cues. The approach naturally extends to sequential generation and motion repair, as both require organizing coherent motion from kinematic cues. The method achieves superior performance across multiple evaluation scenarios while maintaining text-to-motion generation capability.

Abstract: Professional character animation requires both natural motion and precise, versatile control. For example, it is common for the creators to define the timing of a specified action, to control the motion range of the character's arm swing, and the route the character walks through, like specifying various kinematic motion cues on a ``motion canvas''. This motivates us to propose MotionCanvas, a model that supports \emph{cue-conditioned implicit motion planning} to faithfully and coherently connect all cues, dense or sparse, full or partial, into one full-body motion sequence. Specifically, MotionCanvas represents heterogeneous kinematic cues on a shared motion canvas, where position and rotation values are specified across body joints and time. A shared flow-matching model generates motion conditioned on this canvas, with optional language and input motion; cue imputation keeps the specified canvas values fixed in both training and sampling. To learn coherent completion across different cue sets, we train with a compositional cue sampler that varies when cues are applied, which positions or rotations are specified, and how they are combined.
  Together, these designs enable a single generator to synthesize globally coherent actions that jointly satisfy compatible heterogeneous cues. We test this planning ability with temporal, root, and body-part cues---alone and in combination---and language-guided editing. We naturally extend this evaluation to sequential generation and motion repair, since both require the same ability to organize coherent motion from kinematic cues. Across these evaluations, MotionCanvas establishes state-of-the-art results in controlled-motion quality, mixed-cue adherence, sequential generation, instruction editing, and motion repair while preserving its text-to-motion capability.

</details>
